CHAPTER I  1. What the chemist thinks of a substance.-When you look into a chemical laboratory, you are impressed by the large number of bottles it contains and by the 

Courtesy Fisher Scientific Co. 

FIG. 1. 

An early chemical workshop: Faraday's laboratory in the Royal 

Institution, London. strange names upon the labels. Most of the substances in the bottles are white; but some are black, pink, yellow, blue, or violet. Some are solid, others liquid. Perhaps 1

there are some strong cylinders containing gases under pressure. Why does the chemist need so many different materials? One reason is that he must use some substances to prepare others which are not easy to keep and which must be made as wanted. A second reason is that the chemist must learn to know a considerable number of substances very definitely, in order that he may recognize them afterward wherever he finds them. 

The chemist distinguishes one substance from another by its natural form, taste, odor, or color, by the way in which it behaves when he heats it, or holds it near a magnet, or applies some other test to it. The characteristics, or qualities, of each substance as determined by these tests are called the properties of that substance. 

Thus the red color of iron rust is one of its properties. 

By knowing several of its properties a chemist can decide what a given substance is. No two different substances have exactly the same set of properties. 

A few of the materials which chemists use are found, as such, in the earth's crust. Thus rock salt, sulphur, gold, copper, and quartz are found as deposits in some places. But the chemist does not often find a material in the earth as a single substance, unmixed with any other substance. Usually the substances of nature are mixed in a very complicated way. Thus, a piece of white marble is made up of small particles-crystals—which are all alike. The marble is one substance. But granite is a mixture of at least three substances: feldspar, quartz, and mica, all of which crystallize in a different way and differ from one another in their other properties. The quartz is glassy, the mica flaky (as is isinglass). The feldspar may be pink or white, but it is dull as compared with the glassy quartz. If we crush the granite, we can pick out its three ingredients and collect them in separate piles. Soil is a mixture of many substances. Natural water con-

tains many materials dissolved from rocks and soil, as rain water soaks through them. It is only by keeping clearly in mind the properties of the single substances which may be present in these natural mixtures that chemists have succeeded in unraveling the mixtures and have obtained from them single substances in separate form.

We are now ready for a definite statement: 

Every material we know is either a single substance, unmixed with anything else, or it is a mixture of two or more single substances.

This idea seems very simple, but it is a fundamental idea of chemistry. Wherever certain properties appear, there must be substances possessing those properties. 

Thus, the properties of a sample of well water must be due either to the single substance: water, or to other substances mixed with it. Similar reasoning applies to a sample of air.

Can you suggest how this idea may be extended to other materials, such as a sample of milk, the wax of a candle, the rubber of a tire, a sample of paint, a diamond, a piece of glass, some sugar, wheat flour, oak wood, ammonia water, and orange juice? Which of these, as far as you know, are single substances and which are mixtures? 

2. What the chemist thinks of changes in substances. -We can now understand that most of the single substances used in the laboratory, as well as those used in the industries and in our daily life, must be prepared from something else. They are prepared from the rocks, the water, and the air, and the bodies of plants and animals. Modern chemical science began with the study of such natural changes as the rusting of metals and the

burning of fuels. The chemist has extended this study to all the changes by which one substance may be prepared out of another. We think of the preparation of lime from limestone, iron from iron ore, rayon from cotton, soap from fats, of hard fats from soft fats (oils), of 

FIG. 2. 

Home of Joseph Priestley at Northumberland, Pennsylvania. The view shows the back of the house, facing the Susquehanna River. Here, on August 1, 1874, a group of chemists met to celebrate Priestley's discovery of oxygen and took steps to found the present American Chemical Society. jelly from fruit juices, of glucose from starch, of bread from flour, of fuel gas and dyes from coal. 

People sometimes think that in bringing about the change of one material into another the chemist is destroying something, or creating something. No idea could be farther from the truth. Chemical changes do

not cause the loss of any material or the gain of any material. The weight of the substances taken is equal to the weight of the substances produced. 

In chemical changes new substances, with new sets of properties, are always formed, but there is no gain or loss of material. 

Courtesy American Hoist & Derrick Co.

FIG. 3. 

This derrick handles scrap iron by means of a powerful electromagnet. While the current passes, the electromagnet picks up the iron. When the current is cut off, the magnet drops the iron. 

3. Physical and chemical changes.-The chemist distinguishes two kinds of changes: (1) physical, and (2) chemical. In chemical changes, as we have already learned, the properties of the substances used are altered, and new substances, with new sets of properties, are formed. A chemical change is often called a reaction, and the substances which act upon one another ("react") are called reagents.

We can define physical changes better after we have considered a few examples of such changes. We can magnetize a steel knitting needle by stroking one end of it with a magnet. In this condition it has a new property: it can attract bits of iron. Then, by heating the magnetized needle in a flame, we can demagnetize it, and it no longer attracts iron. We can electrify a glass rod by rubbing it with silk. It can then attract bits of paper or cork. 

If we draw the rod through the closed hand, it loses this property. 

We can melt ice by adding enough heat to it; we can then freeze the water by taking the heat away from it. We can pass a current through the tungsten filament of an electric bulb, and heat the filament white hot, so that it gives off a bright light. When we cut off the current, the tungsten filament becomes the same as before the current heated it. We call all these changes physical changes. Physical changes are those in which new substances are not formed. 

4. Physical and chemical properties.-Properties, as well as changes, are either physical, or chemical. The ability of iron to be magnetized and demagnetized, without ceasing to be iron, is a physical property of iron. 

The ability of glass to be electrically charged and then discharged, of ice to be changed to water and water to ice, of a tungsten wire to be heated to white heat while the current passes through it, all these are likewise physical properties of the substances named. 

A substance shows its chemical properties only when it undergoes a chemical change. Thus, the fact that iron rusts in moist air is a chemical property of iron. Before the iron has rusted, it is a bright metal, which can be hammered into sheets or drawn out into wire. It is attracted by a magnet. After the rusting, the product is no longer iron; it has a new set of properties. Iron rust is a red, earthy powder, not capable of being hammered into sheets or drawn into wire, not attracted by a magnet.

All these properties of the iron and the iron rust, except the rusting itself, are physical properties of these two substances.

In describing a solid, such as iron or iron rust, we usually give the following physical properties: its color, taste, and odor, its density, its hardness, its melting temperature, its ability to dissolve in water or other liquids, its ability to be hammered into sheets or drawn into wire. The chemical properties of a substance include the chemical changes it can undergo, especially its reactions with air and water, and with two classes of very active substances which the chemist calls acids and bases. 

5. What the chemist thinks of fire.-The phenomenon of fire has fascinated men for ages. When a piece of wood or coal burns, it seems to disappear, except for the ashes. When gasoline or a candle burns, it seems to vanish entirely. 

What happens to the burning material? For centuries men did not connect the air with the burning, but thought of fire as due to the vigorous escape of something from the burning body. The growth of clearer ideas regarding fire marked the beginning of modern chemistry.

FIG. 4. 

A porcelain cru. cible supported by means of a clay triangle upon the ring of a ringstand, so that it may be heated.

Strangely enough, the explanation of burning came as a result of the correct explanation of the rusting of metals. This was due to the fact that the metals and the "rusts" which they produce can be weighed on a balance. 

Therefore men could find out whether, in the chemical change called rusting, the weight of the metal remains the same, or grows less, or increases. 

In the burning of a candle or a piece of wood, this is

more difficult. Some metals, such as iron, undergo rusting at the ordinary temperature, but others, like lead, tin, and copper, rust rapidly only when heated. If we heat a weighed porcelain dish, containing lead, above the melting temperature 

FIG. 5. 

Joseph Priestley (1733-1804). The original of this picture hangs in the 

Priestley house. of the lead, the liquid metal is soon covered with a scum. If we continue the heating for, say, half an hour, and scrape the scum aside carefully, a considerable amount of the lead is changed into lead "rust," a yellow powder. If we cool the dish and then weigh it, we find that there has been an increase in weight.

A similar result follows the heating of tin, but in this case the rust is white. Copper does not melt as readily as lead and tin, but if it is heated red hot, it becomes covered with its rust, in the form of a black scale. In all these cases the rust weighs more than the metal, and we are forced to conclude that the rusting metal takes up some material from the air. 

The study of the change which metals undergo in rusting was helped greatly when men began to heat the metals in closed vessels instead of in the open air. With such an arrangement no air except that in the vessel could act upon the metal, and the change in the properties of the air, as well as in those of the metal, could be observed. When metals are heated in flasks from which the air has been removed, they do not rust. Why? 

6. Priestley and Lavoisier. The two great chemists, Priestley and Lavoisier, had the most to do with the

explanation of fire, although many others contributed to the result. The apparatus which Priestley used consisted of a flask filled completely with mercury and inverted in a "bath" of mercury. Priestley put various substances separately under the mouth of the flask. 

Since they were lighter than mercury, they floated to the top. He then heated the materials by means of a burning lens, and noted how they were changed. One of the substances was a red powder which we call mercuric oxide. When he heated this, it disappeared, but in its place he obtained a colorless gas. Priestley put into the gas a wood splint with a glowing tip, and the spark burst into flame. A spark does not do this in ordinary, quiet air. 

He also put a live mouse into the gas, and the mouse continued to live. Priestley called the gas "good air" and "perfect air."

SUNLIGHT MERCURY FIG. 6. 

OXYGEN MERCURIC OXIDE 

The apparatus in which Priestley decomposed mercuric oxide by the heat of the sun concentrated by a burning lens.

Lavoisier made his greatest contributions through two famous experiments. In the first of these he sealed some tin and air in a glass flask and then heated the flask for several days. The tin rusted, as when heated in an open dish. When he weighed the sealed flask, he loss, although the change of tin taken place inside. But when he opened the flask, he found that the flask had gained weight. Lavoisier reasoned that in rusting the tin had combined with somefound no gain or into tin rust had

thing from the air. The final gain in weight was due to the air which rushed in to fill the vacant space. 

Lavoisier's second experiment was with mercury instead of tin. 

In a glass retort, which was heated over a furnace, he put some mercury. The drawn-out end of the retort was bent so that it passed through a pan of mercury and up into the air of a bell jar. The air of the retort and of the bell jar was thus cut off from the outer air.

Lavoisier heated the retort for twelve days. Gradually a red powder covered the surface of the mercury in the retort. This showed that the mercury was rusting. At the same time the mercury rose higher in the bell jar.

This showed that the air in the apparatus was losing some material. When no more shrinkage in volume took place, Lavoisier let the apparatus cool and found that the gas which remained had a volume only four-fifths that of the air taken.

This gas did not behave like ordinary air. It extinguished a burning candle, and mice could not live in it.

It was the gas, nitrogen, so called because it is a constituent of niter, or saltpeter. 

FIG. 7. 

Antoine Laurent Lavoisier (17431794), the founder of modern chemistry. He explained burning as the union of the substance burned with the oxygen of the air.

When Lavoisier heated the red powder (the "rust" of mercury) that had collected in the retort, he obtained all the gas that had been lost by the air. This gas set a glowing splint aflame and supported life. It was Priestley's "good air." Lavoisier called it oxygen, meaning "acid former." 

7. Nature of burning and flame.-Lavoisier's experiments proved that in the air there are at least two substances: (1) oxygen, which is active toward tin and

mercury and the glowing splint, and which supports the life of mice; and (2) nitrogen, which is inactive. He concluded that ordinary burning, like rusting, consists in union with oxygen. When the burning body seems to disappear, as in the case of a burning candle, the products of the burning are gases and escape our notice. When we take care to collect the gaseous products and get their weight, we find that the material of the candle has not been lost,

FIG. 8. 

The classic apparatus used by Lavoisier. neither has that part of the air which combined with the candle been lost, but both together are present in the products formed.

We may define ordinary burning as the uniting of the burning body with the oxygen of the air so vigorously that heat and light are produced. 

The most fascinating part of a fire is its flame. Why do a candle, a piece of soft coal, a bonfire, and the fuel gas of our gas stoves, all burn with a flame, while a piece of punk, or charcoal, or coke merely glows? The answer is that a flame is a burning gas. Bodies which do not give off gases in burning do not have flames. Soft coal gives off much gas when heated; it is this gas which burns with a flame. When the coal is changed to coke, it loses its gases; the coke which remains therefore burns with a glow, not with a flame. When you light a candle, a little of the wick burns up, and the heat given off melts some of the wax, forming a small bowl of oil. The oil rises by capillary action (define

this), up the wick to its burning tip. The oil is then turned into a gas, or vapor; this burns with a flame, giving off heat and light. 

The flame extends outward as far as there is burning gas. 

8. How the chemist classifies substances. We have seen that the chemist thinks of every object, or body, as 

FIG. 9. 

A burning candle takes up oxygen from the air. The products of combustion are caught by the caustic soda in the lamp chimney, and the left hand of the balance goes down. either a single substance or a mixture. He then classifies single substances into: (1) simple substances, and (2) compounds.

To which class do the rusts of the metals belong? Is lead rust just a frothy mixture of lead and oxygen, or is it a single substance, like lead itself, with its own set of physical and chemical properties? A thorough study of lead rust shows that it is one substance, not a mixture of two. 

It is a compound substance. The chemist calls it lead oxide (ŏx'id) to show that it is composed of lead and oxygen, chemically combined. 

It is not easy to decompose lead oxide into lead and oxygen by heating it. Such a change is, however, possible with mercuric oxide. In fact, this is what Lavoisier did when he recovered the oxygen with which mercury had combined during his famous experiment (§ 6). By heating the mercuric oxide to a temperature a little higher than that at which it was formed he decomposed it into mercury and oxygen. 

The question now arises: Can mercury or oxygen be formed by combining other substances, as mercuric oxide

can? The answer is that men have not been able to form mercury and oxygen in this way. We call them simple substances, or elementary substances, or elements. Bodies

1. Metals, e.g. iron, silver. 

2. Non-metals, e.g. oxygen, sulphur. a. Two or more elementary substances, e.g. iron and sulphur; oxygen and nitrogen in air. 

2. Mixtures of b. Two or more compounds, e.g. single substances sand and salt; clay, sand, and limestone in soil. c. Elementary and compound substances, e.g. oxygen dissolved in water; carbon, sulphur, and niter in gunpowder.

In the metal radium we have an element that decomposes spontaneously (§ 319). The decomposition of the elements is thus possible. The rate is, however, exceedingly slow. We may still consider an elementary substance to be a single substance which has not been de-

14. 

composed into simpler substances by any ordinary chemical change.

A compound may be defined as a single substance which has been separated by chemical means into two or more elements, or has been prepared by the union of elements. 

9. Differences between mixtures and compounds.- 

FIG. 10. 

3 2 How a filter should be folded. The stem of the funnel is placed against the side of the beaker so that the drops of filtrate shall not spatter the liquid out of the beaker. 

In the classification of substances (§ 8) there are several examples of mixtures. The separation of a mixture is not always easy, because the ingredients in the mixture may be similar in certain properties. We can easily separate sand from salt by treating the mixture with water. The salt dissolves, but the sand does not. We can then pour off (decant) the salt solution from the sand, and drive off the water from the salt solution ("evaporation to dryness"). We can also separate the sand from the salt solution by pouring both together upon a filter. The salt solution passes through as the filtrate; the sand remains upon the filter paper as a residue. 

If, however, we have a mixture of sugar and salt, the separation is not so easy, since both substances dissolve in water.

The behavior of the substances iron and sulphur, first while they are simply mixed together, and afterwards when the elements have

entered into combination, is often used to illustrate the differences between mixtures and compounds. If we grind iron and sulphur together in a mortar, we obtain a mixture in which the colors of the ingredients are blended: the color of the mixture is between iron-gray and yellow. By the use of the microscope we can see that the particles of iron and of sulphur are still there. A magnet still attracts the iron. We can not use water to separate the iron and sulphur, because neither is soluble in water. But there is a liquid, carbon disulphide (dī-sŭl'fid), which dissolves the sulphur and leaves the iron. If we filter the mixture of iron and the carbon disulphide solution of sulphur, the iron remains as a residue, while the solution of sulphur forms the filtrate. When we set the filtrate in an open window, the carbon disulphide evaporates, and the sulphur remains. The mixing of the sulphur and iron in the mortar, the dissolving of the sulphur in carbon disulphide, the filtering process, and the evaporation of the carbon disulphide are all physical operations. They do not involve any chemical change. 

If we weigh out iron and sulphur in the proportion of seven to four parts, respectively, and grind them together in a mortar, and then heat the mixture strongly in a test tube, a violent chemical action (a reaction) begins. The mixture becomes red hot and glows brightly. 

When the mass has cooled, we find that its properties are not those of iron or sulphur. A magnet does not attract its iron. Carbon disulphide does not dissolve its sulphur. 

The iron and sulphur have united chemically to form a compound: iron sulphide. This has its own set of properties. We take iron and sulphur in the proportion of seven parts by weight of iron to four of sulphur because the two substances combine in practically these proportions. If we use an excess of either substance, it is simply left over.

Conclusions regarding mixtures and compounds: (1) 

When substances are mixed, there is generally no forma-

tion of heat or light or any other sign of a chemical change. When substances unite to form a compound, some change of energy usually takes place at the same time. Thus, heat and light accompany burning. (2) In making a mixture we may use any convenient proportion. When elements combine, they unite in definite proportions peculiar to themselves. (3) We can separate a mixture into its parts by some physical change or operation. We can recover the elements of a compound only by some chemical change.

names vary in different languages. Such are gold, silver, sulphur. iron, tin, and lead. Recently discovered metals have names ending in "um" or "ium." Scandium was found in an ore from

While the names of the elements vary somewhat in different languages, the symbols which the chemist uses are the same in all languages. 

Usually the symbol begins with the first letter of the English name, although a number of symbols are derived from the 

Latin names.

Since there are only twenty-six letters and about. ninety elements, the symbols of several elements begin with the same capital letter. A second, small letter is therefore used as a distinguishing mark. Thus we have the symbol C for carbon, and Ca for calcium (kǎl'să-ŭm). Give the other cases of the same kind found in the list of elements.

The elements are commonly thought of in two groups: the metals and the non-metals. The metals have a peculiar surface brightness, called the metallic luster. 

They can usually be hammered into sheets (are malleable) and drawn into wire (are ductile). They conduct heat and the electric current well. The non-metals do not have these properties. There are also great differ-

ences in the chemical properties of the two classes of elements. 

11. Units of length, volume, and weight.-Men everywhere need to measure objects; chemists are no exception. 

Like other scientists, chemists use the metric system (see Appendix I). Its unit of length is the meter (m.), which equals 39.37 inches. 1 2 3 4 5 6 7 8 

FIG. 12. 

A decimeter scale. Each decimeter contains 100 millimeters. 

The metric system is a decimal system, as is the money system of the United States. The dime is of a dollar, the cent is 100, and the mill is 1000.

The subdivisions of the meter are the decimeter (děs'ĭ-mē’těr), centimeter (sen'tĬ-mē'těr), and millimeter (mil'i-mē'těr). 

10 millimeters (mm.) = 1 centimeter (cm.) 

10 centimeters (cm.) = 1 decimeter (dm.) 

10 decimeters (dm.) = 1 meter (m.)

For greater distances the metric system uses the kilometer (km.), which is 1000 meters, or about § of a mile. 

The units of volume in chemical measurements are the liter (lē'těr) and the cubic centimeter (cu.cm.). The liter is equal to one cubic decimeter (cu.dm.) or 1000 cubic centimeters. It is equivalent to 1.0567 United States liquid quarts.

The metric units of weight are the gram and its subdivisions and the kilogram (kg.), which is 1000 grams, or about 2.2 pounds. 

The gram is the weight of 1 cu. cm. of pure water at 4° C.

13. Matter and its states.-Matter and energy are terms which we often use, but which we find hard to define. When we speak of the "substance" iron, we are using a general name for all bodies which consist of iron. 

Such are iron nails, iron dishes, iron stoves, and iron pipes. In a similar way we give a general name: matter, to all the specimens of all substances taken together.

From this point of view a substance is a kind, or species, of matter. In physics we define matter as that which occupies space, or takes up room, so that two bodies of it cannot occupy the same space at the same time. 

Central Scientific Co. 

FIG. 14. 

A hydrometer is an instrument which sinks to different depths in liquids of different densities. The "gravity" tester used for storage batteries is a hydrometer.

Every specimen of matter also has weight. The weight of a body is the measure of the attraction between the earth and that body. The quantity of matter in a body (its mass) remains the same everywhere on the earth; but the weight of the body becomes greater as we carry the body from the equator to the poles. Why is this so? 

We state the mass of a body as so many grams or pounds; that is, we give its weight.

An important property of a substance is the weight of some definite volume of it. 

When we say that lead is heavier than iron, we mean that a given volume of lead is heavier than an equal volume of iron. In scientific work the weight, in grams, of one cubic centimeter of a body is called the density of the body. Thus, if 2 cu. cm. of aluminum weigh 5.34 g., the density of aluminum is 2.67 g. per cubic centimeter.

Density mass (in grams) volume (in cu. cm.) 

How much does a cubic centimeter of water weigh? What is the density of water? Look up, in the Appendix, the densities of , lead, iron, and gold.

For gases the weight of one liter (1000 cu. cm.), in grams, is often used as the density. 

The same substance, water, may exist as a solid (ice),

as a liquid (water), and as a gas (steam, or water vapor). 

Air may also be liquefied and frozen; so may carbon dioxide and other gases. We speak of the solid, liquid, and gaseous conditions as the three states, or physical states, of matter. A change of state is called a physical change, not a chemical one. 

14. Energy and its forms. In physics we define energy as the capacity for doing work. We distinguish between 800

FALLING WATER 

TURBINE 

GENERATOR FIG. 15. 

Transformation of the energy of falling water into heat, sound, light, motion, and chemical change. In which piece of apparatus does each change take place? energy of position and energy of motion. Thus, an upraised hammer, or water at the top of a waterfall, has energy of position. When the hammer or the water descends, it has energy of motion. We also call energy

of position, potential energy, and energy of motion, kinetic energy. The energy of a hammer, a waterfall, a horse, or a steam engine, is physical energy. Heat, light, and electric currents are other forms of physical energy. 

Chemical energy is energy which appears during a chemical change. Thus, coal and air in contact with each other have potential chemical energy. When their carbon and oxygen unite, heat and light are given off, and the energy is kinetic. The gunpowder of a cartridge likewise has potential chemical energy. When chemical action between the ingredients of the powder takes place, the energy becomes kinetic. 

One form of energy can be changed into another. The energy of falling water may be used, in a turbine and generator, to produce an electric current; the current can produce heat, or light, or a chemical change. By means of a motor the current may also be changed into mechanical energy, and operate an electric car or a sewing machine, or a water pump. 

15. Conservation of matter and energy.-When the chemist prepares a new substance, he knows that its elements come from material already in existence; it is not made out of nothing. As far as our knowledge goes, matter can not be created or destroyed. 

We speak of this fact as the law of the conservation of matter. 

There is also a law of the conservation of energy, which states that energy can not be created or destroyed. When we observe that heat, or light, or an electric current, or mechanical energy, appear as the result of a chemical change, we feel that these forms of energy must have come from energy already in existence. 

16. Chemistry in modern life.—The ideas of chemistry, developed chiefly since the time of the American Revolution, have produced their own revolution in man's ways

of living. The ability of the chemist to find out what is present in raw materials and waste products, and then to prepare useful products from them, makes the modern community possible. The automobile and airplane had 2555 

FIG. 16. 

Unloading machines taking iron ore from the vessel at the right and placing it in storage piles. Blast furnaces and their stoves are seen at the left. In the blast furnace iron ore is reduced, by the removal of oxygen, to iron. to wait until the gasoline engine was developed; the fuel, gasoline, was developed by chemists. To find the proportion of gasoline vapor and air needed to run the engine also requires chemical knowledge. To meet the needs of his industries man must have an enormous amount of iron each year; he must prepare every pound of it from some ore. The chemist finds out what is present in the ore and tells the best way to extract the iron. As soon

as iron is prepared, it begins to corrode, or rust; the chemist finds out how to delay corrosion. The chemist not only distinguishes a good drinking water from a dangerous one, but he prepares good water from water that contains the germs of disease. He finds what materials are present in a given soil, and then advises the grower what the soil needs to make it produce certain crops. The dietitian tells us we should take a certain amount of milk each day. Does it make no difference what the quality of the milk is? The laws of the community state that no milk may be sold which can not pass certain tests. These tests are largely chemical. The cheese maker and butter maker can not afford to buy "just milk"; they must know how much cheese or butter can be made out of every 100 pounds of milk. Thus the processes of the chemist are at the foundation of our intelligent use of milk, whether we simply drink the milk, or make butter or cheese from it.

The chemist is the member of the community who determines that most of the materials we buy are what they are supposed to be; otherwise there could be no agreement in recipes for cooking and baking, or in the prescriptions of doctors and dietitians, or in directions for carrying on many of our industries. In a very real sense, the chemist serves as the "eyes" of a civilized community, scouting for the materials it must have for its shelter and clothing, directing its industries, and guarding its food and water supply from fraud and contamination. 

The two sciences, physics and chemistry, are very closely related. Both of them deal with matter and energy on the one hand, and, on the other hand, with the

improvement of community life. Physics considers chiefly the changes that do not result in new substances, while chemistry is concerned mainly with the change of one substance into another, and with the conditions under which the change takes place. 

17. Summary.-The chemist distinguishes substances from one another by their properties.

Every material is either a single substance or a mixture. 

In chemical changes new substances are always formed, but there is no gain or loss of material. 

A chemical change is also called a reaction. 

Physical changes are those in which no new substances are formed.

A substance shows its chemical properties in a chemical change. 

The explanation of burning came with the explanation of rusting.

Priestley obtained oxygen in 1774 by heating mercuric oxide. 

Lavoisier first proved that tin uses up air when it rusts in a closed flask. His second great experiment proved that mercury combines with about of an enclosed portion of air. The substance that combines with the mercury is oxygen. The inactive part of the air is chiefly nitrogen. 

Burning is the uniting of a body with oxygen so vigorously that heat and light are produced. A flame is a burning gas. 

Single substances are either simple substances (elements) or compounds.

An element is a single substance which has not been divided by chemical means.

A compound is a single substance which has been separated into two or more elements. 

When two substances are mixed, no energy change is usually noticed. The substances may be in any proportion, and can be separated by a physical operation. When substances react chemically, an energy change commonly takes place. The substances then react in definite proportions, and can be separated only by a chemical change.

The names of elements are represented by symbols. 

Elements may be either metals or non-metals. 

The unit of the metric system is the meter. Its subdivisions are the decimeter, centimeter, and millimeter. A kilometer is 

1000 meters.

The metric unit of volume is the cubic decimeter, or liter. It contains 1000 cubic centimeters. 

The metric unit of weight is the gram or the kilogram (1000 grams). The subdivisions of the gram are the deci-, centi-, and milligram. One cubic centimeter of pure water at 4° C. weighs 

1 gram.

The fixed points of the thermometer are the melting point of ice and the boiling point of water. 

Fahrenheit reading = (Centigrade reading X)+32. 

Centigrade reading = (Fahrenheit reading-32).

Matter is a general name for all bodies of all substances. Matter takes up room and has weight. 

The mass of a body is the quantity of matter in it. 

Density of a body: mass in grams volume in cu. cm. 

The three states of matter are the gaseous, liquid, and solid states.

Energy is the capacity for doing work. One form of energy can be changed into another. Chemical energy appears during a chemical change.

In chemical changes matter is neither created nor destroyed. 

This is the law of the conservation of matter. 

Energy, like matter, is not created or destroyed. This is the law of the conservation of energy. 

The chemist serves as the "eyes" of a community: on the lookout for its interests, and guarding its necessities.

CHAPTER II OXYGEN 

19. Occurrence and discovery of oxygen.-The figure giving the composition of the earth (§ 10) shows how abundant oxygen is. The atmosphere is 24%, by weight, oxygen, pure water is 88.89%, limestone is 48%, sand (quartz) is over 53%, and the earth as a whole, as far as its rocks have been examined, is almost 50% oxygen. 

The body of a person weighing 150 lbs. contains nearly 

100 lbs. of oxygen (§ 29).

It is hard for us to realize that man had the evidences of this active substance about him for so many centuries, and yet had no idea of its existence. He breathes it, uses it as the "invisible partner" of fuel in his fires, and sees its destructive effects in rusting and in great conflagrations. Priestley's famous experiment is dated 

August 1, 1774. Scheele (shā'lā), a Swedish chemist, prepared oxygen in 1771-73, from mercuric oxide, from niter, and from manganese dioxide, but his discoveries. were not known to the world until 1777. Meanwhile 

Lavoisier had performed his epoch-making experiments and had stated the new doctrine of burning. 

20. Preparation. In order to prepare oxygen we usually decompose some compound of oxygen. Some methods are: (1) To heat mercuric oxide, the red powder used by 

Priestley and Scheele. For use on a large scale, this method is too expensive. 29

The chemist uses an expression called an "equation" to show what takes place in a chemical change. For the present we shall use "word equations." Thus, for the decomposition of mercuric oxide we write: 

Mercuric oxide→Mercury+oxygen ↑ . 

The expression is called an "equation" because the sign (=) was formerly used where we now use the horizontal arrow. This arrow is read "yields" or "produces," and the (+) sign is read "and." The arrow directed upward means that the oxygen produced goes off as a gas. (2) A second method of preparing oxygen is to heat potassium chlorate. This is a white, crystalline solid, of which 39.18%, by weight, is oxygen. The word equation is:

Potassium chlorate-potassium chloride+oxygen ↑ . potassium chlorine oxygen potassium chlorine

The potassium chloride is also a white, crystalline solid; it contains potassium and chlorine, but no oxygen. The chemist uses the ending ide to show that only the elements named are present in the compound. In the name, potassium chlorate, oxygen is understood, but not expressed. (3) The common laboratory method is to heat potassium chlorate mixed with manganese dioxide. A much lower temperature is required than if potassium chlorate is heated by itself. But, strangely enough, the manganese dioxide is all left after the potassium chlorate is decomposed. Its use in this experiment is to increase the speed of the reaction. If we include the manganese

OXYGEN 

31 dioxide in the "word equation," we must put it on both sides of the arrow, that is, among the substances produced as well as among the substances taken: 

Manganese+potassium-manganese+potassium+oxygen ↑. dioxide chlorate dioxide chloride We commonly write the word equation as under method 2, without including the manganese dioxide.

FIG. 17. 

A substance which affects the speed of a reaction by being present, but is not itself permanently altered, is called a catalyst (kǎt'ǎ-list); it is also called a catalytic agent, or contact agent. The action of a catalyst is called catalysis (kăt-ǎl'ĭ-sis), or contact action. 

Preparation of oxygen by the heating of a mixture of manganese dioxide and potassium chlorate. The oxygen is collected by the displacement of water from the bottles. Why does not the water fall out of the bottles? (4) A safe way of preparing oxygen, without heating, is to add a solution of hydrogen peroxide, drop by drop, to some manganese dioxide or potassium permanganate just covered with dilute sulphuric acid. Effervescence (bubbling) takes place, and oxygen escapes.

Methods 5 and 6 are used for the preparation of oxygen on a commercial scale: (5) Action of an electric current of sufficient strength upon water containing a little sulphuric acid or sodium hydroxide. This method is called the electrolysis (e-lěk-trol 'I-sis) of water (see § 35). (6) Distillation of liquid air. The nitrogen boils off at a lower temperature than the oxygen; hence nearly pure oxygen remains 

(§§ 96 and 97.)

21. Physical properties.-The physical properties of a gas include: (1) its color; (2) its odor; (3) its taste; (4) its density, or the weight of 1000 cubic centimeters (cu. cm.) of it; (5) the conditions under which it can be liquefied or frozen; (6) the extent to which it dissolves in liquids, especially in water. Oxygen is colorless, odorless, and tasteless. One liter (1000 cu. cm.) of it weighs 

1.429 grams under standard conditions (§ 45). Under the same conditions one liter of air weighs 1.293 grams.

Oxygen is therefore more dense than air. About 3 cu. cm. of oxygen dissolve in 100 cu. cm. of water under ordinary conditions. The ability of oxygen to dissolve is of the highest importance, for it is in dissolved form that oxygen gets into the bodies of animals and plants. 

Under sufficient pressure and a low temperature oxygen may be condensed to a pale blue liquid, and, at a still lower temperature, may be frozen. 

22. Chemical properties. To give the chemical properties of oxygen is to relate the story of its chemical activity. 

Oxygen forms compounds with nearly all the other elements; these compounds are the oxides. Some oxides are solids, some liquids, and some gases. The chemist calls water, hydrogen oxide.

If one end of a fine, iron wire is heated to redness, and the wire is put into oxygen, a brilliant shower of sparks is produced, as the iron unites with oxygen. A globule of the black product, magnetic iron oxide, is usually found at the burned end of the wire. If a piece of charcoal (a form of the element carbon) is heated to redness, and put into oxygen, it glows very brightly, and is finally used up. The product, carbon dioxide, is a colorless, odorless gas.

OXYGEN 

33 Calcium, magnesium, phosphorus, and sulphur all unite energetically with oxygen, forming the corresponding oxides. Sulphur dioxide is a colorless gas, like carbon dioxide, but it has a sharp odor-the odor of burning sulphur. The "word equations" for these reactions are: 

Iron+oxygen-magnetic iron oxide.

Carbon+oxygen-carbon (di)oxide. 

Calcium+oxygen calcium oxide. oxide. Magnesium+oxygen-magnesium 

Phosphorus+oxygen-phosphorus (pent) oxide.

Sulphur+oxygen sulphur (di)oxide. 

The test for undiluted oxygen is its ability to cause a glowing splint of wood to burst into flame. 

23. Rapid and slow oxidation.-A chemical change in which oxygen combines with another substance is called an oxidation. We say that the other substance is oxidized. 

Thus, iron is oxidized to magnetic iron oxide when it is burned in oxygen.

Oxidation may be rapid, or it may be slow. All burning is a form of rapid oxidation. Heat and light appear because energy is produced in the oxidation more rapidly than it can be given off to the surrounding air or oxygen. 

A piece of magnesium burns rapidly in oxygen, forming magnesium oxide, and the change is accompanied by much heat and light. Magnesium also rusts slowly in moist air, without noticeable heat or light. The product, magnesium oxide, is essentially the same in the two cases, and the amount of energy given off is the same. But in the rusting the energy is dispersed as rapidly as it is set free.

The rusting of iron is another case of slow oxidation, likewise the decay of wood and of other vegetable and 1

'

animal tissues. Decay takes place in the presence of oxidizing bacteria, and consumes oxygen. 

Slow oxidation is a chemical change in which oxygen combines with another substance with- 

FIG. 18. 

Iron rusting in a test tube of air. What change in volume will take place? out the formation of noticeable heat and light. 

24. Combustion.-Combustion is, to the chemist, a general name for any chemical change in which heat and light appear. Thus, the rapid uniting of iron and sulphur to form iron sulphide (§ 9) is a case of combustion as truly as the rapid uniting of phosphorus and oxygen. 

In popular language, however, combustion means burning. Because a combustion (burning) which we start in air continues when we put the burning body into oxygen, we say that oxygen is a supporter of combustion, as air itself is. A substance that can burn in air is a combustible substance, or, simply, a combustible.

The rate at which a combustible burns depends partly upon the condition of the combustible, especially upon how finely divided it is, and partly upon how rapidly oxygen can be supplied. 

The air contains a large proportion of inactive gases, chiefly nitrogen (§ 6); we may therefore think of air as diluted oxygen, much as lemonade is diluted lemon juice. Because of this fact bodies that burn very rapidly in oxygen usually burn more slowly in air.

Deflagration is very rapid, intense combustion. The bursting of a match head into flame, when scratched, is an example of deflagration. The reaction is started by friction, and travels very quickly throughout the match head. Gunpowder deflagrates when ignited in the open air. In a closed space, however, as in a

OXYGEN 

35 cartridge, the pressure of gases formed by the deflagration of the gunpowder produces an explosion. Explosions also take place when mixtures of mine gases and air, and of coal dust or flour dust and air, are set on fire, because a large volume of hot gas is formed "instantaneously" in a small space. 25. Kindling temperature.-By kindling (or ignition) temperature of a combustible we mean the lowest temperature at which the combustible takes fire in air, and continues burning. 

Different combustibles take fire at widely different temperatures. Thus, a lump of white phosphorus is ignited at so low a temperature that it must be kept under water and must not be handled with the fingers; while a lump of sulphur requires a much higher temperature to set it on fire. We cannot give a definite temperature as the kindling temperature of the substance iron. Much depends upon the size of the iron particles. 

An iron nail can not be set on fire in air, but powdered iron readily burns when sprinkled into a Bunsen flame; and there is a very finely divided form of iron which catches fire when exposed to the air. gas.

FIG. 20. 

A Bunsen flame burning above the wire gauze but not below it. Why does not the flame above set fire to the gas below?

In ordinary burning the heat given off by the burning

of one part of a body raises the temperature of nearby parts to the kindling point. A match stick and a city block continue to burn in this way. To maintain a fire 

FIG. 21. 

Sir Humphry Davy (1778-1829). 

He rose from a humble beginning to be the greatest scientist of England. By means of the electric current he discovered sodium and potassium. He proved that chlorine is not a compound but an element. 

He invented the safety lamp for the protection of miners. in a stove or furnace, we see to it that there is a good draft, or air current. This brings fresh oxygen to the fuel, and removes the inactive nitrogen and the gaseous products formed in the burning. We avoid a too rapid air current, because it may cool the fuel below its kindling temperature. Thus, a violent gust of wind "blows out" a candle flame. 

A candle flame may also be extinguished by a spiral of cold copper wire suddenly inserted into it. Why? 

Combustibles, such as candles, wood, and sulphur, cease burning in closed bottles of air long before they have used up all the oxygen. As the supply of oxygen grows smaller, and the gaseous products of the combustion accumulate, the burning body can no longer give off heat rapidly enough to maintain itself at the kindling temperature. Phosphorus, however, burns with a very great liberation of heat, and the product of combustion, phosphorus oxide, is a solid, and does not dilute the oxygen that remains. As a result, phosphorus burns in a closed bottle of air until all the oxygen is used up.

When metals like tin and mercury act upon air in closed vessels, as in Lavoisier's experiment (§ 6), the temperature required to complete the removal of the oxygen must be maintained by heat from the outside.

OXYGEN 

37 

Davy's safety lamp.-The safety lamp (Fig. 22) was invented by Sir Humphry Davy to prevent certain kinds of explosions in mines. It shows how combustible gases may be cooled below their kindling point. When a miner carrying an ordinary lamp enters a mine containing "fire damp" (marsh gas mixer' with air), he is likely to ignite the mixture and to cause a frightful explosion. The safety lamp consists of a lantern in which the space for the flame is entirely surrounded by wire gauze. When a miner carries a safety lamp into a combustible gas mixture, part of the gas passes through the wire gauze and burns inside the lamp. The wire gauze surrounding the flame conducts away the heat produced, so that the gases outside are not heated to their kindling temperature. The small explosions that take place inside the lamp warn the miner of his danger.

FIG. 22. 

Davy's safety lamp. 

26. Fires that begin "spontaneously."-By spontaneous ignition or combustion we mean the taking fire of combustibles without apparent kindling. Thus, heaps of oily cloths or engineers' "waste," stacks of damp hay, and coal packed closely in poorly ventilated bunkers of ships or the basements of buildings, often seem to catch fire "of themselves." The explanation of spontaneous ignition is that the kindling temperature is reached through slow oxidation (§ 23). Thus, the linseed oil of paint absorbs oxygen from the air and forms a thick "scum." This "hardens" the paint. Heat is given off in the process. Ordinarily the heat is dispersed as rapidly as formed, but a heap of oily cloths acts as a non-conductor of heat, and the temperature finally reaches the kindling point of the cloths.

Oily cloths should never be left in a heap nor near anything that can be set on fire; but they should

either be burned at once or put into covered, metal cans. 

27. The products of combustion.-When a fuel burns in air, the products of the combustion are chiefly the oxides of the elements present in the fuel. Thus, when charcoal burns (§ 22), carbon dioxide is formed. We can test for the carbon dioxide by means of limewater. This is a solution of slaked lime, or calcium hydroxide, to use the chemist's name for it. Limewater becomes milky when it acts with carbon dioxide, because of the formation of tiny particles of calcium carbonate. These remain suspended in the limewater. If we burn a candle for some time in a closed bottle of air, and then find that limewater shaken up with the air becomes very milky, we conclude that the burned candle contained carbon. 

Barium hydroxide solution does as well as limewater. In this case the milkiness is due to particles of barium carbonate.

If we place a large, cold object, such as a beaker or teakettle full of cold water, over a burning gas flame, the sides and bottom of the vessel become covered with drops of water. This water does not leak through the vessel, but is formd by the burning of hydrogen in the fuel gas. Water is hydrogen oxide. 

We can prove that a candle, like fuel gas, contains hydrogen as well as carbon; for, if we burn a candle in a cold bottle of air, we can see that droplets of water condense on the inside of the bottle. The water is formed by the oxidation of the hydrogen in the burned wick and wax. 

Our other common fuels usually contain compounds of carbon and hydrogen. When these burn, the products consist chiefly of carbon dioxide and water.

OXYGEN 

39 

28. How we extinguish fires.-Would you expect the products of combustion to burn? Of course not, if their combustible elements have already united with sufficient oxygen. If we wish to fire-proof combustible materials, such as wood, cloth, and paper, we cover them with some solid product of combustion, such as magnesium oxide, or calcium oxide, or silicon dioxide (which has the composition of sand). Asbestos is a compound of magnesium oxide and silicon dioxide.

SODIUM BICARBONATE SOLUTION FIG. 23. 

Soda fire extinguisher. When the extinguisher is turned upside down, the acid is spilled out of the bottle and reacts with the sodium bi- From what we know about burning, we can reason that to extinguish a fire we must either: (1) cut off the air supply, or (2) remove the material that can burn, or (3) lower the temperature of the burning body below its kindling point. When a house is on fire, we throw water upon it, if possible; this lowers the temperature of the burning material, covers the fire with a blanket of water and water vapor, and cuts off the air supply. 

Commercial fire extinguishers usually furnish a blanket of carbon dioxide. Some forms of extinguishers contain a liquid called carbon tetrachloride (tět'rä-klōr'Id), which forms a heavy, non-combustible vapor. For a small fire in the laboratory, we use a wet towel or a dried mixture of sawdust and baking soda solution. We also use sand. carbonate, pro- ducing carbon dioxide. This acts as a blanket over the fire and cuts off the supply of air.

If a fire is so large that the air supply can not be cut off from it, extreme methods of removing the combustible must be used. 

Thus, in a prairie fire, or a forest fire, the combustibles surrounding a house or a town may be burned in a "controlled" fire (a process called "back-firing"), so that when the real conflagration comes, it may find no fuel. In a large city fire buildings in the probable path of the fire are sometimes blown up, purposely, by dynamite.

29. Oxygen and life. Our bodies consist mainly of 15 elements: oxygen, carbon, hydrogen, nitrogen, calcium, phosphorus, potassium, sulphur, sodium, chlorine, magnesium, iron, iodine, fluorine, and silicon. Very small amounts ("traces") of other elements are probably present. Oxygen makes up 65%, or nearly two-thirds, of the weight of the body. It forms a part of all our animal and vegetable food. 

The uncombined oxygen which we inhale in breathing passes through the walls of the lungs into the blood vessels. The blood contains a substance-hemoglobin (he'mō-glō'bin), which combines with the oxygen to form a loose ("unstable") compound, oxyhemoglobin. 

As a part of this compound oxygen is carried to the body cells and given off when needed. One use of the oxygen

carried by the blood is to oxidize our food; another use is to oxidize body materials which wear out, so that the body can get rid of them. In this oxidation, as in the burning of compounds containing carbon and hydrogen, carbon dioxide and water are produced. Heat is also given off, and maintains the body temperature at normal: 98.6° F., or 37° C.

The oxygen-carbon dioxide cycle showing the relation of plants and animals to the air and to each other. 

Practically all living creatures require oxygen for their respiration, or breathing. 

Water animals, such as fishes, find the needed oxygen dissolved in the water. In their gills the same exchange

OXYGEN 

41 of carbon dioxide for oxygen takes place as in the lungs of higher animals. 

Plants use up oxygen in the oxidation of their food and tissues, and give off carbon dioxide, just as animals do. 

But green plants, under the influence of sunlight, also take up carbon dioxide and give off oxygen. Out of carbon dioxide and water they build up such substances as sugar, starch, and cellulose carbohydrates. The process is called photosynthesis (fō'tō-sin 'thē-sis), meaning "put together by light." The carbon dioxide and water used up by plants contain more oxygen than is needed in the carbohydrates; the excess oxygen is returned to the air (§ 282).

We may express photosynthesis by the word equation: carbon dioxide +water+energy of →carbohydrate+oxygen ↑ .

sunlight The equation for the complete oxidation of a carbohydrate, such as sugar, is just the reverse of this: carbo- hydrate +oxygen carbon +water+energy. dioxide These two word equations, taken together, give us a chemical reason why animals and plants are dependent upon one another: the one gives off what the other needs. We may observe this "balance of nature" in an aquarium. If small water animals, such as fish, tadpoles, and snails, are placed in an aquarium containing water, and the proper quantity of water plants is added, the two forms of life live together without the addition of fresh water. The plants use up the carbon dioxide given off by the animals and return oxygen to the animals. 

Decay is, as we have seen (§ 23), a slow oxidation. Oxygen is thus the earth's great purifier. It changes the remains of former life into useful or harmless materials, and so prepares the earth's

surface for each new generation in its turn. To hasten the process of oxidation in a city's water supply the water is often sprayed into the air. Waterfalls and rapids in a stream serve a similar purpose in bringing decaying matter into close contact with oxygen. 

A. G. Dreyfous.

FIG. 25. 

A pulmotor being used to restore normal breathing to a person who has been asphyxiated. 

30. Uses of oxygen. The oxidations of nature take place with the diluted oxygen of the air. To produce a more rapid and intense oxidation we use pure oxygen, or air containing a higher per cent of oxygen than normal. 

A great deal of commercial oxygen is now obtained from liquid air (§ 96). The oxygen cannot be kept, for long, in liquid form, but is allowed to change back to the state of a gas, and is then stored in strong, metal cylinders, under pressure. The two chief applications of such oxygen are to aid human breathing and to produce high temperatures.

OXYGEN 

43 In certain diseases, such as pneumonia, the lungs are not able to supply the body rapidly enough with oxygen, hence oxygen itself, or air containing a high proportion of oxygen, is used. The oxygen is always mixed with a small proportion of carbon dioxide, so that it shall more nearly resemble ordinary air. Oxygen is also used in drowning accidents and in the case of persons who suffer from electric shock or the inhalation of fuel gas or the waste gases of automobiles. The apparatus for supplying the oxygen is called a pulmotor, or lungmotor. 

Aviators and mountain climbers carry "helmets" supplied with compressed oxygen, so that they may breathe a sufficient amount of the gas at high altitudes. Firemen and mine-rescue workers also use such helmets. The oxygen is either carried in cylinders, or is generated, as needed, from chemicals carried by the wearer. 

If oxygen is used, instead of air, to support the combustion of fuel gas, or hydrogen, or acetylene, a flame having a very high temperature can be obtained. Why?

In the flame of the oxyacetylene blowpipe the temperature may reach 2700° to 3500° C. By the use of this blowpipe pieces of steel, such as rails, can be welded together, and wrought-iron and steel girders and plates can be cut off to the size wanted. Holes can also be drilled quickly through a steel plate. In cutting a bar of iron, the oxyacetylene flame is first used to heat the metal exposed to it. Then most of the acetylene is shut off, and only the compressed oxygen is used. This causes the iron to burn away rapidly (§ 22) in its path. 

When we think of fuels as sources of heat, we must not forget the oxygen which combines with them. The burning of 12 grams of carbon requires 32 grams of

oxygen. A ton of coal therefore requires about 2 tons of oxygen for its combustion. 

31. Ozone.-Oxygen has another remarkable chemical property: when it is exposed to a silent electric discharge, A. G. Dreyfous.

FIG. 26. 

Oxyacetylene blowpipe and storage tanks. The oxyacetylene flame is being used to cut through the iron bars of a burning building. 

It also deit gains a peculiar odor, and becomes much more active. 

Thus, it oxidizes the metals silver and mercury at once, whereas ordinary, cold oxygen does not. colorizes substances like indigo and litmus. One of the fundamental ideas of chemistry is that if a given body gains new properties, a new substance must have been formed (§ 4). In this case some of the oxygen has been changed to ozone by the addition of energy from the electric discharge.

OXYGEN 

45 When a given substance has two or more different forms in the same physical state (oxygen and ozone are both gases), we call the forms allotropic (ǎl'ō-trop'îk) forms. Thus, the element oxygen exists in two allotropic forms: (1) ordinary oxygen; (2) ozone. We shall learn, later, that other elements, such as sulphur, phosphorus, and carbon, exist in several allotropic forms. 

It is likely that ozone is formed in many cases in which oxidation takes place, and that it is changed back, in a short time, to oxygen. We say that ozone is very unstable. If we place moist, white phosphorus in a covered dish, we soon perceive the peculiar odor of ozone. When we pass an electric current through water containing a little sulphuric acid (electrolysis of water; see § 35), the oxygen produced contains some ozone. The action of ultra-violet light (meaning of this?) upon air produces some ozone; it is, therefore, probable that sunlight, which contains such light rays, also forms some ozone out of the oxygen of the air.

An ozonizer is an apparatus for preparing ozone out of air or oxygen. It can be made of two plates of glass placed a short distance apart, with plates of metal against the outer sides of the glass plates. When the (+) and (-) poles of an induction coil are brought in contact with the two metal plates, a silent electric discharge

How ozone is prepared from oxygen or air. passes between the glass plates. A current of air or oxygen passed between the glass plates is partly changed to ozone-

up to about 7% of the oxygen present. A battery of such plates is used in a large ozonizer. In smaller ozonizers two glass tubes, one within the other, take the place of the glass plates. 

When oxygen is changed to ozone, three volumes of oxygen yield two volumes of ozone. Ozone is thus 1 times as heavy, volume for volume, as oxygen. 

Ozone is much more soluble in water than oxygen is. It is readily absorbed by turpentine. We can find the amount of ozone in a given volume of ozonized air or oxygen by passing the mixture through turpentine. 

Owing to its power to kill bacteria, ozone is used in a number of European cities to sterilize, or purify, drinking water. Bleaching powder is, however, much cheaper, and is generally used in North America. Ozone can also be used in hospitals, to sterilize bandages. Other uses are to purify indoor air, to bleach fabrics, and to serve as an oxidizing agent in some manufacturing processes. 

32. Summary.-Oxygen is the most abundant element of the earth's crust and the most active gas of the atmosphere. 

Oxygen may be prepared: (1) from mercuric oxide; (2) from potassium chlorate; (3) from potassium chlorate, with manganese dioxide as a catalyst; (4) from hydrogen peroxide added to manganese dioxide or potassium permanganate; (5) commercially, by electrolysis of water, or (6) by distillation of liquid air. 

Oxygen is a colorless, tasteless, odorless gas, denser than air, somewhat soluble in water, and capable of being liquefied and frozen.

Oxygen is active chemically and unites with most elements, forming oxides. 

Oxidation is union with oxygen. It may be rapid, as in burning, or slow, as in rusting and decay. 

Combustion is a chemical change in which heat and light are given off. Popularly it means "burning."

Air and oxygen are "supporters of combustion"; wood, gasoline, illuminating gas, coal, and the like are combustibles. 

Deflagration is very rapid, intense combustion. Explosions result from the sudden formation of a large volume of gas in a small space.

Kindling temperature, or ignition temperature, is the lowest

OXYGEN 

47 temperature at which a combustible takes fire in air, and continues burning.

Drafts bring fresh air (oxygen) to a combustible and carry away the products of combustion. 

Davy's safety lamp protects miners by preventing the ignition of combustible gases outside the lamp.

Fires seem to begin "spontaneously" when slow oxidation takes place, and the heat produced is prevented from escaping by some nonconductor.

The products of combustion, in all common cases of burning, are water and carbon dioxide. 

We extinguish fires by: (1) cutting off the air supply, or (2) removing the combustible material, or (3) lowering the temperature of the burning body below its kindling point. 

The respiration of practically every living creature consumes oxygen, and produces carbon dioxide and water. 

Green plants, in "photosynthesis," convert carbon dioxide and water into carbohydrates and oxygen. 

The two chief uses of commercial oxygen are to aid human breathing and to produce high-temperature flames. 

Ozone is an unstable gas formed by the addition of energy (such as electric energy) to oxygen. It changes back readily into oxygen, and gives off energy in doing so. It is a more powerful oxidizing agent than ordinary oxygen.

CHAPTER III HYDROGEN 

34. Sources. Hydrogen occurs as one of the gases of the earth's atmosphere, but in very small amount. 

Probably there are not over 19 cu. cm. in every 100,000 cu. cm. of air. There is a great deal of hydrogen in the atmosphere of the sun. The element is a constituent of all animal and plant tissues and of all acids and bases. Water is one-ninth hydrogen. 

35. Preparation from water.-Two methods of preparing hydrogen from water are: (1) the electrolysis of water; (2) the action of metals upon water. 

The electrolysis of water is carried out in the presence of some substance which acts as a catalyst (§ 20). Usually dilute sulphuric acid is used, but other acids will do, also certain bases and salts. In electrolysis on a commercial scale iron apparatus is used, and the liquid is a dilute solution of potassium hydroxide or sodium hydroxide (bases). The hydrogen obtained by electrolysis is stored in strong cylinders, under high pressure.

HYDROGEN FIG. 28. 

Simple apparatus for the electrolysis of water. Note the electrode at which each gas collects and the relative vol- umes of the gases.

Electrolysis apparatus is shown in Fig. 28. A current is passed between platinum terminals, or electrodes, through a 5% solution of sulphuric acid. The electromotive force of the current must be over 1.47 volts; it should be 5 or 6 volts. The current should also be direct. 49

As the current flows, gas bubbles rise from the electrodes and push out the acid solutions from the collecting tubes. The electrode at which the current enters the solution is the positive (+) electrode, or anode. The electrode at which the current leaves the solution is the negative (-) one, or cathode. Oxygen collects 

FIG. 29. 

paratus devised by Hoffman. As the gases collect, they push the unchanged liquid up into the reservoir. at the anode and is said to be electronegative. 

About twice as much hydrogen is collected in a given time as oxygen. The electrolysis of sodium hydroxide or of sodium sulphate (a salt) also gives hydrogen and oxygen in the proportion of two volumes of hydrogen to one of oxygen. In all these cases practically all of the catalyst is left after the action; it is the water that has been decomposed. 

36. Preparation by action of metals upon water. When we hear the word metal, we commonly think of such substances as copper, iron, lead, and gold. All of these are hard and have a fairly high density (see Appendix V). Their melting points are also 

Electrolysis ap- high, and they do not react quickly with air or water. There are some metals with quite different properties. Thus, sodium and potassium are lighter than water, and soft enough to be cut easily. They react so energetically with ordinary air that they must be kept under gasoline or kerosene. They have a metallic luster when freshly cut, but tarnish quickly. They react rapidly with water. Calcium is a fairly light metal, but harder than sodium and potassium. It reacts readily with water. 

Only the very active metals, such as the three named, react rapidly with cold water. The products in each case are hydrogen and the hydroxide of the metal.

HYDROGEN 

51 Sodium+water-sodium hydroxide+hydrogen ↑ . hydrogen sodium [oxygen hydrogen oxygen Potassium+water-potassium hydroxide+hydrogen ↑ . 

Calcium +water calcium hydroxide +hydrogen ↑.

Caution: When sodium or potassium is thrown upon water, the piece should be held at arm's length, and a pane of glass should be held between the face and the dish, to prevent accidents due to spattering. Only a small amount should be used at a time. The heat set free in the reaction melts the metal, and the drop of melted metal swims about with a hissing noise until used up.

If sodium is used, a lighted match may be brought near the swimming metal, and the hydrogen may be set on fire. The hydrogen burns in this case with a yellow flame; its color is due to sodium carried off by the hydrogen. The hydrogen produced may be collected in a bottle. One way is to place the sodium in a short piece (10-15 mm. in length) of glass tubing, which is sealed at one end, and then to plunge it quickly, by means of tongs, under the mouth of an inverted bottle filled with water and standing in a water pan. 

The solution left after the sodium has disappeared may be tested for sodium hydroxide. If some of the solution is rubbed between the fingers, they feel slimy, or soapy. 

If the solution is tested with red litmus (paper or solution) the litmus is turned blue. This is the test for a base in solution (§ 194). The sodium hydroxide might be obtained as a white solid by the complete removal of the water ("evaporation to dryness").

Potassium is even more active than sodium, and the hydrogen set free takes fire "of itself." The flame has a violet color from the small amounts of potassium in it. 

Calcium acts upon water more slowly than sodium and potassium, and we can easily collect the hydrogen by holding a test tube, filled with water, over the metal as it reacts with water. 

The calcium hydroxide formed is not very soluble; hence some of it is usually precipitated as a white solid and makes the water "milky."

In these reactions of the very active metals with cold water, only half of the hydrogen of the water that acts is set free. The STEAM

38. Physical properties of hydrogen.-Pure hydrogen is colorless, odorless, and tasteless (see § 21). As usually prepared, the gas has an odor, due to small amounts of other gases in it. Hydrogen is the lightest substance known. One liter weighs less than 0.09 gram under standard conditions. Air is 14.4 times as heavy as hydrogen, and oxygen is 16 times as heavy. Under ordinary conditions 100 cu. cm. of water dissolve only about 2 cu. cm. of hydrogen. By the use of a low temperature and great pressure, hydrogen has 

FIG. 33. 

Soap bubbles filled with hydrogen are lighter than air, and float. been changed to a colorless liquid as heavy as water. This boils at -252.6° C. Solid hydrogen has also been obtained.

Large quantities of gaseous hydrogen are condensed (adsorbed) by such metals as iron, platinum, and palladium. The hydrogen is said to be occluded by the metal. Under certain conditions palladium can occlude 873 times its own volume of hydrogen. 

Much heat is given off in adsorption on so large a scale. If a piece of spongy platinum, which has a very large adsorbing surface, is held in a jet of dry hydrogen, the hydrogen is set on fire. This fact is used in the construction of self-lighting gas-burners. Heating the metal drives off the adsorbed hydrogen.

Hydrogen is so light that it diffuses, or scatters itself, more rapidly than any other gas. If a jar of hydrogen is put over one of air, the two gases soon form a uniform mixture. The hydrogen passes downward, although it is lighter than air, and air passes upward. If we have the two gases in separate jars, with a porous

partition between them, the two gases pass through the jars in opposite directions, so as to mix with each other. However, they move at different rates, the hydrogen nearly 4 times as fast as the air. Hence there is an increase of pressure in the jar that contained the air. By the use of the apparatus of Fig. 34, this difference of pressure can be shown. 

Because hydrogen diffuses so readily, it leaks out of balloons and airships containing it, and they must be recharged frequently. 

HYDROGEN 

AIR

FIG. 34. 

The hydrogen in the beaker flows through the wall of the porous cup more rapidly than air can flow out. As a result there is an increased amount of gas inside the porous cup and the bottle. 

39. Chemical properties.-Hydrogen burns in air or oxygen, producing water (§ 27). The flame is almost colorless, but very hot. 

A jet of hydrogen burning in air will continue to burn if placed in chlorine. The product is hydrogen chloride (§ 130). Hydrogen does not "support combustion,” as oxygen does, because ordinary combustibles, such as wood, paper, and coal, can not unite with hydrogen. A jet of oxygen can, however, burn in hydrogen (§ 24). Under the right conditions hydrogen combines with nitrogen to form ammonia (§ 220), with sulphur to form hydrogen sulphide (§ 178), with carbon to form hydrocarbons, such as acetylene, and with active metals, such as sodium and calcium, to form hydrides of these metals.

Hydrogen is an inactive element when compared with oxygen. A mixture of hydrogen and oxygen does not react at the ordinary temperature except under the influence of an active catalyst, such as finely divided plati-

HYDROGEN 

57 5 The heat set num. If, however, one part of the oxygen-hydrogen mixture is heated above 615° C., as by a burning match or an electric spark, the reaction flashes almost instantaneously through the whole mixture. free expands the gases and the resulting steam very suddenly.

Hence there is, for an instant, so great an increase of pressure that it often breaks the vessel in which the reaction takes place. 

We call such a sudden combustion an explosion (§ 24).
