The oxides must be heated in order to react with the hydrogen. The water appears as steam, but may be condensed. The action is a replacement (§ 37); the hydrogen takes the place of the metal, and sets the metal free.

A substance which removes oxygen from a compound, by itself uniting with the oxygen, is called a reducing agent. The removal of oxygen from a compound in this way is called reduction. We also call a reducing agent a deoxidizing agent, and we call reduction deoxidation. 

The reducing agent always becomes oxidized; there is no

reduction without an oxidation. Most oxides are oxidizing agents, especially when hot. Potassium nitrate, or niter, is the oxidizing agent in ordinary, black gunpowder (§ 233), and potassium chlorate is the oxidizer in match heads and fireworks.

Carbon and hydrogen are the common reducing agents: 

Copper oxide + carbon-copper+carbon dioxide ↑. 

41. Uses of hydrogen. The uses of hydrogen depend partly upon its physical properties and partly upon its chemical properties.

HYDROGEN COPPER OXIDE FIG. 36. 

(1) Hydrogen has great lifting power and is therefore used to inflate balloons and airships. One liter of air weighs 1.293 g., under standard conditions, and one liter of hydrogen weighs 0.09 g. The difference is 1.2 g. Thus every liter of hydrogen has a lifting power (in air) of 1.2 g., or over 13 times (1.2÷0.09) its Own weight. Ordinary balloons are filled with illuminating (fuel) gas, which contains much hydrogen. The gas, helium, is now used in airships, whenever possible. It has twice the density of hydrogen, but it has the great advantage of being entirely non-combustible.

Hydrogen dried by means of calcium chloride is passed over heated copper oxide. It reduces the copper oxide to copper and is itself oxidized by the copper oxide to water. (2) Hydrogen is a good reducing agent. It removes the oxygen from the oxides of many metals, and sets the metals free. (3) It is a good fuel. When 1 g. of it is burned in

HYDROGEN 

59 oxygen, and the steam produced is allowed to condense to water, 34,200 calories of heat (§ 61) are set free; this is enough to heat 342 g. of water from 0° C. to 100° C. 

One gram of carbon, when burned, gives only as much, or 8080 calories.

OXYGEN HYDROGEN FIG. 37. 

An oxyhydrogen blowpipe. (4) The hydrogen flame is very hot and can be used to produce a very high temperature or an intense light. In the oxyhydrogen blowpipe the hydrogen flame is concentrated into a very small space, so that it is intensely hot. 

The blowpipe consists of a small, inner tube connected with a tank of compressed oxygen, and a larger, outer tube supplying compressed hydrogen. The hydrogen is first turned on and lighted; then the oxygen is allowed to enter the hydrogen flame very slowly. If the flame is surrounded by a box of non-conducting materials, a temperature of 2500° C. can be obtained. This is far above the melting point of platinum (1755° C.) and the boiling point of silver (1955° C.). A piece of quicklime becomes white hot where the flame strikes it, and forms a "lime light." This was formerly used for stereopticons, but has now been largely replaced by the electric lamp. As a source of heat, the oxyhydrogen blowpipe is being replaced by the oxyacetylene torch (§ 30). (5) Hydrogen can unite with many oils (oils are liquid fats) and change them into solid fats. The process is called the hydrogenation, or hardening, of oils. Thus cottonseed oil and hydrogen unite, in the presence of a catalyst of finely divided nickel, and give fats similar to beef suet and tallow. These are used as vegetable substitutes for lard and butter, and for the preparation of hard soaps. (6) Hydrogen is needed in large quantities in the fixation of nitrogen by the Haber process. Under proper conditions ammonia is formed, which is used as a plant fertilizer, or is converted into other nitrogen compounds, such as nitric acid (§§ 220 and 222).

The cheapest source of hydrogen for large-scale, commercial use is water gas. This is made by the action of steam upon white hot coal or coke. It is a mixture of carbon monoxide and hydrogen (§ 290). 

FIG. 38. 

Dr. Robert Hare (1781-1858), the 

American chemist who invented the oxyhydrogen blowpipe and prepared potassium. 

42. The Bunsen burner.-The fuel gases we use most commonly in our houses and laboratories are coal gas, water gas, and natural gas. All three are mixtures of gases. All of them contain hydrogen, either free, or in combination with carbon. When these gases burn, the products are chiefly carbon dioxide and water. When the oxygen supply is not sufficient to oxidize all the carbon completely to carbon dioxide, the very poisonous gas, carbon monoxide, may also be formed. This gas is present in the exhaust gases of an automobile engine. 

The Bunsen burner of the laboratory is a device for using a fuel gas to produce a hot, sootless flame. The gas stoves we use in our kitchens are essentially Bunsen burners. When we burn coal gas or water gas in a Bunsen burner with the air holes closed, the flame is a luminous one. When we open the holes, the flame becomes non-luminous. The same amount of gas burns as before, but the flame is much smaller, and consequently hotter. 

FIG. 39. 

The parts of a 

Bunsen burner.

The exterior of the Bunsen burner consists of the base, a tube or barrel, with holes near the base, and a ring for regulating the

HYDROGEN 

61 size of the holes. Inside the barrel there is a small tip, called the spud, through which the fuel gas, under the pressure of the gas in the mains, is forced in a rapid, fine stream. In the barrel the gas becomes mixed with air that enters through the holes. The air is forced in, by atmospheric pressure, to replace that which is dragged along by the stream of gas issuing from the spud. At the top of the barrel the mixture of fuel gas and air finds an excess of oxygen, and combustion is complete. 

The Bunsen burner needs care. down low, and the holes remain wide open, an explosive mixture of gas and air may be formed; the flame will then "strike back" and burn at the tip of the spud. The danger is that the base may become so hot that it will melt the rubber connection tubing, and the gas will escape and catch fire. The Bunsen burner should be ignited with the holes at the base closed, or nearly so, and the gas supply should never be turned down unless the holes are partly closed at the same time.

The blast lamp of the laboratory resembles the oxyhydrogen blowpipe.

Instead of depending upon the air supply that enters, as in the Bunsen burner, we force a rapid blast of air

If the gas supply is turned 

FIG. 40. 

Robert W. Bunsen (1811-1899). 

Investigator in Organic Chemistry, expert in the analysis of gases, and discoverer of spectrum analysis. into the midst of the burning gas. The region of combustion is made still smaller than in the Bunsen flame, and is correspondingly hotter. 

43. Summary.-Hydrogen is a constituent of all living things and of water, acids, and bases. 

Hydrogen is prepared from water, either by electrolysis or by the action of metals. Only active metals act upon cold water. 

The products are hydrogen and the hydroxide of the metal. 

Iron reacts with steam to produce iron oxide and hydrogen.

AIR 

Sufficiently active metals react with acids to produce salts and hydrogen. The reactions are replacements, or displacements. 

Hydrogen is the lightest substance known. It may be liquefied and frozen. Hydrogen. diffuses more rapidly than any other gas. 

Hydrogen is adsorbed, or occluded, by metals like platinum and palladium. A mixture of hydrogen and air is set on fire by the heat given off in the process.

GAS AND AIR FIG. 41. 

AIR GAS

The burning plate of a kitchen gas stove. 

Hydrogen unites with oxygen to produce water. If the mixture of the two gases is in an enclosed space, and is set on fire, an explosion usually results.

Hydrogen unites with nitrogen, with sulphur, and with carbon, but much less readily than with oxygen. 

Hydrogen unites with the oxygen combined with metals in many oxides. It thus reduces the oxides, forming the metals and water. Hydrogen and carbon are our common reducing agents. 

Hydrogen is used in large amounts for: (1) its lifting power, (2) its reducing action, (3) as a fuel, (4) for its intensely hot flame, (5) to hydrogenate, or harden, oils, and (6) in the fixation of nitrogen.

The Bunsen burner changes a smoky, fuel-gas flame into a hotter, smokeless flame.

A laboratory blast lamp is a Bunsen burner in which the air is supplied in a rapid stream, or blast.

CHAPTER IV GASES 

45. The measuring of gases.-Modern science began when men began to measure quantity. The balance opened up a new world to Lavoisier and the chemists of his day. In measuring the quantity of matter in a solid, we weigh it. Liquids resemble solids in having a definite volume; but they have no definite form. We can weigh a liquid body, but it must have a container. A common method of getting the weight of a liquid is to measure its volume in cubic centimeters and then to multiply this number by the density, in grams per cu. cm. Thus, if we know that mercury has a density of 13.6 grams per cu. cm., we calculate that 10 cu. cm. of it weigh 136 grams.

Gases differ from solids and liquids in having no definite form and no definite volume. When the chemist determines the weight of some gas produced in a chemical change, he usually does so, as in the case of a liquid, by measuring the volume and then calculating the weight. 

But there are at least two questions the chemist must ask himself in using the volume of a gas in a calculation: (1) At what temperature was the volume measured? (2) Under what pressure was it measured?

The chemist must take account of temperature and pressure because he knows that cooling a gas contracts its volume, while heating expands it. Increase of pressure 64

GASES 

65 makes the gas volume smaller; decrease of pressure makes the volume larger. The density of a gas is always stated as so many grams per cubic centimeter (or liter) at 0° C. and 760 mm. of mercury pressure. These are called standard conditions. The chemist therefore "corrects the volume for temperature and pressure." That is, he determines what the observed volume would become, if the gas were being measured under standard conditions.

From the corrected volume and the density the chemist then calculates the weight of the gas. 

TO AIR 

PUMP

In the following pages we shall learn more about the properties of gases and then some remarkable conclusions regarding gas structure. 

46. Pressure of a gas.-We live at the bottom of an ocean of air, called the atmosphere, and our bodies, the air about us, and any gases we are measuring are alike under the atmosphere's pressure. 

The atmosphere's pressure is due to the weight of the air. One liter of air, under standard conditions, weighs 1.293 g. One cubic foot of air, under ordinary conditions, weighs about 14 ounces. The lowest layers of the atmosphere are under the greatest pressure, like the bottom pillow of a pile of pillows. We measure atmospheric pressure by means of a barometer. 

FIG. 42. 

When the air is removed from a tube by suction, mercury rises into the tube. How far will it rise?

We can make a simple barometer out of a glass tube about 32 in. (800 mm.) long and closed at one end. 

We fill the tube entirely with mercury, close the open end with the finger, and invert the tube in a dish of

mercury. When we release the mercury of the tube, some of it runs down into the dish, but a column having a vertical height of about 30 in. (760 mm). remains. 

FIG. 43. 

Two forms of the barometer. 

The height of the barometer in each case is the vertical distance between the two mercury levels.

The mercury does not all run out because the pressure of the atmosphere holds it up. 

The height of the mercury column changes with the atmospheric pressure. The average at sea level is about 760 mm. This is called standard pressure, or a pressure of one atmosphere. A column of mercury 30 in. high and 1 sq. in. in cross section weighs 

14.7 lbs. We therefore say that the atmosphere's pressure on every square inch of a body at sea level is about 15 lbs. 

The barometer is used to measure the relatively small changes in atmospheric pressure from day to day. For much greater gas pressure, such as that of steam in a steam boiler, or a compressed gas in a storage cylinder, or of compressed air in an automobile tire, a pressure gauge 74 is used. A very sensitive pressure gauge is also used, in place of a mercury barometer, to tell atmospheric pressure; it is called an aneroid (ǎn'ē-roid) barometer. The aneroid barometer consists of a small metal box from which the air has been removed. The side of the box is elastic, and bends inward a little more when the atmospheric pressure increases, and springs back into place when the extra pressure is removed. The slight movements of the side of the box are magnified by levers and wheels which cause relatively large movements in the "hand," or pointer, of the instru- The aneroid barometer must, of course, be compared ment.

An aneroid barometer.

47. Standard temperature.-A chemical thermometer is made out of a "capillary" tube with thick walls and a bulb at one end. The bulb and part of the tube are filled with mercury. The thermometer is marked, or graduated, according to the Centigrade scale. Its zero mark is the position taken by the top of the 0 mercury "thread" when the thermometer is surrounded by melting ice.

The other fixed point of the Centigrade thermometer (100° C.) is the position of the top of the mercury when the thermometer is surrounded by the steam coming off from boiling water. The water must be boiling under standard pressure (760 mm.). The space between the freezing point and boiling point marks is divided into 100 degrees, and degrees of the same length are measured off above and below the two fixed points. 

Zero Centigrade is standard temperature. The Centigrade scale is sometimes called the Celsius scale, after the inventor.

FIG. 45. 

Determining the freezing point and the boiling point of a thermometer. 

48. How volume changes with temperature. We know that heating a gas expands it, and cooling contracts it. 

Charles, a French scientist, found the amount of this expansion and contraction. If he began with air at 0° C.,

and then heated it to 1° C., the amount of its expansion was 23 of the volume at 0°. If he heated the air to 

10° C., the amount of expansion was 273 of the volume at 0°. At 100° C. the expansion STEAM

In other words, at 273° C. the volume of air is double the volume at 0° C. It is easy for us to see that if we were to take 273 cu. cm. of air at 0° C., the volume would expand to 274 cu. cm. at 1° C., to 283 cu. cm. at 10° C., to 298 cu. cm. at 25° 

C., to 373 cu. cm. at 100° C., and so on.

This regular expansion and contraction is a remarkable fact. It is true not only of air, but of all gases. It is true below 0° C. as well as above it. Thus, at −1° C. a gas contracts 23 of its volume at 0° C., at -10° C. it contracts of its volume at 0° C., and so on. If 273 cu. cm. of a gas, taken at 0° C., are cooled to -1° C., the volume becomes 272 cu. cm. At -10° C. it becomes 

263 cu. cm.; at -100° C. it is 173 cu. cm., and so on. 

It is easy for us to calculate changes in volume from changes in temperature. Suppose we have 100 cu. cm. of a gas at 0° C., what will its volume be at 25° C.? We can see that 273 cu. cm. at 0° C. would become 298 cu. cm. at 25° C.; therefore 100 cu. cm. becomes 100X299, or 109.16 cu. cm.

51. How pressure affects volume.-The relation be tween the volume of a gas and the pressure under which it is measured was first stated by Robert Boyle, a British scientist. Boyle's law is:

If the temperature remains unchanged, the volume of a gas changes inversely as the pressure. 

This means, of course, that the greater the pressure is, the smaller the volume.

There is also a definite relation between the density of a gas and the pressure. The density is the mass in one cubic centimeter (or liter; § 13). Increasing the pressure will, therefore, increase the density also. 

FIG. 47. 

The volume of a gas changes inversely as the pressure, if the temperature is kept constant. The gas in the tube at the right is under the atmospheric pressure plus the pressure due to the difference in level of the mercury. 

52. Solving problems by Boyle's law.-By the use of 

Boyle's law we calculate changes in volume from changes in pressure. (1) A gas has a volume of 50 cu. cm. under 720 mm. pressure. 

Calculate the volume under 760 mm. 

Let vthe volume under 760 mm. 

Since the pressure increases, the volume must decrease. The fraction by which we multiply 50 must have the smaller number in the numerator. 

Therefore v = 50X; 720 or 47.4 cu. cm. 760'

Let vthe volume under 700 mm. 

The pressure decreases; the volume must therefore increase.

54. The structure of gases.-It is to be hoped that the arithmetic of the preceding sections has not blinded the student to the remarkable behavior of gases. Why should all gases expand by the same fractional amount when heated, and contract in the same proportion when cooled? Why should all gases change in volume inversely as the pressure they support? Why do gases mix so readily with one another even against the effect of gravity, as in the case of hydrogen and air (§ 38)? 

The fact that the volumes of all gases are affected in the same way by equal changes in temperature and pressure (Charles' and Boyle's laws) calls for an explanation. Scientists explain this fact by the hypothesis that gases are made up of very small particles called molecules (mol'ē-kiuls). Scientists assume that all the molecules of any given gas are alike, and that the molecules are far apart, with wide spaces between O O O ⚫ 

FIG. 48. 

A gas consists of molecules far apart or close together according to the pressure. them. They also assume that molecules of gases are in rapid motion; that is, that the molecules have kinetic energy (§ 14).

Molecules are very small. One liter of a gas, under standard conditions, contains about 2.69×1022 (2.69 followed by 22 ciphers) of them. The distances between

the molecules of a gas are perhaps 1000 times as great as the diameter of the molecules. 

Molecules occupy space much as a person occupies a room, not by occupying it completely, but by moving about in it. When we compress a gas, it is the average distance between moving molecules that we make smaller, not the size of the molecules themselves. 

The energy of molecular motion is heat. The rapidity with which the particles move increases with the rise in degree of heat, that is, with the temperature. The direction of the motion is along straight lines, except as the molecules collide with one another or with the walls of the vessel holding the gas. The bombardment of the walls of a vessel by the moving molecules produces gas pressure. Increasing the energy of the molecules increases the number of blows rained upon the walls of the vessel, and therefore increases the pressure. 

Because gas molecules have energy, a gas fills any space presented to it. For the same reason gas mixtures do not separate into layers, with the heaviest gas on the bottom. Instead of this, gases mix in all proportions, producing a mixture having a uniform composition. Air is such a mixture. 

The attraction between molecules is called cohesion. 

The cohesion between gas molecules must be very slight. 

In the case of liquids cohesion must be greater than with gases, yet the molecules move about freely enough to allow the liquid to take the shape of the vessel that contains it. 

55. Summary.-Chemical progress depends upon the measurement of volume and weight in the case of gases as well as in solids and liquids.

GASES 

75 Instead of weighing a gas directly the chemist usually measures its volume and then calculates the weight. 

Volume in cu. cm. Xdensity, in grams per cu. cm., = weight. 

Standard conditions are 0° C. and 760 mm. of mercury pressure. 

The pressure of the atmosphere is due to the weight of air. 

One liter of air weighs 1.293 g., under standard conditions.

The atmospheric pressure is measured by the barometer. On every square inch, at sea level, the pressure is about 15 pounds. 

The scale of Absolute temperature has its zero at -273° C. 

To change Centigrade readings to Absolute readings we add 273 to them, algebraically.

Charles' law is that if the pressure remains unchanged, the volume of a gas changes directly as its Absolute temperature. 

Boyle's law is that if the temperature remains unchanged, the volume of a gas changes inversely as the pressure. 

By applying Charles' and Boyle's law we can "correct" the volume of a gas for temperature and pressure. 

Charles' and Boyle's laws, together with the diffusion of gases, point to the hypothesis that gases are made up of moving molecules, with wide spaces between them. 

When we compress a gas, we diminish the average distance between molecules, and therefore diminish the volume. 

When we heat a gas, we add energy, and thus increase the average distance between molecules. As a result we increase the volume.

Because of the energy of gas molecules and the great distances between them, gases mix with one another in all proportions.

57. Synthesis of water, by volume. The electrolysis of water (§ 35) gives us hydrogen and oxygen in the proportion of two volumes of hydrogen to one of oxygen. 

It is a method of analysis, or "tearing apart. The decisive proof of the composition of water is the fact that we can also synthesize it, that is, "put it together," out of hydrogen and oxygen and nothing else. We can carry out the synthesis of water both by volume and by weight.

To show the proportions by volume in which hydrogen and oxygen unite, we use a eudiometer (Fig. 49). This is a strong, bent, glass tube with platinum wires sealed into the upper part of one arm. This arm can be opened or closed by a stopper having a hole bored through it.

FIG. 49. 

A U-shaped eudiometer for the explosion of mixture of hydrogen and oxygen.

The hydrogen and oxygen are introduced through the glass stopper one at a time. When enough hydrogen, for example, has been run in, mercury is added to, or taken out of, the open arm, so as to make the levels of the mercury the same in both arms. We know that the hydrogen is then under atmospheric pressure. 

Suppose that its volume is 20 cu. cm. Oxygen is then added through the perforated stopper, and the mercury levels are made 77

the same, as in the case of the hydrogen. Suppose that the total volume is now 40 cu. cm. The volume of oxygen taken is, therefore, also 20 cu. cm. 

The stopper is then closed tightly, and mercury is run out of the open arm until the expanded gas mixture nearly fills the closed arm.

The gases are thus put under reduced pressure, so as to make the shock of the explosion, when they unite, as small as possible. An electric spark is then passed between the platinum wires, and the union of hydrogen and oxygen takes place with a flash of light. When the apparatus has cooled, the mercury levels same, and the volume of the gas that remains is Suppose that it is 10 cu. cm. A test will show that it is oxygen. Hence 20 cu. cm. of hydrogen united with 10 cu. cm. of oxygen. If the hydrogen had been taken in excess, say 

30 cu. cm. of hydrogen for 10 cu. cm. of oxygen, 10 cu. cm. of are made the determined.

FIG. 50. 

A eudiometer with a jacket in which the volumes of the gases that are exploded and the steam that is formed can all be measured. hydrogen would have been left over. The volume of liquid water formed is too small to be measured. 

All we can see, after the explosion, is a little mist inside the eudiometer. 

58. The volume of the steam formed. 

If we wish to know the volume of steam formed when given volumes of hydrogen and oxygen unite, we must keep the closed arm of the eudiometer hot enough to prevent the steam from condensing. In this case we surround the eudiometer by a jacket (Fig. 50), and pass steam, or the vapor of some other suitable liquid, through the jacket. The volumes of the gases taken, and of the steam formed, are read at the temperature of the jacket. When this is done, the volume of the steam formed is found to be the same as that of the hydrogen used. Hence 2 volumes of

79 hydrogen unite with 1 volume of oxygen to form 2 volumes of steam.

Steam Steam 2 vols. 1 vol. 2 vols. 

The proportions in which hydrogen and oxygen unite, and the proportion of the steam formed, are so simple that we may well wonder if such simplicity is only accidental, or is true also of the combination of other gases. 

Gay-Lussac found, in 1808, that whenever gases combine to form gaseous products, simple proportions by volume are the rule. He stated this fact as the law of simple volumes:

The volumes of gases which react and are produced in a chemical change have definite, simple proportions to one another, and these proportions can be stated in small, whole numbers.

In this way the proportion by weight of hydrogen to oxygen in water can be determined. 

59. Synthesis of water, by weight. The exact proportion, by weight, of hydrogen to oxygen in water is so

important that many methods have been used to obtain it. One of these is to weigh out some cupric oxide, to heat it in a porcelain boat set into a hard-glass tube, and to pass dry, pure hydrogen over it. The hydrogen reduces the cupric oxide (§ 40). The water produced is collected in a tube of calcium chloride or of some other substance that absorbs water readily. The loss in weight of the cupric oxide is the weight of the oxygen which combined with the hydrogen to form water. 

When the weight of the oxygen is subtracted from the weight of the water, the remainder is the weight of the hydrogen. 

According to the best determinations, 1.008 parts of hydrogen combine with 8 parts of oxygen. The composition of water is, therefore:

60. Law of definite proportions.-Every compound has a definite composition, just as water has. We state this fact in the law of definite proportions: 

A given compound is always made up of the same elements united in definite proportions by weight. 

Thus, in carbon dioxide, carbon and oxygen are always present in the proportion of 3 parts of carbon to 8 parts

WATER of oxygen.

We may write the composition of carbon dioxide as we did that of water: 3 Carbon, or 27.27 per cent. 11' Oxygen, or 72.73 per cent. 

61. Physical properties of water.-Water enters into so many changes in nature, and is used for so many purposes by man, that a great deal of space would be needed to give all its properties. (1) Water is the only substance we use commonly in all three physical states of solid, liquid, and gas. The change of ice to liquid water and this to water vapor, or steam, are considered physical changes; yet the water in each of these states has its own set of properties. (2) Liquid water is practically odorless and tasteless, if pure. The taste and odor of natural water are due to dissolved gases (air and carbon dioxide) and to dissolved minerals. Small quantities of water are colorless, but larger masses are blue. The same is true of ice. Even snow has sometimes a distinctly blue color. (3) We might think that water would be most dense at 0° C., just before it turns into solid ice. This is not true. Water has its greatest density at 4° C. At 4° C. 

1 cubic centimeter of water weighs 1 gram, or 1000 cu. cm. (1 liter) weigh 1 kilogram. (4) Water acts as our most common solvent. The minerals of the soil can be taken up by plants only when in solution, and most of the reactions we study likewise take place in the dissolved state. (5) The freezing point of water (0° C., or 32° F.,

under 760 mm. pressure) is the same as the melting point of ice, if the water is in contact with some ice (§ 80). (6) When water freezes, it expands, so that 10 cu. cm. of the liquid at 0° C. become 10.9 cu. cm. of ice. Ice is thus 0.92 as dense as water, and floats in fresh water with about 92% of its volume under water. (7) Water boils at 100° C. under 760 mm. pressure. 

The boiling point depends upon the pressure. Under 

560 mm. pressure water boils at 90° C.; under 3581 mm., at 150° C. At 0° C. the pressure of the vapor of water and of ice is the same: 4.5 mm. Ice has some vapor pressure, and evaporates below the freezing point. (8) Steam is a colorless gas about 0.62 as heavy as air. 

The cloud which we see when steam comes in contact with cold air, as at the spout of a teakettle, is not steam, but a fog made up of droplets of liquid water. The fog is caused by the condensation of the steam. About 

1600 cu. cm. of steam are formed at 100° C. from 1 cu. cm. of water. (9) The unit used to measure quantity of heat is the calorie. 

This is the amount of heat needed to raise the temperature of one gram of water one degree centigrade. In freezing, one gram of water gives off 79 calories of heat. To melt one gram of ice requires the same amount: 79 calories. This is called the heat of fusion (melting) of ice. 

100° C. into steam, also at

The same amount of heat (10) To change 1 gram of water at 

100° C., requires 540 calories of heat. is set free when 1 gram of steam condenses to water. This is called the heat of vaporization of water. 

The heat set free in the freezing of water and taken up in the melting of ice (heat of fusion) is sometimes called the latent heat of water. The heat taken up when water changes into steam, and set free when steam condenses to water (heat of vaporization) is also called the latent heat of steam.

WATER (11) It requires more heat to raise the temperature of 1 gram of water 1° C. than 1 gram of any other substance except hydrogen. 

In other words, the heat capacity of water is greater than that of any substance except hydrogen. Relative, or comparative, heat capacity is also called specific heat. We define the specific heat of a substance as the number of calories needed to raise the temperature of one gram of the substance one Centigrade degree. 

We see that the specific heat of water is 1. That of iron is 

0.11; of granite, 0.192; of limestone, 0.2. Cold water becomes warm much more slowly than rocks and soil do, and warm water cools more slowly, too. Thus a large body of water moderates both the summer and the winter temperature of the land along its shores. (12) What we have learned of the physical properties of water shows that these properties are used as standards in many forms of measurement. The boiling point and freezing point of water are the two fixed points of the thermometer. The weight of 1 cu. cm. of water forms the metric unit of weight, the gram. The density of water is taken as 1. We get the specific gravity of a solid or liquid by dividing its weight by the weight of its own volume of water. The calorie, the unit of heat quantity, is derived from the heat capacity of water. Finally, the specific heat of other substances is stated in relation to that of water. 

62. The water we use.-Water makes up a large part of all animal and vegetable tissue. No life can exist without it. Our own bodies are about 70% water. A table in the Appendix shows the percentage of water in our foods. Water exists not only in the atmosphere, in lakes and streams, and in the ocean, but in the crust of the earth. All rocks and soil below a certain depth are saturated with water.

The water of lakes and streams is never pure, but contains materials dissolved from soil and rocks. Even rain water takes up dust and gases from the air. Ocean water contains perhaps 3.5% of dissolved solids, of which

nearly is common salt. Whether we can call water pure, or not, depends largely upon what we wish to do with it. For use in cooking, drinking, and household cleaning, water should not contain the germs of disease. 

For use in laundries as a cleansing agent, or in boilers to

Proportion of water in some of our foods. produce steam, water should not contain magnesium or calcium salts which make it hard. To the chemist pure water is water containing no dissolved or suspended materials.

We must remember that the appearance of a drinking water is no proof of its purity. A water may be clear, cool, and pleasant to the taste, and yet be polluted; while a dirty-looking water may be safe to drink. If we do not know the history of a well or spring water, our only safe way is to have the water tested. Ice made from polluted water is also dangerous, for disease germs are not all killed by freezing.

We generally purify water: (1) by distillation; (2) by boiling; (3) by filtration; and (4) by the use of chemicals.

WATER 63. Distillation.-Distillation is the change of a liquid into its vapor and the condensation of the vapor back to liquid form. The liquid obtained by the condensation of the vapor is the distillate. Other. liquids, such as gasoline and ether, may be distilled as well as water. 

Distilled water is not necessarily pure. The first portions of the distillate usually contain ammonia, which comes over with the water. 

The solids of the WATER OUTFLOW

FIG. 53. 

WATER ENTERS

Distillation of water in the laboratory. The condenser is called a Liebig condenser. Why is the water introduced at the bottom of the condenser? original water usually remain behind. Chemically pure water is difficult to prepare. It is also hard to keep, since it acts upon the vessels, even of glass, in which it is stored. 

If we boil water in a closed vessel, we can make the pressure higher, or lower, than that of the atmosphere, and can therefore change the temperature at which water boils. In a "vacuum,' or reduced pressure apparatus, we can distill water below 100° C.; in a pressure cooker we can heat it above 100° C. A vessel for heating materials under a high pressure is called an autoclave. 

In the household we sometimes boil water to make it safe to drink. If continued long enough, boiling kills the germs. The flat taste of boiled and distilled water is due partly to the fact that dissolved gases, such as air and carbon dioxide, have been driven off. The taste is improved if the water is aerated, by shaking it

with air or by pouring it in a small stream from one vessel to another.

Other chemicals are used to destroy bacteria directly. One of these, employed in many European cities, is ozone (§ 31). It is added after settling and filtration have taken place. In North 

America chlorine and "chloride of lime" (bleaching powder) are used instead. A small amount of either of these will destroy the germs that get through the sand filters.

Rays of ultra-violet light, having a very short wave length, are also used to make filtered water safer to drink. The water is made to flow at such a rate that it receives the necessary exposure to this light. The lamp consists of an electric arc passing through mercury vapor. The light from the mercury vapor lamp must pass out of the bulb through quartz, since glass absorbs too many ultra-violet rays (§ 298). 

66. Hard and soft water.-Many natural waters must be purified not only to make them fit for drinking, but also for bathing and laundry uses. In rain water and distilled water soap forms a lather, or suds, quickly and easily, while in most well and spring waters soap produces a lather with difficulty and only after a grayish precipitate or scum has separated out. Water that forms suds easily is "soft"; while water which consumes a great deal of soap before producing a lather is "hard." 

Hard water feels harsh and unpleasant to the skin. The water of well and springs was once rain water; it became hard in its passage through rocks and soil, by taking up in solution such minerals as calcium sulphate, magnesium chloride, and calcium carbonate. We define the hardness of water as its soap-consuming power. 

Hard water is objectionable in the laundry because the precipitate formed with soap collects in the pores of soft fabrics making them brittle and easily worn out. It is also objectionable in the factory. Much water is needed in boilers, to produce steam. 

If a boiler is filled again and again with hard water, "boiler scale" is deposited, as on the inside of a teakettle. The scale is a poor conductor of heat; hence the boiler wall is usually overheated as water is turned into steam. The weakening of boilers from this cause has caused serious boiler explosions and loss of life. 

Hardness which may be removed by boiling is called temporary hardness; that which will not, is permanent hardness. Hardness

WATER 

89 is removed by water softeners, such as washing powders, lime, ammonia, and soda (§§ 336 and 355). 

67. Chemical properties of water.-1. Water is a very stable compound, that is, it is not easily decomposed into its elements. At high temperatures (above 1000° C.) it begins to break up into hydrogen and oxygen, but even at 2000° C. only about 1.8% is decomposed. Moreover, at this high temperature hydrogen and oxygen also unite to form water. We have combination going on at the same rate as decomposition. The double arrow shows this fact in the word equation: 

Water hydrogen+oxygen.

If we raise the temperature above 2000° C., the amount of decomposition is increased; if we lower the temperature, combination is favored. A chemical change in which decomposition of a compound and recombination of its parts take place at the same time is called a dissociation. 

Pure water is decomposed by the electric current very, very slowly. If a little sulphuric acid is present, the decomposition proceeds rapidly (§ 35). 

2. Water reacts with active metals (§ 36). The metals replace hydrogen, and hydroxides (or oxides) of the metals are formed.

Calcium+water-calcium hydroxide+hydrogen. 

3. Water reacts with many non-metals, such as chlorine and fluorine (§ 131). These elements replace the oxygen of the water and combine with the hydrogen. 

Chlorine + water hydrogen oxygen hydrogen chloride + oxygen. hydrogen chlorine

4. Water unites with oxides. When it unites with the oxide of a metal, such as calcium oxide (quicklime), calcium hydroxide, a base, is formed. With sodium oxide, water gives sodium hydroxide. 

Calcium oxide+water-calcium hydroxide calcium oxygen Sodium oxide+water-sodium hydroxide sodium calcium, oxygen, hydrogen sodium, oxygen, hydrogen oxygen 5. Water also unites with the oxides of many nonmetals. In this case acids are formed. 

Sulphur dioxide+water→sulphurous acid sulphur hydrogen, sulphur, oxygen oxygen Carbon dioxide+water-carbonic acid carbon oxygen hydrogen, carbon, oxygen 6. When we allow some substances to crystallize from water, they unite with part of the water to form hydrates. Water so combined is called water of hydration, or water of crystallization (§ 81). Thus, if we dissolve white, powdered cupric sulphate in a little water, and let the solution evaporate slowly, what crystallizes out is blue vitriol, or bluestone, which consists of cupric sulphate combined with a definite amount of water. 

7. Besides acting with metals and non-metals, with oxides, and with other substances as water of hydration, water acts as a catalytic agent. Many of the reactions we have studied do not take place if the materials are entirely free from every trace of water. 

Thus, hydrogen and oxygen do not combine readily when a flame is applied, if both are really dry. Even phosphorus burns very slowly in oxygen if both are perfectly dry. We live in a "water world." 

68. Hydrogen peroxide. Hydrogen peroxide, also

WATER 

91 called hydrogen dioxide, is a second compound containing only hydrogen and oxygen. But while water is very stable, and is broken up with great difficulty, hydrogen peroxide is very unstable, and breaks up easily into water and oxygen. The name "peroxide" is given to it because it has a larger proportion of oxygen than water has. 

We can prepare a dilute solution of hydrogen peroxide if we dissolve sodium peroxide, a little at a time, in iced water and then add dilute hydrochloric acid. 

Sodium peroxide+hydrochloric acid-sodium chloride + sodium oxygen hydrogen chlorine sodium chlorine hydrogen peroxide hydrogen oxygen A commercial method of preparing hydrogen peroxide is to add barium peroxide to dilute sulphuric or phosphoric acid. The barium sulphate (or phosphate) formed is insoluble and can be separated from the solution containing the hydrogen peroxide. hydrogen 

Barium peroxide+sulphuric acid→barium sulphate↓ +peroxide barium oxygen hydrogen sulphur oxygen barium sulphur oxygen hydrogen oxygen The reaction for the preparation of hydrogen peroxide belongs to a new class, called double decomposition. We have already studied chemical changes which come under the head of combination, decomposition, and replacement. A double decomposition reaction may be represented by the general equation: 

AB+CD-AD+BC. 

In such reactions two compounds exchange constituents. 

Thus, in the reaction between sodium peroxide and hydro-

chloric acid the sodium of the peroxide combines with the chlorine of the hydrochloric acid, and the oxygen of the peroxide combines with the hydrogen of the hydrochloric acid.

Pure hydrogen peroxide is a colorless, syrupy liquid having a density of about 1.5 g. per cu. cm. The water

93 solution has a bitter taste and produces white spots upon the skin. Hydrogen peroxide is usually sold in a 3% solution. This solution gives off about 10 times its own volume of oxygen.

Hydrogen peroxide is used for its activity as an oxidizing agent. 

The oxygen which it gives off when it is decomposed is much more active than ordinary oxygen. We speak of its as nascent oxygen. 

It destroys organic coloring substances, changing them into colorless products. It can, therefore, be used as a bleaching agent. 

Hydrogen peroxide is used to bleach many fabrics that are injured by chlorine, although chlorine is cheaper (§ 134). Thus, it is used for hair, wool, silk, feathers, and ivory. It is also used as a disinfectant and antiseptic, to destroy harmful germs. In this case, as in bleaching, the active material is the nascent oxygen which the hydrogen peroxide gives off. 

70. Law of multiple proportions. The law of definite proportions is just as true in the case of hydrogen peroxide as in that of water. Analysis shows that hydrogen peroxide is very nearly 5.9% hydrogen and 94.1% oxygen, chemically combined. The proportion of hydrogen to oxygen is practically 1: 16, or 2: 32. In water the pro-. portion is 1 : 8, or 2: 16.

There are many other cases in which two (or more) given elements unite in different proportions to form a series of different compounds. Thus, carbon and oxygen form the compounds, carbon monoxide and carbon dioxide. Carbon and hydrogen form hundreds of compounds, three of which are marsh gas, ethylene, and acetylene. 

Study the table on the next page. 

The cases given illustrate the law of multiple proportions. We may state the law thus: When two elements form a series of compounds, the different weights of one element which combine with a fixed weight of the other

bear a simple ratio to one another. Thus, the weight of oxygen combined with 2 g. of hydrogen is just twice as great in hydrogen peroxide as in water; and the weight 

NAME OF COMPOUND PROPORTION BY WEIGHT OF ELEMENT HYDROGEN CARBON OXYGEN 

Water... 

Hydrogen peroxide..

Carbon monoxide. 

Carbon dioxide.. 

Marsh gas.. Ethylene.. Acetylene.... 22 16 32 12 16 12 32 4 12 42 24 2 24 of oxygen combined with 12 g. of carbon is twice as great in carbon dioxide as in carbon monoxide. 

John Dalton stated the law in 1804. He knew only a few series of compounds at the time; but the work of many chemists since his day has abundantly confirmed his conclusions. 

In the case of gases there is a law of multiple proportions by volume as well as by weight (see §§ 58 and 146). Water and hydrogen peroxide form a good illustration of this fact.

71. Summary.—We can carry out the synthesis of water both by volume and by weight. Two volumes of hydrogen and one volume of oxygen unite to give two volumes of steam. By weight the proportions are 1.008 g. of hydrogen to 8 g. of oxygen.

Gay-Lussac stated, as a law, that gases react in simple propor-

WATER 

95 tions by volume, just as hydrogen and oxygen do, and that the volume of the products, if gases, may also be expressed by small, whole numbers.

Every compound is made up of elements united in definite proportions by weight (law of definite proportions), but the proportions are not so simple as for union by volume. 

Water exists as a gas, liquid, and solid, and forms our most common solvent. It expands on freezing and on boiling. 

At 4° C. water has its greatest density: 1 g. per cu. cm. 

Water forms our standard of heat capacity. The calorie is the quantity of heat used in raising the temperature of 1 g. of water 

1 degree Centigrade, as from 15° C. to 16° C.

The specific heat of a substance is the number of calories required to raise the temperature of 1 g. of that substance 1 degree Centigrade.

No life can exist without water. 

Natural water contains substances dissolved from the air, rocks, and soil.

We purify water for special purposes by distillation, boiling, filtration, and the use of chemicals or ultra-violet light. 

Hardness of water is its soap-consuming power. 

Water is very stable toward heating, but is decomposed by the electric current.

The hydrogen of water is replaced by active metals. Its oxygen is replaced by active non-metals. 

Water unites with the oxides of metals to produce bases. With the oxides of non-metals it forms acids. 

Water unites with many substances to give hydrates (water of hydration or crystallization).

Water also acts as a catalytic agent. 

Hydrogen peroxide is prepared from a metal peroxide and a dilute acid, by a double decomposition reaction. 

Hydrogen peroxide is unstable; it gives off nascent oxygen. It is used for oxidizing purposes, as a bleaching and antiseptic agent. 

Hydrogen peroxide and water, in their composition, illustrate the law of multiple proportions, both by weight and by volume. 

When two or more elements form a series of compounds, the

different weights of one element which combine with a fixed weight of the others, bear a simple ratio to one another. 

72. Exercises.-1. Does a cake of ice sink more, or less, deeply into salt water than into fresh water? Why? 

2. How much of an iceberg remains above water? 

3. Engineers say that 1 cubic inch of water gives 1 cubic foot of steam. Is this exactly correct? Would it be more nearly correct if the steam were hotter than 100° C.? 

4. What effect has the high heat of fusion of ice upon the coming of spring near a frozen lake? 

5. Account for the fact that a light fall of snow sometimes disappears even though the temperature does not rise to the melting point. 

6. Compare the weight of 1 liter of steam with that of 1 liter of air at the same temperature (§ 61, 8). With the weight of 1 liter of hydrogen. 

7. What climate would you expect of a region on the east shore of 

Lake Michigan? Is this a fruit belt, or not? Asummer resort? Why? 

8. What would be some of the results, if ice were denser than water? 

9. Are tubs of water of any use in unheated cellars in which vegetables are stored over winter? Suggest some reasons. 

10. How does a pressure cooker help in the cooking of food? 

11. Which will warm a cold room more, a pound of water at 

100° C., or a pound of steam at 100° C.? Why? 

12. Which will warm a room more, a pound of water at 100° 

C., or a pound of mercury at 100° C.? Tell why. See § 61 and

Appendix III. 

13. When you boil water, its temperature remains the same until all the water has been changed into steam. What becomes of the heat? 

14. When you melt ice, the water in contact with the ice remains at 0° C. until all the ice has melted. What becomes of the heat? 

15. Why does water evaporating from your hand give you a sensation of cold? 

16. Calculate the volumes of hydrogen and oxygen you could obtain by entirely decomposing 18.016 g. of water; also 34.016 g. of hydrogen peroxide. Compare the results.

CHAPTER VI SOLUTION 

73. Solution and suspension.—A solution is a mixture consisting usually of a liquid (the solvent) and of a dissolved substance (the solute). Suppose you put a tablespoonful of lemon juice at the bottom of a glass, then incline the glass, and fill it carefully by pouring water slowly down its side. The lemonade in the upper part of the glass will have little, if any, taste, while that at the bottom will still be practically all lemon juice. The mixture is not yet uniform. But if you stir up the two liquids, a teaspoonful taken from the top will be just as sour as one taken from the bottom. The lemonade is now a uniform mixture. You may take as much lemon juice as you like, and as much water as you like, and the two can always be uniformly mixed. Lemon juice and water are miscible (mis'i-bl; capable of being mixed) in all proportions. Milk and water, alcohol and water, glycerine and alcohol are all miscible pairs of liquids. 

But gasoline and water, and olive oil and water are immiscible pairs of liquids. 

Now let us sweeten the lemonade by dropping into it a tablespoonful of sugar. Most of the sugar falls to the bottom, but if you stir the liquid, all the sugar finally dissolves, and one teaspoonful is just as sweet as another. 

Again there is a uniform mixture. When you begin stir- 97

ring, some sugar remains undissolved, and floats about in the lemonade. It is then suspended. If left to itself, it settles to the bottom. A suspension of sand particles in water also settles quickly; one of clay particles (muddy water) more slowly. Suspended particles make the water turbid, or roily. When a substance has really dissolved, it will not settle, and the solution is clear. Suspended particles can usually be filtered out. Dissolved substances cannot be; they are broken up into particles (molecules) so small that they pass through the pores of the filter. 

Water is the most common solvent. If we do not name a solvent, we always mean water. Other common solvents are alcohol, ether, gasoline, and carbon tetrachloride. These can dissolve some substances that water cannot. Thus gasoline dissolves grease from clothing. 

74. Solubility.—If only a small amount of sugar is dissolved in a given amount of water, the sugar solution is dilute. A dilute solution has a small concentration of solute. If the concentration of solute is large, the solution is a concentrated one. If we evaporate some of the water from a dilute sugar solution, we concentrate the solution. In speaking of a solution as dilute, or as concentrated, we are using very general terms. To say a solution is a 5% one, or a 10% one, is much more precise. 

Thus a 10% solution of sugar has sugar dissolved in water in the proportion of 10 parts by weight of sugar to 90 parts of water. 

If, in sweetening lemonade, we add so much sugar that even after vigorous stirring some is left over, the lemonade is saturated with sugar. The solution is at its greatest concentration under the conditions. But we can know that a solution is saturated only if some of the solute

SOLUTION 

99 remains in contact with it, undissolved. By the solubility of a substance the chemist means the number of grams of it in 100 cu. cm. of water, at saturation. The temperature must also be stated (§ 47). A dilute solution may be a saturated solution, if the solubility is small. Thus, at 18° 

C. only 0.0013 g. of marble dissolve in 100 cu. cm. of water, 150 140 130 120 KI Ca Ciz yet the solution is saturated. 

The terms "insoluble" and "immiscible" must not be taken too literally, for every substance probably dissolves to some extent. The solubility of a solute varies with different solvents. Sugar and salt are very soluble in water, but much less soluble in alcohol. An alcoholic solution is called a tincture. The pharmacist's "tincture of iodine" is usually a 5% solution in alcohol. 

75. Temperature and solubility.-The

"Curves" showing the solubility of several compounds at different temperatures. "solubility curves" of Fig. 58 show the effect of temperature upon solubility. Most solids are more soluble in hot, than in cold water. With common salt, however, changes of temperature make little difference; and calcium hydroxide

(slaked lime) and calcium sulphate are less soluble in hot water than in cold. 

When two liquids are not miscible in all proportions, but dissolve in each other to only a limited extent, the solubility usually increases with temperature. Of course, the limit of heating is the temperature at which one of the liquids boils off.

We can get a solid to dissolve most rapidly by making the conditions right. (1) We raise the temperature, if this favors dis- 

FIG. 59. 

solving. (2) We powder the solid finely, so that it has a large surface to come in contact with the solvent. (3) We bring fresh portions of solvent to it, by stirring or by producing currents. To the manufacturer stirring is an expensive process, hence he makes a solution produce its own currents, if possible. Solutions of solids have a greater density than water itself. Thus a saturated salt brine is nearly 1.2 times as heavy as water. Hence, if we put the solid on a tray or shelf at the top of the solvent (Fig. 59), it will dissolve rapidly without to be stirring, owing to the "convection currents" set up in the solution. 

A tank with a shelf for solids that are dissolved in water. 

76. Solubility of gases. In considering the solubility of gases in liquids, we do best to divide gases into three groups: (1) Very soluble gases, such as ammonia and hydrogen chloride (§§ 221 and 138). 

(2) Moderately soluble gases, such as carbon dioxide 

(§ 281) and chlorine (§ 129). 

(3) Slightly soluble gases, such as oxygen, hydrogen, and nitrogen (§ 90).

SOLUTION 

101 The solubility of a gas becomes less and less as the temperature rises. Thus, 100 cu. cm. of water dissolve up to 4.1 cu. cm. of oxygen at 0° C., 2.9 at 15° C., 1.84 at 50° C., and none at 100° C. The bubbles which arise from cold water brought into a warm room consist of escaping air. They arise still more rapidly when water is heated to boiling. 

If the temperature remains unchanged, the solubility of a gas is proportional to the pressure at which it is brought in contact with the solvent. This is Henry's law. It applies only to moderately soluble gases like oxygen, hydrogen, nitrogen, carbon dioxide, and carbon monoxide, and not to very soluble ones like ammonia and sulphur dioxide.

Carbonated water is water charged, under pressure, with carbon dioxide. Certain springs contain such water charged at the higher pressure that exists underground. Soda water is an artificial carbonated water, made when water and carbon dioxide are brought together under a pressure five or more times as great as atmospheric pressure. When the pressure is removed, the excess of carbon dioxide escapes with foaming, or effervescence. We also see effervescence when a gas, such as hydrogen, is produced in a reaction and escapes through a liquid. 

77. Supersaturated solutions.-One way of preparing a saturated solution of a solid, such as salt, is to shake or stir the solution thoroughly with an excess of the solid. If some is left undissolved, we know the solution is saturated. Another method is to dissolve as much solid as possible by heating the solution, and then to let the solution cool. The excess of solute usually crystallizes out, leaving the solution saturated. However, in the case of some solids, such as photographers' "hypo"

(sodium thiosulphate), the excess of solute does not crystallize out on cooling, if none of the undissolved solid is present. The same is true of sodium chlorate, sodium acetate, Glauber's salt, and other substances. Such solutions are supersaturated. 

If a crystal of the solute is added to a supersaturated solution (Fig. 60), the excess of solute crystallizes out, and the solution becomes saturated. At the same time heat is given off. If the supersaturated solution is jarred, FIG. 60. 

A supersaturated solution. The bottle at the left has a plug of cotton to prevent crystals from getting at the supersaturated solution. 

When a crystal of the solid is added, it grows rapidly by taking material from the solution. or if dust particles get into it, crystallization may be started. In preparing such a solution it is best to stopper the vessel containing the hot solution with a plug of absorbent cotton; this filters the air that enters during the cooling process. 

How can we distinguish between a saturated, unsaturated, and supersaturated solution? Evidently by adding a crystal of the solute. 

In the saturated solution the crystal and solution remain unchanged; in the unsaturated one the crystal dissolves; in the supersaturated one the crystal growsthe solution crystallizes. 

78. Boiling point and freezing point of solutions.—A pure liquid has a definite boiling point. For water it is 

100° C. under 760 mm. pressure. A dissolved substance, such as salt, holds back the change of water into steam. 

It does so by lowering the pressure of the water vapor

SOLUTION 

103 (aqueous tension), so that it does not reach 760 mm. at 

100° C. The temperature of the solution must be raised, before the escaping vapor has the pressure of the atmosphere. A saturated salt solution boils at about 109° C. under 760 mm. The vapor that escapes from boiling salt water gives pure water when condensed, not salt water. 

Hence drinking water can be prepared on ships at sea, by the distillation of sea water. 

A pure liquid has also a definite freezing temperature. For water it is 0° C. under 760 mm. If there is salt in the water, the salt holds back the change from liquid water to ice. The salt water must therefore be cooled below 0° C. before ice separates. 

If the salt solution is a dilute one, the ice is practically free from salt. It is pure water, not salt water, that is freezing. Hence we can make a dilute solution more concentrated, that is, we can remove some of its water, by freezing out the water as ice. This method is used, in cold countries, in order to save fuel in the preparation of solid salt from salt water. Much of the water can be frozen out instead of being boiled off. The last of the water can be removed either by slow evaporation or by heating. 

A saturated solution of salt freezes at -21° C. under 760 mm. 

When it freezes, ice and salt separate out together. "Anti-freeze" solutions are used in automobile radiators in cold weather; they are usually mixtures of water with alcohol, glycerine, or glycol. 

79. Freezing mixtures.-When a substance dissolves, an energy change usually takes place. In the dissolving of a gas, heat is commonly set free, and the temperature rises. When a solid dissolves, heat is ordinarily absorbed, and the temperature falls. When equal weights of water and ammonium nitrate are mixed at 0° C., the temperature falls to -15° C. 

Salt and ice form our common freezing mixture. 

When the mixture is stirred and protected from outside

heat, its temperature becomes -21° C. This is the freezing point of saturated salt brine. Both the dissolving of the salt and the melting of the ice require heat. 

When we put salt upon ice, the ice begins to melt, and some salt dissolves in the water. The ice is really melting in salt brine, and the heat used to melt the ice (79 calories for each gram; § 61) is taken from the brine. 

When we freeze ice cream and ices, this heat comes finally from the material being frozen. A hydrate of calcium chloride (§ 82) mixed with ice gives a mixture freezing at about -50° C. ALUM

80. Crystals. The regular forms which solids take when they separate from solution or from the liquid state are called crystals (Fig. 61). To crystallize a solid we usually prepare a hot, saturated solution of it, and let the solution cool. If we cool the solution suddenly, the crystals will be small ("crystal meal"); the substance may even be amorphous, or non-crystalline, because it has not time to crystallize. To form large crystals a solution must cool slowly. On this account crystals are

SOLUTION 

105 often formed by the slow evaporation of the solvent. 

Sometimes a string or stick is suspended in the solution, and the crystals form upon it. When this is done with a sugar solution, the sugar is deposited as large, transparent crystals of rock candy.

Crystals are also formed when liquids freeze. Thus water, melted sulphur, and melted lead crystallize when they become solid. In ice the crystals fit closely together, but in snow and frost the crystal masses remain separate (Fig. 62). 

FIG. 62. 

Crystals of snow. 

OW.A. Bentley

Some liquids do not crystallize at their freezing temperatures, especially if they are not jarred, and if no crystal of the solid is present. They are supercooled, just as some solutions are supersaturated. Water in a dish, or in a pond, on a very still, cold night, may not freeze, even though its temperature is below 0° C. 

But if it is disturbed, so that it comes in contact with an ice crystal, it at once begins to crystallize. When a supercooled liquid freezes, its temperature rises to its true melting point. 

A solid which is formed suddenly in a solution is called a precipitate. It may be produced: (1) by the sudden cooling of a hot, saturated solution; (2) by the addition of a new solvent in which the solute is insoluble; or (3) by a chemical change in which a new, insoluble solid is produced. We use method (2) when we add alcohol to a saturated solution of salt in water.

If, when crystals form, some of the solution is caught between them, the crystal masses will "snap," or decrepitate, when they are heated. Thus, when large salt crystals are heated in an open dish, the imprisoned water boils, and escapes so explosively that it scatters the crystals out of the dish. 

81. Water of crystallization.-When a substance dissolves in water, we think of the change as a physical one; and when the dissolved substance crystallizes from the solution, we expect to get back the substance which we dissolved. This is true for salt, sugar, potassium chlorate, and many other substances. But cupric sulphate and many other substances behave differently (§ 67, 6). 

Cupric sulphate is usually a grayish-white powder, which can be crystallized from its solution in hot, concentrated sulphuric acid in the form of white needles. When we add a little water to some of the powder, it becomes blue and very hot. When we dissolve it in water, it forms a blue solution. If we prepare a hot, concentrated, water solution of it, and allow it to cool slowly, we obtain large, blue crystals, called blue vitriol, or bluestone. If we heat some of the crystals of blue vitriol, they break up into water, which escapes as steam, and the grayishwhite powder: cupric sulphate, with which we started.

The water with which a substance combines when it crystallizes from water we call water of crystallization, or water of hydration. The union of cupric sulphate and water is a chemical change, because: (1) a new substance is formed; (2) heat is evolved; and (3) the cupric sulphate and water combine in definite proportions by weight. 

A substance like blue vitriol, which contains water of crystallization, is called a hydrate; cupric sulphate itself is an anhydrous ("without water") substance.

SOLUTION 

107 82. Efflorescent substances.-Blue vitriol does not lose its water of hydration in ordinary air, but washing soda, the hydrate of sodium carbonate, does. It crumbles to a fine, white powder. Substances which lose their water of hydration when left in the air are said to effloresce (ĕf'lō-res'). The phenomenon is called efflorescence. Glauber's salt, the hydrate of sodium sulphate, is another efflorescent substance. Water of hydration escapes from a crystal if the vapor pressure of the water in the crystal exceeds that of the water vapor ordinarily in the air. In very dry air blue vitriol itself effloresces. 

83. Deliquescent substances. Some substances, when exposed to the air, absorb water vapor. We call them hygroscopic (hi'grō-skŏp'îk) substances. Cupric sulphate is hygroscopic because it takes up water of crystallization; quicklime, because it combines with water to form slaked lime. Candies made for dry weather change their qualities in very damp weather. Dry silk may absorb 

30% of its weight of water vapor. Crude wool is so hygroscopic that its surface sometimes becomes distinctly wet. Table salt "cakes" in damp weather because it contains hygroscopic impurities which cement its particles together. 

If you leave a lump of potassium hydroxide in an open dish, you will soon find a little pool of potassium hydroxide solution in its place. The hydrate of calcium chloride behaves in the same way. A substance which takes up so much atmospheric moisture that it dissolves is said to be deliquescent (děl'ĭ-kwěs'ent). The phenomenon is deliquescence.

Deliquescence takes place only with very soluble substances. 

The pressure of water vapor (aqueous tension) has a definite value

for a given temperature (see Appendix). Moreover, a solute lowers the vapor pressure of the water in which it is dissolved (§ 78). 

The more calcium chloride, for example, there is in 100 g. of water, the more the vapor pressure of the water is lowered. So it happens that when there is a great deal of solute in solution, as in the case of a very soluble substance, the pressure of the water vapor of the solution is reduced to a point at which it is less than that of the water vapor of the air. As a result, water vapor from the air is added to the solution. Such a solution does not give up water to the air (evaporate), but gains water from the air, and becomes more and more dilute. 

When we expose a lump of anhydrous calcium chloride to the air, it first absorbs water to serve as water of hydration; then the hydrate dissolves in the water which it continues to take up from the air. Calcium chloride is often used to "lay the dust" of a road. It does so by absorbing water from the air, and thus dampens the road.

Anhydrous substances like cupric sulphate and calcium chloride, which unite greedily with water, are much used as drying agents, to remove water vapor from gases and dissolved water from liquids. Thus, we dry hydrogen by passing it through a tube of anhydrous calcium chloride. We also "dry" ether, containing dissolved water, by letting it stand for a time with calcium chloride. 

Anhydrous cupric sulphate removes the water mixed with ordinary alcohol. Quicklime, or calcium oxide, is our cheapest drying agent. It combines with water to produce the base: slaked lime, or calcium hydroxide. 

When the chemist wishes to keep a substance in dry air, either to remove water which the substance contains, or to prevent water vapor from getting at the substance, he uses a desiccator (des'i-kat'ôr; Fig. 63) containing a drying agent. 

84. The act of dissolving.-What does the chemist

SOLUTION 

109 think of the act of dissolving, as of sugar in water? To him it is a movement of the sugar particles from the crystals into the water, and from one part of the solution to another: 

Sugar (solid)-sugar (dissolved).

Even if we do not stir the solution, the energy of the sugar particles will finally cause them to be distributed uniformly throughout the solution. Stirring hastens the process, but is not necessary. 

If crystals of sugar remain undissolved, some of the particles may leave the solution and attach themselves to the crystals. This movement will be the reverse of the other. It will become more and more frequent as the solution becomes more and more concentrated. 

Sugar (dissolved) sugar (solid).

Finally there will be as many par- C.S.CO 

Central Scientific Co. 

FIG. 63. 

A desiccator in which substances may be kept in an atmosphere practically free from water vapor. ticles adding themselves to the crystals in a given time as leave them in the same time. We can express this fact by the equilibrium equation: 

Sugar (solid) sugar (dissolved).

The double arrow shows that the changes in the opposite directions balance each other. When this is true, the solution is saturated. Sugar continues to dissolve, but it crystallizes at the same rate, and the concentration remains unchanged. 

85. Colloids. In § 73 we learned some differences between suspended and dissolved substances. Suspended

substances consist of particles which settle out after a time and are large enough to be caught and held by a filter. A dissolved substance, on the other hand, is broken up into molecules, which do not settle out, and are so small that they readily pass through a filter. 

Suspended particles are so large that they reflect light 

FIG. 64. 

The Tyndall effect. Colloidal particles of arsenic sulphide in the water show the path of the beam of light. does. rays just as dust particles in a dark room reflect the light of an entering sunbeam. A suspension is therefore turbid, or roily. The molecules of a dissolved substance are too small to reflect a beam of light; hence a solution is clear. 

Colloids (the word means "gluelike") form a class of bodies which, when mixed with water, have some of the characteristics of suspended particles and at the same time some of the qualities of dissolved molecules. Gelatine, for example, seems to dissolve in hot water to form a uniform mixture, just as salt The gelatine particles in the "solution" pass through a filter, as salt particles do. On the other hand, a beam of light is visible when passed through the gelatine "solution." We conclude that the particles of colloids like gelatine are larger than molecules, but not so large as ordinary suspended particles. Starch, gums, resins, and glue resemble gelatine in these respects. 

While we cannot separate the "dissolved" particles of two such substances as starch and salt by means of an ordinary filter, since both pass through its pores, we can

SOLUTION 

111 succeed if we use a much finer filter, such as one made from egg skin, or parchment paper, or cellophane (Fig. 

65). If a cellophane or parchment bag containing starch and salt solutions is set in distilled water which is changed frequently, the salt passes out through the walls, while the starch remains inside. Separation of two substances in this way is called dialysis (di-ăl 'I-sis), and the apparatus is called a dialyzer (dî'ăl-i'zěr). The reason why the starch does not pass through the pores of the bag is that the starch particles are too large. The particles of dissolved salt are of the size of molecules; those of the starch must be much larger. 

FIG. 65. 

A dialyzer. A dissolved through the walls of the cellophane bag into the substance can pass water of the beaker, while a colloid cannot, and remains in the bag. 

Substances like salt and sugar, which pass through the wall of a dialyzer, are called non-colloids, or crystalloids. 

Colloidal particles are in suspension, not in real solution. The difference in behavior of suspended particles, colloidal particles, and dissolved particles is due essentially to a difference in size. As a matter of fact, many noncolloids can be prepared in colloidal form. Thus gold and silver may be suspended in water for a long time, if their particles are kept sufficiently small. 

A celebrated chemist has said that the study of colloids is a study of grains, drops, bubbles, filaments, and films. 

Grains are tiny solid particles, drops are liquid, and bubbles are gaseous. Filaments are small threads, and films are thin sheets. When particles in these fine forms of division are dispersed, or scattered, through another material, they have properties differing from those of

molecules, on the one hand, and from those of larger, suspended particles, on the other. The behavior of the fine particles of the soil, of coloring materials in glass, of the silver salts in a kodak film, of paint, varnish, rubber, paper, the proteins of our food, of emulsions such as milk, are all colloidal in character. The clay particles used in the making of bricks and pottery, and the colored particles used in dyeing fabrics, are of colloidal size. Even the formation of a delta from the particles carried by rivers to the sea is a phenomenon of Colloid Chemistry. 

86. The ultra-microscope.-Colloidal suspensions can be distinguished from true solutions not only by dialysis, but by the ultra-microscope (Fig. 66). The ultra-microscope is a microscope for examining a solution by means of a strong beam of light 

LIQUID BEING EXAMINED FIG. 66. 

An ultra-microscope. If colloid particles are illuminated by a horizontal beam of light they can be seen by an observer looking through the vertical microscope. passed through the solution in a horizontal direction, while the observer looks through the microscope in a vertical direction. The room must be dark. If there are particles of sufficient size in the liquid examined, the observer sees tiny points of light, which are reflected from the suspended particles. A suspension of a colloid, such as starch, glue, soap, or mucilage, even though clear to the unaided eye, shows these flashes of light in the ultra-microscope; but a solution of salt or sugar does not. 

Colloidal particles have another interesting property when viewed through the ultra-microscope: they not only reflect light, but they show a zigzag movement (Fig. 67). The smaller the particles are, the more rapidly they move. This movement is

SOLUTION 

113 called the Brownian movement. It is caused by the blows which the invisible, but swiftly moving molecules of the solvent give to the larger colloid particles with which they collide. The Brownian movement thus gives us an idea of the restless energy of the particles which we assume are present in gases and liquids. 

87. Summary.-A solution is a uniform mixture of solvent and solute. Solutions are clear.

Suspensions are temporary mixtures. The suspended particles either settle out or can be filtered out. Suspensions are roily. 

To concentrate a solution is to evaporate some of the solvent.

A concentrated 

FIG. 67. 

which has the Brownian movement.

A dilute solution is one having a small The path of a particle concentration of the solute. solution has a large concentration of solute. 

By the solubility of a solid we mean the largest amount of it that will dissolve in 100 cu. cm. of water at a given temperature and pressure, if an excess of the solid is present. 

The solubility of a moderately or slightly soluble gas is proportional to the pressure at which it is brought to the solvent. 

Carbonated water is charged, under pressure, with carbon dioxide.

Effervescence is due to the escape of a gas from a liquid. 

Some solutions may be supersaturated if none of the undissolved solute is present. A supersaturated solution becomes saturated if a particle of the solute is added. 

A pure liquid has a definite boiling point. A solution of a solid has a higher boiling point than the pure solvent. 

A pure liquid has a definite freezing point, if some of its solid form is present. A dissolved solid lowers the freezing point of the solvent.

In our common freezing mixture salt is dissolving in water from the melting ice, and ice is melting in salt brine. 

Crystals are the regular solids formed when solids separate from solution or from the melted state.

A solid formed suddenly in a solution is called a precipitate. It may be crystalline or amorphous.

Water of crystallization, or water of hydration, is the water which with some solids combine when they crystallize from water. 

The resulting hydrate is a compound of the anhydrous substance with water, in definite proportions by weight. 

Efflorescence is caused by the spontaneous escape of water of crystallization from a hydrate. 

A deliquescent substance is one that takes up so much atmospheric moisture that it dissolves. Deliquescence takes place with very soluble substances. 

A drying agent is used to remove water vapor or dissolved water from gases and liquids.

In a saturated solution the dissolving of the solute does not cease, but the solute crystallizes out as rapidly as it dissolves. 

In the colloidal state, particles are larger than dissolved molecules and smaller than suspended particles. They do not settle out, and cannot be filtered in ordinary filters, but they reflect light as suspended particles do. Colloids can be filtered out in a dialyzer. 

Colloidal particles may be grains, drops, bubbles, filaments, or films; they are dispersed through solids, liquids, or gases. Usually we study them as scattered through a liquid. 

The ultra-microscope enables us to see the light reflected from suspended colloidal particles, and the Brownian movement of the particles.

CHAPTER VII  

89. The nitrogen of the air.—We have already learned that air is not a single substance, but a mixture. By heating metals in an enclosed portion of air Lavoisier and others showed that there is a limit to the rusting, or oxidation, of the metals, and that this limit is reached when a little more than (20.9%) of the air has been used up. This represents the proportion of oxygen present in air. We can remove the oxygen much more quickly by means of phosphorus (Fig. 68). On a larger scale we can pass the air through a tube of heated copper (Fig. 69). In all these cases the gas that remains is a colorless, inactive substance which for more than a century (1774-1894) was supposed to be only nitrogen. We now know that "atmospheric nitrogen" is itself a mixture containing about 99% nitrogen and about 1% of the "rare gases"-chiefly argon and helium.

FIG. 68. 

Phosphorus burning in air over water removes the oxygen.

If we wish to prepare pure nitrogen, we usually heat a mixture of solutions of sodium nitrite and ammonium chloride. When these two substances react in solution, a double decomposition (§ 68) occurs, and the new products in the solution are sodium chloride and ammonium nitrite. But the ammonium nitrite is 116

decomposed, when the solution is heated, into nitrogen and water, and the nitrogen, being insoluble, passes off as a gas.

AIR COPPER GAUZE ATMOSPHERIC NITROGEN FIG. 69. 

Atmospheric nitrogen is formed when air is passed over heated copper.
