90. Properties of nitrogen.-Pure nitrogen, like oxygen and hydrogen, has no odor, taste, or color. One liter of nitrogen weighs 1.25 g. under standard conditions, while one liter of air weighs 1.293 g., consequently air is slightly heavier, volume for volume, than nitrogen. The solubility of air in water is really the solubility of a mixture of nitrogen and oxygen, in which each gas dissolves as if it were by itself; 100 cu. cm. of water dissolves up to about 1 cu. cm. of nitrogen under ordinary conditions.

NITROGEN 

As compared with oxygen and hydrogen, nitrogen is an inactive element. It does not burn as hydrogen does, nor support combustion like oxygen. It can, however, be made to combine with heated metals such as magnesium and aluminum. The nitrides of the metals are produced. 

Nitrogen can also be made to combine with hydrogen to give ammonia (§ 220) by the use of the proper temperature, pressure, and catalyst. When electric sparks are passed through a mixture of nitrogen and oxygen, such as the air, a small quantity of nitrogen oxides is formed. This capture of the uncombined nitrogen of the air, and its conversion into nitrogen compounds, is called the fixation of nitrogen. The fixation of nitrogen is of great importance to our modern life. It makes possible the artificial preparation of the nitrogen compounds used for dyes and explosives, and for fertilizers of soils on which crops are grown (§ 238). 

FIG. 70. 

Preparing nitrogen from sodium nitrite and ammonium chloride. 

91. Importance of nitrogen.-While nitrogen does not support respiration, it forms a part of the complex nitrogen compounds, called proteins, which are needed by the cells of all plants and animals. Such foods as meat, cheese, eggs, and milk form our chief sources of proteins. 

Animals get their nitrogen compounds from the plants upon which they feed. Even plants cannot ordinarily take up nitrogen directly from the air, but must get it from the nitrogen compounds in the soil. Plants which produce their seeds in pods, or legumes, have special im-

portance for the nitrogen supply of a soil, for they usually have upon their roots small lumps ("tubercles") which are colonies of certain bacteria (Fig. 71). These bacteria are nitrogen-fixers: they take up nitrogen from the air and build it into nitrogen compounds for their own use. These compounds are also used by the leguminous plants (beans, peas, alfalfa, clover, and others) to which the bacteria colonies are attached. In this way crops of clover and other leguminous plants, with their nitrogen-fixing bacteria, prepare the soil for the use of grain crops, such as wheat. In fact, farmers have for a long time grown clover crops, and "plowed them under," in order to add nitrogen compounds to soil. 

Courtesy U. S. Department of Agriculture. 

FIG. 71. 

Roots of vetch plants showing nodules produced by nitrogen-fixing bacteria. 

Nitrogen is inactive not only when it comes to entering into combination, but it is also hard to keep in combination. In the decay of vegetation much of the nitrogen returns to the air from which it came. This is especially true when leaves, stubble, and grass are burned. Most of our explosives are compounds or mixtures containing nitrogen in a loose form of combination. 

When the explosive is set on fire or given a shock, the nitrogen is set free with great violence. The pressure developed is used to

hurl a projectile, dig holes for posts or trees, break up coal or rock, or do other work. 

92. The rare gases.-The gases helium, neon, argon, krypton, and xenon are found in the part of air that remains after its nitrogen and oxygen have been removed. How are these two gases removed?

The rare gases (also called "noble" gases) are too inactive to combine with any other material, and are left by themselves. They may all be obtained by the distillation of liquid air. Helium was discovered, by means of the spectroscope, in the atmosphere of the sun (§ 347). At that time it was not known to exist upon the earth. It is found, not only in the atmosphere, but also in certain minerals, and in some springs and natural gases. 

Its chief use is to fill large airships, owing to its lightness and non-inflammability (see § 40). 

FIG. 72. 

Sir William Ramsay (1852-1916), a skillful experimenter, and discoverer of argon and other rare gases of the atmosphere.

Helium has a smaller solubility in water than any other gas. Liquid helium boils at -267.9° C. and freezes at about -272° C.

Neon Argon can be used to fill electric light bulbs, because it will not react with the filament, even when the bulb is in use. is used in the neon lamp, in electric signs, in fog beacon lights, and for television apparatus. The neon lamp transforms the delicate changes of an electric current into light waves, and so

reproduces a face or a scene sent by telephone or radio. Neon glows with a bright, red light when a current passes through it. 

Courtesy U. S. Navy Department. 

FIG. 73. 

U.S. S. Los Angeles. 

93. Carbon dioxide in the air. We have already learned that green plants, using the energy of sunlight, change some of the carbon dioxide and water of the air into carbohydrates, such as starch and sugar (§ 29). 

By this action, and by the fixation of the gas in certain rocks, the proportion of carbon dioxide is kept constant. 

Cities and factories using coal increase the amount of carbon dioxide in their neighborhood, but the winds stir up the air so thoroughly that the effect is noticeable for only a short distance. Besides, the proportion of air over the oceans, the polar areas, and the waste places of the earth is so great that the carbon dioxide produced by man is soon diluted.

Scientists estimate that if the carbon dioxide were spread evenly over the earth's surface, under 760 mm. pressure, it would make a layer about 13 feet deep. The 

FIG. 74. 

Cylinders of helium for the dirigible Graf Zeppelin at Lakehurst, New Jersey. 

argon, not quite so dense, would form a layer, on top of it, 270 feet deep; the oxygen, 1 mile deep; and the nitrogen, 4 miles deep.

Usually there are only about 3 or 4 parts by volume of carbon dioxide in 10,000 parts of air. In closed rooms, where several persons are gathered, the amount may rise to 7 or 8 parts in 10,000. Air which we expire contains about 4% carbon dioxide, or 400 parts in 10,000. We can test for the carbon dioxide in the air by exposing a dish of limewater or of barium hydroxide solution, or by drawing a stream of air through either of these solu-

tions. What product is formed in each case? See § 27. 

94. Water vapor and humidity. The air we exhale is "saturated" with water vapor. We see the deposit of this water as fog or droplets when we "breathe" upon a cold object or go out of doors on a cold day. Water is constantly evaporating from the surface of our bodies even when we do not know we are prespiring (insensible perspiration). Water also gets into the air by exhalation from the leaves of plants and by evaporation from wet ground, streams, lakes, and the ocean. 

Water evaporates into a given space until there is a definite amount of it in that space. This amount has nothing to do with the presence, or absence, of air in that space. As much water vapor will enter the space when there is air present as if the space is a vacuum. 

The water vapor and the air simply form a mixture. We say that the "air has a capacity for holding" a definite amount of water vapor. The higher the temperature, the more water vapor there can be in a given space; the greater the capacity. The amount of water vapor which the air contains at any given time is called its absolute humidity at that time. The absolute humidity divided by the capacity is the relative humidity. 

Absolute humidity capacity =relative humidity.

When the relative humidity is 100%, air is at the dew point, and the slightest lowering of temperature produces dew or fog.

We can calculate the relative humidity of the air by determining the air's temperature and dew point (Fig. 75). Suppose that the temperature of a room is 26° C., and the dew point is found

to be 10° C. The Table of Vapor Pressure in the Appendix shows that at 26° C. the pressure of water vapor is equal to 25 mm. of mercury, while at the dew point (10° C.) the pressure is 9.2 mm. 

The relative humidity must be 9.2÷25, or 36.8%.

Our comfort or discomfort depends not so much upon the absolute humidity as upon the relative humidity; that is, not upon the amount of water actually in the air, but upon the amount present as compared with what the air can hold at that temperature. If the relative humidity is very large, say, 60 to 80 per cent, the perspiration does not evaporate properly, and we feel "sticky." If, on the other hand, there is too little moisture in the air, the perspiration evaporates too rapidly, and the moisture of the nose and throat also evaporates too much, leaving us parched and dry. 

We are most comfortable when the relative humidity is 45-50%. We sometimes say that air is "heavy" when it is near its dew point, or nearly saturated with water vapor. This is a mistake. Water vapor is really lighter than air, and moist air is lighter, volume for volume, than dry air. The oppressiveness which we feel is due not to the density of the air, but to the unfavorable humidity conditions.

DEW 

DEW

FIG. 75. 

Determining the dew point by means of a metal beaker cooled by iced water.

The dust of the atmosphere is important, for without some of it raindrops probably could not form. It also aids in the formation of sunrise and sunset colors. 

95. Need of ventilation.-Ventilation means the bringing of fresh air into our rooms and the removal of used air. Natural ventilation depends upon the entrance of cold, heavier air at the bottom of a room opening, and the leaving of warm, lighter air at the top of the opening. 

We can easily prove that there are strong currents at such openings by holding a lighted candle at the top

and bottom of an open doorway between a warm room and a cold one. A similar exchange takes place between the rooms of a house and the outdoor air, especially in cold weather.

In houses sealed tightly to prevent the entrance of outdoor air ventilation must be attended to very carefully, or the health of the people in the house will suffer. In large buildings forced ventilation is generally used. 

Heating and ventilating are thus done together. One method is to force fresh air over hot steam coils in the basement. Fans driven by motors are used for the purpose. The warmed, fresh air is then forced into the rooms above, while the used air escapes through openings in walls or ceiling. 

In a large auditorium all the air may be changed in 10 or 15 minutes. The air may be blown through a spray of water to give it the proper humidity and to wash out dust and germs. The ventilation system may also be used to cool a building in summer, if the warm, outside air is first passed over ice or pipes of cold brine (§ 224). 

The air of a bedroom needs special attention. The room should have at least one window open every night and should be aired thoroughly during the day. The fact that a bedroom is cold is no sign that its air is pure. 

Gas stoves and kerosene stoves not connected with a chimney also require careful attention, or they may injure health. A person in a garage when an automobile is running should be sure to provide for the removal of the products of combustion, for carbon monoxide, a poisonous gas, is formed by incomplete oxidation of the carbon of the gasoline. Tunnels through which many automobiles pass need to be ventilated for the same reason. 

Stoves and hot-air furnaces provide some ventilation, since outdoor air must come in to replace that which goes out through

the chimney, but hot-water and steam heating systems of themselves give no ventilation whatever.

The air of a room becomes unfit to breathe not so much because it contains more carbon dioxide than normal, as because it is stagnant. Somewhat impure air, if in motion, is better for us than very pure air that is motionless. The great need is a free circulation of air. 

96. How air is liquefied.-Gases like hydrogen, oxygen, and nitrogen cannot be liquefied by any pressure, how- 

AIR. AT LOW PRESSURE 

WATER

AIR AT 200 ATMOSPHERES TO BE LIQUEFIED LEAVES

HIGH PRESSURE COIL. 

COOLED BY H2O. 

COMPRESSOR FIG. 76. 

EXPANDING AIR ENTERS 

JACKET

LIQUID AIR LIQUEFYING COIL AND JACKET 

Diagram of apparatus for liquefying air. ever great, unless the temperature is made very low. 

The only practical way to get a low enough temperature is by the expansion of the compressed gases themselves. 

When a gas is compressed, heat is given off. We note this in forcing air into a bicycle or automobile tire; the air becomes warmer. When a gas under pressure is released, it becomes colder. We take advantage of this fact in liquefying air. The temperature to which a gas

must be cooled before it can be liquefied by pressure is called the critical temperature of the gas. For hydrogen this is -234° C., for nitrogen, -146° C., for oxygen, -118° C.

The apparatus for preparing liquid air (Fig. 76) consists essentially of an air compressor and two long pipes. The outer pipe forms a jacket surrounding the inner one. Air is forced through the inner pipe at a pressure of about 200 "atmospheres," or 3,000 -4,000 pounds per square inch. Heat is given off in the compression, but the compressed air is at once cooled by running water or ice.

In the liquefier the compressed air is forced out, through a needle valve, into the larger pipe which forms its jacket. In this outer pipe the pressure is low, perhaps only one atmosphere. The compressed air expands, and becomes somewhat colder. The expanding air in the outer pipe thus cools the highly compressed air of the inner tube. At first the cooling effect of each expansion is small, but it becomes greater as the temperature falls. Finally the air in the inner tube reaches the critical temperatures of its gases, and drops of liquid air escape through the needle valve, along with air which is still in the gaseous state. The liquid air is caught in a reservoir and drawn out. 

97. Storage and properties of liquid air. When fresh, liquid air is almost colorless, but as liquid nitrogen boils off first (at -196° C.) the color gradually becomes blue, the color of liquid oxygen. 

Its density is about that of water. 

Liquid air cannot be stored in closed vessels; they must be left unstoppered.

The flasks used for storing and ship- 

FIG. 77. 

Dewar flask for holding liquid air. ping it are built after the pattern of Dewar flasks (Fig. 77) packed in a box or pail of insulating material,

such as wool felt. The Dewar flask has double walls, and the space between them is a vacuum. The outer wall is usually silvered as well, so as to reflect away heat from outside sources. Thermos bottles for keeping hot liquids hot and cold liquids cold, are built on the plan of Dewar flasks. 

Liquid air has other very interesting properties. Alcohol, liquid carbon dioxide, and mercury become solid when placed in it. A hammer head may be made out of the solid mercury. A rubber ball, an orange, or a piece of meat becomes so brittle in liquid air that it can be broken into fragments, like glass. A burning piece of wood continues to burn in the liquid. A steel wire will also burn in it. However, you can put your finger into liquid air for a short time without injury, because a non-conducting film of gaseous air is formed between the skin and the liquid. 

98. Air is a mixture.-There are many proofs that air is a mixture, and not a compound of nitrogen and oxygen. (1) Each gas retains its own properties. The nitrogen and oxygen of air each dissolve as though the other were not present. Air that has been dissolved in water and then expelled from it by heat contains about 35% of oxygen by weight instead of 23%. The density of air depends upon the densities of its components and their amounts. (2) If air were a compound, it should have the same constant composition in the liquid state as in the gaseous. 

This is not true. (3) When nitrogen and oxygen are mixed in the same proportion as found in air, they produce air, without any sign of chemical change.

The differences between mixtures and compounds are clear, if we compare the properties of oxygen in water

with those of the oxygen in air. Air acts like diluted oxygen. It supports combustion as oxygen does, but not so vigorously. It supports respiration. It is used by bacteria in bringing about the oxidation of decaying material. The oxygen of water, on the other hand, does not act like oxygen at all. It cannot support combustion. 

It cannot be used by air-breathing creatures. It cannot oxidize decaying material. In the compound, water, each element has lost the properties it had while free. In air the mixed elements are still free. 

99. Summary.-We obtain nitrogen from the air by removing the oxygen.

Atmospheric nitrogen contains also the rare gases of the air. 

Pure nitrogen is produced from a mixture of sodium nitrite and ammonium chloride, or by heating ammonium nitrite. 

Nitrogen is colorless, tasteless, and odorless, slightly soluble in water, and slightly less dense than air. 

Nitrogen is an inactive substance, but combines with active metals to form nitrides. With hydrogen it forms ammonia, and with oxygen it forms nitrogen oxides. 

Fixation of nitrogen means the conversion of the uncombined nitrogen of the air into nitrogen compounds. 

Nitrogen forms a part of all proteins, and is therefore necessary for the cells of all living things. All fertile soils contain it. 

Colonies of bacteria growing upon the roots of leguminous plants are natural fixers of nitrogen. 

Nitrogen, because of its inactivity, easily escapes from combination. 

The rare gases, or noble gases, are helium, neon, argon, krypton, and xenon. They are not able to form compounds. 

Carbon dioxide makes up about 3 or 4 parts in 10,000 parts of "pure" air. The air which we exhale contains about 400 parts of carbon dioxide in 10,000.

Carbon dioxide is taken from air by green plants in sunlight, and the balance between carbon dioxide and oxygen is maintained.

The humidity of air is determined by the amount of water vapor mixed with it. It may be absolute or relative. 

Relative humidity equals absolute humidity divided by capacity. 

At the dew point relative humidity is 100%. 

Air containing water vapor has a lower density than dry air. 

Ventilation is necessary for health and comfort. Air becomes unfit to breathe chiefly because it becomes stagnant. 

Air is cooled to the critical temperature of its gases by successive expansions from a state of very high to one of low pressure. 

Liquid air is bluish from the presence of liquid oxygen. Its nitrogen boils off at a lower temperature than its oxygen. The changes it produces in other substances are due: (1) to its low temperature, and (2) to oxygen in very concentrated form. 

Air is a mixture, not a compound. Each gas in it retains its own properties, but is diluted by the presence of the others.

CHAPTER VIII  

101. Fundamental laws of chemistry.-A law of chemistry is a general rule of behavior observed when substances combine, or are decomposed, or undergo some other chemical change. In this chapter we shall review some of the laws we have learned, and then try to picture to ourselves how elements and compounds must be constructed, or made up, so that these rules, or laws, hold true. (1) The first rule of all (§ 1) is that every material must be either a single substance, with its own, peculiar set of properties, or it must be a mixture of two or more such substances. By the use of this idea, and the methods built upon it, chemists have obtained a multitude of substances in a pure state. (2) The second rule (§§ 2 and 15) is that, in a chemical change, even though "new" substances are formed, no gain or loss of material takes place, but only some new arrangement of elements already present before the change. We call this rule the law of the conservation of matter. (3) The third rule is the law of definite proportions. 

It states: When two or more elements unite to form a compound, they do not combine in haphazard proportions, but always in certain definite proportions by 132

weight. Every case of true combination takes place according to this rule. The fact that in air the nitrogen and oxygen are not found in definite proportions is to the chemist the most decisive proof that air is not a compound. (4) Finally there is the law of multiple proportions (§ 70), which states: When two elements form a series of compounds, the different weights of one element which combine with a fixed weight of the other are in a simple, whole ratio to one another. We have already seen that the pair of compounds: carbon monoxide and carbon dioxide, illustrate this law; also the pair: water and hydrogen peroxide. 

102. The atomic theory. When chemists were convinced that substan- 

FIG. 78. 

especially of carbon dioxide. ces react according to Joseph Black (1728-1799), investigator of gases, these rules of behavior, they asked themselves why. They made up an explanation; that is, they suggested a hypothesis, or theory, which if true would explain the rules observed. The test of a hypothesis is its success: (1) in explaining the facts we have observed, and (2) in helping us to get more facts.

How shall we explain the laws of definite and multiple

proportions? To John Dalton, who thought long and hard over them in the years 1800-1808, they seemed to indicate several things very clearly: (1) That all bodies must be made up of small, indivisible, unit particles, called atoms. (2) That atoms of the same element are all alike, and 

From an old engraving.

FIG. 79. 

John Dalton (1766-1844). He de vised the modern theory of atoms to account for the laws of definite and multiple proportions. have the same weight, while atoms of different elements are unlike, and have different weights. (3) That chemical combination of elements to form compounds consists in the union of the atoms of those elements. 

We may define an atom as the smallest, unit part of an element that takes part in a chemical change. 

The smallest, unit particle formed by the union of atoms of elements we call a molecule. In the combination of atoms of different elements to form molecules of compounds, one atom of one element may combine with one atom of another element, or two atoms with one, or two with three, and so on; but it is always whole numbers of atoms that unite, not parts of atoms. 

103. The atomic theory and definite proportions.-The atomic theory "explains" the law of definite proportions. 

The theory states that elements consist of atoms, that all the atoms of a given element have the same weight, and that combination between two elements consists in the union of the atoms of those elements. Actual combination takes place as though the hypothesis is true. The

accompanying table gives the names of a number of compounds and the proportions, by weight, of the elements in each compound. Note that the number we select for an element in one compound also represents its proportion in other compounds. Thus, a definite weight of

Moreover, in potassium chlorate, in which chlorine is combined with both potassium and oxygen, the same number (35.46) appears for the proportion of chlorine.

It is these fundamental weights of the elements, which appear again and again in compounds, that Dalton called

atomic weights. If the atomic weight does not represent the proportion of an element in a compound, then a whole multiple of the atomic weight does. Thus, the proportion of oxygen in carbon dioxide is represented by 2×16, or 32, while its proportion in carbon monoxide is represented by 16. That is, in the molecule of carbon monoxide, there is only one atomic weight of oxygen, while in the molecule of carbon dioxide there are two. 

104. The atomic theory and multiple proportions.-The theory that every element consists of unit packages, or atoms, of matter, and that each atom has a definite weight, also explains the law of multiple proportions. Thus, in both carbon monoxide and carbon dioxide (see Table, preceding section) we express the proportion of carbon by 12, its atomic weight. The proportion of oxygen which combines with the 12 parts of carbon is 16 in carbon monoxide and 32, exactly twice as much, in carbon dioxide. The atoms of oxygen act as wholes, and so there must be 2, 3, or some other whole number of them, acting in every combination. The same explanation applies to the pair: water and hydrogen peroxide.

Let the student now show from the table that the law of multiple proportions holds true in each of the series of compounds given. Let him show also how the atomic theory explains the proportions in each case. 

The student must remember that the atomic weights are relative, or comparative, weights. To decide upon these weights chemists had to use a standard weight. The standard weight is that assigned to oxygen, which is 16. Other standards might have been used. Some early chemists wanted to use 100 as the atomic weight of oxygen. Originally it was intended that the atomic weight of hydrogen should be exactly 1. On this basis that of oxygen would be a little less than 16. However, the atomic

weight of oxygen is now taken as exactly 16. That of hydrogen is therefore about 1.008. The atomic weights of the other elements represent the relative weights of their atoms, if we consider the atomic weight of oxygen to be exactly 16.

105. Atoms and molecules.-We reach the conclusion that there are molecules from the study of the laws of gases: Boyle's law and Charles' law. molecules "explains" these laws. 

It also explains the motion of gas particles which must take place when gases diffuse, or mix, with one another. We reach the conclusion that there are atoms by studying the laws of definite and multiple proportions. By assuming that all the atoms of a given element have the same weight, and that combination between elements is really the uniting of the atoms of those elements, we explain these laws in a very satisfactory varies inversely as the pressure. way. We must now ask what 

FIG. 80. 

Robert Boyle (1626-1691). He proved that the volume of a gas is the relation between atoms and molecules. In a compound like carbon monoxide, we think of the molecule as made up of two unlike atoms: one of carbon and one of oxygen, chemically united. In the molecule of carbon dioxide, we believe that there are three atoms: one of carbon and two of oxygen. How are we to think of the particles which make up a gas that is an element, such as oxygen? Are the oxygen particles atoms, or molecules?

In the case of oxygen, nitrogen, hydrogen, and chlorine,

the four most common gases that are elements, chemists believe that the molecule consists of two like atoms chemically united. So we can have an oxygen atom united with another oxygen atom to form an oxygen molecule, just as we can have an oxygen atom united with a carbon atom to form a carbon monoxide molecule. 

We may define a molecule as the smallest particle of an element or compound that can exist by itself in the gaseous state. 

In § 3 we learned to distinguish between physical and chemical changes. We may now define a physical change as one that does not alter the relation of the atoms of the molecule to one another; 

FIG. 81. 

a physical change affects the molecule as a whole. In a chemical change the relation between the atoms in the molecule is disturbed, so that new molecules, with new sets of properties, are produced. 

106. Symbols and formulas of elements.-As we have already learned (review § 10), the chemist represents an element by one or two letters called a symbol. He uses the 

 symbol not only as an abbreviation for the name of an element, but also to represent the atom of the element. Thus 

O represents oxygen in general and also the atom of oxygen. 

Moreover, since the atom of an element has a definite weight, the symbol stands for this weight-the atomic weight of the element. Hence the symbol C stands for one atomic weight (12 parts) of carbon, while O 

John Jacob Berzelius (1799-1848). 

Invented an electro-chemical theory of compounds, determined many equivalent weights, and introduced our chemical symbols. A great teacher of chemistry.

stands for one atomic weight (16 parts) of oxygen. 

The symbol stands for one thing more: as many grams of the element as there are units in the atomic weight. 

This weight is called the gram-atomic weight. Thus C means, besides other things, one gram-atomic weight (12 grams) of carbon; and O means one gram-atomic weight (16 grams) of oxygen.

We can represent the molecule of oxygen by a formula which shows that it contains two atoms chemically united. 

We might use OO, but we prefer O2. In the same way we write H2, N2, and Cl₂ for the molecules and molecular weights of hydrogen, nitrogen, and chlorine. 

107. Formulas of compounds.—In the formula of carbon monoxide (CO) the chemist writes the symbols of carbon and oxygen side by side. CaO is the formula of calcium oxide, or quicklime, and CuO, that of cupric (copper) oxide. Similarly, NaCl is the formula of sodium chloride (common salt), and KCl of potassium chloride. 

Since a symbol represents a definite weight (atomic weight) of an element, the formula represents a definite weight of a compound. We call this weight a formula weight. Thus CaO stands for 56 parts (40+16) of calcium oxide. We get the formula weight by adding the atomic weights which the symbols represent. The formula CaO also stands for a gram-formula weight of calcium oxide (56 grams), that is, for as many grams as there are units in the formula weight. 

Calcium oxide is a solid which we cannot obtain as a gas, and for which we cannot get a real molecular weight. 

We therefore content ourselves with its formula weight. 

Carbon monoxide is a gas, hence we can speak of its molecule as consisting of two atoms, of its molecular

weight as 28, and its gram-molecular weight as 28 grams. 

By the gram-molecular weight of a substance we mean as many grams of it as there are units in its molecular weight.

Examine again the table in § 103. The smallest number in each vertical column represents the atomic weight of the element concerned. In carbon dioxide one atomic weight of carbon is combined with two atomic weights of oxygen; we therefore write its formula CO2. In a similar way we use H2O as the formula of water (steam), to show that in its molecule two atoms of hydrogen are united with one of oxygen. In representing sodium hydroxide (a solid) by NaOH we show that one formula weight of it contains one atomic weight of sodium, oxygen, and hydrogen. 

To represent more than one molecule, or molecular weight, or formula weight, of an element or compound, we put the proper numeral (2, 3, or more) as a coefficient before the formula. Thus, 2CO means two molecules, or two molecular weights, or two gram-molecular weights of carbon monoxide; 3H2O means three molecules or three gram-molecular weights of water (steam); and 

2H2 means two molecules (a total of four atoms) or two grammolecular weights of hydrogen. 

Sometimes a formula contains a group of symbols repeated two or more times. Thus, the formula of calcium hydroxide (slaked lime) is Ca(OH)2. If we write the formula without the parenthesis, it is CaO2H2. The formula of aluminum hydroxide is 

Al(OH)3; it might be written AlO3H3. The formula with the parenthesis is the one used. The small figure (called a subscript number) written after the parenthesis multiplies the group of symbols inside the parenthesis. A group of atoms, like OH, which acts much like a single atom, is called a radical. In the formula of sodium hydroxide (NaOH) the radical (OH) appears once, in the formula Ca(OH)2, twice, and in Al(OH)3, three times. When a radical appears only once in a formula, the parenthesis is not used.

A hydrate, as we have learned (§§ 67 and 81) is a compound of water with some other substance. Thus blue vitriol is the

hydrate of cupric sulphate, and washing soda is the hydrate of sodium carbonate. We represent these substances by the formulas 

CuSO4.5 H2O and Na2CO3.10 H2O, respectively. Another common hydrate is Glauber's salt, Na2SO4.10 H2O. We call this the decahydrate of sodium sulphate. Name blue vitriol and washing soda in the same way. 

108. How to calculate from a formula. From the formula of a compound and the atomic weights of its elements we can calculate the proportion of each element in a given weight of the compound. Suppose we ask: "How many grams of carbon and of oxygen are there in 100 grams of carbon monoxide?" We know the formula is CO. The atomic weight of carbon is 12, and that of oxygen is 16.

109. Naming compounds.-The chemist uses the ending -ide in naming a compound of two elements. When the compound contains a metal and a non-metal, the last syllable of the non-metal's name becomes ide. Thus we have sodium chloride (NaCl) and calcium oxide (CaO).

When oxygen is one of the two elements, the compound is always an oxide. In compounds of hydrogen with other non-metals, we name the hydrogen first, as though it were a metal. Thus the names hydrogen sulphide (H2S) and hydrogen chloride (HCl). 

An acid consisting of only two elements: hydrogen and a non-metal, has both elements in its name. Thus we have hydrochloric acid, HCI.

In naming the compounds having the formulas SO2, PCl3, N2O4, and PCl5 we use a numeral prefix to show how many atoms (or atomic weights) of the second element are present. Thus SO₂ represents sulphur dioxide; 

PCl3, phosphorus trichloride; N2O4, nitrogen tetroxide; and PCl5, phosphorus pentachloride. Carbon monoxide is so named because it has only one atomic weight of oxygen represented in its formula, while carbon dioxide has two. Name CCl4, SO3, FeS2, and P2O5. 

When a metal combines with a non-metal in two proportions (review §§ 103 and 104), we also use the suffixes -ous and -ic to distinguish between the two compounds. 

Thus, there are two copper chlorides: cuprous chloride (CuCl) and cupric chloride (CuCl2). The suffix, -ous, attached to the name of metal, shows that the smaller proportion of non-metal is present in that compound. 

The suffix, -ic indicates the larger proportion of the nonmetal. Apply the rule, and name the following pairs of compounds: Hg2O and HgO; HgCl and HgCl2; FeCl2 and FeCl3.

Nitrous acid (HNO2) and nitric acid (HNO3) also illustrate multiple proportions. Plainly, nitrous acid has the smaller proportion of oxygen, and nitric acid the larger proportion. Two other similar pairs of acids are sulphurous acid (H2SO3) and

sulphuric acid (H2SO4), and phosphorous acid (H3PO3) and phosphoric acid (H3PO4). The hydrogen of each of these acids can be replaced by a metal, such as sodium (Na). The resulting compounds are the sodium salts of these acids. Thus, NaNO2 is the formula of sodium nitrite, and NaNO3 that of sodium nitrate. 

Likewise, Na2SO3 represents sodium sulphite, and Na2SO4, sodium sulphate. Sodium phosphite is Na3PO3, and sodium phosphate,

Na3PO4. From these illustrations we derive the rule that, of a pair of acids, the one having less oxygen has the suffix, -ous; while the acid having the more oxygen gets the suffix -ic; likewise that the salts of an -ous acid end in -ite, while the salts of an -ic acid end in -ate.

The radical of an acid is that group of elements (NO3 in nitric acid) which is present in all the salts of that acid. Thus we have 

HNO3, NaNO3, Ca(NO3)2, and Al(NO3)3. Name each of these compounds. Similarly all sulphates contain the radical, SO4. 

Thus we have H2SO4, K2SO4, CaSO4, and Al2(SO4)3. Name each of these compounds.

The radical (OH) is present in the formulas of all hydroxides (see 107). Name the compounds having the formulas KOH, Mg(OH)2, Al(OH)3, Fe(OH)2, and Ca(OH)2. There is one radical which we shall need to recognize and which takes the place of a metal like sodium; it is ammonium (NH4). Examples of the formulas of ammonium compounds are: NH4OH, NH4Cl, NH4NO3, 

(NH4)2SO4, (NH4)3PO4. Name each of these. 

110. Summary.-A law, in chemistry, is a general rule regarding the behavior of substances.

The atomic theory was used by John Dalton to explain the laws of definite and multiple proportions. 

An atom is the smallest part of an element that exists in any compound or takes part in a chemical change. 

A molecule is the smallest part of an element or compound that can exist by itself in the gaseous state. 

Molecules of elements are either single atoms, or consist of two or more like atoms. Molecules of oxygen, hydrogen, nitrogen, and chlorine contain two atoms each. 

Molecules of compounds contain two or more unlike atoms.

A formula is a combination of symbols. It expresses the composition of a compound, or of an element known to have more than one atom in its molecule (O2). 

A formula weight is the sum of the atomic weights in the formula of a compound. If the compound is a gas, the formula weight is also the molecular weight. 

A radical is a group of atoms which is transferred, in chemical changes, like a single atom.

We can calculate the percentage composition of a compound from the formula. 

A compound of two elements has the ending -ide. 

An acid consisting of only hydrogen and a non-metal has both elements in its name. Thus, hydrochloric acid, HCl. 

The ending -ous, or -ic, attached to the name of a metal, means less, or more, respectively, of the non-metal with which the metal is combined.

In acids containing oxygen, such as HNO2 and HNO3, the ending -ous indicates less oxygen, and -ic indicates more oxygen. 

Acids ending in -ous have salts ending in -ite. 

Acids ending in -ic have salts ending in -ate. 

The radical of an acid is present in all its salts. 

The hydrogen compounds of acid radicals are the acids themselves; e.g. hydrogen nitrate, HNO3.

The radical hydroxyl (OH) is combined with metals in hydroxides. 

The radical ammonium (NH4) acts like a metal.

CHAPTER IX VALENCE 112. What valence is.-We shall learn in § 155 how chemists determine the formula of a compound from the proportions by weight of its elements. When we compare the formulas of the hydrogen compounds of several elements, we see that different numbers of hydrogen atoms combine with one atom of the other element: HC1 H₂O NH3 CHA The chemist expresses these differences by saying that each of the four elements combined with hydrogen has a different valence. He calls the valence of hydrogen 1. 

The chlorine is united with hydrogen atom for atom, hence the chlorine has the same valence as the hydrogen, namely, 1. The oxygen atom of the water molecule has the ability to hold 2 hydrogen atoms in combination, hence oxygen has a valence of 2. The nitrogen of ammonia, since its atom holds 3 atoms of hydrogen in combination, has a valence of 3; while the carbon of marsh gas, or methane (CH4) has a valence of 4. We can represent the valences of the elements in these four compounds more strikingly as follows:

H-C1 H-O-H H-N H-C-H Every "bond" represents a valence of each of the two atoms which it holds together. 147

We can also measure valence by displacement. In § 37 we studied the displacement of the hydrogen of acids by means of metals. Equations showing such reactions are:

H₂ ↑ 2 (1) 2Na+ 2HC1-2NaCl + (2) Mg+2HCl→ MgCl2+ H₂ 个 ​(3) Zn+2HC1→ ZnCl2 + H2 ↑ (4) 2A1+6HC1→ 2A1C13+3H2 ↑ In equation (1) two atoms of sodium displace two atoms of hydrogen and unite with two atoms of chlorine. Each sodium atom and chlorine atom has therefore a valence of 1, as shown in the formula, NaCl. The magnesium of 

MgCl2 has a valence of 2 because its atom has displaced 2 atoms of hydrogen and united with 2 atoms of chlorine. 

In ZnCl2 zinc has a valence of 2 for the same reason; while the aluminum of AlCl, has a valence of 3. 

The valence of an element is that property which determines the number of atoms of other elements, such as hydrogen or chlorine, one atom of the element can combine with or displace. 

An element with a valence of 1 is said to be univalent (yūniv'a-lent); one with a valence of 2, bivalent (biv'a-lent); one with a valence of 3, trivalent (triv'ā-lent); one with a valence of 4, quadrivalent (quâd-riv'a-lent); and one with valence of 5, quinquivalent (kwin-kwiv'a-lent). Tět'ra-va 'lent, pěn'ta-va'lent, and hex'a-vā'lent are often used for valences 4, 5, and 6, respectively. 

We often attach the valence mark to the symbol by placing small Roman numerals above and after the symbol. Thus, Na¹, Zn", Alш, and CIV mean that the elements named have valences of 1, 2, 3, and 4, respectively. The formulas of some compounds, written with valence symbols, are: Na¹Cl¹, Ca¹¹Cl₂, AlшCl¹, CIVCI, CIVO₂, and PVCI'. Name each of them.

III 

113. Valence is electrical.-When we pass an electric current through water containing a small amount of

VALENCE 

149 sulphuric acid (§ 35) electrolysis takes place. Hydrogen collects at the cathode (-electrode) and oxygen at the anode (+electrode). Likewise, when we electrolyze hydrochloric acid (§ 127), hydrogen goes to the (-) electrode and chlorine to the (+) electrode. From the fact that in electrolysis the hydrogen shows this preference for the cathode, we assume that the hydrogen of hydrochloric acid and of water is charged with positive (+) electric charges. The oxygen and the chlorine of these compounds show a decided preference for the anode, and it is natural to assume that they carry negative (−) charges. The chemist carries the idea of charged atoms over from electrolysis to valence, and writes the valences of the elements as electric charges. Thus he writes the valence formula of hydrogen chloride as H+Cl¯, and that of water as H₂+0². The total number of (+) charges on one element of a compound must be equal to the total (-) charges upon the other element. The chemist writes the valence formula of salt as Na+Cl-, and of calcium chloride as Ca++Cl₂. The atoms of hydrogen and metals in combination have (†) charges, while those of non-metals, like oxygen and chlorine (in chlorides), (-) charges.

The modern view of the structure of atoms (Chapter XXIV) includes the idea that valence is electrical. It is therefore easiest for the student to learn the valences of elements as either (†) or (−). The Valence Table given in § 114 includes the valence symbols of a number of metals, non-metals, and radicals. Some elements are given with (+) valences in one set of compounds and with (-) valences in another set. This is true of sulphur. In the sulphides its valence symbol is S2-; in sulphur dioxide it is S4+.

114. Use of valence. The practical use of valence is that it helps us to write correct formulas. We need not remember the formula of every compound. We need only recall the valence of the elements which combine. 

Thus, if we know that the valence of zinc is 2, we know also that the formula of zinc chloride must be ZnCl2 (Zn++Cl₂¯), since in all chlorides the chlorine atom has a valence of 1 (Cl). Zinc oxide must have the formula 

ZnO (Zn++O²¯), and zinc sulphide must have the formula ZnS (Zn++S2-).

Radicals (CIO3)— (SO3)2 (SiO3)2 (NO3)- (CO3)2 In order to use the valence of an element in constructing formulas we may do one of several things: (1) Remember the valence with the symbol.

VALENCE 

151 (2) Remember the formula of some compound of the element, such as its chloride or oxide, or its hydrogen compound, and then derive the valence from this formula. (3) Look up, in the text, the formula of some compound of the element, and derive the valence in this way. 

Thus, suppose you wish to write the formula of silicon iodide. You will not find this substance described in this book. But silicon dioxide is described. Its formula is 

SiO2. You decide that the valence of silicon is 4. Its valence symbol is Si4+. You next look up, if you do not remember, the formula of some iodide. You find that the formula of hydrogen iodide is HI. You conclude that, in the iodides, iodine has a valence of 1. Its valence symbol must be I-. From these two valence symbols you construct the formula Si¹+14, or SiI4, for silicon iodide. 

There are two rules that are very helpful: (1) If two elements have the same numerical valence, they unite atom for atom. This rule is shown in such formulas as H+Cl-, Ca++02-, Al3+N³-. 2- (2) If the elements that unite have different numerical valences, then the valence of one element multiplied by the number of its atoms is equal to the valence of the second element multiplied by the number of its atoms. In other words, the total number of (+) charges carried by the atoms of one element must equal the total number of (-) charges carried by the atoms of the other element. Two atoms of a univalent element combine with one atom of a bivalent element, as in H₂+O²- and Mg++Cl₂-. Two atoms of a trivalent element unite with three atoms of a bivalent element, as in Al3+S32- and Ca3++N23. One atom of a quadrivalent element combines with four univalent atoms, as in C4-H+4 and Si4+Cl. One atom of a quadrivalent element combines with two atoms of a bivalent one, as in C4+S22- and Mn4+022-. Three atoms of a quadrivalent element unite with four of a trivalent one, as in Al3+C34-.

115. Valence of radicals. We have already learned what a radical is (§§ 107 and 109). In displacement and double decomposition reactions radicals act as units, like atoms. We therefore consider that they have valence, and we apply to them the rules laid down for single atoms. 

The valence of a non-metallic radical (see Valence Table) is determined from the number of hydrogen atoms with which one unit of the radical combines. 2 We represent the molecule of water by H+ (OH)¯ as well as by H₂+0²-. The radical (OH) has therefore a valence of 1. In calcium hydroxide, Ca++(OH)2, and in aluminum hydroxide, Al³+(OH)3, the univalent radical (OH) is taken 2 and 3 times, respectively, just as if it were itself an atom. The formula of nitric acid (HNO3) contains the nitrate radical (NO3)¯ combined with one atomic weight of hydrogen. This radical has therefore a valence of 1. Cupric nitrate has the formula, Cu++ (NO3)2. The sulphate radical (SO4)2 present in sulphuric acid, H2SO4, is bivalent; so is the carbonate radical, (CO3)2, of carbonic acid (H2CO3) and the silicate radical (SiO3)2, of silicic acid (H2SiO3). The phosphate radical, (PO4)3, is trivalent, as in H3PO4. The radical, (SiO4)¹-, present in orthosilicic acid, H4SiO4, is quadrivalent. 

116. Elements with more than one valence.-The Valence Table (§ 114) shows several cases in which the same element has two or more valences. Thus copper has a valence of 1 in the cuprous compounds (CuCl) and of 2 in the cupric compounds (CuCl2). In ferrous compounds (FeCl2, FeO, and FeSO4) iron has a valence of 2; but in ferric compounds: FeCl3, Fe2O3, and Fe2(SO4)3, iron has a valence of 3. In carbon monoxide (CO), carbon has a valence of 2; but in carbon dioxide (CO2)

VALENCE 

153 its valence is 4. It is because some elements have more than one valence that these elements form compounds in multiple proportions by weight (§§ 70, 101, and 104).

From the cases just studied it is plain that we cannot assign a valence to such elements as iron, copper, and carbon in the uncombined state. We must know the compound, or class of compounds, in which they are present. Helium (He) and argon (A) form no compounds. 

Their valence is 0. 

117. Summary.-The valence of an element is that property which determines the number of atoms of other elements, such as hydrogen or chlorine, one atom of the element combines with or displaces.

We take the valence of hydrogen, and of chlorine in the chlorides, as 1.

Valence is electrical, and the valence of an atom is the number of (+) or (-) charges it uses in chemical combination. 

Metals, in their compounds with non-metals, have (+) charges; the non-metals have (-) charges. 

The total number of (+) charges in a molecule equals the total of (-) charges.

If two elements have the same valence, they unite atom for atom.

If two elements that unite have unequal valences, the valence of one element, times the number of its atoms, equals the product of the valence and number of atoms of the other element. 

Radicals act as units in displacement and double decomposition reactions, and are assigned valences, as atoms are. 

The valence of a non-metallic radical is 1, 2, 3, or more, according to the number of hydrogen atoms with which one unit of the radical combines.

Atoms of many elements have more than one valence. Such elements form compounds according to the law of multiple proportions.

CHAPTER X EQUATIONS 119. Writing an equation.—An equation stands for a reaction, and expresses in compact form the rearrangement of elements that has taken place. Hitherto we have used only "word equations" (§ 20), but from now on we are to employ equations made up of symbols and formulas. Thus, the equation, Fe+S→FeS, shows us not only what substances are taken and produced, but also that one atomic weight of iron (56 parts) unites with one atomic weight of sulphur (32 parts) to produce one formula weight (88 parts) of ferrous sulphide. 

As in the "word equation," the arrow (→) is read: "give," or "produce"; the sign (+) means "and." The symbols (or formulas) on the left of the arrow represent the substances put together, that is, the reagents (§ 3); those on the right stand for the products. The equation does not tell us that heating is necessary to start the reaction between iron and sulphur; neither does it tell us that when the reaction is once started it becomes a combustion, giving off heat and light (§§ 9 and 24). In using this equation we must supply, from our own knowledge, the conditions under which the reaction takes place, as well as the energy change which accompanies the reaction. 

The simple substances iron and sulphur are solids under 155

ordinary conditions; hence we make no attempt to show their molecular formulas. We merely show an atomic weight of each. In the equation, C+02→CO, we represent one atomic weight of carbon (a solid), and one molecular weight of each of the gases: oxygen and carbon monoxide. Remember that the molecules of hydrogen, oxygen, nitrogen, and chlorine contain two atoms each (see § 105). The equation is, however, incorrect, for it shows two atomic weights of oxygen in the reagents and only one in the products. We remedy this error by writing "2" as a coefficient before both the "C" and the "CO." 2C+02→2CO. 

We have now balanced the equation; that is, we have made the number of atomic weights of each element the same on both sides of the arrow. A balanced equation must show the source of every atomic weight of every element used, also what becomes of it. 

We express the union of hydrogen and oxygen to form water (steam) by molecular formulas, since all are gases. H₂+02-H2O. 

The equation needs to be balanced. We note that O2, on the left, contains 2 atomic weights of oxygen, while H2O, on the right, has only one. We accordingly multiply 

H2O by 2, and H2 also by 2, and obtain the balanced equation: 2H2+02→2H2O. 

120. Equations for the four kinds of reactions.-We have already learned that there are four main groups of

EQUATIONS 

157 chemical changes: (1) combination; (2) decomposition; (3) replacement, or displacement; and (4) double decomposition. All the changes given in the preceding section are cases of combination. The formation of a compound from its elements is also called a synthesis of the compound. Why?

Decomposition, the second kind of chemical change, is illustrated by the breaking up of mercuric oxide, when heated, into mercury and oxygen:

HgO→Hg+02. 

Again the oxygen, a gas, is written as molecules. To balance the equation we write it: 2HgO-2Hg+0₂ ↑ . 

Since the oxygen escapes as a gas, while the mercury usually remains behind, we use a second arrow, pointing upward, after the formula of oxygen. 

The preparation of oxygen from potassium chlorate also involves a decomposition:

KC1O3-KC1+O2. 

This simple equation must be balanced:

2KC1O3-2KC1+302 ↑. 

We must not write the formula of the solid product KClO or KClO2, but KCl. It is potassium chloride. 

Displacement, or replacement, is illustrated by the reaction between zinc and dilute hydrochloric acid:

Zn+HCl-ZnCl2+H2. 

The formula for zinc chloride must not be written ZnCl,

because one formula weight contains two atomic weights of chlorine. We balance the equation by writing Zn+2HC1→ZnCl2+H2 ↑ . 

On the other hand, the simplest equation for the reaction between zinc and dilute sulphuric acid is already balanced. Zn+H2SO4 ZnSO4+H2 ↑. 

The reaction between sodium and water (§ 36) is also a replacement. The simplest equation is: 

Na+H2O-NaOH+H2. 

In balancing this equation, we must not change the formula of any substance, but use the proper coefficients before the symbols and formulas. Then we have: 2Na+2H2O→2NaOH+H₂ ↑ . 

In the first two cases of replacement zinc (a metal), displaces hydrogen from acids and takes its place, forming the salts, zinc chloride and zinc sulphate, respectively. 

In the third case we have a metal reacting with water and displacing half of its hydrogen. The metal unites with the remaining half of the hydrogen, and with all the oxygen, to form a hydroxide. One metal may also displace another metal, as when zinc reacts with cupric sulphate solution and forms copper and zinc sulphate solution.

Zn+CuSO4-ZnSO4+Cu↓. 

The arrow pointing downward shows that the copper is insoluble and is precipitated from the solution. 

Double decomposition, the fourth class of reactions, is illustrated by the change that takes place when a solu-

EQUATIONS 

159 tion of sodium chloride (common salt) is mixed with one of silver nitrate. A white precipitate of silver chloride appears. Sodium nitrate remains in solution. 

NaCl+AgNO3-AgCl↓ +NaNO3. 

In the double decomposition each reagent (sodium chloride and silver nitrate) acts as though it were first decomposed into two parts, and the metals then exchanged places. The nitrate radical (NO3), like the sulphate radical (SO4) and the hydroxide radical (OH), acts like a non-metal. In this reaction the chlorine and the nitrate radical have, of course, exchanged places too. 

In the reaction for the preparation of nitrogen (§ 89) we have an example of decomposition following double decomposition: (1) NaNO2 +NH4Cl sodium nitrite →NH4NO2 +NaCl. ammonium ammonium sodium chloride (2) NH4NO2→N2 ↑ +2H2O. nitrite chloride 121. Things to remember.-We need to remember several things regarding equations: (1) An equation means nothing unless it stands for a real chemical change. Thus, the equation, 2Ag+2HC1→2AgC1+H2 ↑, is absurd, since silver does not react with hydrochloric acid. (2) We must know the names, and the correct symbols and formulas, for all the substances taken and for all the products. (3) We must use molecular formulas for the elements that are gases. What are the common ones? (4) A small subscript number, as in Cu(NO3)2, following a parenthesis, multiplies all the atomic weights inside the parenthesis. Thus, Cu(NO3)2 means CuN206.

(5) A large number-a coefficient-written before a formula multiplies all of the formula, including the subscript numbers. 

Thus, 3Cu(NO3)2 means 3 atomic weights of copper, 6 of nitrogen, and 18 of oxygen, all contained in 3 formula weights of cupric nitrate. (6) The number of atomic weights of each element must be the same on both sides of the equation (Law of the Conservation of Matter). (7) We must not change a formula in order to balance an equation, but must use the correct coefficients before the formulas. (8) The equation does not tell us the conditions necessary for the change, nor the energy changes that take place. These we must know in order to interpret the equation correctly. 

122. How to calculate from equations.-Equations are important to the chemist not only because they show him how the elements rearrange themselves in a chemical change, but also because they tell him (by use of the atomic weights) the proportions by weight of all the substances taken and produced in the change. The chemist is thus able to calculate how much of each reagent he should use and how much of each product he can get. 

Let us first learn how to solve a problem which involves the combination of iron and sulphur to produce ferrous sulphide, FeS. (a) How much ferrous sulphide can we obtain by heating 20 grams of iron with sufficient sulphur? (b) How many grams of sulphur are needed?

We need to proceed in a definite way, so as to avoid mistakes and save time. (1) We write down the equation and balance it, necessary, as we have already done (§ 119). Fe+ S-FeS. if (2) We put under each symbol and formula the parts,

EQUATIONS 

161 by weight, which it represents. For this we use the approximate atomic weights (back cover). Fe+S-FeS. 56 32 88 (3) We now put, above the symbol for iron, the weight of iron given in the problem (20 g.), and put x above the formula for ferrous sulphide. We are not concerned with the sulphur. 

20 g.

Fe+S x g. FES 56 32 88 (4) We now read the equation thus: 56 parts of iron give 88 parts of ferrous sulphide, therefore 20 g. of iron will give x grams of ferrous sulphide. This is put into the form of a proportion: 

56 88: 20 : x 

Therefore 56 x=88X20; 88 X 20 and x= 56 or 31.4 g.

We can also solve such problems by the use of fractions instead of a proportion. The weight of ferrous sulphide shown in the equation is 88 56 that of the iron; hence the amount of ferrous sulphide produced from 20 g. of iron 88 will be

X20 g. 56 88 

Therefore x= X20, or 31.4 g. 56 Let us now solve part (b) of the problem: How many grams of sulphur are needed?

We write down the equation once more, with a y over the symbol for the sulphur. We are not concerned, this time, with the ferrous sulphide. 

20 g. y g.

Fe+S-FeS. 56 32 88 

Since 56 g. of iron unite with 32 g. of sulphur, 20 grams of iron require y grams of sulphur. 

56: 32: 20 : y 

Therefore 56 y = 32×20; 32 X 20 and y

The 32 weight of sulphur shown in the equation is that of the iron;

56 hence the amount of sulphur needed to combine with 20 g. of iron is 32 56 X20 g. 32 

Therefore y 

X20, or 11.4 g. 56 We should also make an approximate common-sense calculation, so as to make sure that our answer is not an absurd one. 

We note in part (b) that 32 is a little more than half of 56, hence the weight of sulphur must be a little more than half of 20 g. 

Our result, 11.4 g., is, therefore, what we should expect.

124. Summary.—An equation stands for a reaction. Its symbols and formulas show what the reagents consist of and what the

products are. The equation does not tell us the conditions under which the reaction takes place. 

To balance an equation is to make the number of atomic weights of each element the same on both sides of the equation. 

We must not alter the formula of a compound in balancing an equation.

A balanced equation stands for the proportions by weight of the reagents and the products. By using the atomic weights for which the symbols stand, and adding up the proper number of them, we can build up the reacting weights represented in the equation.

A balanced equation, with the reacting weights of its elements and compounds, allows us to calculate the weight of any substance taken or produced in the reaction. 

In making calculations from equations, we use the reacting weights of only two substances at a time. 

The reacting weight of one substance (A): the reacting weight of the other substance (B) :: the actual weight of (A): the actual weight of (B). If three of these are known, the fourth can be calculated.

We should always make a common-sense calculation to make sure we have not obtained an answer which, because of some mistake on our part, is an absurd one.

CHAPTER XI CHLORINE 

126. Chlorine in nature.-Chlorine was first prepared by the Swedish chemist, Scheele, in 1774. It is a characteristic non-metal, like oxygen. It is so active chemically that we never find it "free" in nature, but always as a part of some compound, usually a chloride. The most abundant compound of chlorine is common salt, NaCl. HYDRO- GEN

CHLOR- INE 

FIG. 82. 

Electrolysis of hydrochloric acid. 

Note at which electrodes the gases collect. 

Potassium chloride (KCl), magnesium chloride (MgCl2), and calcium chloride (CaCl2) also occur in considerable amounts. Hydrochloric acid is a solution of hydrogen chloride, HCI. 

127. Preparation of chlorine by electrolysis. If we melt sodium chloride, and pass a suitable electric current through it, we obtain sodium and chlorine. 2NaC1-2Na+Cl₂ ↑. 

The sodium collects at the (−) electrode (the anode) and the chlorine at the (+) electrode (the cathode). The cathode should not be a metal, not even platinum, since chlorine unites energetically with metals to form chlorides. Generally we use carbon electrodes. 

We can electrolyze sodium chloride more easily by using a concentrated water solution of it. The apparatus is like that for the electrolysis of water (§ 35). As the current passes, the greenish 168

CHLORINE 

169 gas, chlorine, appears at the anode; at the cathode we collect hydrogen. After the chlorine has saturated the solution at the anode, the two gases are obtained in equal volumes. We would expect to obtain, at the cathode, both sodium and hydrogen: the sodium from the salt and the hydrogen from the water. The less active one, the hydrogen, is the one set free. Even if sodium were actually formed, it would act upon the water to produce sodium hydroxide and hydrogen (§ 36). We obtain both of these at the cathode: the hydrogen as a gas and the sodium hydroxide in solution. We may use two equations to express the result. (1) 2NaC1-2Na+Cl₂ ↑. (2) 2Na+2H2O-2NaOH+H2 ↑. 

The Nelson cell (see § 334) is an especially large and efficient apparatus for preparing chlorine by electrolysis. 

128. Common laboratory method. We usually prepare chlorine in the laboratory by the reaction between hydrochloric acid and manganese dioxide.

MnO₂+HCI Laboratory

CHLORINE FIG. 83. 

method of preparing chlorine. The bottle will contain gaseous chlorine, and the beaker of water will contain chlorine water. MnO2+4HC1→→MnCl2+2H2O+C1½ ↑ · The manganese dioxide is not a catalytic agent here as in the decomposition of potassium chlorate (§ 20), but is an oxidizing agent. Potassium permanganate, KMnO4, may also be used. Both these compounds readily give up some of their oxygen; this unites with the hydrogen of hydrochloric acid to form water, and sets chlorine free.

The manganous chloride (MnCl2) remains dissolved. 

In the presence of certain catalysts even the free,

gaseous oxygen of the air can oxidize hydrogen chloride and set chlorine free. 4HC1+02→2H2O+2C12. 

We may also prepare chlorine by warming a mixture of sodium chloride, sulphuric acid, and manganese dioxide. 

In this case the sodium chloride and sulphuric acid first produce hydrogen chloride, which is then oxidized by the manganese dioxide as before.

MnO2+2NaC1+3H2SO4→2NaHSO4+MnSO4+2H2O+C12 ↑ . 

The student should now review calculations from equations, as given in Chapter X. The law of definite proportions is true for more than the definite composition of a compound; it also means that there is a definite proportion by weight between any two of the substances taken or produced in a reaction. These proportions are given by the formula weights shown in the balanced equation.

Write down the first equation in this section, and put under each formula the correct reacting weight. Then calculate how many grams of chlorine and other products can be obtained by the use of 100 grams of manganese dioxide, also how much hydrogen chloride is necessary.

Then do the same for the last equation in this section. 

129. Physical properties.-Chlorine was so named because it is green. The gas is about 2.5 times as dense as air and 35.5 times as dense as hydrogen. It is usually collected by being allowed to flow to the bottom of a vessel of air, so that it displaces the air upward. More than two volumes of it dissolve in one volume of water under ordinary conditions. The solution is chlorine water. In a warm, saturated salt brine chlorine is only slightly soluble; hence it may be collected over this liquid instead of under air.

CHLORINE 

171 The odor of chlorine is very disagreeable. The gas is also dangerous to inhale, as it attacks the lining of the throat and lungs. The results are like those of a bad cold. They may usually be relieved by the inhalation of a little gaseous ammonia.

Chlorine may be easily liquefied by pressure (§ 96). 

The liquid is stored and shipped in steel cylinders. 

130. Chemical properties.-Chlorine is an intensely active substance. We may classify its reactions under three heads: (1) direct combination; (2) replacement; (3) substitution, a kind of double decomposition and displacement combined.

Chlorine unites with metals. Thus, shavings of sodium at once lose their lustre in chlorine, and sodium chloride is produced. 2Na+C12-2NaCl.

When powdered antimony is dropped into a jar of chlorine, the two unite with the evolution of much heat, and glowing, hot particles of antimony chloride are produced. 2Sb+3C12-2SbC13. 

Copper foil, if heated, likewise burns in chlorine; the product is cupric chloride, CuCl2. Iron, tin, zinc, and magnesium act in a similar way. A combustion (§ 24) is a chemical change in which heat and light are produced. Chlorine is, therefore, a supporter of the combustion of the metals named, just as oxygen is a supporter of the combustion of charcoal and sulphur. 

Chlorine unites with hydrogen to form hydrogen chloride, HCl. 

The reaction gives off much heat, just as the union of oxygen and hydrogen does. If we light a jet of hydrogen in air, the jet will continue to burn in chlorine. H2+C12-2HC1.

If we mix hydrogen and chlorine, and keep the mixture cold and in the dark, the gases do not combine; but exposure to sunlight

or other bright light causes the union to take place with explosive violence. Raising the temperature by means of a flame or an electric spark produces the same result. 

Chlorine unites with non-metals, such as phosphorus and sulphur. Phosphorus burns in chlorine, forming phosphorus trichloride, PC13. If an excess of chlorine is used, phosphorus pentachloride, PCl5, is formed. The sulphur compound has the formula, S2Cl2.

Chlorine unites with compounds, such as carbon monoxide and water. We expect carbon monoxide to unite with oxygen, forming carbon dioxide. 2CO+02 2CO2. 

Instead of adding oxygen it can add chlorine.

CO+C12→COC12. 

The product is carbonyl chloride, or phosgene. 

If we pass chlorine into iced water, we can obtain crystals of chlorine hydrate, Cl2.8H2O. 

131. Replacement by chlorine. We have seen that chlorine unites vigorously with hydrogen. Because of this activity it removes the hydrogen from many compounds. Thus, if we wet a strip of filter paper with hot turpentine, C10H16, and then plunge it into a jar of chlorine, the turpentine soon takes fire and burns with a very smoky flame. The chlorine unites with the hydrogen and sets the carbon free.

C10H16+8C12-10C+16HC1. 

We can prove the formation of hydrogen chloride by blowing the breath into the jar. A white fog results from the dissolving of the gas in the moisture of the breath. 

Chlorine acts upon paraffin (white wax) as it does upon turpentine. If we put a wax taper, burning vigorously, into chlorine, it burns for a time, with a very smoky

CHLORINE 

173 flame. The products are carbon and hydrogen chloride. 

Chlorine displaces not only carbon, but other nonmetals, such as bromine, iodine, and sulphur, from their compounds with hydrogen or with metals. Cl₂+2HBr→2HC1+Br2. C12+2KI →2KC1+I2, C12+H2S →2HC1+S. 

132. Chlorine and water.-When we pass chlorine into water, we obtain a solution, called chlorine water. If we expose chlorine water to sunlight, the chlorine disappears, and oxygen is formed.

The equation represents a displacement of oxygen by chlorine. 2C12+2H2O→→→4HC1+02. 

There are at least two reactions taking place here. First, two acids are formed: hydrochloric acid and hypochlorous acid,

HOCI. 

Cl2+H2O-HC1+HOC1. 

Sunlight does not affect the hydrochloric acid, but it causes the decomposition of the hypochlorous acid. 2HOC1-2HC1+02. 

FIG. 84. 

When chlorine water is exposed to sunlight, oxygen is the gaseous product.

The behavior of chlorine water shows that the solution contains free chlorine as well as the two acids. The reaction between chlorine and water belongs to the class of incomplete, or reversible, reactions (Chapter XIX). 

C12+H2O HC1+HOC1.

The equation has a double arrow to show that it may be read in both directions. All four of the substances

named are present. Sunlight makes the reaction a complete one, since it decomposes the hypochlorous acid. 

133. Substitution by chlorine.-In § 120 we learned to distinguish four kinds of reactions: combination, decomposition, replacement, and double decomposition. To which class does the equation,

Cl2+H2O→HC1+HOCI, belong? We see that one of the two atomic weights of chlorine in Cl2 has displaced one of the atomic weights of hydrogen in H2O, producing HOCl. The displaced hydrogen has not, however, been set free, but has combined with the second atomic weight of chlorine, forming HCl. The reaction is like a replacement and at the same time like a double decomposition. We call it a substitution reaction. The equation, CH4+C12 CH3C1+HC1, represents another case of substitution. 

134. Uses of chlorine.-(a) Chlorine is used to bleach cloth. Because hypochlorous acid gives up its oxygen so easily, chlorine water is a good oxidizing agent. This property accounts for the use of chlorine in bleaching cotton goods. Usually the chlorine is not used directly, but is first changed into bleaching powder, or chloride of lime, CaOCl2.

Ca(OH)2+Cl2→CaOCl2+H2O. 

When the cloth to be bleached is soaked, first, in a solution of bleaching powder and then in an acid, hypochlorous acid is liberated in the pores of the cloth, in contact with the coloring matter.

CaOCl2+H2SO4-CaSO4+HC1+HOCI. 

As a result the hypochlorous acid gives up its oxygen,

CHLORINE 

175 and the coloring matter is changed into colorless substances.

Dry chlorine is not a good bleaching agent unless water is present, because hypochlorous acid is needed. 

It is possible to use sodium hypochlorite, NaOCl, as the source of the chlorine instead of bleaching powder. 2NaOC1+H2SO4 Na2SO4+2HOC1 After being bleached, the cloth must be freed from any excess of chlorine, or of hypochlorous acid, or it will be seriously injured.

FIG. 85. 

Diagram of the bleaching of cloth by bleaching powder.

A substance like sodium sulphite (Na2SO3) or sodium thiosulphate (Na2S2O3) is used as an "antichlor," to remove the excess of chlorine.

Na2SO3+C12+H2O-Na2SO4+2HC1. 

The coloring matter of cotton and linen fibers is attacked by moist chlorine (hypochlorous acid) much more easily than the fibers themselves; hence chlorine may be used for bleaching cotton and linen cloth. This is not true of natural silk, wool, and

feathers, since the chlorine destroys not only the coloring matter, but the fibers as well. For these materials a less active bleaching agent, such as sulphur dioxide, is used (§ 182). (b) Chlorine as a disinfectant.-Chlorine is also used to destroy microscopic, living creatures that may cause disease. Bleaching powder acts as a disinfectant because, when exposed to moist air containing carbon dioxide, it gives off hypochlorous acid. The disease germs, like coloring materials, are destroyed by oxidation. Water for drinking and the water of swimming pools is sterilized by means of bleaching powder, sodium hypochlorite, or chlorine water. Javelle water is a solution of sodium hypochlorite. (c) Chlorine as a source of compounds. Two important chlorine compounds are carbon tetrachloride (CCl4) and chloroform (CHCl3). The first is used as a noninflammable cleaning agent; its vapor is also an extinguisher of fires, as carbon dioxide is. Chloroform is the well known anesthetic. Chlorine is also used to change certain metals present in natural ores into their chlorides, so that they can be separated from the rest of the ore. 

From the chloride the metal can afterwards be obtained in the free state. 

135. Summary.-Chlorine is found in nature in chlorides. It is prepared by the electrolysis of a water solution of sodium chloride. It collects at the (+) electrode. Hydrogen and sodium hydroxide collect at the (-) electrode. 

The common laboratory method of preparing chlorine is to warm a mixture of manganese dioxide and hydrochloric acid. 

Chlorine is a green gas about 2.5 times as dense as air, soluble in water, and capable of being liquefied by pressure. 

Chlorine is intensely active. It unites with hydrogen, metals, non-metals, and many compounds.

CHLORINE 

177 Chlorine displaces carbon, bromine, sulphur, and other nonmetals from their compounds with metals and with hydrogen. 

Chlorine reacts with water to form hydrochloric and hypochlorous acids. In sunlight, chlorine water (hypochlorous acid) decomposes, liberating oxygen.

We use chlorine to bleach cloth, disinfect rooms, sterilize drinking water, and to manufacture chlorine compounds. 

The bleaching, disinfecting, and sterilizing action of chlorine is due to hypochlorous acid, and is an oxidizing action.

140. Uses. Hydrochloric acid is made on a large scale and has many uses. It is a source of chlorine (§ 128). 

It cleans metals by removing their rusts, or oxides. 

Many important metal chlorides are prepared from it. 

Hydrochloric acid makes up about 500 of the gastric juice, and is very necessary for healthy digestion. 

Aqua regia (ā’kwa rēj'ĭ-ä) is a mixture of concentrated hydrochloric and nitric acids in the proportion of three volumes of hydrochloric acid to one of nitric acid. The nitric acid oxidizes the hydrochloric acid (§ 230), liberating chlorine. The chlorine unites with the metal. Aqua regia is used to get soluble compounds of inactive metals, such as gold and platinum, which do not react with either acid alone (§ 229).

Many elements, like chlorine, are very active at the instant of liberation from some compound. In this condition they are said to be in a nascent state. 

141. Chlorides.-Most chlorides are soluble in water. 

Two common ones are insoluble: silver chloride (AgCl), and mercurous chloride (HgCl). A third, lead chloride (PbCl2), is only slightly soluble in cold water, but much more in hot water. Cuprous chloride (CuCl) is a less common, insoluble chloride. If we treat the solution of a chloride, such as sodium chloride or hydrochloric acid,

HYDROGEN CHLORIDE 

183 with a solution of a silver salt, a mercurous salt, or a lead salt, we obtain a precipitate of the chloride of one of these metals.

AgNO3 +NaCl-AgCl +NaNO3. HgNO3 + HCl →HgCl + HNO3. Pb(NO3)2+2HC1→PbCl2 ↓ +2HNO3. 

To which class of reactions do these belong?

The test for a soluble chloride is based upon these reactions. 

If we treat an "unknown" solution with dilute nitric acid, and then with silver nitrate solution, and obtain a white precipitate which darkens in the light, we know that the original solution contained a chloride. The addition of nitric acid is necessary, since other solutions besides those of chlorides give white precipitates with silver nitrate. Silver chloride is insoluble in dilute nitric acid. 

142. Composition of hydrogen chloride, by volume.- 

We have already learned (§ 130) that hydrogen burns in chlorine to form hydrogen chloride. The proportions by weight are expressed in the formula, HCl. The chemist needs also to know the proportions by volume. In §§ 35 and 57 we used two methods to prove the proportions by volume in which hydrogen and oxygen unite and the proportion of water vapor (steam) that is formed: (1) The electrolysis of water (containing a little sulphuric acid). This method shows us that, in water, hydrogen and oxygen are combined in the proportion of two volumes of hydrogen to one of oxygen. It does not tell us the proportion of the steam. (2) The direct combination, or synthesis, of water from its elements. This method proves that when 2 volumes of hydrogen unite with 1 volume of oxygen, 2 volumes of steam are formed.

When we carry out the electrolysis of concentrated hydrogen chloride solution (hydrochloric acid), hydrogen collects at the (-) electrode and chlorine at the (+) electrode. At first the hydrogen collects more rapidly than the chlorine, but this is due to the fact that the chlorine is more soluble in the water present. When the solution in the collecting tube at the (+) electrode is saturated with chlorine, the two gases collect at the same rate.

The electrolysis of hydrochloric acid proves that in hydrogen chloride hydrogen and chlorine are united in equal proportions by volume; it does not prove anything regarding the proportional volume of the hydrogen chloride. We learn this additional fact by treating dry hydrogen chloride with sodium. In order to keep the action from becoming violent, we dilute the sodium by mixing it with mercury, and form sodium amalgam. HYDROGwz BHEEGET 

HYDROGEN CHLORIDE FIG. 89. 

Two volumes of hydrogen chloride give one volume of hydrogen.

A heavy-walled measuring tube or test tube is filled with dry hydrogen chloride, a sufficient amount of sodium amalgam is added, and the open end of the tube is closed with the thumb or a rubber stopper.

The tube must be shaken vigorously, so that the sodium amalgam comes in contact with all the hydrogen chloride. When the thumb or stopper is removed, under water, water rushes up into the tube. If the water levels inside and outside the tube are now made the same, the gas that remains fills the tube half full. This gas is hydrogen. The hydrogen has a volume half as great as that of the hydrogen chloride from which it was obtained. 

The two experiments: (1) the electrolysis of hydrochloric acid and (2) the displacement of the hydrogen in hydrogen chloride by sodium amalgam, together prove that one volume of hydrogen

HYDROGEN CHLORIDE 

185 and one volume of chlorine unite to give two volumes of hydrogen chloride.

H2 + Cl2 → 2HC1. 1 vol. 1 vol. 2 vols. 

143. Summary.-Hydrogen chloride is a colorless gas. Its solution in water is hydrochloric acid.

We prepare hydrogen chloride from a chloride (common salt) and concentrated sulphuric acid.

The test for hydrogen chloride is its fuming in moist air. It also forms a white smoke (ammonium chloride) with gaseous ammonia.

Hydrochloric acid is a strong acid. It reacts with metals, and with their oxides and hydroxides, giving chlorides. It reacts with strong bases, neutralizing them and forming salts (chlorides) and water.

Hydrochloric acid is used as a source of chlorine, to dissolve rusts (oxides) from the surface of metals, and (along with nitric acid) to form aqua regia.

The chlorides insoluble in water are those of silver, mercury (ous), and lead.

The test for a soluble chloride is to treat the unknown substance with a solution of silver nitrate in the presence of dilute nitric acid.

One volume of hydrogen unites with one volume of chlorine to form two volumes of hydrogen chloride.
