CHAPTER XIII  

145. Importance of molecular weights.-We have already learned why chemists believe in atoms and molecules, and we have used atomic weights to calculate the quantities of reagents and products in a chemical reaction. Thus far, how- ever, we have simply taken the atomic weights from a table prepared for us and have used formulas as given, without knowing how either is obtained. In this chapter we are to learn several things: (1) How molecular weights are obtained. (2) How the atomic weight of an element is determined from the molecular weights of its gaseous compounds.

FIG. 90. 

Joseph Louis Gay-Lussac (1778-1850). Investigator of the combining volumes of gases and of many other branches of chemistry. (3) How the formula of a compound is derived from its molecular weight and from the proportions, by weight, of the elements that make it up. 187

146. When gases react.—In order to obtain the molecular weights upon which atomic weights and formulas depend, chemists were forced to start with the properties of gases, since in certain respects all gases behave alike. 

This similarity of behavior is expressed in: (1) Boyle's law: the volume of any gas, if the temperature remains unchanged, varies inversely as the pressure. (2) Charles' law: the volume of any gas, if the pressure remains unchanged, varies directly as the absolute temperature.

Upon these laws the hypothesis of molecules is based. 

Boyle's and Charles' laws are the expression of physical properties of gases.

Gay-Lussac, as we have already learned (§ 58), found a general chemical property of gases. He noted that when two gaseous elements unite, they do so in definite proportions, not only by weight, but also by volume. We have seen (Chapter 

XI) that this is true for the combination of hydrogen and chlorine: hydrogen chloride. 2 vols. Hydrogen+chlorine 1 vol. 1 vol. 

In § 58 we found it true for the combination of hydrogen and oxygen: Hydrogen + oxygen→→steam. 2 vols. 1 vol. 2 vols. 

It is likewise true for the combination of nitrogen and hydrogen to form ammonia (§ 220): Hydrogen+nitrogen-ammonia. 

3 vols. 1 vol. 2 vols. 

147. Avogadro's hypothesis.-The remarkable regularity in the behavior of gases led Avogadro, in 1811, to state his explanation, or hypothesis: 

Equal volumes of all gases, at the same temperature and pressure, contain the same number of molecules.

This statement means that if the temperature and pressure are the same, a liter of hydrogen has just as many molecules as a liter of chlorine, oxygen, hydrogen chloride, steam, nitrogen, carbon dioxide, or any other gas.

In connection with the atomic theory (§ 102) we learned that the test of a hypothesis is its success: (1) in explaining facts we already know, and (2) in helping us to get more facts. 

Judged by these standards Avogadro's hypothesis takes its place in importance alongside Dalton's atomic theory. We shall now take up some of the consequences which follow if we accept Avogadro's explanation as true. 

FIG. 91. 

Avogadro 

Amedeo (1776-1856). 

Italian scientist who stated Avogadro's hypothesis. 

148. Molecules of gaseous elements. As a first test let us apply Avogadro's hypothesis to the problem: How many atoms are there in the molecule of a gaseous element such as hydrogen or chlorine? We have been writing the formula of hydrogen in the free state as H2 and that of chlorine as Cl2. We have also written the formula of oxygen as O2 and of nitrogen as N2 (§§ 105 and 106). Let us now take the case of hydrogen and chlorine: 

Hydrogen+chlorine-hydrogen chloride 

1 vol. 

1 vol. 

2 vols.

That these two gases combine in this proportion is a fact, established by experiment. According to Avogadro's

hypothesis any given volume of hydrogen contains as many molecules as the same volume of chlorine. Two volumes of hydrogen chloride must contain twice as many molecules as one volume of hydrogen or chlorine. 

We are therefore justified in writing: 

Hydrogen+chlorine hydrogen chloride 

1 mol. 

1 mol. 

2 mols.

Each of the 2 molecules of hydrogen chloride must contain a molecule of hydrogen and a molecule of chlorine:

Hydrogen chloride contains hydrogen+chlorine 

1 mol. mol. mol.

No volume equation is known which makes it necessary for us to assume that the hydrogen and chlorine molecules are ever divided into more than 2 parts each; hence we conclude that the half-molecules are atoms. 

The correct molecular formulas are therefore H2 and Cl2, respectively.

We can prove that the formula of oxygen is O2 by the volume equation for the union of hydrogen and oxygen to form steam: Hydrogen+oxygen-steam 2 vols. 1 vol. 2 vols. 

According to Avogadro's hypothesis, Hydrogen + oxygen→→→ steam 

2 mols. 1 mol. 2 mols.

Therefore each of the 2 molecules of steam must consist of 1 molecule (2 atoms) of hydrogen and a molecule of oxygen. 

Steam contains hydrogen+oxygen 

1 mol. 

1 mol. mol.

Hence we conclude that the oxygen molecule is likewise divisible

into two half-molecules, each of which is an atom. The formula of free oxygen is therefore O2. 

From the volume equation for the formation of ammonia from nitrogen and hydrogen (§ 220) we learn that there must be halfmolecules of nitrogen, and we write its formula N2. 

Not all gaseous elements contain 2 atoms in each molecule. 

In the ozone molecule (§ 31) 3 atoms are present, and we write the formula 03. In phosphorus vapor there are 4 atoms in each molecule (P4). The molecular formula of sulphur as a vapor is 

S2 or S8, according to temperature. In the rare gases of the atmosphere, such as helium and argon, there is only one atom per molecule. The same is true for the vapors of such metals as sodium, zinc, and mercury. 

149. A standard for molecular weights.-Avogadro's hypothesis makes it possible for us to find the molecular weights of gases. If there are equal numbers of molecules in equal volumes of two gases, then the relative, or comparative, weights of equal volumes must give us the relative weights of the molecules themselves. Thus, one liter of oxygen is practically 16 times (1.429÷0.09) as heavy as one liter of hydrogen. We must therefore conclude that the molecule of oxygen is practically 16 times as heavy as the molecule of hydrogen. The same reasoning applies to other gases. GAS

WEIGHT OF 1 LITER, IN GRAMS RELATIVE WEIGHTS OF EQUAL VOLUMES AND OF MOLECULES 

Hydrogen. 0.09 Oxygen... 1.43 1 16 Chlorine.... 3.18 35.5 

Hydrogen chloride.. 1.63 18.2 

Carbon monoxide... 1.25 14

Since there are two atoms in the molecule of hydrogen (§ 148), the oxygen molecule must be 16×2, or 32, times as heavy as the hydrogen atom. We therefore take 32 as the exact molecular weight of oxygen and use it as the standard for molecular weights. 

150. How to find molecular weights.-The chemist uses the term, gram-molecular weight, for as many grams of a substance as there are units in its molecular weight. 

22.4 LITERS FIG. 92. 

The gram-molecular volume of a gas is 22.4 liters. This is the volume of a cube 11.1 in. (28.2 cm.) on each edge. 

Thus, 32 grams of oxygen are one gram-molecular weight of oxygen, and 36.5 grams of hydrogen chloride form one grammolecular weight of hydrogen chloride. A gram-molecular weight is also called a molar weight, or mole.

The chemist uses the term, gram-molecular volume, for the volume occupied by one grammolecular weight of a gas, under standard temperature and pressure. Thus, 32 grams of oxygen (one grammolecular weight) have a volume of 22.4 liters at 0° C. and 760 mm. pressure. We derive this number by dividing 32 g. by 1.43 g., the weight of 1 liter of oxygen. The gram-molecular volume is the same for all gases. This is the same as saying that the weight, in grams, of 22.4 liters of any gas is its gram-molecular weight. 

It has been calculated that 22.4 liters of a gas, at standard temperature and pressure, contain 6.06×1023 molecules. This number is the same as 606 followed by 

21 ciphers.

We have, therefore, a very simple method for obtaining the molecular weight of a gas. We find the weight, under standard conditions, of any convenient volume of the gas, and then calculate the weight, in grams, of 22.4 liters, or 22,400 cu. cm., of it. Thus 1 liter of carbon monoxide weighs 1.25 g. Therefore 22.4 liters weigh 

22.4×1.25, or 28 g. The molecular weight is therefore 28. 

Suppose that 203 cu. cm. of a gas weigh 0.4 g., what is its molecular weight? 

203 0.4:: 22,400 : x x=44 g.

Therefore the molecular weight of the gas is 44. 

The student will see that if we know the molecular weight of a gas, we can calculate the weight of 1 liter (at 0° C. and 760 mm.). We divide the gram-molecular weight by 22.4. Thus, how much does a liter of ammonia gas weigh? The formula is NH3; the gram-molecular weight is 17 g.; 1 liter therefore weighs 17÷22.4, or 0.76 g.

FIG. 93. 

A Dumas flask for determining the weight of a given volume of a gas.

We can also calculate very quickly whether a gas is heavier, or lighter, volume for volume, than air. The weight of 1 liter of air, under standard conditions, is a little less than 1.293 g. A gram-molecular volume of air weighs 

22.4×1.293, or 28.95 g. If the gram-molecular weight of a gas is greater than this, the gas is heavier than air; if smaller, then the gas is lighter than air. A gas having the formula C3H8 has a gram-molecular weight of 44 g.; it is therefore 44÷28.95, or 1.52 times as heavy as air. 

151. Finding molecular weights in solution.—We use the weight of the gram-molecular volume to get the molecular weights of all substances that are gases, also for substances which we can turn into gases without decomposing them. We cannot use the method for a substance like sugar, because we cannot get sugar in the

form of a vapor. However, in this case we have a method based upon the freezing point of solutions. 

A solution of sugar in water, or of alcohol in water, freezes below 0° C. (§ 78). We can determine the grammolecular weight of alcohol (C2H6O), as a vapor, to be 46 g. The gram-molecular weight of glycerine (C3H8O3), as a vapor, is 92 g. When 46 g. of alcohol, or 92 g. of glycerine, are dissolved in 1000 g. of water, the solution freezes in each case at -1.86° C. The gram-molecular weight of many other substances likewise lowers the freezing point of 1000 g. of water 1.86° C. Hence we conclude that if we dissolve enough sugar in 1000 g. of water to make the solution freeze at -1.86° C., we have used a gram-molecular weight of sugar. Thus we find the molecular weight of cane sugar to be 342, corresponding to the formula C12H22O11. By the same method we find the molecular weight of grape sugar to be 180, corresponding to the formula C6H12O6. 

We can also determine the molecular weights of many substances from the boiling points of their solutions. Pure water boils at 

100° C. under 760 mm. pressure; but a solution containing a grammolecular weight of dissolved substance, such as sugar, in 1000 g. of water boils at 100.52° C. If therefore we add a substance of unknown molecular weight to 1000 g. of water until the solution boils at 100.52° C., we conclude that we have added a grammolecular weight of the substance.

The dissolved substance used in the boiling point method must not, of course, pass off before, or with, the water. Moreover, neither the freezing point nor the boiling point method can be used in the case of acids, bases, or salts (see Chap. XVII). 

152. Reading volumes of gases from equations.— 

Equations in which the formulas of all the gases, including those of the elements, represent molecules, are called

molecular equations. In a balanced, molecular equation we can read off the relative volumes of its gases from the coefficients used to balance the equation. Thus, in the equation, 2CO+02→2CO2, the coefficients: "2" before CO, "1" (understood) before

O2, and "2" before CO2, represent the number of grammolecular weights of these substances. But the grammolecular weight of a gas is the weight of a gram-molecular volume (22.4 liters) of it. Hence we may read the equation thus: 

44.8 liters CO+22.4 liters O2-44.8 liters CO2. 2 vols. 1 vol. 2 vols. 

If, for example, we burn 500 cu. cm. of carbon monoxide in air, we can calculate that 250 cu. cm. of oxygen will combine with it. Again, when methane (CH4) burns in air, we can read the volumes of the methane and oxygen, as well as those of the carbon dioxide and water produced, from the equation. 

Simple equation: CH4+ O2 

Balanced:

CH4+202 CO2 + H2O CO₂+2H2O (steam) Relative vols.: 1 2 1 2 

From this equation we can calculate how much oxygen is needed to burn, say, 50 cu. cm. of methane. Evidently twice as great a volume as of the methane burned, or 100 cu. cm. How many cu. cm. of carbon dioxide will be formed? Of steam? 

153. How to find atomic weights. Since Avogadro's hypothesis makes it possible for us to get the molecular weights of gases, it gives us a way of selecting the atomic weights of elements that form gaseous compounds. The molecule of a compound is made up of the atoms of its

elements, consequently the weight of the molecule is the sum of the weights of all the atoms present in it. The molecular and atomic weights are, of course, relative, or comparative weights, based upon the atomic weight of oxygen as exactly 16, and its molecular weight as exactly 32. In the table given herewith there is a list of carbon compounds, with their gram-molecular weights as determined from the gram-molecular volume (22.4 liters). 

The proportions of carbon, as determined by exact analysis, are in column III. If multiplied by 100, these decimal fractions become percentages. Thus, 0.4286 equals 42.86%.

We might make a much longer list; but in no case have less than about 12 grams of carbon been found in one gram-

molecular weight of any of its gaseous compounds. We therefore decide that the atomic weight of carbon is about 12. When we find 24, 36, and 48 grams of carbon in the gram-molecular weights of certain carbon compounds, the molecules of those compounds must contain 2, 3, and 4 carbon atoms, respectively. 

The atomic weight of an element is the smallest weight of it found in the molecular weights of its compounds. 

154. Exact atomic weights.-While the table in § 153 leads to the conclusion that the atomic weight of carbon is about 12, it does not give us the exact atomic weight, such as we find in the atomic weight list. The proportion of carbon in each compound (column III) is determined with great exactness, but the gram-molecular weights (column II) obtained from the gram-molecular volume are less precise. Consequently the atomic weights of carbon in column IV are only approximate.

The true atomic weight of an element is obtained, directly or indirectly, from the exact proportion by weight in which the element combines with or replaces 1.008 parts of hydrogen or 8 parts of oxygen. These weights of hydrogen and oxygen are called equivalent weights of these two elements. They are, of course, the proportions in which these elements combine to produce water. The equivalent weight of chlorine is 35.46, since 

35.46 parts, by weight, of chlorine combine with 1.008 parts of hydrogen.

The gram-equivalent weight of any element is that weight of it, in grams, which combines with or replaces 

1.008 grams of hydrogen or 8 grams of oxygen.

When carbon and oxygen combine to form carbon

dioxide, the proportion of carbon to oxygen is 3.003 8. 

The equivalent weight of carbon (3.003) is about of the approximate atomic weight (12). We therefore multiply the equivalent weight by exactly 4 to get the exact atomic weight of carbon. This gives us the number 

12.01. Similar methods are used for other elements. In the case of chlorine the equivalent weight (35.46) is the true atomic weight. In the case of oxygen the equivalent weight (8) is the atomic weight.

If we now review Chapter IX, we shall see that the valence of an element comes into the calculations of its exact atomic weight. 

We measure the valence by the number of hydrogen atoms which one atom of the element combines with or displaces. Thus the valence of oxygen in H2O is 2; that of nitrogen in NH3 is 3; and that of carbon in CH4 is 4. By our definition of equivalent weight (above) the equivalent weight of chlorine (35.46) is numerically the same as its atomic weight. The valence of chlorine (in chlorides) is 1. The equivalent weight of oxygen in H2O is 8, or its atomic weight. By the same reasoning the equivalent weight of nitrogen in ammonia is of its atomic weight, or 4.67 (14÷3). 

Finally, the equivalent weight of carbon in CH4 and CO2 is 3, or of its atomic weight.

We may therefore make a general rule: Atomic weight equivalent weight Xvalence We may state the rule in another form: atomic weight Valence equivalent weight 155. How to determine a formula. We have already learned (§§ 107 and 108) that the formula of a compound is a chemist's expression for the elements in the compound and the proportions by weight in which they are combined. In this chapter we have learned that in the case of elements and compounds that are gases, or can

be changed into the gaseous state, the formula expresses also the relative weight and composition of one molecule, including the number of atoms contained in one molecule. 

In order that we may determine the formula of a compound, we must have at least the first two of the three following sets of facts: (1) The atomic weights of the elements in the compound; (2) The proportion by weight of each element; usually this is given in percentages, or parts in a hundred; (3) The gram-molecular weight of the compound, that is, the weight of 22.4 liters of it in the gaseous state. 

Let us now apply these facts in the case of magnesium oxide, and determine its formula. 

ELEMENTS PERCENT- AGE OF ELEMENTS ATOMIC WEIGHTS RELATIVE NUMBERS OF ATOMS SIMPLEST RELATIVE NUMBERS 

Magnesium .. Oxygen... 60 24 

60+24, or 2.5 1 40 16 

40+16, or 2.5 1 Since the magnesium makes up 60%, by weight, of the magnesium oxide, all the atoms of magnesium, taken together, must weigh 60% of the magnesium oxide molecule. 

The oxygen atoms must make up the remaining 40%. 

We can find the relative numbers of atoms of each element if we divide the percentage of each element by its atomic weight. We thus find that the relative numbers are the same for each element. There are 2.5 atoms of magnesium for every 2.5 atoms of oxygen. We wish to obtain the relative numbers of atoms in the simplest whole numbers that are possible. If we divide each of

the relative numbers by 2.5, we see that there is one atom of magnesium for every atom of oxygen, and the simplest formula that is possible is MgO. 

Is the simplest formula of magnesium oxide also the correct one? According to the formula, MgO, the formula weight is 40 (24+16). Magnesium oxide is a solid which we cannot readily vaporize. We do not know the weight of 22.4 liters of its vapor. formula, MgO.

16 g. (see Table in § 153). Therefore the simplest formula is also the correct molecular formula. 

Finally, we use the method in finding the formula of acetic acid.

156. Summary.-Molecular weights are important because atomic weights and formulas depend upon them. 

Gay-Lussac's law states that the proportions by volume of gases that react with one another and the proportions by volume of the products, if they are also gases, can be expressed by small, whole numbers.

Avogadro's hypothesis is an explanation of Gay-Lussac's law and of the laws of Boyle and Charles. It states that equal volumes of all gases, at the same temperature and pressure, contain the same number of molecules.

Avogadro's hypothesis, together with Gay-Lussac's law, leads to the determination of the number of atoms in the molecules of gaseous elements. Hydrogen, oxygen, nitrogen, and chlorine have 2 atoms each in their molecules. 

By Avogadro's hypothesis we find the molecular weights of gases.

The relative weights of equal volumes of any two gases must also be the relative weights of their molecules. The standard molecular weight is that of oxygen : 32.

The gram-molecular weight of an element or compound is as many grams as there are units in the molecular weight. 

The gram-molecular volume of a gas is the volume occupied by one gram-molecular weight of it. This volume is the same for all gases: 22.4 liters, at standard temperature and pressure.

We determine the gram-molecular weight of a gas by getting the weight of 22.4 liters of it.

Molecular weights of soluble solids and liquids (not acids, bases, or salts) may be obtained by freezing point and boiling point methods.

In a balanced, molecular equation the coefficients used before formulas represent relative volumes of all gases used or formed. 

The equivalent weight of an element is that weight of it which combines with or replaces 1.008 g. of hydrogen, or 8 g. of oxygen, or 35.46 g. of chlorine.

To obtain the atomic weight of an element we take the following steps: (1) We find the gram-molecular weights of a large number of its gaseous compounds by calculating the weight of the gram-molecular volume of each compound. (2) We determine the proportion of the element in each compound by careful analysis. (3) We get the number of grams of the element in the grammolecular weight of each of its gaseous compounds. (4) We select the smallest weight of the element found in the gram-molecular weight of any of its compounds as the gram-atomic weight. 

Steps 1-4 give us the approximate atomic weight. The equivalent weight is sometimes the true atomic weight, as in the case of hydrogen and chlorine, but in other cases the equivalent weight must be multiplied by a small, whole number (the valence) to give the true atomic weight. 

We obtain the simplest formula of a compound by using the percentage of the elements present and the atomic weights. 

We obtain the molecular formula of a compound by multiplying the simplest formula by a whole number, if necessary, to change the weight represented by the simplest formula to the grammolecular weight.

158. Halogens.-Halogen, meaning "salt producer," is a general name for a group, or "family," of four elements: fluorine, chlorine, bromine, and iodine. We have already prepared chlorine (§ 127). The other elements resemble 

FIG. 94. 

it in chemical behavior. 

All are active non-metals which unite with hydrogen to give compounds resembling hydrogen chloride. The compounds of the other halogens with metals (halides) resemble the corresponding chlorides. 

159. Fluorine.-Fluorine is the most active of the elements, and its compounds, the fluorides, are very stable. The Henri Moissan (1852-1907). First to obtain principal fluoride is calfluorine. Used the electric furnace to prepare rare metals and artificial diamonds. cium fluoride, or fluorspar (CaF2). Cryolite is a double fluoride: AlF3.3NaF, or NaзAIF6. Because its compounds are so stable and fluorine itself is so active, the element is difficult to prepare. The method 204

of Moissan (1886) was to electrolyze a solution of potassium fluoride (KF) in anhydrous, liquid hydrogen fluoride. H2F2→H2 ↑ +F2 ↑ · Glass apparatus cannot be used, because hydrogen fluoride reacts with it. Moissan used platinum and copper apparatus, with windows of transparent calcium fluoride.

In a more recent method melted potassium hydrogen fluoride (KHF2) is electrolyzed, and much larger amounts of fluorine are obtained.

Fluorine is a yellow gas that unites with hydrogen with great violence, even in the dark. It unites with metals, forming fluorides. It also reacts with water, liberating oxygen and ozone (§ 31). 

With silicon it forms the fluoride, SiF4, a gas. Some fluorides, such as NaF, are poisonous enough to be used in insect powders. 

160. Hydrogen fluoride.-Hydrogen fluoride is a colorless gas which fumes in 

FLUORINE FIG. 95. 

Preparation of fluorine by the electrolysis of melted potassium hydrogen fluoride. moist air, like hydrogen chloride. We prepare it from a fluoride and concentrated sulphuric acid.

CaF2+H2SO4→CaSO4+H2F2 ↑. 

Its formula is H₂F₂ under ordinary conditions, but HF at higher temperatures. 

The water solution of hydrogen fluoride is hydrofluoric acid. This reacts with active metals, liberating hydrogen. It acts very slowly with copper, lead, and

platinum. The solution of H2F2 attacks the skin, forming painful sores. The gas which the solution gives off (hydrogen fluoride) is also dangerous, and must not be inhaled. Both hydrogen fluoride and its solution react with sand, or quartz (SiO2). SiO2+2H2F2→→→SiF4 ↑ +2H2O. 

Hydrofluoric acid is kept in bottles of wax or lead. It is used to etch designs upon glass. Glass is a mixture of the silicates of metals, especially those of sodium and calcium. Hydrofluoric acid acts upon calcium silicate as it would upon a mixture of calcium oxide (CaO) and silicon dioxide (SiO2): CaSiO3+3H2F2-CaF2+SiF4 ↑ +3H₂O. 

With the sodium silicate of glass the reaction is:

Na2SiO3+3H2F22NaF+SiF4 ↑ +3H2O. 

We prepare glass for etching by covering it with a thin layer of paraffin or wax and by removing the coating from the part to J.H.2.A

Then we wash off the excess of hydrofluoric acid and remove the paraffin or wax coating. 

161. Bromine.-Bromine, like fluorine and chlorine, is an active element not found uncombined in nature. 

Bromides occur in sea water and in salt deposits. They are obtained from many salt brines after the salt has crystallized out. The most common bromides are those of sodium, potassium, and magnesium.

We prepare bromine from a bromide much as we prepare chlorine from a chloride; that is, we warm a mixture of the bromide, manganese dioxide, and slightly diluted sulphuric acid. Hydrogen bromide is first formed, and is then oxidized by the manganese dioxide. (1) NaBr+H,SO,NaHSO4+HBr. (2) MnO2+4HBr→MnBr₂+2H2O+Bг₂ ↑ . 

The complete equation is: 

2NaBr+MnO2+3H2SO4→2NaHSO4+MnSO4+2H2O+Br2 ↑ · On a commercial scale chlorine is often used to displace bromine.

MgBr₂+Cl2 MgCl2+Br2 ↑. 

162. Properties and uses. While fluorine and chlorine are gases at ordinary temperatures, bromine is a liquid about 3.2 times as dense as water. The liquid is dark red; its vapor is reddish brown. 

The vapor has a very disagreeable odor and affects the eyes and throat.

Ammonia may be inhaled to counteract it. The liquid should not touch the skin, for it produces sores that are hard to heal. A paste of sodium bicarbonate (baking soda) with water or a vegetable oil is used to treat bromine "burns." Bromine dissolves in water, chloroform, and other solvents. The solutions are orange red. The water solution is called bromine water. 

Bromine, like chlorine, unites with hydrogen and with metals, but is less active. It reacts with water, forming 

FIG. 97. 

Bromine is being prepared in the flask, and its vapor is being condensed under water.

hydrogen bromide and oxygen, especially if a substance is present that can be oxidized. Bromine water is therefore a bleaching agent and an oxidizing agent, like chlorine water.

Bromine is used in organic dyes such as eosin. Silver bromide, like silver chloride, is affected by light, and is used in photography. Sodium and potassium bromides are used as medicines. 

163. Hydrogen bromide (HBr).—Hydrogen bromide, like hydrogen chloride, is a colorless gas that fumes in moist air. Its solution in water is hydrobromic acid. 

When concentrated sulphuric acid is added to a bromide, hydrogen bromide is formed.

KBr+H2SO4→KHSO4+HBr ↑ . 

Hydrogen bromide is, however, less stable than hydrogen chloride, and is oxidized by some of the sulphuric acid. Thus bromine is set free, and sulphur dioxide is formed. 2HBr+H2SO4→2H2O+Br₂+SO2. 

We prepare pure hydrogen bromide by adding bromine to a mixture of red phosphorus and water. Phosphorus tribromide is first formed. 2P+3Br2-2PBг3. PBr3+3HOH→H3PO3+3HBr ↑ . 

164. Iodine.-Iodine was first obtained from the ashes of certain sea plants. The present source is the nitrate deposits of Chile, in which the iodine is found chiefly as sodium iodate, NaIO3. 

We prepare iodine by heating an iodide with sulphuric acid and manganese dioxide: 2KI+MnO2+ 3H2SO4-2KHSO4 + MnSO4+2H₂O+I2 ↑ ·

Hydrogen iodide (HI) is first formed, and is then oxidized by the manganese dioxide, giving water and iodine.

We can also displace iodine by means of chlorine or bromine. 2KI+C12→2KC1+I2. 

165. Properties and uses. Unlike the other halogens, iodine is a solid under ordinary conditions. It is dark gray and crystalline. When heated, it first melts and then vaporizes. Its vapor has a violet color and an irritating odor. We can sublime iodine, that is, we can change the solid, by heat, into the vapor and then condense the vapor, upon a cold surface, to the solid form.

The In

Iodine is being liberated at the bottom of the beaker and condensed on the test tube containing cold water.

Iodine dissolves readily in alcohol and in ether, but only slightly in water. alcohol solution is tincture of iodine. a solution of potassium iodide iodine is much more soluble than in water. All these solutions are brown. The solution of iodine in chloroform, carbon tetrachloride, or carbon disulphide is violet or purple. 

Iodine gives a deep blue color to a dilute "solution" (suspension) of starch paste. Iodine stains the skin brown. It reacts with metals to form iodides. The reaction between hydrogen and iodine is much more slow than in the case of the other halogens. 

Iodine is used in medicine and surgery. an important antiseptic containing iodine.

Tincture of iodine is used to reduce swellings and as an antiseptic.

Some iodine is necessary in our food if we are to develop properly and maintain health. Iodine is also used in certain dyestuffs and in photography. 

166. Hydrogen iodide (HI).-Hydrogen iodide, like hydrogen fluoride, chloride, and bromide, fumes in moist air. Its solution, hydriodic acid, is a strong acid. This reacts with active metals to produce hydrogen and the iodides of the metals. It also neutralizes strong bases forming iodides and water. 

Hydrogen iodide is even less stable than hydrogen bromide. 

It cannot be prepared in a pure condition from an iodide and sulphuric acid, because the hydrogen iodide is oxidized by the sulphuric acid, forming water and iodine.

We can prepare pure hydrogen iodide by mixing red phosphorus with powdered iodine and adding water, drop by drop, to the mixture. Phosphorus tri-iodide is first formed. 2P+312-2PI3. PI3+3HOH→H3PO3+3HI ↑ . 

167. Tests for the halogens.—If we had a water solution of bromine or iodine, we could determine which was present by a simple test. When the solution is shaken up in a test tube with some chloroform, the bromine or iodine is taken up by the chloroform and gives it a characteristic color. Bromine colors it orange brown, while iodine colors it violet. Carbon disulphide or carbon tetrachloride may be used instead of chloroform. 

A bromide or iodide does not color chloroform; the halogen must be uncombined. If to a bromide or iodide we add a little chlorine water, and then shake up the solution with chloroform, we get the characteristic color test of bromine or iodine.

A solution of iodine gives a characteristic blue color with dilute starch paste. An iodide does not give this color until its iodine has been set free.

We can distinguish, by one test, between a chloride, a bromide, and an iodide. If we heat any one of them with manganese dioxide and dilute sulphuric acid, it will give off the halogen itself. This can be identified by its color in the gaseous state. 

All the halogens, except fluorine, form insoluble silver salts. 

168. Halogens as a family.—The four halogens form a natural group, as is plain when we tabulate the properties of the elements in the order of the atomic weights:

Other properties besides those given in the table follow the order of the atomic weights. Perhaps the most striking one is the order in which the halogens displace one another from their compounds with metals and hydrogen. As we have learned, fluorine displaces the other three, chlorine all but fluorine, and bromine displaces iodine.

The column headed: "Heat of formation of hydrogen compound," needs explanation. The heat of formation of a compound is the number of calories absorbed or given off when a grammolecular weight of the compound is formed from its elements.

The thermo-chemical equation for the formation of a grammolecular weight of hydrogen chloride is: 

H+C1→HC1+22,000 cal.

The equation means that during the formation of 36.5 g. of hydrogen chloride from 1 g. hydrogen and 35.5 g. chlorine, 22,000 calories are given off. Hydrogen chloride has a large positive heat of formation, but hydrogen fluoride has a larger one: 37,600 calories. 

Both of these compounds are very stable. 

The thermo-chemical equation for the formation of hydrogen iodide is different.

H+I→HI-6,100 cal. 

The equation shows that in the formation of a gram-molecular weight (128 g.) of hydrogen iodide, 6,100 calories are absorbed.

Hydrogen iodide has a negative heat of formation, and is the most unstable compound of the series. 

169. Summary.-The halogens are fluorine, chlorine, bromine, and iodine. They are active non-metals. 

Fluorine is the most active element. It is formed by the electrolysis of hydrogen fluoride or potassium hydrogen fluoride. 

It reacts with metals, hydrogen, water, and silicon. 

Hydrogen fluoride in solution is hydrofluoric acid. It acts with metals, liberating hydrogen. Its action in etching glass is due to the formation of fluorides of the metals and silicon tetrafluoride (a gas).

We test for a fluoride by producing hydrogen fluoride and testing its power to etch glass.

Bromine is a heavy liquid which gives off a heavy, brown vapor. Its common compounds are bromides. In chemical properties bromine resembles chlorine, but is less active. We test for a bromide by liberating the element bromine, as a gas or in solution, and then identifying it as bromine. 

Hydrogen bromide resembles hydrogen chloride, but is less stable. It is partly oxidized by concentrated sulphuric acid to free bromine and water. 

Iodine is a heavy, crystalline, dark solid which gives off a violet vapor when warmed.

Chlorine, bromine, or iodine can be prepared from a chloride, bromide, or iodide, respectively, by heating it with manganese dioxide and sulphuric acid. We test for iodine in an iodide by setting the iodine free as a vapor or in solution, and then identifying it as iodine.

Hydrogen iodide resembles hydrogen bromide, but is still less stable. It is almost completely oxidized by concentrated sulphuric acid, forming iodine and water.

The other hydrogen compounds of the halogens have a positive heat of formation, but that of hydrogen iodide is negative. 

The halogens form a natural family, in which the properties of the elements and their compounds change in the order of the atomic weights of the elements.

171. How sulphur is found and extracted.-Sulphur has been known since very early times. The chief deposits in Europe are in Sicily; the chief deposits being worked in this country are in Texas. Sulphur compounds are much more widely distributed than free, or native, sulphur. Iron pyrite, or fool's gold, has the formula, FeS2. Galena is lead sulphide, PbS. Zinc blende is ZnS. 

Gypsum is the hydrate of calcium sulphate, CaSO4.2H2O. 

LIQUID SULPHUR SAND CLAY LOOSE ROCK CALCIUM-

CALCIUM CARBONATE CONTAINING SULPHUR CALCIUM SULPHATE SKY OD ROCK SALT 

The problem of extracting the sulphur from the deposits on the Gulf Coast was worked out by Frasch, an American chemist. His method is to drill a hole (shaft) about a foot in diameter down to the bottom of the sulphur beds. A large pipe, or casing, lines the hole for the entire distance, perhaps 700 feet.

Inside the casing are three other pipes, one within the other; these are 6 inches, 3 inches, and 1 inch, respectively, in diameter. The 6-inch pipe carries down water, at about 170° 

C. and under pressure, to melt the sulphur. pipe-1 inch in diameter-carries down hot,

FIG. 99. 

How sulphur occurs in the rocks from which it is extracted by the Frasch process. 

The innermost compressed air. 215

The air and water mix with the melted sulphur and form a frothy mixture which rises to the surface in the 3-inch pipe. 

The mixture of sulphur, air, and water is pumped to huge wooden bins, 150 by 250 ft., in which the sulphur solidifies. When the bin is removed, a large block of sulphur remains. This may weigh 160,000 tons. When it is to be shipped, the sulphur block is blasted to smaller fragments and loaded upon cars. The sulphur obtained is remarkably free from impurities (99 to 99.5% pure).

Flowers of sulphur is a flour-like variety formed when sulphur vapor is chilled in a cold condensing chamber. 

Roll brimstone is formed when melted sulphur is allowed to solidify in a long, cylindrical mold. We commonly see sulphur in the laboratory as either "roll sulphur" or "flowers of sulphur." 

172. Physical properties of sulphur. -There are several different forms of sulphur, and the list of physical properties of one of them will not do for the others. Ordinary sulphur is roll sulphur, or brimstone. This consists of crystals of the rhombic variety. It is a yellow, odorless, and tasteless solid, insoluble in water, easily soluble in carbon disulphide (CS2). Its density is 2.06 grams per cu. cm. When a carbon disulphide solution of sulphur is allowed to evaporate, crystals of rhombic sulphur are obtained.

When rhombic sulphur is heated, it melts, at 114.5° C., to a yellow, easily poured liquid. Above 160° C. it becomes dark and thick (viscous). At about 230° it can

hardly be poured at all. When heated further, it becomes less viscous, and at 445° it boils, producing a light-brown vapor.

Natural sulphur is of the rhombic form; all other varieties finally change into the rhombic form. 

FIG. 101. 

Courtesy The Texas Gulf Sulphur Co. 

A large block of sulphur ready for shipment. 

173. Other forms of sulphur.-Three other varieties of sulphur are: (1) prismatic, or needle-shaped sulphur; (2) amorphous or plastic sulphur; and (3) milk of sulphur.

Prismatic sulphur is formed by the slow cooling of melted sulphur at as low a temperature as possible. To obtain it we melt sulphur in a deep dish, such as a crucible, and let it cool until a crust forms on the surface. The liquid inside will contain needle-shaped crystals. We can obtain these by breaking a hole through the crust and pouring out the sulphur that still remains liquid. 

The needle-shaped crystals are almost colorless, melt at 119° C., and dissolve, like the rhombic variety, in carbon disulphide. But when the carbon disulphide evapo-

rates, rhombic crystals remain. The density of prismatic sulphur is 1.96.

If the prismatic crystals are kept below 96° C., they AA 

FIG. 102. 

Needle-shaped crystals of sulphur (prismatic). change into the rhombic form, giving off heat as they do so. When rhombic sulphur is kept above 96° C. it changes to the prismatic variety. The prismatic variety is the stable form between 96° and 114° C. 

Amorphous, or plastic sulphur is formed when sulphur near its boiling point is suddenly chilled, as is the case when we pour it into cold water. 

As first obtained it is an elastic, gummy substance. After a while this becomes hard. It is now a mixture of amorphous sulphur, which is insoluble in carbon disulphide, and rhombic sulphur, which is soluble. The sudden chilling does not give the liquid sulphur time to change into ordinary, rhombic sulphur. "Flowers of sulphur," obtained by chilling the vapor of sulphur, is a mixture of rhombic and amorphous sulphur. When the amorphous forms of sulphur change into rhombic sulphur, heat is given off.

Milk of sulphur is a variety of amorphous sulphur suspended in water. To obtain it we first allow sulphur to react with an alkali, such as sodium hydroxide solution. The solution then contains sodium polysulphide, Na2Sx. When this is treated with an acid, sulphur is precipitated in a very finely divided form, which settles out only very slowly. 

174. Allotropic forms.-The different physical forms which the same element may assume are called allotropic (ǎl'ō-trop'ik) forms of the element. Sulphur is by no means the only element to appear in different varieties.

Oxygen and ozone are allotropic forms. The cause in this case is the different number of atoms in the two molecules, oxygen being O2 and ozone, O3. Carbon exists as charcoal, as graphite (the “black lead" of pencils), and as diamond. Phosphorus has a white (unstable) and a red (stable) form. Usually, when an unstable form of an element changes into the stable form, energy is given off. 

175. Chemical properties of sulphur.-Sulphur must be classed as an active element, especially at higher temperatures. It reacts with the oxygen of the air (burns), giving sulphur dioxide, SO2.

With metals it forms sulphides. Thus, iron and sulphur react vigorously when heated, giving off heat and light (combustion). The product is ferrous sulphide, FeS. Mercury and sulphur unite when rubbed together in a mortar. 

Copper foil burns vigorously in sulphur vapor. A mixture of zinc dust and powdered sulphur burns with a flash. when set on fire. The operation should be carried out in the open, on a piece of asbestos.

FIG. 103. 

Burning copper in sulphur vapor.

We see the action of sulphur upon metals in the blackening of silver egg-spoons. Silver sulphide (Ag₂S) is black. It is formed from the silver of the spoon and sulphur compounds of the egg. Mustard also contains sulphur compounds. The illuminating or fuel gas used in our houses often contains small amounts of hydrogen sulphide. This reacts with such metals as copper, bronze (a copper alloy), lead, and silver, and tarnishes them. 

176. Uses. Sulphur or one of its compounds is used

in hardening, or vulcanizing, rubber. The process was discovered by Charles Goodyear, a New Englander, in 1839. Ebonite is a very hard rubber. Black gunpowder contains sulphur, charcoal, and potassium nitrate (niter). 

The older forms of matches and fireworks contained sulphur. Modern matches contain phosphorus sulphide (§ 253). Sulphur is used in the preparation of sulphur CARBON

DISULPHIDE CAR BON SULPHUR 

CARBON

SULPHURI LIQUID SULPHUR FIG. 104. 

SULPHUR

SULPHUR 

Commercial preparation of carbon disulphide. dioxide, which is a bleaching and disinfecting agent, as well as a source of sulphuric acid. Carbon disulphide is made on an extensive scale from carbon and sulphur. Lime-sulphur mixtures are used by fruit growers for spraying trees and shrubs, in order to destroy injurious insects and fungi. 

177. Carbon disulphide.-The method of preparing carbon disulphide is to heat a mixture of hot charcoal and sulphur vapor in an electric furnace (see Fig. 104). The resistance which the current meets causes its energy to be changed into heat. The carbon disulphide passes off as a vapor. This is led through condensers, which cool it to the liquid state.

Pure carbon disulphide is colorless and has an agreeable odor; but the commercial product is usually impure and has a yellow color and an offensive odor. The liquid boils at 46° C., has a density of 1.27 g. per cu. cm., and is insoluble in water. It burns very readily to form carbon dioxide and sulphur dioxide. What is the equation?

Carbon disulphide is used as a source of carbon tetrachloride (§ 275) and as a solvent for sulphur and for rubber. It is also used to destroy ants, rats, and mice. It is employed, with sodium

hydroxide, to convert cellulose into viscose, a material out of which rayon, cellophane, and other cellulose products are made. 

178. Hydrogen sulphide.-Hydrogen sulphide (H2S) is a colorless gas having the odor of rotten eggs. It is formed in the decay of animal substances containing sulphur. "Sulphur springs" and some volcanic gases contain it. Hydrogen sulphide is produced when we pass hydrogen over melted sulphur. It is, however, so unstable that some of it is decomposed as soon as formed. We write the equation with a double arrow to show that the reaction is reversible (§ 243).

H₂+SH₂S. 

In the laboratory we prepare hydrogen sulphide from a sulphide, which furnishes the sulphur, and an acid, which furnishes the hydrogen. Ferrous sulphide and dilute hydrochloric or sulphuric acid are most convenient.

FeS+2HC1→FeCl2+H2S ↑. 

FIG. 105. 

Preparation of hydrogen sulphide in the laboratory. 

A Kipp generator (§ 36) may be used. All experiments with hydrogen sulphide should be carried out in a ventilated fume chamber or near a window with an outgoing draft. The gas is somewhat poisonous and may produce headache and nausea. 

179. Properties. With an abundant supply of air hydrogen sulphide burns readily. Each of its elements is completely oxidized in the process. 2H2S+302-2H2O+2SO2. 

When the supply of oxygen is limited, as when we set

fire to a bottle of the gas, much sulphur remains unburned.

2H2S+O₂→2H₂O+2S ↓ . 

Under standard conditions about 3 volumes of hydrogen sulphide dissolve in one volume of water. When the air has access to it, the solution soon loses its odor, and sulphur is deposited. What is the equation? The sulphur is displaced by the oxygen of the air. Chlorine, bromine, and even iodine likewise displace the sulphur. H2S+I2-2HI+S↓· The solution of hydrogen sulphide is hydrosulphuric acid. This is a weak acid. Its salts are the sulphides of the metals. 2NaOH+H2S-Na2S+2H₂O. 

Both hydrogen sulphide and its solution react with metals. 

Pb+H2S PbS+H2. 

Because hydrogen sulphide breaks up so easily into hydrogen and sulphur, it is a powerful reducing, or deoxidizing agent (§ 40). 

Thus when sulphur dioxide and hydrogen sulphide are brought together, they react, producing water and sulphur. SO2+2H2S→2H₂O+3S ↓ . 

The sulphur dioxide is the oxidizing agent, and the hydrogen sulphide, that is, its hydrogen, is the reducing agent. Hydrogen sulphide easily reduces hydrogen peroxide to water. Write the equation.

The common test for hydrogen sulphide is: (1) its odor, and (2) its blackening of a strip of filter paper which is wet with lead acetate solution. Lead sulphide is formed.

Pb (C2H3O2)2+H₂S→PbS ↓ +2HC2H3O2. 

180. How sulphides are formed and used. We have already learned several ways in which sulphides are formed:

(1) By direct union of metals with sulphur; (2) By displacement of the hydrogen of hydrogen sulphide by some metal; (3) By the reaction (double decomposition) between hydrogen sulphide and a base. Write an equation illustrating each of these. (4) A fourth method of preparing a sulphide is to reduce a sulphate.

Na2SO4+2C-Na2S+2CO2 ↑ · (5) A fifth method is illustrated by the action of hydrogen sulphide and lead acetate (§ 179). Other examples are: (1) CuSO4+H₂S→CuS ↓ +H2SO4. (2) 2AgNO3+H2S→Ag₂S ↓ +2HNO3. (3) FeCl2+H2S→FeS+2HC1. (4) FeCl2+(NH4)2S→FeS | +2NHẠC.. (5) 2NaC1+H₂S⇒Na₂S+2HC1. 

In cases (1), (2), and (3) an acid must be formed, as well as the sulphide of the metal. In cases (1) and (2) the sulphide is precipitated, because it is very insoluble, and therefore does not react with the acid produced at the same time. In case (3) very little, if any, FeS is precipitated. This sulphide is not so insoluble as CuS and Ag₂S, and the part of it in solution reacts with the hydrochloric acid produced. This reaction is, in fact, the reverse of the one by which hydrogen sulphide is prepared (§ 178). To precipitate sulphides like FeS we use, not hydrogen sulphide, but a soluble, metal sulphide, such as (NH4)2S. In this case no acid is formed. In case (5) no precipitate is obtained, because Na2S is a very soluble salt.

The facts just given illustrate how a mixture of metals is separated in that branch of chemistry called Qualitative Analysis. If we have a mixture of the salts of several metals, such as copper, iron, and sodium, in solution, and add to the solution a little HCI and then H2S, only the very insoluble cupric sulphide (CuS) is

precipitated. We can filter this off. To the remainder of the solution, containing now the iron salt and the sodium salt, we add ammonium hydroxide (to neutralize the acid) and then ammonium sulphide. The moderately insoluble ferrous sulphide * (FeS) is then precipitated. The very soluble sodium sulphide (Na2S) remains in solution. 

Not all sulphides are black. Arsenic sulphide (As2S3) is yellow; so is cadmium sulphide (CdS). Zinc sulphide (ZnS) is white. 

Antimony sulphide (Sb2S3) is orange red. 

181. Sulphur dioxide (SO2).—The two common oxides of sulphur are the dioxide and the trioxide (SO3). When sulphur is burned in moist oxygen (§ 22), sulphur dioxide is the chief product. However, it contains traces of sulphuric acid in the form of a white mist. This comes from the union of the trioxide with water. Sulphur dioxide is also formed when the sulphide ores of various metals are heated strongly in air (roasted). 2ZnS+302-2ZnO+2SO₂ ↑. 

Three methods of preparing sulphur dioxide are: (1) Burning of sulphur, including the roasting of sulphides. (2) Double decomposition between sulphites and acids.

Sulphurous acid is first formed, and then decomposed. Na2SO3+2HC1→2NaC1+H2SO3(SO2 ↑ +H2O). NaHSO3+HC1→NaC1+H2SO3(SO2 ↑ +H2O). 

This is the most convenient laboratory method. (3) Reduction of concentrated sulphuric acid.—When concentrated sulphuric is heated, it shows a tendency to break down into water, sulphur dioxide, and oxygen. If a substance like copper, lead, sulphur, or carbon (char-

coal) is present, it is oxidized, and the sulphuric acid is thereby reduced to sulphur dioxide. 2H2SO4+C→2H₂O+2SO₂ ↑ +CO₂ ↑. 2H2SO4+Cu→2H2O+SO2 ↑ +CuSO4. 

In the second case cupric oxide (CuO) may first be formed; if so, it reacts, by double decomposition, with more of the sulphuric acid, giving cupric sulphate and water.

H₂SO SODIUM SULPHITE SULPHUR DIOXIDE FIG. 106. 

Preparation of sulphur dioxide by the double decomposition between sulphite and a dilute acid. 

182. Properties.-Sulphur dioxide is a colorless gas, of sharp odor. Calculate the weight of 22.4 liters of it under standard conditions, and compare it with air. At ordinary temperatures about 50 volumes of it dissolve in one volume of water. The solution contains sulphurous acid, H₂SO3. 

Write the equation. Sulphur dioxide is the anhydride of sulphurous acid. 

SULPHUR DIOXIDE CONC H₂S FIG. 107. 

FREEZING MIXITURE SODIUM HYDROXIDE SOL 

Liquefaction of sulphur dioxide in a U-tube surrounded by a freezing mixture. 

Gaseous sulphur dioxide is liquefied when it is passed through a U-tube cooled by ice and salt. Liquid sulphur dioxide is sold in strong, glass bottles or steel cylinders. It boils at -8°C. under ordinary pressure.

Sulphur dioxide does not burn or support combustion. Why?

The uses of sulphur dioxide are given chiefly under

sulphurous acid. The liquefied substance is used in modern refrigerators as liquid ammonia is (§ 224). When the liquid is made to boil rapidly, a great deal of heat is taken up. Thus coils of brine may be cooled and used to freeze water or to lower the temperature of a refrigerator. 

More sulphur dioxide is used to prepare sulphuric acid than for any other purpose. 

183. Sulphurous acid.-Anhydrous sulphur dioxide is not very active, but in the presence of moisture, or in solution, it is a reducing agent, a bleaching agent, and an antiseptic. The active substance is really sulphurous acid, H2SO3.

Sulphurous acid is slowly oxidized (in the air) to sulphuric acid. 2H2SO3+02→2H2SO4. 

An oxidizing agent, such as chlorine water or bromine water, carries out the oxidation rapidly. Cl₂+H2O-HC1+HOC1. H2SO3+HOC1→H₂SO4+HCl. 

The complete equation is:

H₂SO3+Cl2+H2O¬H2SO4+2HC1. 

A solution of potassium permanganate (KMnO4), which is purple, is instantly reduced to colorless compounds by sulphurous acid.

As a bleaching agent sulphurous acid is less active than chlorine; nevertheless it is very valuable. It is used to bleach silk, wool, and straw, which are injured by chlorine.

An interesting experiment is to bleach a red carnation by means of it. The sulphurous acid probably acts upon a natural dye, such as that of yellow straw, by combin-

ing directly with it to form colorless compounds. Sometimes the bleaching action may be a reducing action. 

The bleaching effect of sulphurous acid is not permanent, and the natural color returns in time. White straw hats become yellow. Dilute sulphuric acid restores the natural color to many materials bleached by sulphurous acid. 

Dried fruits, canned corn and cherries, and molasses are often bleached by sulphurous acid.

Sulphurous acid is used to destroy insects and to prevent the growth of molds, yeast, and bacteria in preserved foods. In its presence fermentation and decay cease or are made very slow. 

184. Sulphites.-When sulphurous acid reacts with a base, a sulphite or a bisulphite is produced. We usually pass the gaseous sulphur dioxide directly into the solution of the base as long as it is absorbed. With sodium hydroxide as the base we obtain sodium bisulphite, NaHSO3. This is also called sodium hydrogen sulphite, or acid sodium sulphite.

NaOH+H2SO3→NaHSO3+H₂O. 

If we add to the solution of the bisulphite as much sodium hydroxide as we used in preparing it, we obtain the normal sulphite. NaOH+NaHSO;→Na2SO3+H₂O. 

These two sulphites have the same relation to each other as sodium hydrogen sulphate and normal sodium sulphate (§ 137).

Sodium sulphite is oxidized by chlorine water as sulphurous acid is; sodium sulphate is produced (§ 183). Write the equation. 

Sodium sulphite is used as an "antichlor" (§ 134).

Calcium bisulphite, Ca(HSO3)2, is used in the manufacture of paper from wood. Wood contains an undesirable substance called lignin, mixed with the cellulose of which paper consists. It also needs to be bleached. Calcium bisulphite removes the lignin, and leaves the white cellulose.

The chemical test for either a sulphite or a bisulphite is to treat it with a dilute acid, such as hydrochloric or sulphuric acid, and then to identify it by the odor of the sulphur dioxide that is given off (182).

NaHSO3+HC1→NaC1+H2O+SO₂ ↑ . 

185. Sulphur trioxide (SO3).—The successful commercial preparation of sulphur trioxide from sulphur dioxide OXY- GEN

SULPHUR DIOXIDE CATALYST FIG. 108. 

SULPHUR TRIOXIDE 

Small-scale apparatus for preparing sulphur trioxide by the contact process. and air was one of the great triumphs of chemistry, for it is the foundation of the contact process of preparing sulphuric acid. A small amount of sulphur trioxide is formed even when sulphur burns in air. The reaction between sulphur dioxide and oxygen (air) is, however, a slow one, and is always incomplete; because, at the temperature at which the combination takes place, sulphur trioxide also decomposes to form sulphur dioxide and oxygen. 2SO2+022SO3.

The combination of sulphur dioxide and oxygen proceeds rapidly and almost to completion if finely divided platinum (platinized asbestos) is used as a catalyst, and if the temperature is kept at about 360° C. (see figure).

We prepare platinized asbestos by soaking asbestos in a solution of chlorplatinic acid, and then heating it. 

Very finely divided platinum is left throughout the asbestos.

H2PtCl Pt+2C12 ↑ +2HC1 ↑ . Sulphur trioxide is a crystalline solid below 15° C., its melting point. It boils at 46° C. A trace of water (catalyst) causes it to change to an allotropic form, which is a mass of threadlike crystals. Sulphur trioxide is the anhydride of sulphuric acid. It hisses violently when dropped into water, and the reaction gives off much heat. H₂O+SO:→H₂SO4. 

Sulphur trioxide is readily absorbed by concentrated sulphuric acid; the product is fuming sulphuric acid, or pyrosulphuric acid.

H2SO4+SO3→H2S2O7.

The contact process of preparing sulphuric acid. manufactured in enormous quantities for the use of the world's industries. Hydrochloric acid is prepared from chlorides, and nitric acid from nitrates, but sulphuric

acid is not prepared from sulphates. It must be synthesized from sulphur dioxide, oxygen, and water. There are two processes for bringing about the combination: (1) the contact process, and (2) the chamber process. (1) Contact process. In the contact process the uniting of sulphur dioxide and oxygen is hastened by the use of a contact agent, or catalyst,

Small-scale apparatus for preparing sulphuric acid by the chamber process. concentrated sul- phuric acid (98% 

H2SO4). First sulphur trioxide is run into the acid, and then enough water is added to convert acid. By this the absorbed trioxide into sulphuric process anhydrous, pure sulphuric acid can be prepared. Cheaper catalysts, such as ferric oxide, may be used. The mixture of sulphur dioxide and oxygen must be carefully purified, or the catalyst will cease to act. 

The temperature must not be too low, or combination will be slow. The temperature must not be too high, or the sulphur trioxide will be decomposed into the dioxide and oxygen (§ 185). (2) Chamber process. The chamber process consists in oxidizing sulphur dioxide in the presence of an excess of water. Dilute sulphuric acid is produced. If the con-

centrated acid is wanted, the water must be evaporated. 

The agents used in the chamber process to cause sulphur dioxide and oxygen to unite rapidly are the nitrogen oxides, chiefly nitric oxide, NO (§ 235). This is a colorless gas, but it turns brown instantly when exposed to air, owing to the formation of nitrogen dioxide, NO2. 2NO+02-2NO2.

When the brown gas (NO2) comes in contact with moist sulphur dioxide, it becomes colorless. It is reduced to nitric oxide.

H2O+SO2+NO2→H2SO4+NO. 

Thus nitric oxide (NO) acts as a carrier of oxygen. It takes oxygen from the air and transfers it to the sulphur dioxide.

Chamber process of preparing sulphuric acid. (see Fig. 111). The sulphur dioxide is produced in burners, from iron pyrite (or sulphur) and air. The gases from the burners pass through a tower called the Glover tower, containing lumps of

quartz. In the Glover tower the sulphur dioxide is supplied with nitrogen oxides. The mixed gases then pass through a series of large, leaden chambers (rooms), into which steam is introduced, and in which sulphuric acid is formed and collected. From the last leaden chamber the gases (containing much nitrogen from the air) enter a second tower-the Gay-Lussac tower. This contains lumps of coke, over which concentrated sulphuric is flowing. In the Gay-Lussac tower the nitrogen oxides are taken up by the concentrated sulphuric acid, to prevent their escaping into the air and being lost. The concentrated Gay-Lussac acid, with its charge of nitrogen oxides, is then transferred, by pumps, from the Gay- Lussac tower to the top of the Glover tower. The Gay-Lussac acid is there mixed with some of the dilute acid from the leaden chambers, and the mixture is allowed to trickle down over the quartz lumps of the Glover tower. The Gay-Lussac acid now gives up its nitrogen oxides to fresh portions of sulphur dioxide and air coming from the sulphur burners. At the same time the hot gases from the burners evaporate much of the water of the dilute acid, and produce a more concentrated acid.

The nitrogen oxides are used over and over again, but there is always a little loss. Consequently nitric acid (HNO3) must be produced at intervals in the niter pot (see Fig. 111) and added, in vapor form, to the gases from the burners, to replace the nitrogen oxides that are lost. In many modern plants the nitrogen oxides needed for the chamber process are produced by the catalytic oxidation of ammonia (§ 222).

The sulphuric acid obtained in the leaden chambers is, perhaps, 50 to 60% H2SO4. That which collects at the bottom of the 

Glover tower is about 65 to 70% H2SO4. If more concentrated acid is wanted, more of the water must be evaporated. Pure, highly concentrated sulphuric acid is prepared at present by the contact process. The chamber acid, which is much cheaper, is used for processes in which the very concentrated acid is not needed. 

187. Physical properties. When we speak of the properties of sulphuric acid, we must distinguish carefully

between the concentrated acid, which is hydrogen sulphate, and the solution of hydrogen sulphate, which is sulphuric acid.

Hydrogen sulphate is a thick liquid called, popularly, "oil of vitriol." As obtained by distillation it boils at 

338° C. and contains about 98.5% of hydrogen sulphate and 1.5% water. Its density is 1.854 at 0° C. How much will 10 cu. cm. weigh? 

188. Chemical properties. The chemical properties of sulphuric acid may be grouped under the following heads: (1) Its action as an acid. (2) Its action in preparing other acids from their salts. (3) Its action with water and as a dehydrating agent. (4) Its action as an oxidizing agent. 

Dilute sulphuric acid is a strong acid, like hydrochloric acid. It reacts with active metals and with the oxides of metals, and neutralizes solutions of strong bases like sodium hydroxide (§§ 139 and 184). What products are formed in each case?

Concentrated sulphuric acid reacts with such salts as chlorides and nitrates, producing hydrogen chloride and hydrogen nitrate, respectively. This action is due in part to the fact that hydrogen sulphate has a high boiling point. See, however, the other conditions in § 197.

When we dilute hydrogen sulphate with water, much heat is given off. Sometimes the solution boils. We should always pour the concentrated acid, in a very small stream, into the water; never the water into the acid. If we forget this rule, we may spatter the hot acid upon our clothes or skin or into our eyes. Hydrogen sulphate forms painful burns. The reaction between

hydrogen sulphate and water forms the hydrates:

H2SO4.H2O and H2SO4.2H2O. Name them. 

Because of its great activity toward water, hydrogen sulphate is used as a drying, or dehydrating agent (§ 83). Many gases, such as hydrogen, oxygen, chlorine, hydrogen chloride, and carbon dioxide, are freed from water vapor when they are bubbled through concentrated sulphuric acid. The air of a desiccator may be kept "dry" if a little concentrated sulphuric acid is put into the desiccator (§ 83).

Concentrated sulphuric acid chars organic materials such as paper, cotton, wood, dust, and sugar. These materials contain carbon, hydrogen, and oxygen. The hydrogen sulphate removes the elements of water (hydrogen and oxygen) and leaves the black carbon. Concentrated sulphuric acid often becomes black from the dust which has fallen into it and been carbonized. 

Concentrated sulphuric acid also acts as an oxidizing agent (§§ 163, 166, and 181). It is thereby reduced to sulphurous acid (H2SO3), which by dehydration gives sulphur dioxide. In some cases the sulphuric acid is reduced even to hydrogen sulphide. Metals like lead, copper, silver, and mercury, which have little or no action with dilute sulphuric acid, react vigorously with the hot, concentrated acid. Non-metals, like carbon and sulphur, do the same. These all act as reducing agents and remove oxygen from the hydrogen sulphate. 

189. Uses. Many uses of sulphuric acid have already been named. The concentrated acid is a drying agent for gases on a commercial as well as on a laboratory scale. 

It is used to remove water from "the sphere of action" in many reactions in which water is one of the products. 

Nitroglycerine, nitrobenzene, and artificial oil of wintergreen are prepared by such reactions. It is also used to remove objectionable substances from petroleum products

like kerosene and gasoline. Sulphuric acid enables us to prepare acids like hydrochloric and nitric acids. It is used in one of the processes for preparing soda from salt (§ 336).

By its action upon insoluble calcium phosphate sulphuric acid produces soluble phosphates for soil fertilizers 

(§ 256). 

The diluted acid is used in electroplating and in the storage batteries of automobiles. When the "gravity" (really the density) of the battery solution is high, say at 1.3, (usually called "1300") the battery is highly charged, and there is a good deal of sulphuric acid in it. 

When the "gravity" is low, the sulphuric acid has largely reacted with the other materials of the battery, leaving chiefly water as the battery liquid. Such a battery may freeze in cold weather. 

The dilute acid is also used in the "pickling" of metals. It removes surface rust, so that the metal may be coated with other metals like tin or zinc. 

FIG. 112. 

Storage battery. 

190. Sulphates and thiosulphates. Most sulphates are soluble. The test for a sulphate radical, in any soluble sulphate, is to add a little dilute hydrochloric acid and then a solution of barium chloride. The result is a white precipitate of barium sulphate. BaCl2+Na2SO4 BaSO4 ↓ +2HC1. 

The hydrochloric acid prevents any other common, in-

soluble barium salt from being precipitated. Lead and strontium also form insoluble sulphates. Calcium sulphate dissolves to a small extent. 

The hydrates of cupric sulphate, zinc sulphate, and ferrous sulphate are called blue, white, and green vitriols, respectively. 

Many sulphates are unstable when heated, and break down to form the oxide of the metal, and sulphur trioxide (or sulphur dioxide and oxygen):

CuSO4 CuO+SO3 Al2(SO4)3→Al2O3+3SO3 ↑ · 

Sulphates are easily reduced to sulphides by hot carbon (§ 180).

When sodium sulphite (Na2SO3) unites with oxygen, sodium sulphate (Na2SO4) is formed. When it unites with sulphur, the product is sodium thiosulphate, Na2S2O3. This is known as hypo. 

It is used to dissolve silver chloride and silver bromide in the "fixing" of negatives in photography. 

191. Summary.-Sulphur occurs in both the free and the combined state. The most important North American deposits are in 

Texas and Louisiana.

The physical properties of sulphur are different for its different allotropic forms. 

Ordinary sulphur consists of crystals of the rhombic form. 

Other forms are: (1) needle-shaped (prismatic); (2) amorphous, or plastic sulphur; (3) milk of sulphur.

Sulphur is an active element, especially at higher temperatures. 

Sulphur is used in vulcanizing rubber, as an ingredient of gunpowder, in lime-sulphur sprays, and as a source of sulphur compounds, such as sulphur dioxide, sulphuric acid, carbon disulphide, and hypo.

Hydrogen sulphide is formed in the decay of organic substances containing sulphur. In the laboratory we prepare it from ferrous sulphide and a dilute acid. Its solution is hydrosulphuric acid. 

Hydrogen sulphide is used chiefly as a source of the sulphide radical in chemical analysis. 

Metal sulphides are: (1) very insoluble; (2) moderately insoluble; or (3) soluble.

Sulphur dioxide is formed by: (1) the oxidation of sulphur and sulphides; (2) double decomposition between sulphites and dilute acids; and (3) reduction of concentrated sulphuric acid. 

Sulphur dioxide is the anhydride of sulphurous acid. 

Sulphur dioxide is used as a refrigerant, antiseptic and insecticide, and as a bleaching agent.

Sulphur trioxide is formed by the union of sulphur dioxide and oxygen in the presence of a catalyst. The reaction is reversible. 

Sulphur trioxide is the anhydride of sulphuric acid. 

Sulphuric acid is prepared: (1) by the contact process; and (2) by the chamber process. Both start with sulphur dioxide. 

Dilute sulphuric acid is a strong acid, reacting with metals, and with their oxides and hydroxides. It is used in storage batteries. 

Concentrated sulphuric acid is hydrogen sulphate. It acts with salts, and thus aids in preparing other acids. It is also a dehydrating agent and an oxidizing agent. 

Sulphuric acid is of the greatest importance to modern industries. 

Most sulphates are soluble. We test for a soluble sulphate by means of a solution of barium chloride, in the presence of dilute hydrochloric acid. 

192. Exercises.-1. At reduced pressure sulphur vapor has a gram-molecular weight of 256 g. What is its formula? Under ordinary pressure sulphur vapor has a gram-molecular weight of 

64 g. What is its formula then? 

2. Write the balanced, molecular equation for the burning of sulphur vapor in oxygen. For the union of sulphur vapor and hydrogen. 

3. Write, in one vertical column, the formulas of the following oxides: water, mercuric oxide, zinc oxide, sodium oxide, aluminum oxide, and carbon dioxide. In a second vertical column write the formulas of the corresponding sulphides. 

4. What is the valence of sulphur in sulphides? In sulphur dioxide? In sulphur trioxide? 

5. How could you prove that a yellow solid handed you is, or is not, sulphur? 

6. What is the gram-molecular weight of hydrogen sulphide? 

What volume of the gas has this weight? Calculate how much a

liter of the gas weighs under standard conditions. How much does a liter of air weigh? Is the gas heavier, or lighter, than air?

Cd(C2H3O2)2.

CHAPTER XVI  

193. Acids. When we hear the word "acid," we usually think of a liquid which, if spilled upon the skin, "burns" it, which destroys cloth, paper, and wood, and which corrodes metals. Acids like sulphuric acid and nitric acid, especially in concentrated form, do act in this way, but others, such as the citric acid of oranges, lemons, and grapefruit, do not have this destructive action. Anhydrous sulphuric and nitric acids are liquids under ordinary conditions, anhydrous hydrogen chloride and bromide are gases, and anhydrous tartaric, oxalic, boric, and citric acids are solids. The solution of each of these substances in water is the real acid, according to the chemist. Concentrated sulphuric acid should really be called hydrogen sulphate, and concentrated nitric acid, hydrogen nitrate. All acids contain hydrogen (§ 34). 

As in the case of hydrochloric acid, a substance is said to have "acid properties" if its solution has a sour taste and the ability to give to blue litmus or neutral litmus a red color. An "acid reaction" means the ability to turn blue litmus to red. Acid solutions also cause soda (Na2CO3) or marble (CaCO3) to effervesce, because of the formation and escape of carbon dioxide (CO2). The strong acids, such as dilute sulphuric and hydrochloric acids, can, in addition, react with active metals, such as magnesium, zinc, and iron. The hydrogen of the acid is 240

displaced by the metal (§ 37), and a salt of the acid is formed. See, however, nitric acid (§ 230). Metal+Acid-Salt+Hydrogen.

Another characteristic property of acids is that they react with oxides of metals and hydroxides of metals (§ 139) to form salts and water. 

Metal Oxide +Acid→Salt+Water. 

Metal Hydroxide +Acid→Salt+Water.

These reactions belong to the fourth class of chemical reactions: double decomposition.

The hydrogen which every acid contains is responsible for the "acid properties." We must remember that many hydrogen compounds are not acids. Such are alcohol, glycerine, and sugar. The hydrogen must be combined with an acid radical. This may be a non-metal, like chlorine in HCl, or it may be a radical, containing a non-metal and oxygen, like the sulphate radical (SO4) or the nitrate radical (NO3). Acids like hydrochloric acid, containing only two elements (a non-metal combined with hydrogen) are called binary acids; acids containing a third element (usually oxygen) are ternary acids. 

194. Bases. We have already studied the reaction of sodium with water (§ 36). 2Na+2H2O-2NaOH+H2 ↑. 

The sodium hydroxide can be obtained by evaporation of the excess of water. It is a white solid, which takes up moisture from the air (deliquesces), and also absorbs carbon dioxide, forming sodium carbonate (Na2CO3). 

Potassium hydroxide resembles sodium hydroxide. Ammonium hydroxide is obtained when ammonia unites with water (§ 222). Sodium and potassium hydroxides are examples of the strong bases, or alkalies. Bases in

solution have a biting (sometimes bitter) taste, they feel soapy to the touch, and they turn red litmus or neutral litmus to blue.

We have already seen that one molecular (formula) weight of a base contains one atomic weight of a metal combined with one, two, or more formula weights of the hydroxide radical (OH). Thus we have the bases NaOH, Ca(OH)2, and Al(OH)3, and both Fe(OH)2 and Fe(OH)3, according to the valence of the metal. 

195. Neutralization. In their reaction with litmus and other indicators, acids are the opposites of bases (§ 139). When the two are put together in the right proportions by weight, the one neutralizes the other. The indicator, litmus, tells us when these proportions are reached, for it gains a color-lavender -intermediate between red and blue. 

A drop more of base makes the litmus blue; a drop more of acid makes it red. The indicator thus shows us the end-point of the reaction. Another indicator: phenolphthalein (fēn'ōl-thăl'ē-in), is entirely colorless as long as the acid is in excess in the solution, but it becomes pink when a slight excess of base is present. The following equations represent some additional neutralization reactions. The reactions can be carried out with either litmus or phenolphthalein as the indicator. 

FIG. 113. 

Letting a base drop into an acid from a pipette. 2KOH+H2SO4→K2SO4+2H2O. 2NH4OH+H2SO4→(NH4)2SO4+2H2O. Ca(OH)2+2HNO3-Ca(NO3)2+2H2O. Ba(OH)2+2HC1→BaCl2+2H2O.

If we take a solution of each of the acids named, put in a drop of phenolphthalein solution, and then add the base indicated until the phenolphthalein just turns pink, we know the base has just neutralized the acid.

A study of all the equations for reactions between acids and bases shows that neutralization is the uniting of the hydrogen of the acid with the hydroxyl (OH) of the base. One product in every case is water, the other is a salt. The composition of the salt depends of course upon what metal was present in the base and what acid radical was present in the acid. Using 

FIG. 114. 

a burette for neutralization.

We see that in the reactions of acids with bases there are two definite materials present: (1) hydrogen; (2) hydroxyl. Combined with the hydrogen is the acid radical, which varies for different acids; combined with the hydroxyl is the metal, which is also different for different bases. Hydrogen Metal. 

Acid radical -Hydroxyl In this diagram horizontal pairs unite, and diagonal pairs unite, but not vertical pairs. What is formed in each case? 

196. Salts. We have seen that a salt contains two parts: (1) a metal (or the radical NH4), and (2) an acid radical. A salt may therefore be prepared by methods that will bring these two together: (1) Combination of metal and non-metal.

Fe+S-FeS. 2Na+C12-2NaCl.

(2) Displacement of H of acid by a metal. Give several examples. (3) Double decomposition between the oxide of metal and an acid. See § 139. (4) Neutralization (also a double decomposition) of base by acid. Give several cases, with equations. (5) Displacement of one metal in a salt by another metal.

Fe+CuSO4 FeSO4+Cu↓ . (6) Displacement of one halogen in a salt by another. 

2NaBr+C12-2NaC1+Br2 ↑. (7) A double decomposition between two salts in solution.

AgNO3+NaC1→AgCl ↓ +NaNO3. 

From method (2) we can define a salt as an acid with its hydrogen replaced by a metal. In an acid like sulphuric acid two sets of salts may be formed: (1) Those in which all the replaceable hydrogen of the acid is replaced by a metal: Na2SO4, K2SO4. These are called normal salts. (2) Those in which only part of the hydrogen is replaced: 

NaHSO4, KHSO4. These are called acid salts. Phosphoric acid,

H3PO4, has the acid salts, NaH2PO4 and Na₂HPO4, besides the normal salt, Na3PO4. 

We must remember that an acid salt does not necessarily have an acid reaction toward litmus.

We prepared acid sodium sulphate (or sodium hydrogen sulphate, NaHSO4) when we treated salt with concentrated sulphuric in the preparation of hydrogen chloride (§ 137). We can also prepare it if we treat sulphuric acid with sodium hydroxide in the proportion of one gram-molecular weight of the acid to one gram-molecular weight of the base. NaOH+H2SO4→NaHSO4+H₂O.

Of course the equation represents only half the amount of sodium hydroxide necessary to neutralize the sulphuric acid. 

197. How acids are prepared. An acid consists of hydrogen and an acid radical. To prepare an acid we must bring together these two parts so that they will combine. (1) Direct union of hydrogen and a non-metal. H2+C12-2HC1.

H2+S→H2S. (2) Displacement of a non-metal in a binary acid by a more active non-metal. Cl₂+2HI→2HC1+I2. Cl2+H2S→2HC1+S ↓ . (3) Union of oxide of non-metal with water (§ 67). 

SO3+H2O-H2SO4. 

CO₂+H2O-H2CO3. P2O5+3H20-2H3PO4. 

An oxide of a non-metal which unites with water to produce an acid is called an acid anhydride. (4) Double decomposition between a salt and an acid (§ 137).

NaCl+H2SO4 NaHSO4+HC1 ↑ . 

Nitric acid, HNO3, is prepared from a nitrate by a similar reaction.

NaNO3+H2SO4 NaHSO4+HNO3 ↑. 

In the preparation of hydrochloric and nitric acids by double decomposition, the method is successful because: (1) The acid being prepared escapes when gentle heat is applied, while the sulphuric acid, owing to its high boiling point, remains behind.

(2) The sulphuric acid is active enough to furnish readily the hydrogen needed. (3) The sulphuric acid does not oxidize the acid being formed. 

Note that in §§ 163 and 166 we learned that this method does not work for hydrogen bromide or iodide.

An insoluble acid can be precipitated out. An insoluble salt can likewise be precipitated, leaving the acid in solution. Na2SiO3+2HC1→2NaC1+H2SiO3 ↓ . Ba(NO3)2+H2SO4 BaSO4+2HNO3. 

198. How bases are prepared.-A base consists of a metal (or NH4) and the hydroxyl radical. We can bring these parts together by: (1) Action of active metals with water (§ 36). 2Na+2HOH-2NaOH+H2 ↑. Ca+2HOH Ca(OH)2+H2 ↑ . (2) Union of the oxide of a metal (a basic anhydride; § 67) with water.

Na₂O+H2O-2NaOH. CaO+H2O-Ca(OH)2. (3) Double decomposition between a base and a salt. The hydroxides of most metals are insoluble. These may be precipitated by means of the soluble bases. AlCl3+3NaOH→Al(OH); ↓ +3NaCl. CuSO4+2KOH-Cu(OH)2 ↓ +K2SO4. FeCl3+3NH4OH→Fe(OH)3 ↓ +3NH4C1. 

199. How acids and salts are named.-In § 109 we learned how the chemist names compounds in general. 

We should now review those rules, especially as they apply to acids and salts. Use the accompanying Table to verify each rule. Set down each formula, and add the proper valence number (+ or -) after the hydrogen and

the acid radical of each acid, and after the metal and acid radical of each salt.
