Rules: (1) The name of a binary acid, like HCl, includes the names of both its elements. The salt of such an acid is not named from the acid, but as a compound of two elements, and its name ends in ide. (2) Acids containing hydrogen, oxygen, and a non-metal are named from the non-metal. The hydrogen and oxygen are understood to be present, but are not given in the name. (3) If there are two such acids containing different proportions of oxygen, the one with the larger proportion of oxygen ends in ic, the one with the smaller proportion ends in ous. (4) When the name of the acid ends in ic, the name of its salt ends in ate. When the name of the acid ends in ous, that of its salt ends in ite. (5) If the name of the acid has a prefix, the prefix appears in the names of its salts. Hypo is used as prefix for an acid having

less oxygen than an ous acid. Thus hypochlorous acid has, as salts, hypochlorites. Per is used as prefix for an acid having more oxygen than an ic acid. Thus perchloric acid has, as salts, perchlorates. 

200. Summary.-An acid consists of hydrogen and an acid radical. If soluble, an acid has "acid properties," that is, it has a sour taste and an "acid reaction" with indicators.

The reactions of strong acids take place best in solution. They include: (1) the reactions with metals, to produce hydrogen and salts; (2) the reactions with oxides of metals, to produce salts and water; and (3) the reactions with the hydroxides of metals, water and salts being the products. 

A base consists of a metal (or + radical) combined with hydroxyl (OH). The strong bases are called alkalies. 

Neutralization is the uniting of the hydrogen (H+) of an acid with the hydroxyl (OH-) of a base. Water and a salt are produced. 

The end-point of a neutralization is shown by an indicator. 

A salt consists of a metal and an acid radical. It is an acid with its hydrogen replaced by a metal. 

Salts may be formed by many reactions of combination, replacement, and double decomposition. 

A normal salt is an acid with all its replaceable hydrogen replaced by a metal. An acid salt still contains replaceable hydrogen. 

Acid salts do not necessarily have an acid reaction. 

Acids are prepared by reactions involving direct union, displacement, and double decomposition. 

The oxide of a non-metal which unites with water to produce an acid is called an acid anhydride. 

Bases are prepared: (1) by the action of active metals with water; (2) by the union of water with oxides of metals; and (3) by double decomposition between bases and salts. The hydroxides of most metals are insoluble. 

A metal oxide which unites with water to form a base is called a basic anhydride.

CHAPTER XVII  202. Reactions in solution.-In §§ 187 and 188 we learned what a difference there is between the reactions of dilute sulphuric acid and its reactions in the anhydrous form, as hydrogen sulphate. Other acids, such as hydrochloric acid (§ 139), show the same difference in behavior; so do bases and salts. The theory of ions, which we study in this chapter, contains an explanation of the chemical behavior of these substances in solution. But before we take up this theory, we must consider two other peculiarities of solutions which need explanation: (1) Solutions of acids, bases, and salts usually conduct the electric current readily, while solutions of other substances do not. (2) The rules which we have learned regarding the effect of a gram-molecular weight of a dissolved substance in lowering the freezing point of 1000 g. of water, and in elevating its boiling point (§ 151), do not apply to acids, bases, and salts, the very substances which, in solution, conduct the electric current. 

203. Solutions as conductors.-When metals, such as copper or iron, conduct the current, they are apparently not changed by it; but when a solution acts as a conductor, a chemical change always takes place in the 250

solution. This chemical change we call electrolysis (§ 35), and the solution which conducts the current is called an electrolyte. We may readily test solutions to find out whether they are electrolytes or non-electrolytes.

FIG. 115. 

11 Apparatus used to determine whether or not a solution is a conductor. If the lamp glows the current must be passing through the solution. 

Fig. 115 shows one form of apparatus. There must be an electric current, from a battery or other source, and an incandescent lamp, connected "in series," to show whether, or not, a current is passing. The "break" in the circuit is closed by the solution to be tested. If the lamp glows brightly, we know the solution is a good conductor; if it barely glows, the solution is a poor conductor; if the lamp does not glow at all, we call the solution a nonconductor, or non-electrolyte.

A good set of substances to be tested is the following. The water solutions of numbers 2-8 should be made with distilled water. 

They should contain 1 gram-molecular weight of solute per liter of solution. (1) Distilled water. (2) Dilute hydrochloric acid. (3) Dilute sodium hydroxide. (4) Sodium chloride solution. (5) Ammonium hydroxide solution. (6) Acetic acid solution. (7) Ammonium acetate solution. (8) Sugar solution. (9) Hydrogen chloride dis- solved in dry toluene or benzene. (10) Solid sodium chloride.

The cell in which each solution is placed for the test should be rinsed out thoroughly with distilled water after each of tests 2-8. 

Before 9 is tried, the cell should be dried thoroughly, and again

before 10 is tested. Distilled water will not conduct the current, neither will the sugar solution, the hydrogen chloride in toluene, nor the solid sodium chloride. The ammonium hydroxide and the acetic acid conduct the current, but not so readily as hydrochloric acid and sodium hydroxide. Ammonium acetate, obtained when ammonium hydroxide and acetic acid are put together, is a good 

FIG. 116. 

Apparatus in which the distance between the electrodes can be varied. If the lamp glows when the electrodes are far apart, the solution must be a good conductor. conductor. A solution of glycerine or alcohol behaves like sugar solution; these solutions are not electrolytes. 

204. Freezing points of solutions. We already know that a gram-molecular weight of sugar, alcohol, or glycerine dissolved in 1000 g. of water, depresses the freezing point of the water to -1.86° C. (see § 151).

Half a gram-molecular weight would lower the -0.93° C. The amount the number of particles freezing point half as much: to of depression is in proportion to of dissolved substance present. Because of this fact we can use the "freezing-point method" to get the molecular weights of substances like cane sugar, which we cannot obtain in the gaseous state. 

When we dissolve sodium chloride (NaCl) in water, we find that its effect in lowering the freezing point is nearly twice what we should expect. If we use 342 g. of cane sugar (a gram-molecular weight) in 1000 g. of water, the freezing point of the solution will be -1.86° C. If, however, we use 58.5 g. of sodium chloride (a gram-molecular

weight) in 1000 g. of water, the lowering of the freezing point of the solution will be almost twice as great, or -3.42° C. In the case of sugar, or glycerine, or alcohol the amount of the lowering of the freezing point of the water depends upon the number of particles of dissolved substance present; we must therefore conclude that in a dilute solution of sodium chloride each of the particles of sodium chloride breaks up into two particles, and that each of these particles has its effect in lowering the freezing point of water. The elevation of the boiling point of water by salt in dilute solution is also twice what we should expect.

Acids, bases, and salts behave like common salt, and cause an abnormally great lowering of the freezing point, and an abnormally great elevation of the boiling point of water. All these are electrolytes. Nonelectrolytes behave normally, like sugar and glycerine. In the case of an electrolyte like sodium sulphate (Na2SO4), or sulphuric acid, the freezing point depression in dilute solution is practically three times what we should expect; hence we conclude that one particle is broken up into three particles, each having its effect in lowering the freezing point. 

Courtesy William M. Blanchard

FIG. 117. 

Svante August Arrhenius (18591927). Proposed the modern theory of dissociation into ions. 

205. Theory of ions.-We have now taken up several of the peculiar properties of acids, bases, and salts in solution. To account for these properties,

Arrhenius, a Swedish chemist, proposed the theory of ions (1887). We now assume that when acids, bases,

and salts are dissolved, their particles break up at once into charged ions. Thus, for the dissolving of sodium chloride, we write the equation, NaCl-Na++Cl−. 

The ions must have electric charges, because when a current is passed through the solution, the ions move toward the electrodes. The sodium ions (Na+) move toward the cathode, or (-) electrode; they must possess (+) charges. The chlorine ions (Cl) move toward the anode, or (+) electrode; they must have (−) charges. 

The two sets of ions become separated in the act of dissolving; but, because they are oppositely charged, some pairs are constantly entering into a loose electrical combination (Na+Cl¯). Hence the reaction may be called reversible:

NaCl Na+Cl¯Na++C1-. 

When hydrogen chloride is dissolved in water, its particles are largely broken up into hydrogen and chlorine ions. 

HCI H++C1-.

Sodium hydroxide gives sodium and hydroxyl ions. NaOH Na++OH-. 

Sodium sulphate gives sodium and sulphate ions.

Na2SO4 2Na++SO4²-. 

To explain why electrolytes have abnormally great effects in raising the boiling point and lowering the freezing point of water, we assume that these charged particles, or ions, act as single particles. If each particle of salt, for example, were to break up into two ions: one

of sodium and one of chlorine, the freezing-point effect and the boiling-point effect would be twice as great as if the salt particles remained undivided. 

206. Ions and atoms.-An ion is an atom or radical bearing one or more electric charges. Ions must not be confused with atoms. Ions are atoms or radicals with new properties, because of the electric charge, or charges, which each ion possesses. The change of a particle of hydrogen chloride into hydrogen and chlorine ions is a chemical change, and the change of the ions to atoms and molecules is likewise a chemical change. Both hydrogen ions and chlorine ions are colorless and very soluble in water, and quite different from the elements as we ordinarily know them. The characteristic properties by which we know the elements in the free state are the properties of their molecules. In solution, however, charged atoms and radicals act like individual free particles in raising the boiling point and lowering the freezing point of the water in which they are dissolved. 

FIG. 118. 

Michael Faraday (1791-1867). Began by washing dishes for Sir 

Humphry Davy. Devised chemical equivalents, liquefied gases, discovered benzene, and by his work on magneto-electric induction made possible the invention of the dynamo. 

207. Ions and electrolysis. Electrolysis, as we have learned, is the chemical change that takes place when an electrolyte conducts a current. Electrolytes are not broken up into ions by the current. The ions become separated in the act of dissolving. The current is con-

ducted by the electrolyte because the ions are already present. The battery or generator producing the current keeps the electrodes charged.

When we put the electrodes into a solution of hydrogen chloride, the hydrogen ions are attracted toward the (−) electrode. The chlorine ions, on the other hand, are attracted by the (+) electrode. When the hydrogen ions reach the (−) electrode, their (+) charges are neutralized, and they become hydrogen atoms. These combine in twos and become molecules (H2). By similar steps the chlorine ions become chlorine atoms and then chlorine molecules (Cl2). We may represent these facts by the equations, (1) 2HCl⇒2H++2C1¯. (2) 2H++2(−)+2C1−+2(+)→→H₂ ↑ +C12 ↑ · The ions, by carrying their charges to the electrodes, conduct the current through the solution. 

A solution of sulphuric acid contains hydrogen ions, sulphate ions, and molecules of hydrogen sulphate:

H2SO42H++SO4²-. 

During electrolysis the hydrogen ions reach the (-) electrode and become, first, atoms, and then molecules (H2). At the (+) electrode we obtain, instead of the sulphate radical, sulphuric acid and oxygen. 2H2O+2SO4-2H2SO4+O2 ↑. 

The equations for ionization and electrolysis, in the case of sodium hydroxide, are: (1) Ionization: NaOH Na++OH¯. (2) Electrolysis: (a) At the (-) electrode: 4Na+4H2O-4NaOH+2H2 ↑. (b) At the (+) electrode: 40H→2H2O+O₂ ↑.

The corresponding equations for cupric sulphate are: 

(1) Ionization: CuSO4 

(2) Electrolysis:

Cu2++SO42- (a) At the (-) electrode: 2Cu (b) At the (+) electrode: 2SO4+2H2O→2H2SO4+O2 ↑ • 

208. Ions of acids and bases.-We have learned that all acids are hydrogen compounds. We may now define an acid as a hydrogen compound which, in solution, gives hydrogen ions, (H+).

When we dissolve hydrogen chloride in water, the negative ion (Cl) is derived from the chlorine atom; when we dissolve hydrogen sulphide, the negative ion (S2-) is derived from the sulphur atom. 

HC1 H++C1-.

H₂S 2H++S2-. 

The coefficient 2 in 2H+ multiplies the number of charges as well as the number of atoms. 

The ionization equations for the breaking up of nitric acid and sulphuric acid are:

HNO3 H++NO3¯. H2SO42H++SO²¯. 

In these cases the negative ions are derived from the univalent nitrate radical and the bivalent sulphate radical, respectively. Sulphuric acid is also ionized into two univalent ions: H2SO4H++HSO₁¯.

Thus the action of concentrated sulphuric acid with sodium chloride produces the acid sulphate, NaHSO4 (§ 196).

The characteristic properties of acids are not present

in pure anhydrous hydrogen sulphate, hydrogen nitrate, or hydrogen chloride because these substances do not have enough hydrogen ions. The sour taste which water solutions of acids possess, and their ability to turn blue litmus red, are really properties of hydrogen ions (H+). 

The characteristic ion in the solution of every base is the hydroxyl ion (OH-). The positive (+) ion of a base is a metal, or a radical which acts like a metal, such as NH4.

NaOH Na+ + OH- 

NH4OH NH4++OH Ca(OH)2 Ca²++2(OH)− In 2(OH) the coefficient 2 multiplies the (−) charge as well as the units of the radical. 

The properties which distinguish bases, such as the ability to turn red litmus blue, to "cut" fats, and to neutralize acids, are really the properties of hydroxyl ions (OH)-. 

A base is a hydroxide which, in solution, gives hydroxyl ions. 

209. Neutralization.-We have already noted (§ 195) that in every case of neutralization the hydrogen of the acid and the hydroxyl of the base unite to form water, while the metal of the base and the acid radical of the acid unite to form a salt. From the theory of ions we consider that neutralization is the union of hydrogen and hydroxyl ions to form water. The water is practically non-ionized. H++OH-→H₂O. 

When we put a dilute sodium hydroxide solution with dilute hydrochloric acid, the ionic equation is: Na++OH-+H++Cl→→Na++C1-+H₂O.

If we use such quantities of base and acid that the number of hydrogen and hydroxyl ions is equal, the solution will be neutral to litmus. The solution will then contain sodium ions (Na+) and chlorine ions (Cl). 

These unite in part to form sodium chloride. The percentage of these ions that unite depends upon how dilute the solution is. If we evaporate all the water, the sodium and chlorine ions unite completely to form crystals of sodium chloride.

We assume that neutralization consists in the union of hydrogen and hydroxyl ions to form molecules of water. We find a striking proof of this when we measure the heat of neutralization, that is, the amount of heat given off when definite amounts of strong bases and strong acids neutralize one another. Thus, a gram-molecular weight each of sodium hydroxide and hydrochloric acid, when they react in solution, give off 13,700 calories of heat. A gram-molecular weight each of sodium hydroxide and nitric acid, of potassium hydroxide and nitric acid, of potassium hydroxide and hydrochloric acid, give off the same amount: 

13,700 calories. We write the ionic heat equations thus: K++OH-+H++Cl− →K+ + C1− + H₂O+13,700 cal. Na++OH-+H++NO-3→Na++NO-3+H2O+13,700 The essential part of these equations is, of course, H++OH-→H₂O+13,700 cal. cal. 

210. Strong, and weak, acids and bases. We must not confuse a "strong" acid with a concentrated acid, nor a "weak" acid with a dilute one. A strong acid is one that is largely broken up, in water solution, into its ions. Such an acid is a very good conductor of the electric current and is chemically active. Hydrochloric and nitric acids are very strong acids; sulphuric acid, not quite so strong; phosphoric acid, still less strong. Acetic

acid, HC2H3O2, is an example of a weak acid. Its solution is not nearly so good a conductor as that of hydrochloric acid, and its action with metals is much slower. 

Acetic acid is a weak acid because its degree of ionization is small. Only a small percentage of its particles are present as free ions. A gram-molecular weight of hydrogen chloride in a liter of solution is about 78% ionized under ordinary conditions; a gram-molecular solution of acetic acid is only about 0.4 of 1% ionized. When there are so few ions in a given volume, there is a correspondingly small activity. Carbonic acid (H2CO3) and hydrosulphuric acid (H2S) are still weaker acids (see Appendix). 

Just as there are strong and weak acids, so there are strong and weak bases. Sodium, potassium, and calcium hydroxides, in solution, are strong bases. Ammonium hydroxide is a weak base. 

We do not speak of salts as strong or weak, since practically all salts are largely broken up into ions in water solution. 

211. Hydrolysis. While many common salts have a neutral reaction in solution, others have an acid or an alkaline reaction. Thus, sodium carbonate solution (Na2CO3) turns red litmus blue, and aluminum chloride solution (AlCl3) turns blue litmus red. The theory of ionization has an explanation of these reactions. 

Water is ionized to only a very small extent, but enough for its ions to take part in many reactions: H₂OH++OH-. 

If the ions of water were to act with common salt, there would be a reaction just the reverse of neutralization; for a base and an acid would be formed: NaCl+HOH⇒NaOH+HC1.

Such a reaction, by which a salt reacts with the ions of water to produce a base and an acid, is called hydrolysis (hi-drŏl'i-sis). 

When sodium chloride reacts with water, the base and acid which we should expect (NaOH and HCl) are both strong, that is, highly ionized. Practically all their ions are free. The H+ ions of the acid are free to react with the OH- ions of the base to form water. As a result we get no evidence of the acid or base in the salt solution. 

But with sodium carbonate the case is different: Na2CO3+2HOH÷2NaOH+H2CO3. 

The sodium hydroxide is a strong base and highly broken up into its ions. The carbonic acid, on the other hand, is a weak acid and is separated into relatively few ions. If we write the equation in ionic form, it becomes: 

2Na++CO32-+2H++20H2Na++20H-+H2CO3. 

The uniting of H+ ions from the water with CO3²- ions from the sodium carbonate leaves an excess of free OHions in the solution. These turn red litmus to blue. 

They also cause soda solution to act like a base in cutting grease and making soap.

Aluminum chloride gives, by hydrolysis, a weak base and a strong acid. In this case free H+ ions remain, and the solution reacts acid.

A13++3C1-+3H++30H¯Al(OH)3+3H++3C1.- 

We have considered the hydrolysis of three classes of salts. There is still a fourth: those which are hydrolyzed to produce both weak bases and weak acids. Such salts cannot exist in the presence of water. Aluminum sul-

phide, Al2S3, is such a salt. We can prepare it by heating aluminum with sulphur. When treated with water, the substance is completely hydrolyzed: Al₂S3+6HOH-2A1(OH)3 | +3H₂S ↑. 

212. Conditions affecting ionization.-There are several conditions besides the amount of solvent that affect the degree of ionization. (1) The solvent used. Water is a favorable solvent, but most other solvents are not. Thus, hydrogen chloride dissolves in ether, benzene, and toluene, but the solution does not act like an acid (§ 208). (2) The physical state. Salt is not a conductor in the solid state, but when melted it can be electrolyzed. The products are the metal sodium at the (-) electrode and chlorine at the (+) electrode. 2NaCl 2Na++2C1→→2Na+Cl½ ↑ • When the salt is in solution, the products at the cathode are sodium hydroxide and hydrogen. Why? (3) The temperature. The degree of ionization of an electrolyte increases as the temperature is raised. Gases are somewhat ionized at high temperatures and become conductors. 

213. Ionic reactions and tests. The theory of ions is of great practical use to the chemist, because it explains many reactions which he must use in making tests for substances in solution. These tests consist largely of displacement and double decomposition reactions. Displacements which we have studied are: (1) Displacement of hydrogen by active metals, as when zinc displaces hydrogen from hydrochloric acid (§ 37); (2) Displacement of a less active metal by a more active one, as when iron displaces copper (§ 196); (3) Displacement of a less active non-metal by a more

active one, as when chlorine displaces bromine or iodine (§§ 131, 161, 164, and 167).

Ionic equations for these displacements are: (1) Zn+2H++2C1→→Zn2++2C1-+H₂ ↑ . (2) Fe+Cu2++SO42→→Fe2++SO4²+Cu ↓ . (3) 2K++2Br-+C12→2K++2C1−+Br½ ↑ · The chemist explains displacement (1) by saying that the (neutral) zinc atom loses 2 (-) charges, and these neutralize the 2 (+) charges of the hydrogen ions (2H+). As a result, neutral hydrogen atoms and molecules are formed, while zinc atoms, having lost 2 (-) charges each, become zinc ions (Zn2+). In equation (2) the iron atom loses 2 (-) charges, and becomes a ferrous ion (Fe2+). The 2 (-) charges lost by the iron atom convert the cupric ion into a copper atom. In equation (3) the active chlorine atoms gain the (-) charges of the bromine ions, forming chlorine ions and bromine atoms. By reaction (1) we prepare hydrogen or test for an acid; by (2) we test for a cupric salt in solution; and by (3) we test for a bromide in solution. 

While many "test" reactions are displacements, most of them are double decompositions. Usually a precipitate or a gas is one of the products. Thus we test for a soluble chloride by means of a solution of silver nitrate (§ 141). 

The ionic equation for this double decomposition is: Ag++NO+Na++C1→→AgC1 | +Na++NO3-. 

The equation shows that the silver nitrate, sodium chloride, and sodium nitrate are in the solution essentially as ions, since all are soluble salts. The silver chloride, however, is shown in the non-ionized form, since it is insoluble.

We must remember that the test for a chloride is not a test for chlorine atoms, any more than the test for a sulphate is a test for sulphur atoms. Sodium chlorate, NaClO3, contains

chlorine atoms, but a silver nitrate solution does not react with it to give silver chloride:

Ag++NO+Na++C103¯Ag++NO3¯+Na++C1O3¯. 

When the two solutions are mixed, no precipitate appears. The sodium chlorate solution forms chlorate ions (ClO3-), but not chloride ions (Cl-). The silver chlorate formed is soluble and is not precipitated.

The color of many salts in solution is due to one of their ions. 

Thus, cupric sulphate is a white solid; cupric chloride is green; and cupric bromide, a very dark brown. In dilute solution they are all blue. The blue color is due, not to the salts themselves, but to the cupric ion, Cu²+. . 

214. Valence of ions.—Originally the valence of atoms and radicals was represented by Roman numerals, as in Cl¹, Ca¹¹, and SO¹¹4. In § 113 we learned that valence is now considered to be electrical, and is represented by the proper number of (+) or (-) charges. The valences of the ions are the same as those of the charged atoms or radicals which produce the ions. A valence table of the ions, much shorter than the valence table of Chapter IX, is given herewith. The student will note again that the hydrogen ion and the ions of metals and the radical ammonium have (+) charges, and that the hydroxyl ion, and the ions of non-metals and acid radicals have (−) charges. A few additional valence symbols are included. 

Among these are the two ions, MnO4 and MnO42-. In these two radicals we have the same two elements in the same proportions, but the radicals (and the ions which they form in solution) have different valences. The first, MnO4, is the permanganate ion, found in the solution of potassium permanganate, KMnO4. Its color is purple. 

The second is the manganate ion, found in the solution

of potassium manganate, K,MnO4. Its color is green. 

The difference in the number of charges causes the difference of properties.

VALENCE OF IONS

NO3 MnO4- 215. Source of the charges of ions. While we have been studying about ions, have you been wondering where their (+) and (−) charges come from? When 

Arrhenius proposed his theory of ions in 1887, he had no satisfactory idea regarding the source of the electric charges. Since that time much has been learned regarding the nature of electricty, and scientists now think that the atoms themselves are made up of electric particles.

An atom, according to the modern view, is composed of a nucleus, or kernel, and of smaller particles revolving around it, much as planets revolve about a central sun. 

The nucleus contains an excess of positive (+) particles, called protons. Around the nucleus there are negative (-) particles, called electrons. The total number of

protons in an atom equals the number of electrons, so that the atom is electrically neutral. 

Nearly all the mass of an atom is in its nucleus, but the chemical activity of an atom depends upon the electrons in the outer part of the atom. The outer electrons may be lost or gained. The number of electrons in the outer part of the atom correspond to the valence. The hydrogen atom has one outer electron, but the atom as a whole is neutral (H°). When hydrogen and chlorine combine to form hydrogen chloride, our modern theory says that the outer electron of the hydrogen atom passes from the hydrogen to the chlorine. The loss of this electron leaves the hydrogen atom with one excess unit of (+) electricity (H+). The chlorine atom, with its extra electron, is negative (Cl−).

H°+C1° H+C1-. 

In the molecule of gaseous hydrogen chloride these two charges hold the hydrogen and chlorine in combination. 

But in solution these charged atoms separate, and we have (+) and (-) ions. 

In Chapter XXIV we shall take up the reasons why scientists have assumed that atoms have a definite structure. 

216. Summary.-The theory of ions is an explanation of the behavior of acids, bases, and salts in solution. 

Water solutions of acids, bases, and salts conduct the electric current; solutions of other subtances do not. 

Acids, bases, and salts have an abnormally great effect in depressing the freezing point of water, and in raising its boiling point.

The theory of ions is that when acids, bases, and salts are dissolved in water, their particles break up into charged ions.

The charged ions act as single particles in raising the boiling point or lowering the freezing point of water. 

An ion is an atom or radical having one or more electric charges. 

A solution conducts the electric current because ions are present. In conducting a current, a solution undergoes a chemical change (electrolysis). The charged ions have their charges neutralized at the electrodes.

An acid is a hydrogen compound which in solution gives hydrogen ions.

A base is a hydroxide which in solution gives hydroxyl ions. 

Neutralization is the union of hydrogen ions and hydroxyl ions to form water molecules.

Water is practically non-ionized. 

The heat of neutralization for equivalent amounts of strong acids and strong bases is always the same: 13,700 calories. 

Acids and bases may be highly ionized (strong) or slightly ionized (weak); but practically all salts are highly ionized. 

Hydrolysis is the reaction of the ions of water with the ions of a salt. It is the reverse of neutralization. 

The degree of ionization of a substance is affected by the solvent used, the physical state of the substance, and the temperature. 

Reactions and tests in solution are largely due to the reactions of ions.

The valences of ions are the same as those of the corresponding atoms or radicals.

The source of the charges of ions is explained by the theory of the structure of atoms.

218. Ammonia in nature.-Ammonia is a colorless gas with a well known, pungent odor. Small amounts of it are found in the air, in natural water, and in soil. Distilled water prepared from natural water also contains it, since the ammonia is vaporized with the water. monia is formed in the decay of many materials containing nitrogen compounds.

A solution of ammonia is used (as "ammonia water") in the household.

The solution is a base (§ 194), and is therefore a good cleansing agent for dirt of a fatty nature. NH,C1+Ca(OH),

219. Preparation of ammonia. — In the laboratory we usually prepare ammonia from an ammonium salt, such as ammonium chloride, and a base, such as slaked lime, Ca(OH)2, or sodium hydroxide. In the equation (below) ammonium hydroxide is represented as formed first. Since this is unstable, it is decomposed into ammonia and water. (1) 2NH4C1+Ca(OH)2→CaCl2+2NH4OH. (2) 2NH4OH→2NH; ↑ +2H20.

The complete equation is therefore: (3) 2NH4Cl+Ca(OH)2→CaCl2+2NH3 ↑ +2H2O. 269

220. Commercial preparation. 

Ammonia is obtained commercial scale: (1) by the on a be dried by means of a base. Why? heating of soft coal; (2) by direct union of nitrogen and hydrogen; and (3) by the "dry distillation" of horns, bones, and hides. The name "spirits of hartshorn" means ammonia water.

Soft coal is heated ("dry distilled") in the preparation of fuel gas and in "by-product" coke ovens (§ 271). The complex compounds of carbon, including nitrogen compounds, present in the coal are thereby decomposed. 

Those that are volatile pass off. The products include combustible gases (fuel gas), water, tar, and coke. Ammonia passes off with the other gases, and is collected by passing it through water, in which it is very soluble. 

The ammonia can be obtained directly from the water solution, or it can be combined with sulphuric acid, forming ammonium sulphate (NH4)2SO4.

The direct union of nitrogen and hydrogen is also a method of great importance. If electric sparks are passed through a mixture of nitrogen and hydrogen, the gases unite to a small extent to produce ammonia. But no matter how long the sparks are passed, the union of the two elements cannot be made complete, because the rate at which the ammonia is decomposed soon equals the rate at which it is formed; the action is reversible: N₂+3H22NH3. 

A better way for producing the direct combination of nitrogen and hydrogen is the Haber process (§ 246). The mixture of the two gases is heated to about 500° C. At the same time it is kept

Under these conditions a small percentage of the gases quickly unites to form ammonia. The ammonia is removed, and the gases that remain are again passed over the catalyst. 

221. Physical properties of ammonia.-The test for ammonia is its odor. The solubility of the gas is remark-

able. Under standard conditions 1146 cu. cm. dissolve in 1 cu. cm. of water. The gas is therefore more than twice as soluble as hydrogen chloride (§ 138). The "ammonia fountain" shows this interesting property. The solution of ammonia loses its ammonia when exposed to the air for some time, also when heated. Ammonia water is therefore called the volatile alkali. 

JAMMONIA WATER FIG. 122. 

An ammonia fountain.

Gaseous ammonia can be liquefied by pressure. A gram-molecular volume of it (how many liters?) weighs 17 g. under standard conditions, while the same volume of air weighs 28.95 g. (§ 150). Calculate the weight of one liter of ammonia. 

222. Chemical properties. Ammonia burns in undiluted oxygen, and forms an explosive mixture with oxygen. 4NH3+302-6H2O+2N2.

It is also readily oxidized by heated cupric oxide. 2NH3+3CuO-3H2O+N₂ ↑ +3Cu. 

When a catalyst, such as heated platinum, is present, ammonia can be oxidized by air to nitric oxide, NO. 4NH3+502-6H2O+4NO.

This is the first stage in the Ostwald process, by which ammonia is oxidized to nitric acid. The Ostwald process, combined with the Haber process, forms the modern way of synthesizing nitric acid from atmospheric nitrogen. 

Some metals, such as magnesium, replace the hydrogen of ammonia, and form nitrides. 3Mg+2NH3→Mg3N₂+3H2 ↑ ·

223. Ammonia and water.-The basic properties of ammonia water are due to the ammonium hydroxide (NH4OH) which it contains. Ammonia water also contains much ammonia in solution. There is an equilibrium between ammonia, water, and ammonium hydroxide, and the ions of ammonium hydroxide. NH3+H₂O NH4OH⇒NH4++OH¬. 

Ammonium hydroxide is a weak base compared with sodium and potassium hydroxides, and conducts the electric current much less readily (§ 203). Relatively few ions (NH4++OH¯) are present. But if an acid, such as hydrochloric acid, is added, the OH- ions are removed (by neutralization), and more and more ammonium hydroxide is needed to keep up the supply. As a result more and more ammonia and water combine to produce ammonium hydroxide, until all the ammonia is used up. 

224. Uses of ammonia.-Large quantities of ammonia are used in the production of nitric acid (§§ 222 and 227) and of ammonium salts, and in the Solvay process for making soda (§ 336). Aside from its use in cleaning, ammonia comes closest to our daily life in the process of refrigeration and in the making of artificial ice. Ammonia is suitable for this use because it can be readily liquefied by pressure even at the ordinary temperature (§ 221), and because the liquid ammonia easily changes back into gaseous form when the pressure is removed. 

When the gas is changed into a liquid, heat is given off. 

Then, when the liquid ammonia boils off (evaporates), the same amount of heat is absorbed. By taking heat from objects in contact with it the evaporating ammonia cools these objects.

Fig. 123 shows the principle of the ammonia ice machine. 

The compression of the ammonia gas by the pump heats the gas to 130° C. Hence the gas must first be cooled to the ordinary temperature, 17° C. The cooling is done by sprays of water. By the combination of pressure and cooling the ammonia gas is liquefied. COLD

Preparing artificial ice by the ammonia process. 

When the liquid ammonia is allowed to escape through the expansion valve, it takes up a great deal of heat. So the pipes in which the ammonia expands (evaporates) become very cold, and the salt brine through which they run may be cooled to -15° C. or lower. The cold brine is used for cooling purposes, as hot water coils are used for heating. When molds containing water are put into the brine, heat is taken from the water until it is frozen. Cold brine (or cold calcium chloride solution) is also piped through cold-storage rooms and refrigerator cars. Thus meat, butter, fruit, and other foods are prevented from spoiling. 

Iceless refrigerators are cooled by small ammonia ice machines, or by machines using liquid sulphur dioxide or carbon dioxide.

225. Ammonium salts.-Ammonium salts resemble those of sodium and potassium. The group ammonium (NH4), which they all contain, has not been obtained in a free state because it breaks up into ammonia and hydrogen. In the electrolysis of ammonium salts it goes to the cathode (-electrode) and is therefore considered a metal radical. Its valence is 1.

Ammonium salts are formed by the neutralization of ammonium hydroxide by strong acids. They may also be formed by the direct union of ammonia with the acids. 

The fuming which takes place in laboratories in which ammonia water and hydrochloric acid are being used is due to the combination of the gases ammonia and hydrogen chloride.

NH3+HC1→NH4C1 ↓ . 

The white smoke is composed of particles of solid ammonium chloride. Ammonium chloride is also called sal ammoniac. When the solid is heated in a test tube, it sublimes, or distils, and then collects upon the cold walls of the tube. It sublimes because the heat decomposes it into the gases: ammonia and hydrogen chloride. These pass off and reunite when cold. The reaction is thus reversible. 

NH3+HCI NH4C1. 

226. Oxides and acids of nitrogen.-Nitrogen and oxygen form a series of five compounds, which together give us an excellent illustration of the law of multiple proportions (§ 70). These compounds are nitrous oxide (N2O), nitric oxide (NO), nitrogen trioxide (N₂O3), nitrogen dioxide (NO2), and nitrogen pentoxide (N2O5). A sixth compound, nitrogen tetroxide (N2O4), has the two

elements in the same proportions as NO2, but has twice the molecular weight.

Nitrogen trioxide is also called nitrous anhydride, because it unites with water to form nitrous acid, HNO2. H2O+N2O3→2HNO2. 

For a similar reason nitrogen pentoxide is called nitric anhydride, the anhydride of nitric acid. H₂O+N2O5→2HNO3. 

Nitric acid is one of the most useful substances known to chemists. The alchemists knew how to prepare it as early as the 9th Century, A.D. They called it aqua fortis (strong water), because it is so active. 

227. Preparation of nitric acid.—We prepare nitric acid by heating sodium nitrate with concentrated sulphuric acid.

NaNO3+H2SO4-NaHSO4 + HNO3 ↑ . 

FIG. 124. 

In the laboratory we use a glass retort for carrying out the reaction.

We condense the vapor of the acid to the liquid form in a test tube or flask set into cold water. On a commercial scale the retort is of iron, and the acid vapors are cooled in a series of condensers. As in the case of hydrogen chloride (§ 137) the reaction succeeds, not because the sulphuric acid is a stronger acid than the nitric acid, but because the nitric acid has a low boiling point and distils off, leaving the excess of sulphuric acid and the sodium hydrogen sulphate behind.

Laboratory method of preparing nitric acid.

In addition to the double decomposition method, just described, chemists have worked out methods of preparing it by the oxidation

of ammonia (§ 222) and by the direct union of nitrogen and oxygen (§ 238). 

228. Physical properties.-The nitric acid obtained by heating a nitrate with sulphuric acid is usually yellow from the presence of some nitrogen dioxide, NO2. When air is bubbled through it, it becomes colorless.

Anhydrous nitric acid boils at 86° C. It is prepared by the distillation of the concentrated acid with concentrated sulphuric acid. When it contains a considerable amount of nitrogen dioxide, it is yellowish brown and is called fuming nitric acid. 

229. Chemical properties. Two marked properties of nitric acid are: (1) it is one of the strongest acids (§ 193); and (2) it is unstable and breaks down readily, giving off some of its oxygen. 

Nitric acid is a strong acid because it gives a large concentration of hydrogen ions (H+) when mixed with water. As a result it neutralizes solutions of bases, forming nitrates and water.

Na++OH-+H++NO-Na++NO3-+H2O.

Most of the common metals are converted into nitrates when treated with the acid. Gold and platinum are exceptions. Aluminum reacts with nitric acid only very slowly.

The second characteristic of nitric acid is that it is unstable. This property is shown when the concentrated acid is merely exposed to light, especially to direct sunlight. The acid becomes yellow, and the air above it contains brown fumes of nitrogen dioxide. 4HNO3-2H2O+4NO2 ↑ +O2 ↑. 

The instability of nitric acid is more marked when its vapor is passed through a hot tube. The acid is then completely decomposed into water, nitrogen dioxide, and oxygen.

Because nitric acid gives up oxygen so easily it is a powerful oxidizing agent. A stick of charcoal with a glowing tip can be made to burn in the acid much as in oxygen itself. At the same time nitrogen dioxide escapes. 4HNO3+C→2H2O+4NO2 ↑ +CO₂ ↑. 

Sulphur is oxidized to sulphuric acid when boiled with nitric acid. If the nitric acid is somewhat dilute, the gas formed is nitric oxide. 2HNO3+S-H2SO4+2NO ↑. 

If the nitric acid is concentrated, the equation is: 6HNO3+S→H2SO4+6NO2 ↑ +2H₂O. 

Nitric acid destroys cotton and woolen cloth, as well as the organic dyes which they contain. 

230. Reaction with metals.-The behavior of nitric acid with metals needs special attention. Dilute hydro-

chloric and sulphuric acids react with such metals as zinc and iron with the formation of hydrogen. These acids in dilute form do not react with inactive metals like copper, silver, and mercury. Dilute nitric acid, on the other hand, reacts with these metals, but the gas evolved is nitric oxide, not hydrogen. 3Cu+8HNO3-3Cu(NO3)2+4H2O+2NO ↑. 3Ag+4HNO3→3AgNO3+2H2O+NO ↑ . 

When zinc is treated with nitric acid, the reduction of the nitrogen of the acid goes farther than with copper. Nitrous oxide (N₂O), nitrogen, and even ammonia may be produced. If ammonia is formed, it reacts with some of the nitric acid, giving ammonium nitrate, NH4NO3. 

4Zn+10HNO3-4Zn (NO3)2+NH4NO3+3H₂O. 

According to this equation no gas whatever is evolved. We can show the reduction of nitric acid to ammonia by adding a little of the dilute acid to a mixture of zinc dust and sodium hydroxide solution. The odor of ammonia is at once perceptible. 

By themselves zinc dust and sodium hydroxide react thus: 

Zn+2NaOH Na₂ZnO2 (sodium zincate) +H₂ ↑ . 

The metal copper is generally used with nitric acid when we wish to prepare nitric oxide (NO), but the concentration and temperature of the acid determine which oxide of nitrogen is actually given off. With acid of density 1.2 the chief product is nitric oxide. If, however, the acid is concentrated, especially if warm, the gas is mainly nitrogen dioxide (NO2). We can see that even if nitric oxide were first formed, at the bottom of the generator, it would be oxidized to nitrogen dioxide in passing through the layer of concentrated acid above it. 

As was stated in § 140, aqua regia, which is used to "dissolve" gold and platinum, is a source of chlorine. 2HNO3+6HC1→4H2O+2NO ↑ +3C1½ ↑ •

231. Nitrates.-The two most important nitrates are those of sodium and potassium. Potassium nitrate is known as saltpeter, or niter. It was originally obtained from desert regions in Asia, and is still used as an in- 

Courtesy Chilean Nitrate of Soda Educational Bureau.

A nitrate oficina (refining plant) in the Atacama Desert. The nitrate ore (caliche) is hauled on small railways to the refining plant in the center, and the refined sodium nitrate is sent to the coast for shipment. gredient of black gunpowder and as a preservative for corned beef. Sodium nitrate is Chile saltpeter. It is found in "caliche," a raw saltpeter, which forms large deposits in Chile and Peru.

Nowadays potassium nitrate is prepared by the double decomposition between sodium nitrate and potassium chloride.

K++Cl-+Na++NO3¯→K++NO3¯+NaCl ↓ . 

The two salts are mixed in hot, concentrated solutions, and much of the sodium chloride is precipitated. The sodium chloride is filtered off while the solution is still

hot. As the filtrate becomes cool, potassium nitrate crystallizes out. 

Other nitrates are prepared from nitric acid: (1) By the action of metals, as in the case of copper and silver (§ 228). Lead and mercury act in the same way. (2) By reaction with oxides and hydroxides of metals: CaO+2HNO3→Ca(NO3)2+H2O. Ca(OH)2+2HNO3→Ca(NO3)2+2H2O. (3) By action with salts, such as carbonates, which give off a gas as one product:

CaCO3+2HNO3→Ca(NO3)2+H2CO3(H₂O+CO2 ↑ ). 

All nitrates are decomposed by heat. 

Those of sodium and potassium give off of their oxygen, forming nitrites: 2KNO3-2KNO₂+02 ↑. 

Ammonium nitrate is decomposed, when heated, into water and nitrous oxide. NH4NO3 2H₂O ↑ +N₂O ↑ · Other nitrates, when heated, give the oxide of the metal, nitrogen dioxide, and oxygen: 2Pb(NO3)2⇒2PbO+4NO₂ ↑ +0₂ ↑ · 232. Test for nitrates. We test for chlorides, that is, for chloride ions, by means of silver ions, since the two produce silver chloride, an insoluble precipitate.

Na++Cl−+Ag++NO;¯→Na++NO3¯+AgCl ↓ . 

In a similar way we test for sulphate ions by means of barium ions, since barium sulphate is insoluble. 2K++SO4²-+Ba²++2C1−→2K++2C1−+BaSO4 ↓ · We cannot test for a nitrate in this way, since all nitrates are soluble in water. For the same reason we

cannot prepare nitrates by precipitation. To test for nitrates we take advantage of the instability and oxidizing power of nitric acid. If we warm a solid nitrate with concentrated sulphuric acid (only a very small amount of each is needed), and then add a small piece of copper, the brown fumes of nitrogen dioxide (NO2) will be produced. The nitric acid set free acts upon the copper. 

To test for a nitrate in solution we first add a few drops of the solution to about 5 cu. cm. of a concentrated solution of ferrous sulphate, in a test tube, and carefully pour down the side of the test tube 3 to 5 cu. cm. of concentrated sulphuric acid. The heavy acid sinks to the bottom and forms a layer below the other solution. 

If the suspected solution contained a nitrate, a brown ring will be formed just above the layer of sulphuric acid. 

233. Uses of nitric acid and nitrates.-Nitrates are used as fertilizers of soil (§ 91) and in explosives. Black gunpowder is a mixture of potassium nitrate, charcoal, and sulphur in varying proportions. It owes its efficiency to the large volume of gases formed when its components react. One of the equations for the reaction is: 2KNO3+C+S→K₂SO4+CO₂ ↑ +N½ ↑ . 

Ammonium nitrate, NH4NO3, is used in explosives and to prepare nitrous oxide, N2O (§§ 231 and 237). 

The dye industry consumes large amounts of nitric acid, also the manufacture of explosives like nitroglycerine (glyceryl nitrate), guncotton, and tri-nitro-toluene (T. 

N.T.). The reaction of nitric acid with glycerine takes place thus:

C3H5(OH)3+3HNO3→C3H5(NO3)3+3H2O. 

The water formed is removed by concentrated sulphuric acid

which is mixed with the nitric acid. When the nitroglycerine "explodes," its carbon and hydrogen are oxidized to carbon dioxide and water respectively, and its nitrogen is set free. 4C3H5(NO3)3-12CO₂ ↑ +10H₂O ↑ +6N2 ↑ +0₂ ↑ • The necessary oxygen is already in the molecule of the nitroglycerine, and the reaction therefore takes place with great speed. 

Nitroglycerine is a thick, greenish oil, and very explosive. It is used chiefly in the form of dynamite, in which it is mixed with a porous earth. In this form it is easier to handle. Dynamite is used by farmers for removing stumps and boulders, for digging ditches, and for preparing holes for fence posts and young trees. 

The cellulose present in cotton reacts with a mixture of nitric and sulphuric acids much as glycerine does. Cellulose nitrates of varying composition can be formed. Those having the largest proportion of nitrogen are the most explosive. They are used for guncotton and smokeless powder. The cellulose nitrate produces no smoke because the products of its explosion are colorless gases: carbon dioxide, carbon monoxide, water vapor, and nitrogen, as in the case of nitroglycerine. 

Collodion is a cellulose nitrate that is soluble in a mixture of alcohol and ether. When the solvents evaporate, they leave the cellulose nitrate in the form of a tough, transparent membrane. 

This is used as a protective covering for wounds: a "new skin." 

Celluloid is a mixture of cellulose nitrates and camphor. 

234. Nitrous acid and nitrites.-Nitrous acid, HNO2, has not been prepared in a free state, but is formed in solution if a nitrite is treated with a dilute acid. 2NaNO2+H2SO4↔Na2SO4+2HNO2.

Nitrous acid, formed in this way, is used for preparing a class of nitrogen dyes.

Sodium and potassium nitrites are formed by the heating of their nitrates (§ 231). The loss of oxygen takes place more easily if the nitrate is heated with lead. 

KNO3+Pb-KNO₂+PbO.

Nitrogen trioxide, N2O3, is the anhydride of nitrous acid (§ 226).

When a nitrite, either in the solid state or in concentrated solution, is treated with dilute sulphuric acid, brown fumes are given off. This reaction distinguishes a nitrite from a nitrate. 

235. Nitric oxide (NO).-Nitric oxide is formed when dilute nitric acid reacts with copper (§ 230): 3Cu+8HNO3→3Cu(NO3)2+4H₂O+2NO ↑. 

The weight of 22.4 liters of the gas is 30 g.

under standard conditions (§ 150). What is the weight of 1 

liter? Is the gas heavier, or lighter, than air? Nitric oxide may be collected over water. It is colorless. Its most striking property is the speed with which it unites with oxygen when it comes into contact with air. The product is the red-brown nitrogen dioxide, NO2. This property makes nitric oxide very useful in the preparation of sulphuric acid by the Chamber process, for it serves as a carrier of oxygen (§ 186). Briskly burning substances, like phosphorus and magnesium, continue to burn in nitric oxide, but ordinary combustibles do not. 

236. Nitrogen dioxide (NO2).—As we have just learned, nitrogen dioxide is the red-brown gas formed when nitric oxide reacts with the free oxygen of the air. It is also formed when most nitrates are heated (§ 231). 2Pb(NO3)2⇒2PbO+4NO2 ↑ +0₂ ↑ • 2 When the gas is passed through a U-tube surrounded by a freezing mixture, it is condensed to a liquid, and even frozen. The cool gas is much lighter in color than the warm gas. The cool gas is made up chiefly of N2O4

molecules, while the warm gas is chiefly NO2. The ordinary gas is a mixture of the two. 2NO2 N2O4. (brown) (colorless) 

237. Nitrous oxide (NO).-Nitrous oxide is interesting chiefly for two reasons: (1) it is the anesthetic which dentists use, as "laughing gas," to produce temporary unconsciousness of pain; (2) it is the only gas which supports combustion with a vigor like that of free oxygen.

It re-ignites a glowing splint 

(§§ 6 and 22). This serves as a test if oxygen is not present. 

Nitrous oxide does not oxidize nitric oxide to nitrogen dioxide, as free oxygen NH,NO3

H₂O 

FIG. 127. 

84 When ammonium nitrate is heated, it is decomposed into water and nitrous oxide. does. Hence a brown gas is not formed when the two gases are brought together. Another difference between nitrous oxide and free oxygen is that sulphur must be burning very vigorously, or it will not continue to burn in nitrous oxide.

Nitrous oxide is prepared by the heating of ammonium nitrate to about 170° C. A mixture of ammonium sulphate and sodium nitrate may also be used.

NH4NO3 N₂O ↑ +2H₂O ↑ . 

Compare this with the equation for the decomposition of ammonium nitrite (§ 89). 

NH4NO2 N2 ↑ +2H2O.

Nitrous oxide becomes liquid under pressure. The liquid is kept in cylinders. When the pressure is removed, the liquid vaporizes. The gas is fairly soluble in cold water: 1.3 cu. cm. of gas in 1 cu. cm. of water at 0° C. It is less soluble in warm water. 

Calculate the molecular weight of the gas and the weight of 1 liter. Then compare nitrous oxide with air (§§ 150 and 235); which is heavier? 

238. Fixation of nitrogen.-We have already learned (§ 90) that the capture of the uncombined nitrogen of the air, and its conversion into nitrogen compounds, is called the fixation of nitrogen. There are several ways in which the capture of nitrogen is brought about: (1) By the nitrogen-fixing bacteria which grow upon the roots of leguminous plants. Review § 91. 

(2) By the combination of nitrogen and hydrogen in the presence of a catalyst to produce ammonia (§§ 90 and 220). This is the Haber process. (3) By the union of heated calcium carbide, CaC2, with the nitrogen of the air to form calcium cyanamide (sĩ′ăn-ăm’id), CaCN,. CaC₂+N2-CaCN₂+C. 

The calcium cyanamide is decomposed by water. Ammonia and calcium carbonate are formed: CaCN₂+3H20→CaCO3+2NH3 ↑. 

Calcium carbide is the well known, grayish solid which reacts with water to produce acetylene (§ 293). (4) By the combination of some of the nitrogen and oxygen of the air to produce nitric oxide (§ 90). This takes place in an electric "arc" at a very high temperature.

The direct combination of nitrogen and oxygen is called the Birkeland-Eyde process. The air is forced through an "electric flame" several feet in diameter. The "flame" is produced by powerful electromagnets and an electric arc. The magnets spread out the arc discharge, so that it has this great size. After the air has passed through the electric flame, it contains about 1% of nitric oxide, NO. If it is cooled quickly, the nitric oxide unites with more oxygen, producing nitrogen dioxide. 2NO+02-2NO2.

The nitrogen dioxide is then passed through towers containing air and dripping water, with the result that nitric acid is produced. 4NO2+2H2O+02→4HNO3. 

The nitric acid is of course very dilute. It may be concentrated, or it may be converted into calcium nitrate for use as a soil fertilizer. How may this be done? 

239. The nitrogen cycle.-We have seen that there is a cycle, or circle of changes, for the oxygen and carbon dioxide of nature (§ 29). The air is the great reservoir from which the oxygen needed by plants and animals chiefly comes, and to which it largely returns. There is a similar cycle of changes for nitrogen. In this case, too, the air is the reservoir. Animals cannot build this nitrogen directly into the proteins which they must have; neither can most plants. But the nitrogen-fixing bacteria growing in the nodules of leguminous plants (§ 91) perform this service. The proteins which the bacteria produce afterwards decay and yield nitric acid and nitrates. 

As nitrates are very soluble, they are taken up in soil water by the roots of plants. The plants then build up the nitrogen of the nitrates, along with carbon, hydrogen, and oxygen, into proteins. Thus it happens that clover

fertilizes a soil not only for the clover plant, but also for the other crops grown upon that soil. The nitrogenfixing bacteria are cultivated by Departments of Agriculture, and can be purchased by farmers for the "inoculation" of seed.

The proteins are unstable compounds. As they decay, they produce ammonia and nitrates, also free nitrogen, which is returned to the air. When the waste products of animal and plant life are returned to the soil upon which the living forms grew, the supply of nitrogen is kept up for the growth of successive generations. But when these are carried away, by the transportation of food and forage crops to distant places, the soil becomes poor in nitrogen. In order that new crops may be grown, year after year, nitrogen compounds must be returned to the soil; if not by natural decay, then by artificial fertilizers containing nitrogen. It is for this reason that ammonium salts and nitrates are put into soils. Waste products of animal and plant life, as found in garbage and sewage, may be used for fertilizing soil, if care is taken to destroy dangerous bacteria. 

The need for combined nitrogen, both for explosives and soil fertilizers, makes the problem of the fixation of nitrogen one of enormous importance to our modern life. 

240. Summary.—Ammonia is a colorless gas of pungent odor, formed in the decay of complex nitrogen compounds. 

In the laboratory ammonia is prepared from an ammonium salt and a base. It is lighter than air and collected "over air.” 

On a commercial scale ammonia is obtained from the gases set

free in the "dry distillation" of coal; it is also prepared by the union of nitrogen and hydrogen by the Haber process. 

Ammonia is not readily combustible in air, but burns in oxygen. 

It is oxidized by air in the presence of a catalyst, also by cupric oxide.

Ammonia is very soluble in water, producing ammonia water. 

This contains the base, ammonium hydroxide. 

Ammonia is used for the production of ammonium salts and of ammonium hydroxide, in the manufacture of nitric acid and of soda, and in refrigeration and the making of ice. 

Ammonium salts are formed by the direct union of ammonia and acids, also by the neutralization of ammonium hydroxide by acids.

The oxides of nitrogen are nitrous oxide, nitric oxide, nitrogen trioxide, nitrogen dioxide and tetroxide, and nitrogen pentoxide. 

Nitrogen trioxide is the anhydride of nitrous acid. Nitrogen pentoxide is the anhydride of nitric acid. 

Nitric acid is prepared by the reaction between sodium or potassium nitrate and concentrated sulphuric acid.

Nitric acid is a strong acid, but unstable in the light, or when heated, especially in the presence of a reducing agent. 

Nitric acid oxidizes carbon to carbon dioxide, and sulphur to sulphuric acid. It reacts with most metals. 

With the less active metals nitric acid forms the nitrate of the metal, nitrogen dioxide or nitric oxide, and water. With zinc the dilute acid forms zinc nitrate, ammonium nitrate, and water. 

In aqua regia nitric acid oxidizes hydrochloric acid to water and chlorine.

All nitrates are decomposed by heat. Most nitrates give the oxide of the metal, nitrogen dioxide, and water. 

We test for a solid nitrate by means of concentrated sulphuric acid and copper; for a nitrate in solution by means of the brown ring test.

Nitric acid and nitrates are used in the preparation of explosives and dyes, as oxidizing agents, and as soil fertilizers. 

Nitrites give a brown gas (N2O3) when treated with dilute sulphuric acid.

Nitric oxide is colorless, but becomes brown in the air because of the formation of nitrogen dioxide, NO2. 

Nitrous oxide is laughing gas. It re-ignites a glowing splint, much as oxygen does. It is formed by the heating of ammonium nitrate.

Nitrogen is "fixed" by bacteria, by combination with hydrogen (Haber process), by union with calcium carbide (cyanamide process), and by union with oxygen (Birkeland-Eyde process).

Fixed nitrogen is necessary for plant growth.

CHAPTER XIX  

242. Kinds of chemical changes. In this chapter we are to consider the reasons why certain reactions take place. We have already studied the four general classes of chemical changes. Instances of each of these will be given again as a review: (1) Combination includes such reactions as those of metal with non-metal, other non-metals with oxygen, of oxides with water, and many more. 2Na+C12-2NaCl.

S+O2 SO2. SO3+H2O-H2SO4. (2) Decomposition has, as examples, the breaking up of an oxide by heat, the breaking up of a salt by the loss of oxygen, the electrolysis of a chloride, and the like. 2HgO→2Hg+02. 2KNO3-2KNO2+02. 

CaCl2 Ca+C12. (3) Displacement may be of hydrogen by a metal, of one metal by another, of one halogen by another, and so on. 

Mg+H2SO4 MgSO4+H2.

Cl₂+2KBr-2KC1+Br2. 292

(4) Double decomposition we see in the reactions of acids, bases, salts, and water. KOH+HBr→KBr+H₂O. NaC1+H2SO4 NaHSO4+HCI ↑ . 

NH,C1+NaOH—NaCl+NH4OH(NH3 | +H,O). 

243. Actions that are reversible. The equations just given are all written with arrows pointing from left to right to indicate that the change goes only in that direction. We have, however, had several cases in which we wrote an equation with a double arrow, to show that the change goes in opposite ways. We call such reactions reversible reactions. Some illustrations are: (1) Iron+steam iron oxide+hydrogen (§ 36). (2) Iron oxide+hydrogen-iron+steam (§ 40).

The equation for both of these is the reversible one: 3Fe+4H20 Fe3O4+4H2. (3) Nitrogen and hydrogen combine under the right conditions to give ammonia (§ 220), but under the same conditions ammonia is being broken up (dissociated) into nitrogen and hydrogen. N2+3H22NH3. 

Other reversible reactions are represented by the equations:

NH3+H₂ONH4OH. 

NH3+HCI NH4C1. 

2NO2 N2O4. 2S02+022SO3. 

244. How reactions become complete.-When we pass steam over heated iron (preceding section), the steam

sweeps out one product of the reaction: the hydrogen, as fast as it is formed. 3Fe+4H2O-Fe3O4+4H₂ ↑ . 

The hydrogen cannot, therefore, take part in the reverse action, and reduce the iron oxide. When we carry out the reverse reaction, and pass hydrogen over iron oxide, the excess of hydrogen sweeps out the steam as fast as it is formed.

Fe3O4+4H2-3Fe+4H₂O ↑. 

This action prevents the steam from reacting with the iron. Each of these reactions becomes complete, if we arrange matters so that one of the products of the reaction is removed from the "sphere of action." The reversible equation is true when steam acts upon hot iron, or hydrogen upon hot iron oxide, in a closed tube, so that all the four materials remain in contact with one another. If one of the materials can escape, the reaction goes in one direction and becomes complete. 

Reactions usually become complete in one of three ways: (1) One of the products is an insoluble gas, and passes off (is "volatile"), as in the preparation of hydrogen chloride.

Na++Cl-+H++HSO4¯→Na++HSO4-+HC1 ↑ . (2) One of the products is an insoluble solid (or liquid) and is precipitated from solution: Ag++NO3¯+H++Cl−→AgCl ↓ +H++NO3¯. Pb2++2NO3+2H++2C1→→PbCl₂ ↓ +2H++2NO3¯ The reaction with lead nitrate is less complete than

that with silver nitrate, because lead chloride is more soluble than silver chloride. (3) The third way is that the product though soluble, is non-ionized. This is the case in neutralization, which goes to practical completion because water is so slightly dissociated. That this union of H++OH- is really not complete is shown in the two-way equation, H₂OH++0H¯. 

As a result we have the phenomena of hydrolysis (§ 211), in which neutralization is reversed. In the hydrolysis of a salt the ions of water react with the ions of the salt to produce an acid and a base.

In all these reactions of double decomposition the change goes to completion to the extent that one of the products is removed from the others. The reaction by which hydrogen chloride is prepared is worthy of careful study, for we can easily carry out the reverse change. If we mix concentrated solutions of sodium hydrogen sulphate and hydrochloric acid, sodium chloride is precipitated.

Na++HSO4+H++C1--NaCl↓ +H++HSO¯. 

The reaction by which we prepare potassium nitrate (§ 231) from sodium nitrate and potassium chloride is an excellent illustration of a reversible reaction. If we mix ordinary solutions of these substances, we observe no result, not because double decomposition does not take place, but because all the products are soluble.

Na++NO3-+K++Cl¬¬Na++Cl−+K++NO;¯. 

But if we prepare hot, concentrated solutions of the two reacting salts, and mix them, sodium chloride, the least soluble of the four salts present, is largely precipitated. Na++NO+K++C1→→NaC1 | +K++NO3¯. 

245. Law of concentration.-It is now plain that many reactions that seem to be one-way reactions are really reversible. It is often necessary for the chemist to force

a two-way (reversible) reaction as far as possible in one direction. He does this by recognizing a rule called the law of concentration, often called the law of mass action. 

In our study of solutions (§ 74) we learned that the concentration of a dissolved substance is the amount of it in a given volume. If the concentration is small, the solution is dilute; if the concentration is large, the solution is a concentrated one. The law of concentration is: 

If we increase the relative amount (concentration) of one of the reacting substances in an equilibrium reaction, or decrease the relative amount of one of the products, we force the reaction toward completion in one direction. 

The three ways, already given, of making a reaction more complete (§ 244) all depend upon our decreasing the relative amount of one of the products. One of the products is removed: (1) as a gas that escapes; or (2) as an insoluble precipitate; or (3) as a non-ionized substance. 

As the substance is removed, its concentration becomes correspondingly less, so that it cannot take part to any great extent in the reverse action. 

The chemist also drives a reaction toward completion by increasing the relative amount-"active mass"-of one of the reacting substances. If we pass gaseous hydrogen chloride into a saturated solution of salt, some of the salt is precipitated from the solution. We cannot write an ordinary chemical equation for this reaction. In the salt solution there is salt in equilibrium with its ions (§ 205). 

NaCl Na++C1-.

When we add hydrogen chloride, we are putting in a large excess of chlorine ions. 

HCI H++Cl-.

The meeting of sodium ions with chlorine ions is now much more frequent, and more combination takes place between them. But as the solution was already saturated with sodium chloride before the hydrochloric acid was added, the excess of salt formed is precipitated out as crystals. We use this method in preparing pure sodium chloride.

Let us study another case. If we wish to precipitate the sulphuric acid in a solution, we use barium chloride. 2H++SO42-+Ba²++2C1→BaSO4+2H++2C1-. 

If we use exactly the amount represented by the equation, considerable barium sulphate remains in solution, since not even barium sulphate is entirely insoluble. The equilibrium equation between barium sulphate and its ions is: 2+ BaSO4 Ba²++SO4²¬ We can make the precipitation of barium sulphate more complete if we add more barium chloride than is needed. BaCl2

Ba²++2C1−. 

The excess of Ba ions causes the "capture" of more SO4 ions, with the formation of more precipitated barium sulphate. In the reaction between silver nitrate and hydrochloric acid:

Ag++NO+H++Cl→→AgCl ↓ +H++NO3, we make the precipitation of the AgCl more complete if we use an excess of either Ag or Cl ions, that is, either more silver nitrate solution, or more hydrochloric acid, than is needed according to the equation.

By recognizing the law of concentration the chemist can take a weak base, or weak acid, and make it weaker. 

Thus, as compared with sodium hydroxide, ammonium hydroxide is a weak base (§ 223). By this we mean that

the solution contains a relatively small number of OH ions. 

NH4OH NH4++OH−.

But the chemist often wants to use a base containing a still smaller concentration of OH ions than ammonium hydroxide has. He takes advantage of the rule of concentration, and adds ammonium chloride (NH4Cl) to the ammonium hydroxide. NHẠC.NH,++Cl-.

The excess of NH4 ions removes a considerable number of the OH ions by forcing them into combination as NHẠOH.

In the same way we decrease the concentration of H ions of a weak acid, such as acetic acid, by adding a very soluble salt of the acid, such as sodium acetate. The salt furnishes an excess of the negative ions of the acid. 

246. Effect of temperature, pressure, and catalysts. 

The reversible reactions we have studied are such as take place mainly in solution; there are also the reactions between gases, as between sulphur dioxide and oxygen, to produce sulphur trioxide (contact process, § 185). 

Then there is the reaction between nitrogen and hydrogen, to produce ammonia (Haber process, § 220). A third is the equilibrium between nitrogen dioxide and nitrogen tetroxide (§ 236): 

2NO2 N2O4.

In this last reaction a change in temperature causes the relative amounts of the two gases to change, or, as we say, causes the "equilibrium to be shifted." If the temperature is raised, the action is forced backward and

more of the brown NO2 is formed. If the temperature is lowered, more combination of NO2 molecules takes place to form the colorless substance, N2O4.

Generally a rise of about 10° C. in temperature doubles the speed of a reaction.

We have had several instances of the use of catalysts, as in the contact process and Haber process. We have also learned of the effect of manganese dioxide (MnO2) in hastening the decomposition of potassium chlorate (§ 20), of copper in hastening the reaction between zinc and dilute sulphuric acid (§ 37), and of platinum in speeding up the union of hydrogen and oxygen (§ 39). 

A catalyst does not alter the relative amounts of the reacting substances and the products in a reversible reaction; that is, it does not change the equilibrium. What it does is to cause the reaction to reach equilibrium with greater speed. Thus it makes a slow reaction useful, by cutting down the time required for the reaction to complete itself.

The Haber process of "fixing" nitrogen shows admirably how the chemist deals with a reversible reaction and makes it serve his purpose. The union of nitrogen and hydrogen takes place with an evolution of heat (see § 168 for heat of formation). 

N2+3H2-2NH3+24,000 calories.

The proper thing to do to help the reaction along is to use the lowest possible temperature at which the reaction will go at all. 

The equation also states that 1 volume of nitrogen combines with 3 volumes of hydrogen to form 2 volumes of ammonia. The volume of the product (ammonia) is less than the sum of the volumes of the reacting gases. The proper thing to do is to use as high a pressure as possible, since this, too, reduces the volume and so helps the reaction toward completion.

The catalyst (iron, or iron and molybdenum) speeds up the reaction, but a temperature of about 550° C. is needed to make the reaction rapid enough for manufacturing purposes. 

So the combination of conditions employed in the Haber process is to use the best catalyst that can be prepared, a pressure of about 200 atmospheres, and a temperature of about 550° C. 

One important precaution remains: the gases used must be free from certain impurities, or these will "poison" the catalyst and slow down or stop the reaction. 

247. Summary.-Many reactions are reversible, or two-way reactions.

The two opposite chemical changes in a reversible reaction continue even after equilibrium is reached. The equilibrium is kinetic.

Reactions usually become complete by the removal of at least one of the products: (1) as a gas; (2) as a precipitate; or (3) as a non-ionized substance. 

The law of concentration shows the chemist that he can force a reversible reaction nearer to completion by increasing the relative amount (active mass) of one of the reacting substances. 

In order to precipitate a substance as completely as possible we use an excess of one of its ions. 

We make a weak base still weaker, that is, we diminish its OH ions, by adding to it an excess of its positive (+) ion. 

We likewise decrease the concentration of the H ions of a weak acid by adding to the acid one of its soluble salts. This increases the concentration of the negative (−) ion. 

A change in temperature affects a reversible reaction by changing the relative amounts of reacting substances and products. 

A catalyst causes a reversible reaction to reach equilibrium quickly.

The Haber process takes place in the presence of a catalyst, and at as low a temperature and high a pressure as possible.

Is it possible to precipitate any AgI from a solution of AgCl? 

7. Why does quicklime liberate ammonia from ammonium chloride? Why does hydrochloric acid liberate carbon dioxide from marble? 

8. Why is not all of a given amount of oxygen converted into ozone by the electric discharge? 

9. Would you expect calcium sulphate (a slightly soluble salt) to be more soluble in water, or in dilute sulphuric acid? 

10. Why is ammonium hydroxide a weaker base when ammonium chloride is present? 

11. What is the effect of a catalyst upon a reaction?
