CHAPTER XX PHOSPHORUS 

249. Sources of phosphorus.-Most of the phosphorus found in nature is a constituent of some phosphate, chiefly of calcium phosphate, Ca3(PO4)2. This compound exists in all fertile soil and is taken up by plants. Bones and teeth are about 26% calcium phosphate. Phosphate rock, used as a fertilizer on the farm, comes from Ten- 

FIG. 129. 

Preparation of phosphorus in an electric furnace. nessee, the Carolinas, Florida, Georgia, and North Africa.

There is also a large deposit of phosphate rock in the group of states: Utah, Wyoming, Montana, and Idaho. 

250. How phosphorus is prepared. Phosphorus is prepared from calcium phosphate, at a high temperature, by reaction with carbon and sand (SiO2). 

The necessary temperature is obtained in an electric resistance furnace. As the electric current passes through the mixed materials, it meets with so much resistance that its energy is changed to heat. At the high temperature obtained the reaction takes place, and phosphorus vapor distils off. The vapor 302

PHOSPHORUS 

303 is condensed under water. The phosphorus is purified and then run into molds to harden. It comes into the market as a white, waxy solid. The equation is: 2Ca3(PO4)2+6SiO2+10C→6CaSiO3+10CO ↑ +P ↑ · If we write Caз(PO4)2 as 3CaO, P2O5, we see that the reaction consists in the replacement of P2O5 by SiO2 and the reduction of P2O5 by carbon. 

251. Properties of white phosphorus.-Phosphorus has two common forms: the white and the red. After being kept a short time, especially in the light, the white form becomes covered with a film of the red form, and looks yellow. Hence white phosphorus is often called yellow phosphorus. White phosphorus has a density of about 

1.8 grams per cu. cm., melts at 44° C., and boils at 280° 

C. It is insoluble in water, and is kept under water. 

The best solvent for it is carbon disulphide (CS2). 

White phosphorus is very poisonous if taken internally. 

It is used in poisons for roaches and rats.

The molecular weight of phosphorus, as determined from the weight of a given volume of its vapor, is 124. 

Consequently its formula in the vapor state is P4. 

White phosphorus is an exceedingly active element. It reacts with the oxygen of the air slowly even in the cold. It therefore glows (phosphoresces) if seen in the dark. From this it gets its name: phosphorus, which means "light producer." It takes fire from the heat of the hand (35° to 40° C.), and must be cut under water and handled with tongs. 

Phosphorus burns in air to form phosphorus pentoxide, P2O5. 

The flame in oxygen is of dazzling brilliancy. Phosphorus also unites energetically with chlorine, bromine, and iodine. With chlorine it forms the trichloride (PCl3), a colorless liquid. With an excess of chlorine, the pentachloride (PCl5), a solid, is produced.

Phosphorus also unites with sulphur. With the active metals it forms phosphides, e.g., CaзP2. Phosphor bronze is an alloy of phosphorus with copper and tin. It is not corroded by water. 

252. Red phosphorus.-When white phosphorus is heated to about 250°, it is changed to the red variety. 

The heating must be carried out in a closed vessel, and air must not be present. This form is a red powder having a density about 2.2. What is that of the white variety? Red phosphorus need not be kept under water, does not phosphoresce, is insoluble in carbon disulphide, and is not poisonous. It does not take fire below 240° C. 

When white phosphorus is changed into the red form, heat is given off. The red form has, therefore, less energy than the white. This fact accounts for its comparative inactivity.

When red phosphorus is heated, it is changed directly, without melting, into the vapor of white phosphorus. 

When this is condensed, white phosphorus is obtained. 

253. Matches. While some phosphorus is used for vermin poisons and phosphor bronze (§ 251), its chief use is for matches. Modern matches are of two kinds: (1) ordinary friction matches; (2) safety matches.

The friction match, as now made, consists of a stick of soft wood tipped with materials to set the stick on fire. The stick is first dipped a short distance (half an inch) into melted paraffin, or sulphur, to gain a coating that can be easily ignited. Then it is dipped into a mixture which gives the "head" to the match. This contains some active oxidizing agent such as potassium chlorate or red lead (Pb304). The "tip" of the match consists of phosphorus sulphide (PS3) and some rough material, such as powdered glass. Glue is used to hold

PHOSPHORUS 

305 the materials together. The tip has the low kindling material. When the tip is rubbed against a rough surface, the friction causes the phosphorus sulphide to react with the oxidizing material of the match head. Then the paraffin, or sulphur, is ignited, and this, in turn,  

FIG. 130. 

The large "brushes" on the left are not brushes, but matches being dipped into mixtures which form their tips. heats the wood to its kindling temperature. White phosphorus was formerly used in friction matches, but it caused so dreadful a disease ("phossy jaw") in those who worked with it that it was discontinued. 

Safety matches are made to avoid the fire-risk of ordinary friction matches. They are not easily set on fire by being rubbed

on rough surfaces, although they can generally be ignited on a smooth surface like glass. 

In the safety match the materials that react are divided. The match head contains no phosphorus compound, but oxidizing material (potassium chlorate or dichromate) and material to be oxidized, like antimony sulphide (Sb2S3). Powdered glass, together with glue (or dextrin), is also present. The match box has a striking surface consisting of red phosphorus, antimony 

FIG. 131. 

PH3 BURNING 

The phosphine prepared in the flask takes fire as it comes into the air and forms white vortex rings. sulphide, dextrin (or glue), and sand, or powdered glass. When the match head is rubbed against the prepared surface on the box, a bit of the red phosphorus is changed to white phosphorus. This by its burning starts the reaction on the match head.

Many fires are caused by the glowing ends of used matches, hence the sticks of some matches are treated with fire-proofing materials, such as alum. 254. Phosphorus compounds. Phosphorus forms a gaseous compound (PH₂) called phosphine, or hydrogen phosphide. This may be prepared from calcium phosphide (Ca3P2) by the action of water. Ca3P2+6HOH→3Ca(OH)2+2PH; ↑ . 

As ordinarily prepared, the gas takes fire when it comes in contact with air. Phosphine and hydrogen iodide unite to form phosphonium iodide, PH4I. 

Phosphorus forms a trioxide (P203) and a pentoxide (P2O5). 

Phosphorus pentoxide is formed as a white smoke when phosphorus burns in an abundant supply of air or oxygen. The smoke

PHOSPHORUS 

307 settles out, if the air (or oxygen) is dry, in the form of a white powder. When the pentoxide is put into water, the action is very violent. The final product is phosphoric acid, H3PO4. P2O5+3H2O→2H3PO4. 

Phosphorus pentoxide is a good drying agent for many gases owing to its great activity toward water. It is also called phosphoric anhydride. Phosphorus trioxide is the anhydride of phosphorous acid, H3POз. 

Phosphorus trichloride (PCl3) and pentachloride (PCl5) have already been described (§ 251). The reaction of phosphorus tribromide with water (hydrolysis) is described in § 163, that of phosphorus tri-iodide in § 166. 

255. Phosphoric acids and phosphates.-Phosphoric acid (H3PO4) is prepared from phosphorus pentoxide and water, or by the oxidation of red phosphorus with nitric acid (§ 229). 

5HNO3+P→H3PO4+H2O+5NO₂ ↑ . 

When the water and excess of nitric acid are evaporated, phosphoric acid remains as a colorless syrup. crystallizes when cold. This The commercial method is to treat phosphate rock with sulphuric acid. Calcium sulphate is precipitated, and phosphoric acid remains in solution. The solution is concentrated to a thick syrup. Ca3(PO4)2+3H2SO4→2H3PO4+3CaSO4. 

Phosphoric acid is a tribasic acid (§ 196), that is, it has in each molecule three hydrogen atoms that can be replaced, one at a time, by metals. It has, therefore, three sodium salts: (1) NaH2PO4, sodium di-hydrogen phosphate, or sodium acid phosphate, (2) Na2HPO4, di-sodium hydrogen phosphate, or sodium phosphate,

(3) Na3PO4, tri-sodium phosphate, or normal sodium phosphate. 

The formulas of the three calcium salts are Ca(H2PO4)2, CaHPO4, and Ca3(PO4)2. Sodium phosphate, Na₂HPO4, is used as a medicine. Calcium acid phosphate, Ca(H2PO4)2 is used in phosphate baking powders and as a fertilizer for soils. 

Besides ordinary phosphoric acid (H3PO4) there are metaphosphoric acid (HPO3) and pyrophosphoric acid (H4P2O7). All three have as their anhydride, phosphorus pentoxide (P2O5). 

Metaphosphoric acid, 2HPO3, is P2O5, H2O. 

Pyrophosphoric acid, H4P2O7, is P2O5, 2H2O. 

Phosphoric acid, 2H3PO4, is P205, 3H2O.

Metaphosphoric acid is the first product when phosphorus pentoxide is added to water. After standing, the solution contains phosphoric acid (H3PO4).

P2O5+H2O-2HPO3. HPO3+H2O-H3PO4. 

256. Phosphates and fertilizers.-In § 239 we learned of the need of nitrogen fertilizers for soils. To be fertile, a soil must also contain phosphorus compounds and potassium compounds. A complete fertilizer contains all three of these elements. Calcium phosphate is only very slightly dissolved by water, hence manufacturers of fertilizers often treat it with sulphuric acid, to produce the calcium acid phosphate, which is soluble. Ca3(PO4)2+2H2SO4+4H2O⇒2(CaSO4, 2H2O)+Ca (H₂PO4)2. 

The mixture of the calcium acid phosphate and the hydrate of calcium sulphate (gypsum) is called "superphosphate of lime," or "land plaster."

The potassium compounds needed by soils could be obtained from wood ashes, which contain potassium carbonate (K2CO3); but these are not abundant enough. 

Some kinds of lava give a potassium fertilizer. The usual source of potassium compounds on a large scale is some

PHOSPHORUS 

309 deposit found in France and Germany. The potassium salt used in fertilizer is usually the sulphate or chloride (K2SO4 or KCI). 

Under certain conditions soils become acid, or "sour," and lose their productiveness. The cure is a base, such as lime or limestone. This neutralizes the acid and "sweetens" the soil. 

257. Arsenic (As).-Arsenic, like phosphorus, belongs to the nitrogen family of elements (§ 262). Antimony and bismuth are likewise members of this family. 

Arsenic is prepared from its ores, first, by roasting them in air, to convert them into the trioxide (As2O3), and then by reducing the trioxide with carbon. As2O3+3C-2As+3CO ↑. 

258. Properties and compounds.-Arsenic is a gray solid with a crystalline structure. When freshly prepared, it has a luster like a metal, but this is soon tarnished. When struck, arsenic crumbles to powder. It burns in air, forming arsenic trioxide. 

This is also called "white arsenic." Arsenic burns in chlorine, producing the trichloride, AsCl¸. The resemblance between arsenic and phosphorus appears chiefly in the valence of the two elements and, therefore, in the corresponding formulas of compounds. The vapor of arsenic has the formula As4. The oxides, As2O3 and As2O5 correspond to P2O3 and P2O5, and AsCl3 corresponds to PCl3. Arsenic forms a hydrogen compound, AsHз, which is called hydrogen arsenide, or arsine. This corresponds to phosphine, PH3. Arsenic forms a disulphide, As2S2, a trisulphide (As2S3), and a pentasulphide (As2S5). These are all bright-yellow solids.

Arsenic trioxide is the anhydride of arsenious acid, and arsenic pentoxide is the anhydride of arsenic acid. As2O3+3H20→2H¿ASO3. As2O5+3H20-2H3ASO4.

The salts of arsenious acid are called arsenites; those of arsenic acid, arsenates. 

259. Uses.-Two arsenic sulphides: As2S2 and As2S3, are found in nature and used as pigments. Arsenic trioxide is used in medicine and as a poison, also in the manufacture of glass. Lead arsenate and calcium arsenate are used in arsenic sprays for fruit trees. Paris green is a 

Courtesy Bureau of Entomology, U. S. Dept. of Agriculture. 

FIG. 132. 

Spraying an orchard with a lead arsenate mixture. compound containing both copper arsenite and copper acetate. It is used as an insecticide. Arsenic compounds are also used as weed killers, "dips" for cattle, and as preservatives for hides. 

Arsenic itself is used in the manufacture of shot. Shot is made from melted lead. When the lead falls (in a shot tower), the lead drops take the form of spheres. 

Lead alone cools too soon, before the spheres have become perfect. Hence arsenic is added to lower the freezing point of the lead, so that the freezing may take place

PHOSPHORUS 

311 more slowly. The action is like that of salt in lowering the freezing point of water (§ 78). Shot containing arsenic is also harder than if made of lead alone. 

260. Antimony (Sb). Most of our antimony comes from China. It is found free and in the form of the sulphide, Sb2S3. The sulphide may be melted with iron. 3Fe+Sb2S3-3FeS+2Sb. 

Antimony is like a metal in having a bright, silvery luster, but it is unlike most metals in that it can not be hammered into sheets, that is, it is not malleable. Its luster does not tarnish in air, and antimony is therefore, used to cover metals that do tarnish. 

When antimony melts, there is a contraction in volume. 

When the melted antimony solidifies, there is an expansion. This property makes antimony valuable as a component of alloys like type metal, for the casting (the type) must have sharp outlines. Liquid lead, for example, shrinks on solidifying. A casting made from it does not fill a mold sharply. When antimony is added to lead and tin, the resulting alloy is used as type metal. 

Two other alloys containing antimony are Babbitt's metal, used for bearings, to lower friction, and britannia metal, used for making table-ware. 

Antimony combines with chlorine to form a trichloride, SbCl3, as arsenic and phosphorus do. When cold, this forms a pasty mass, called "butter of antimony." Antimony chloride is hydrolyzed by water, giving the insoluble compound, SbOCl, but the reaction is a reversible one, and is forced backward by an excess of hydrochloric acid.

SbC1:+2H2O-Sb(OH)2C1+2HC1. Sb(OH)2C1→SbOC1 | +H₂O.

Antimony burns in air to form antimony trioxide, Sb2O3. There is also a tetroxide (Sb2O4) and a pentoxide (Sb2O5). The trioxide is the anhydride of antimonious acid; the pentoxide, of antimonic acid.

Sb2O3+3H20→2H3SbO3. Sb2O5+3H20→2H3SbO4. 

With concentrated sulphuric acid antimony reacts as a metal does 

(§§ 181 and 188). 

2Sb+6H2SO4 Sb2(SO4)3+3SO₂ ↑ +6H₂O. 

In this respect it differs from phosphorus and arsenic, but resembles bismuth.

Besides its use in alloys, antimony finds application in the vulcanizing of "red" rubber. Tartar emetic, K(SbO)C4H4O6, is a white solid used as a medicine and as a mordant, in the dyeing of fabrics. 

261. Bismuth.-Bismuth looks much like antimony, but is distinctly reddish. Like antimony, the melted metal expands when it becomes solid. It is used chiefly as a component of fusible, or low melting alloys. The best known are Wood's metal, which melts about 60° C. and Rose's metal, which melts at about 94° C. Wood's metal contains 4 parts by weight of bismuth, 2 of lead, 1 of cadmium, and 1 of tin. Rose's metal has 9 parts bismuth and 1 each of lead and tin. The effect of the presence of one metal in lowering the melting point of another is like that of arsenic in lead (§ 259).

Fusible alloys are useful for a number of safety devices. Thus, in the electric wiring of our houses, each circuit has a fuse of lowmelting alloy. If there is too much current in the circuit, so that the wires become too hot, the fuse melts and breaks the circuit. 

An automatic sprinkler has a safety plug which melts when the temperature reaches a certain degree, and releases water to put out the fire. An automatic fire alarm works in the same way, also

PHOSPHORUS 

313 the automatic fire doors of buildings and of fireproof safes. Boilers have safety plugs which melt when the water becomes too low or the steam pressure too high.

Bismuth burns at red heat, forming the trioxide, Bi2O3. The hydroxide is Bi(OH)3. 

Bismuth chloride (BiCls) and nitrate (Bi(NO3)3), are hydrolyzed by water, giving insoluble basic salts, or oxy salts. Bi(NO3)3+2H₂O→Bi(OH)2NO3+2HNO3. Bi(OH)2NO3→BIONO; ↓ +H₂O. 

Bismuth oxynitrate is also called the sub-nitrate of bismuth. It is used in medicine, also as a face powder. 

262. The nitrogen family. The four elements described in this chapter, together with nitrogen, form a natural

263. Summary.-Phosphorus is prepared from calcium phosphate, sand, and carbon, heated in an electric furnace. 

Phosphorus has a white and a red form.

White phosphorus is low melting, soluble in carbon disulphide, must be kept under water to prevent ignition, is very poisonous, and glows (phosphoresces) in the dark. 

Red phosphorus does not melt, but at a high temperature forms the vapor of white phosphorus. Red phosphorus has a high ignition point, is insoluble in carbon disulphide, can be kept in air, is not poisonous, and does not phosphoresce. 

Phosphorus sulphide is used in friction matches; red phosphorus is put on the scratching surface of safety-match boxes. 

Phosphorus burns to form phosphorus trioxide and pentoxide. 

These are the anhydrides of phosphorous acid and phosphoric acid, respectively.

Phosphoric acid is prepared commercially by the double decomposition between calcium phosphate and sulphuric acid. 

Phosphoric acid is a tribasic acid, and forms two acid salts besides the normal salt. 

Phosphorus compounds are needed by animals and plants and must be present in fertile soil. A "complete" fertilizer contains nitrogen, phosphorus, and potassium compounds. 

Arsenic is prepared by the reduction of its oxide with carbon. 

Arsenic forms oxides and a hydride, arsine. Its oxides are anhydrides of arsenious and arsenic acids. 

Arsenic is used as a component of "lead" shot, to make the lead solidify at a lower temperature. 

Arsenic compounds are used in poisons for insects and weeds, as pigments, as constituents of glass, and as a preservative of skins. 

Antimony is a bright metal used chiefly in type metal and other alloys, and in vulcanizing red rubber. It burns in air and

combines with chlorine. Its oxides are the anhydrides of weak acids.

Bismuth is a heavy metal used chiefly in making low-melting alloys. These are used in many safety devices. Bismuth forms bismuth hydroxide, a base, without acid properties. 

The nitrogen family includes nitrogen, phosphorus, arsenic, antimony, and bismuth. The valences of these elements in their compounds are essentially the same. In their other properties the elements show a gradation in the order of the atomic weights.

CHAPTER XXI CARBON 

265. Importance of carbon.-Carbon is important not only in its uncombined forms, such as diamond, graphite, and charcoal, but because of its many compounds. There are literally hundreds of thousands of carbon compounds. 

These are studied mainly in "organic chemistry." Many "organic" compounds are found in nature, but many others are produced in chemical laboratories and factories. 

Among the natural compounds of carbon are carbon dioxide, the sugars, starches, fats, and celluloses. Among the artificially prepared carbon compounds are substances used as dyes, cleaning fluids, organic solvents, drugs, textiles, and explosives. The bodies of all living creatures consist largely of complex carbon compounds. Limestone, which forms great deposits in the earth's crust, is calcium carbonate, CaCO3. 

266. The charring process.-The "burnt" end of a match is not wood, but charcoal; so are the black remains of a wood fire. If we heat wood in a tube or flask, combustible gases escape, and a residue of charcoal remains. 

If we heat soft (bituminous) coal in the same way, coke remains. Both charcoal and coke consist largely of the element carbon, but contain also the minerals of the original wood and coal. We usually represent them by the symbol, C. 318

CARBON 

319 Most organic substances behave like wood when heated. They char, or "turn to carbon," while much of their material escapes in gaseous form. We see this process when we scorch toast, or cloth, or sugar, or milk. 

The high-boiling liquids present in petroleum are overheated and charred purposely, so that they may break down into lowboiling gasoline. The process is known as the "cracking" of the petroleum. Coal is the residue left when wood and other plant materials of past geological 

FIG. 133. 

Heating wood and collecting the liquid and gaseous products. Two ages decayed slowly under layers of mud and sand. 

267. Forms of carbon.-Carbon, like sulphur and phosphorus, exists in several allotropic forms. forms: diamond, and graphite (“black lead") are crystalline; such forms as coal, coke, charcoal, and lampblack do not seem to be crystalline, and are called amorphous (that is, "shapeless") substances. But when charcoal is examined by means of x-rays (§314), it is found to consist of crystals just like those of graphite, but very small. So charcoal cannot be classed as amorphous. In its appearance diamond is not at all like the other forms of carbon. But when a diamond is heated between the poles of a powerful battery, it is changed to graphite, and when it is burned in oxygen it produces only carbon dioxide and a small amount of ashes. 

268. Wood charcoal.-Men still prepare much char-

coal by setting fire to heaps of wood covered with sod. 

Small openings admit a little air and allow gases to escape. Part of the wood burns, but in doing so chars the rest.

The modern method of preparing charcoal is to heat wood in iron or steel retorts with condensers and gas receivers attached, so that the by-products may be collected and used. The liquid by-products consist of wood alcohol (also called methyl alcohol), acetic acid (the acid of vinegar), and acetone. There is also a tarry material and much water. About 20-25% of the weight of the wood is obtained as charcoal. A very fine quality of charcoal is made from cocoanut shells and fruit pits. 

Pure charcoal, free from ash, may be prepared from pure cane sugar.

The uses of charcoal depend upon its peculiar properties. It is very porous, and the total surface of its pores (its "internal" surface) is enormous when compared with the volume of the charcoal lump. When freshly prepared charcoal is put into such gases as ammonia, hydrogen sulphide, or oxygen, it takes them up in its pores. One volume of boxwood charcoal can hold 90 volumes of ammonia, or 9 volumes of oxygen. The chemist calls this phenomenon, adsorption, and says that the surfaces of the pores "adsorb" the gases. After charcoal has adsorbed a great deal of air, it is lighter than water, and floats. After the air has been expelled, it sinks. 

Because of its power to adsorb gases charcoal is used to deodorize air and to remove gases like hydrogen sulphide from liquids. 

It is also used in gas masks, to adsorb poisonous gases. Charcoal can also remove the organic coloring matter from liquids that are filtered through it.

CARBON 321 Charcoal unites readily with oxygen when heated, and is therefore used as a fuel, and as a reducing agent, to remove oxygen from oxides of metals (§ 40). 2 CuO+C→2Cu+CO₂ ↑ . 2 PbO+C-2Pb+CO2 ↑. 

269. Animal charcoal.-Just as wood charcoal is prepared by the "destructive distillation" of wood, so bone black, or animal charcoal, is prepared by the destructive distillation of bones (or other animal material). Animal charcoal is especially good for removing organic coloring substances from liquids. It is therefore used to decolorize solutions of crude (brown) sugar, in the preparation of white, granulated sugar; also to clarify vinegar. Animal charcoal is also used to decolorize products obtained from petroleum. 

FIG. 134. 

Herbert Photos

A gas mask containing charcoal as an adsorbing agent. 

270. Lampblack. To prepare lampblack, or soot, we burn carbon compounds with insufficient air so that they have sooty flames, and then collect the unburned carbon upon some condensing surface. For crude lampblack resinous wood is burned in shacks, and the smoke is collected upon burlap strips. 

For the best quality of lampblack oil or gas is used. The smoking flame is allowed to deposit its carbon upon a revolving condenser cooled with water. Carbon black is obtained by the incomplete burning of natural gas. 

Lampblack is a soft powder. It is used in preparing

printers' ink, India ink, shoe polishes, typewriter ribbons, and black paints. It gives toughness to rubber tires, and the ability to resist the action of water. 

271. Coke.-Coke is what is left when soft coal is "cracked," or distilled destructively. It is a gray solid, harder than charcoal, and of greater density. Coke is 

PUSHING COKE OUT OF RETORT FIG. 135. 

Preparation of by-product coke (side view of retort). When all the volatile products have been driven off, a powerful "arm" pushes the hot coke into cars containing water to "quench" it. readily oxidized and forms a very efficient, smokeless fuel. It is used in enormous amounts in the preparation of metals from their ores (§§ 385 and 411). 

Formerly all coke was prepared in "bee-hive" ovens, in which the coal was partly burned, without sufficient air to burn all of it. The burned part of the coal furnished the heat for the "coking" of the remainder. All that was obtained from the process was the coke. "Byproduct" coke ovens have replaced the bee-hive type. In the by-product ovens coke is obtained, as before, but all

CARBON 

323 gaseous and liquid products of the cracking process are obtained, too. These include combustible gases, benzene, coal tar, and ammonia. Out of the benzene and coal tar we obtain a large number of organic chemicals which are very valuable sources of dyes, drugs, and flavoring substances. 

Gas carbon is the coke that collects upon the walls of the retorts. It is a good conductor of electricity and is used for the carbons of "arc" lamps and the negative plates of electric cells. Oil coke is the solid left after the distillation and "cracking" of petroleum.

VOLATILE BY-PRODUCTS TO CONDENSERS FIG. 136. 

By-product coke retorts, end view. 

272. Diamond.-Diamonds are found chiefly in Brazil and 

South Africa. They form natural crystals. They are the hardest of substances, and the densest form of carbon (3.5 g. per cu.cm.). Like other forms of carbon they are hardly affected by acids and alkalies, but they burn when heated in oxygen to about 700° C. 

As jewels, diamonds are prized for their luster and their ability to refract and reflect light. They are “cut” by grinding them with diamond dust. The form given a stone is for the purpose of producing the greatest reflection of light from its interior. Diamonds are weighed in carats. A carat is one-fifth of a gram. The largest diamond yet found (the Cullinan diamond) weighed 3032 carats. Diamonds of low grade, especially black diamonds, are used for glass cutters and rock drills, as well

as for the powder with which jewel diamonds are cut and polished. 'Artificial diamonds have been made by the crystallization of carbon from solution in melted iron. If the melted mixture cools slowly, graphite crystallizes out; but if it is chilled suddenly, small diamonds are formed instead. The chilling of the exterior produces great pressure upon the interior of the "solution" while the dissolved carbon is crystallizing. 

273. Graphite.-Graphite is also called plumbago, or black lead. It consists of crystals, but of a very different form from those of the diamond. Graphite crystals are gray, or black, six-sided plates. These give, when powdered, small scales that slide easily over one another. 

As a result graphite is used to lubricate machinery, so as to diminish friction. When made into an emulsion with water or oil, graphite forms an especially good lubricant. 

Because graphite is easily rubbed off, it is used for "lead" pencils. Clay is mixed with the graphite in different amounts to make the pencils "hard" or "soft."

Graphite is also used as a stove polish. Graphite is so difficult to melt that it is used for crucibles which must be heated to a very high temperature. Because graphite is a conductor of electricity, it is used for electrodes in the electrolysis of salts, especially chlorides, on a commercial scale. A thin layer of it is used in electrotyping (§ 387), to give a conducting surface on which copper can be deposited. Much graphite is now manufactured from coke in an electric furnace. 

274. Electric furnace.—The arc furnace (Fig. 137) is essentially an electric arc lamp with carbon rods. The rods are first placed together and the current turned on. When the current flows, the

CARBON 

325 rods are separated. The resistance developed between the separated rods produces an arc of carbon vapor with a very high temperature (3000° C. or above). The arc is surrounded by a covering of very difficultly fusible materials, such as asbestos and magnesium oxide.

FIG. 137. 

In graphite manufacture, coke is mixed with pitch and a little sand. The mixture is placed between electrodes and the current is turned on. As the current flows through the mixture, it develops a very great resistance. The current does not produce electrolysis, but only a very high temperature. A charge of several tons can be heated at one time. Graphite made in this way is very pure. 

275. Chemical properties of carbon.-Carbon is not soluble in the usual solvents 

Courtesy Acheson Oildag Co.

FIG. 138. 

Edward Goodrich Acheson (1856- ). 

Inventor of "carborundum," "silalso of several products containing graphite in a colloidal state. 

A small arc furnace for producing high temperatures. and does not react readily with acid or bases. Its chief chemical property is that it combines with oxygen to form carbon monoxide or dioxide. It reacts with oxidizing acids, such as nitric acid (§ 229) and sulphuric acid (§ 188). Carbon also takes oxygen from the oxides of many metals. It thus acts as a reducing agent.

Carbon unites directly with sulphur (§ 177) to form carbon oxicon," and artificial graphite, disulphide, CS2. It also unites with metals, such as iron and calcium, as well as with silicon (Si), to produce carbides. Calcium carbide (CaC2; § 293) and silicon carbide (SiC; § 297) are both very important

substances.

Both are prepared in electric furnaces. 

Carbon and chlorine do not unite directly. Indirectly, by the action of chlorine upon carbon disulphide, the union of the elements is brought about, and carbon tetrachloride is formed.

CS2+3C12-CC14+S2C12. 

This is a well-known, colorless liquid boiling at 77° C. 

It is used in "dry cleaning," also to extract grease and oil from wool and seeds. It has an advantage over gasoline in that it is not inflammable. It is also used in "Pyrene" fire extinguishers (§ 28). 

276. Coal and its fuel value.-As we have already learned (§ 266) coal is the residue left from the slow decay of the living vegetation of the past. Plant materials: wood, leaves, and roots, accumulated for a long time, much as peat accumulates today in forest swamps. 

Then they were buried under beds of mud and sand. 

Thus they were kept away from the oxygen of the air and were subjected to pressure and increase of temperature. As a result we have coal. We may call coal fossil wood. Often the structure of the original wood and leaves is plainly seen in the coal. During the process of coal formation gaseous material escaped more or less completely. Anthracite, or hard coal contains 90 per cent, or more, of carbon, most of it uncombined. When it burns, there is very little flame. Why? What is flame? See § 7. In bituminous (soft) coal the change is not so complete, and many compounds of carbon remain. 

When heat is applied to soft coal, gases are driven off. 

As a result soft coal burns with a large flame. 

When we use carbon as a fuel, we carry out its oxida-

CARBON 

327 tion for the sake of the heat energy given off. The coal and oxygen possess potential chemical energy (§ 14), which appears when they unite. We measure the value of a coal as a fuel by determining how many heat units are given off when a given weight of the coal is burned. 

The unit used in science is the calorie (cal.; § 61). Define a calorie. A large calorie is 1000 calories. A unit used by engineers is the British Thermal Unit (B.T.U.). This is the amount of heat needed to heat one pound of water through one Fahrenheit degree, as from 60° to 61° F. 

One British Thermal Unit equals 252 calories. A reaction in which heat is given off is called an exothermic reaction; one in which it is absorbed is endothermic. 

277. How heat is measured.-The instrument by which we determine the amount of heat produced in a reaction is called a calorimeter (kǎl'ō-rìm´ē-ter). 

This is a metal vessel (bomb) in which a body may be surrounded by compressed oxygen and burned. Instead of oxygen, an oxidizing agent, like sodium peroxide (Na2O2), may be used. To find the heat-producing power of a coal we put a sample of it (about 1 g.) into the bomb calorimeter, add the necessary oxygen, and set the coal on fire by a wire heated by an electric current. The heat given off in the burning is taken up by a known amount of water in a vessel surrounding the bomb. From the weight of the water taken and the number of Centigrade degrees through which the water is heated, we obtain the number of calories of heat produced. There must be an accurate thermometer to measure the change in temperature, and a stirrer to mix the water so that it will all be of 

SUBSTANCE TO BE BURNED FIG. 139. 

A calorimeter. Note the devices for burning the substance and for stirring the water surrounding the "bomb."

the same temperature. The apparatus must also be heat-insulated by non-conducting materials. Corrections must be made for the heat taken up by the calorimeter itself. 

By the complete burning of 12 grams (a gram-atomic weight) of carbon, 44 grams of carbon dioxide are obtained and 96,480 calories are set free (§ 41).

C+O2 CO2 ↑ +96,480 calories. 

By means of a calorimeter a factory or railroad using a large amount of coal may test the heating value of the coal it buys. 

The investigator of foods can determine their heating value in the same way. What do we mean by a "100-calorie" portion of bread?

In the calculation of food values the large calorie (1000 cal.) is used. 

278. Smoke. When coal is burned improperly, a great deal of it may escape unburned, as soot. There may also be a great waste of the combustible gas, carbon monoxide (CO), which has a high fuel value. The remedy is a furnace which has the proper construction, so that the right amount of air may enter. There must also be scientific stoking, so that coal may be added in the proper way. The unburned carbon of smoke is a waste of valuable fuel and a serious menace to the health and cleanliness of all who live in the neighborhood. A smoky town is sometimes thought of as a busy town; it is really a wasteful town. 

279. Carbon dioxide (CO2).-Carbon dioxide is found in the air, in natural waters, in mines (as choke damp), and in the gases given off by certain volcanoes. In some places it issues from the earth. Carbon dioxide is constantly being poured into the air by the oxidation of carbon and its compounds. Name the different ways in which this takes place (§§ 29 and 93).

329 280. Preparation. We commonly prepare carbon dioxide in the laboratory by treating a carbonate, such as marble, with a dilute acid. The gas may be collected over water or under air.

CaCO3+2HC1→CaCl2+H2CO3(H2O+CO₂ ↑ ).

Laboratory preparation of carbon dioxide. It may be collected over water or under air. is used commercially because of its cheapness, but the other product, calcium sulphate, is very slightly soluble, and soon covers the limestone with a deposit. This slows the reaction.

281. Physical properties.-Carbon dioxide is colorless, like air and oxygen. The gas is about 1½ times as dense as air and may be poured like water from one vessel to another. Calculate the weight of one liter of it under standard conditions.

About 180 cu.cm. of carbon dioxide dissolve in 100 cu.cm. of water at 0° C. and 760 mm. The solution

has a mild, acid taste. The solubility of the gas increases proportionally to the pressure (Henry's law). If water is charged with carbon dioxide at, say, two atmospheres pressure, and the pressure is then lowered to one atmosphere, the excess of dissolved gas escapes, and the solution effervesces like soda water. Beneath the earth's surface more carbon dioxide can dissolve in water than at the surface, because of the increased pressure. 

Springs of such water ("carbonated" springs) likewise effervesce on reaching the surface.

AL 

FIG. 141. 

Pouring carbon dioxide from a bottle into a beaker. Why does the left pan of the balance go down?

Carbon dioxide may be liquefied by sufficient pressure, if the temperature is kept below 31° C., the critical temperature of this gas. 

The liquid is colorless, like water. 

If some of it is evaporated rapidly, so much heat is taken up from the remaining liquid that it is frozen to a white, crystal mass resembling snow. This is now pressed into compact form and used in refrigeration, especially to keep "ice cream" from melting and to preserve fresh fish and other foods in transportation. Solid carbon dioxide vaporizes without melting; as a result it may be used with food products in paper cartons-a decided advantage over the use of the ice-salt mixture. 

282. Chemical properties. Toward ordinary burning bodies gaseous carbon dioxide is inactive. It extinguishes a burning match, or candle, or piece of charcoal, instantly.

Carbon dioxide is not, however, entirely inactive to burning bodies. Thus, a briskly burning metal, such as sodium or magnesium, continues to burn when put into the gas.

Red-hot carbon also removes half of the oxygen of the carbon dioxide molecule, reducing it to carbon monoxide. C+CO2-2CO. 

The reduction of carbon dioxide is seen most commonly in a coal stove or furnace. 

At the bottom of the fire the supply of oxygen is abundant, and carbon dioxide is formed. But as the carbon dioxide passes through the hot carbon, it is reduced to carbon monoxide. 

Then, at the top of the fire, where the supply of oxygen is again abundant, the carbon monoxide

283. Uses of carbon dioxide. The uses of carbon dioxide depend upon its properties. It is present in effervescent drinks, such as soda water and pop, and escapes from them at ordinary temperature and pressure. Its solid form is used as a refrigerant. 

Liquid carbon dioxide is used, instead of liquid ammonia, to cool brine and freeze water (§ 224).

Carbon dioxide is used to make dough "light," or porous. In bread making it is prepared by the fermentation of sugar or starch by means of a ferment present in yeast. The change is partly represented by the equation, C&H12O6(grape sugar)→2CO2+2C2H6O (alcohol).

CARBON 333 Leavening means "lightening." It consists in giving a large surface to a given amount of dough. Bubbles of a gas are needed. In beaten eggs air bubbles serve the purpose. Yeast and baking powders give carbon dioxide bubbles dispersed through the dough.

Baking powders contain baking soda and some substance to liberate its carbon dioxide. A "cream of tartar" baking powder contains potassium hydrogen tartrate, KHC4H4O6 (cream of tartar) and sodium hydrogen carbonate, NaHCO3 (baking soda or sodium bicarbonate) together with flour or corn starch to dilute the mixture. Some tartaric acid may be present. The carbon dioxide is given off when the powder is moistened.

Carbon dioxide is also used as a fire extinguisher (§ 28). 

Foamite is a modern fire extinguisher used to combat large fires of burning petroleum. It consists essentially of a mixture of baking soda and aluminum sulphate with an extract of licorice root. When these materials, with water, are thrown upon a fire, the licorice root binds the carbon dioxide together in a thick foam, or froth. This foam covers the burning material with a blanket,

286. Physical properties.-Car bon monoxide is only slightly soluble. It is colorless and tasteless. It is odorless, if pure. It is difficult to liquefy, since it has a low critical temperature.

Cal- culate the weight of the grammolecular volume (22.4 liters) of carbon monoxide and the weight of one liter. Is the gas heavier, or lighter, than air? 

287. Chemical properties and uses.-Carbon monoxide burns with a blue flame giving carbon dioxide. We see its flame when fresh coal or coke is put upon a red-hot bed of coals. Carbon monoxide reduces the oxides of many metals. The oxides must be hot. CuO+CO-Cu+CO2. 

In the blast furnace (§ 412), in which iron is prepared from ferric oxide, the coal or coke used is largely oxidized to carbon monoxide, which, being a gas, penetrates the heated iron ore and reduces it to iron. Fe2O3+3CO→2Fe+3CO₂ ↑ ·

CARBON 

337 What is the valence of carbon in carbon monoxide? 

Instead of uniting with oxygen, carbon monoxide can unite with chlorine, forming phosgene, COC12. Write the equation.

Carbon monoxide is a very poisonous gas; even a small amount in much air produces very serious effects. It unites with the hemoglobin in the red blood corpuscles and so prevents the blood from taking up the necessary oxygen. Fuel gases, such as water gas and producer gas, are poisonous chiefly because of its presence. The incomplete combustion of gasoline in automobile engines also produces the gas. Many persons have died because they have allowed automobile engines to run in closed garages. 

288. Hydrocyanic acid (HCN).-Hydrocyanic acid is also called prussic acid. It is a colorless liquid prepared from sodium cyanide or potassium cyanide and dilute sulphuric acid. The liquid and its vapor are extremely poisonous. KCN+H,SO4→HCN | +KHSO4. 

Hydrocyanic acid is used on fruit trees as an insecticide. Its soluble salts, such as potassium and sodium cyanides, are also very poisonous. When silver cyanide (AgCN) or mercuric cyanide. [Hg(CN)2] is heated, cyanogen (C2N2) is produced. This is a colorless, poisonous gas which burns readily in air. Hg(CN)2-Hg+C₂N2 ↑ •

289. Gaseous fuels and illuminants.-Fuels may be solids, liquids, or gases. Practically all of them consist of carbon or compounds of carbon with hydrogen. Among solids, coal, coke, charcoal, and wood are the most common. Among liquids, gasoline, kerosene, and crude

petroleum are the most important. Among gaseous fuels, we have natural gas, coal gas, water gas, producer gas, oil gases, and acetylene. Of course hydrogen and carbon monoxide must be included. While gasoline is ordinarily a liquid, yet when used as a fuel it is in the gaseous state, whether in a gasoline torch, a cook stove, or a gasoline engine. Natural gas is largely methane, or marsh gas (§ 444).

Coal gas (Fig. 148) is the mixture of combustible gases formed when soft coal is heated. The retorts are prac-

290. Water gas. In the water-gas process (Fig. 149) steam is forced over white-hot coke or anthracite coal. 

C+H,O−CO 1+H,1. 2 Water gas is essentially a mixture of carbon monoxide and hydrogen. It has a high heating value, but burns with a practically colorless flame. Why? To make an illuminating gas of it, it is "enriched" by hydrocarbons obtained from petroleum.

The coal or coke is raised to white heat by a blast of air. Then the air is cut off and steam is blown through the hot carbon. The mixture of CO and H2 is forced into superheaters in which petroleum is being "cracked" by heat. Here the "enriching" takes place. From the superheaters the gas is forced through the scrubbers, where it is washed, and through a condenser in which any volatile petroleum oils are removed. The gas is stored for use, as in the case of coal gas. 

291. Producer gas.-Producer gas is made when air in limited amount is forced through hot coal or coke. Partial oxidation of the carbon takes place, and carbon monoxide is formed. This, mixed with nitrogen from the air that was used, forms a cheap fuel gas for factory use. Although the gas contains about 60% nitrogen, it burns readily. Sometimes steam, as well as air, is blown through the burning carbon, and a mixture of producer gas and water gas is obtained. 

292. Oil gases.-Oil gases result from the "cracking" of petroleum. Such a gas is Pintsch gas, at one time very generally used for lighting railway coaches. Blaugas (meaning "blue" gas) is an oil gas. It is stored in cylinders and used for gas stoves in country places. It is a mixture of liquid and gaseous hydrocarbons kept under pressure until wanted as gas. Some oil gases are stored in gas holders after being washed and purified, as coal gas is. Byproducts from the oil gas manufacture are naphthalene (C10H8) and finely divided carbon. The carbon is made into briquettes, for fuel (§ 271). 

293. Acetylene.-Acetylene is one of the hydrocarbons present in coal gas. It is formed when a Bunsen burner "strikes back" and burns at the base. The gas is prepared by the reaction between water and calcium carbide. CaC₂+2H2O→Ca(OH)2+C₂H₂ ↑ · A jet of acetylene burns with a very smoky flame, but

CARBON 

341 the flame is of dazzling brightness if a proper burner is used. 2C2H2+502-4CO2+2H2O+313,800 cal. In

Acetylene is an unstable compound. In order to decompose carbon dioxide or water into its elements we must use up a great deal of energy. the case of acetylene the opposite is true: energy is given off when the compound decomposes into its elements, carbon and hydrogen. 

Acetylene has a negative heat of formation, as hydrogen iodide has 

(§ 168). 

AIR 

+AIR FLAME +AIR

ACETYLENE FIG. 151.

H₂O 

CALCIUM CARBIDE ACETYLENE FIG. 150. 

Preparation of acetylene. 

C2H2-2C+H2 +58,100 cal. 

Acetylene burner designed to mix the acetyshall burn with a sootlene with air so that it less flame.

Such compounds are unstable. When the decomposition of acetylene is started, heat is given off. As decomposition goes on, the temperature rises higher and higher. The rise in temperature causes the decomposition to proceed faster and faster. As a result the decomposition may become explosive. In the burning of acetylene we obtain not only the heat due to the oxidation of its carbon and hydrogen, but also the heat produced in the decomposition of acetylene into carbon and hydrogen. 

Acetylene cannot safely be stored by itself under pressure, as it may explode from some shock. It is kept, under pressure, in solution in liquid acetone. The cylinder usually

contains a spongy mass of asbestos as well. Prestolite is such a solution, kept like the acetylene used for the oxyacetylene torch. 

It is used as an illuminant. 

Mixtures of acetylene and air are very explosive, just as mixtures of gasoline vapor and air, or hydrogen and air, are explosive. 

When the kindling temperature of such a mixture is reached, the molecules of the combustible find molecules of oxygen close at hand, and the oxidation travels with enormous speed through the whole mixture. Different gases vary in the range of their explosibility. If acetylene makes up 3% to 82% of a mixture, and air makes up the remainder, the mixture will explode if ignited. 

On this account a small leakage from acetylene generators into the surrounding air, or small amounts of air entering acetylene tanks, may cause frightful explosions when a flame is applied.

Carbon dioxide.. 0.5 1 2 Nitrogen.. 2 5.5 59 100.0 100 100.0 100 294. Summary.-Carbon compounds are also called organic 

Most carbon compounds char or "crack" when compounds. heated.

Carbon has several allotropic forms: diamond, graphite, charcoal, coke, lampblack, and coal.

CARBON 

343 Charcoal is used as a reducing agent and to adsorb gases and coloring matter.

Lampblack and carbon black are obtained by incomplete burning of gases, oils, and resins.

Coke and coal are used as fuels and reducing agents on a large scale.

By-product coke ovens save the volatile products of the "cracking" of coal.

Diamonds are used as jewels and as points for cutters and drills. 

Graphite is used for pencils, polishes, electric conductors, lubricants, and crucibles.

Carbon unites directly with oxygen, sulphur, and metals. It has numerous compounds with hydrogen (hydrocarbons). 

We determine the fuel value of a coal or a food by oxidizing a sample in a calorimeter. 

Smoke contains wasted fuel. 

Carbon dioxide is prepared in the laboratory by the action of a carbonate and a dilute acid. 

Carbon dioxide is colorless and odorless. Its solution contains carbonic acid. Carbon dioxide may be liquefied and frozen. 

Under the influence of sunlight and chlorophyll, carbon dioxide and water produce carbohydrates and oxygen. 

Carbon dioxide is used as a fire extinguisher, as a leavening agent, as a refrigerant, and to produce effervescence in soda water and pop.

The test for carbon dioxide is its reaction with limewater, to produce a precipitate of calcium carbonate. 

Carbon monoxide is colorless and tasteless, also odorless if pure. 

It burns in air, forming carbon dioxide. It reduces heated oxides of metals. It is very poisonous. 

Carbon monoxide is prepared by the reduction of carbon dioxide with hot carbon. The laboratory method is to warm sodium formate or formic acid with concentrated sulphuric acid. 

Our gaseous fuels are natural gas, coal gas, water gas, producer gas, oil gases, and acetylene. Gasoline is really used in fuels as a vapor rather than a liquid.

Coal gas is formed by the dry distillation (cracking) of soft coal. Water gas results when steam is passed over hot coke or coal.

Producer gas is made when a limited amount of air is passed over coal or coke. 

Oil gases come from the "cracking" of petroleum.

Acetylene is prepared by the action between calcium carbide and water. It must be stored in solution in acetone and burned in special burners.

CHAPTER XXII  

296. Silicon.-Silicon is not found free in nature, but its compounds are very abundant. Some of them are: sand, quartz, agate, sandstone, clay, and feldspar. Silicon forms over 25% of the earth's solid crust, and is next to oxygen in abundance. Together these two elements make up about 75% of the earth's crust.

Silicon belongs to the same natural family as carbon. 

Its valence is 4, except in silicon monoxide (SiO), which corresponds to carbon monoxide. Like carbon, silicon is a non-metal. It exists in both amorphous and crystalline forms. Amorphous silicon is a brown powder; the crystalline form consists of steel-blue needles. 

Silicon is prepared by the reduction of its dioxide. In the laboratory magnesium is used. SiO2+2Mg→Si+2MgO. 

On a commercial scale silicon is made from quartz sand and coke:

SiO2+2C-Si+2CO. 

The operation takes place in electric furnaces, as at Niagara Falls. If ferric oxide is heated with the coke and sand, the product is an alloy of silicon and iron: ferrosilicon. Both ferrosilicon and silicon are used to remove oxygen bubbles from melted steel. 

When silicon burns, its dioxide, SiO2, is formed. Silicon reacts 346

with a solution of sodium hydroxide; the products are sodium silicate and hydrogen.

Si+2NaOH+H2O-Na2SiO3+2H2 ↑. 

Ferrosilicon and solid sodium hydroxide form hydrogenite, a commercial product which is used, with water, to prepare hydrogen. for filling balloons and airships. Another iron-silicon alloy, called duriron or tantiron, is 14 or 15% silicon. It is rust proof and resists the action of acids. It is therefore used in chemical laboratories and industries for acid-proof dishes, sinks, and pipes. 

297. Silicon carbide. If an excess of coke is heated with sand in the electric furnace, the product is not silicon, but silicon carbide (SiC). One brand of this is called carborundum (§ 275).

Usually sawdust and salt are heated with the coke and sand. 

Courtesy Carborundum Company FIG. 152. 

Carborundum brand silicon carbide crystals as they come from the electric furnace.

Carborundum has a very high degree of hardness, probably next to that of the diamond. It forms beautiful crystals which are not attacked by acids nor by alkali solutions. The crushed carborundum is mixed with cementing materials and fashioned into whetstones and grindstones of many varieties. In powdered form carborundum is also a most efficient abrasive, that is, polishing and grinding material. 

298. Silicon dioxide (SiO2).-Silicon dioxide is also called silica. It is found in nature in many forms. 

Quartz is practically pure silica. If it has traces of the

proper coloring materials, it is valuable for gems. Such forms are amethyst, rose quartz, and milky quartz. 

Colorless quartz is called rock crystal. It forms six-sided crystals with six-sided pyramids at each end. 

Sand is "weathered" quartz, broken into small, sharp 

FIG. 153. 

Courtesy Carborundum Company Carborundum brand silicon carbide furnace. particles. The particles are hard, and sand is therefore useful as an abrasive. Sand paper, used for smoothing wood and metals, is paper to which sand has been attached by means of glue. We have learned of the use of hydrofluoric acid in etching designs upon glass (§ 160).

A rapid current (blast) of air, carrying sharp sand, also etches, or roughens, glass. It can polish and cut hard surfaces, even of metals. Sand was formerly used with water for scouring wooden floors, and before the days of blotters was shaken upon fresh ink writing to absorb the extra ink.

Sandstone consists of sand particles cemented together. 

It occurs in great rock deposits. It is used as a building stone and for grindstones.

Silicon dioxide exists not only in crystalline form, but as an amorphous substance. 

Stones like flint, carnelian, opal, onyx, and agate consist of amorphous silica, often in bands of beautiful colors.

Silica is taken up by most plants, and helps to make their stems and leaves stiff, so that they can hold them- 

Field Museum of Natural History FIG. 154. 

A mass of quartz crystals. selves upright. The straw and husks of grain and the stems of bamboo contain it, also the skin, nails, and hair of animals. Sponges contain much silica taken from sea water.

Large deposits of very fine particles of silica are found in some places. They were formed when the land was under water for a long period. These deposits are the remains of multitudes of microscopic creatures, usually diatoms. The silica in such deposits is called diatomaceous (di'ă-tō-ma'shus) earth, or infusorial earth. 

It forms a very fine abrasive, often called electro-silicon. It is also very porous, and is used to absorb nitroglycerine in the preparation of dynamite (§ 233). A third use of diatomaceous earth is as an adsorbent, in place of animal charcoal (§ 269). In its minute pores colloidal coloring particles are adsorbed and removed from liquids. Petroleum products and sugar are decolorized in this way. 

There are many other uses of silica. Sand is used in mortar and concrete, and in the manufacture of glass. Quartz can be melted in the oxyhydrogen blowpipe and fashioned into chemical dishes and tubing. Quartz, unlike glass, does not increase or decrease

greatly in volume when heated or cooled; hence it does not crack readily from sudden changes in temperature. A white-hot quartz dish may be chilled in cold water without cracking. Quartz is also much less soluble than glass. 

Johns-Manville Co.

FIG. 155. 

Diatomaceous earth magnified 380 diameters. The figure shows the mineral remains of many tiny organisms.

Quartz allows "ultra-violet" light rays to pass more readily than glass does. For this reason one window in a hospital or schoolroom is often made of quartz, so that a person inside may be treated with the ultra-violet rays present in sunlight. A mercury-vapor lamp with a quartz lens is also used, in place of sunlight, to give such rays. 

299. Properties of silicon dioxide. Many of the physical properties of silica have already been given. Chemically it is the anhydride of silicic (sil-iss 'ik) acid, H4SiO4 or H2SiO3. 

When silicic acid is heated, it loses water and pure silica is left.

H4SiO4 H2SiO3

H2SiO3+H₂O ↑. 

SiO2+H2O ↑.

When the silicic acid is not quite dehydrated, it forms silica gel, a valuable adsorbent and collector of gases.

Silica gel is very porous and has a large "internal" surface. As a result it adsorbs moisture from industrial gases and dries them. It collects low-boiling gasoline and other commercial solvents that would otherwise be lost. 

It is also a valuable catalyst. 

300. Silicic acid and silicates.-Silicic acid always contains the anhydride, SiO2, but the silica is combined with

water in many different proportions. The two simplest forms of the acid are H4SiO4 and H2SiO3. When we add hydrochloric acid to a soluble silicate, such as sodium silicate, a gelatinous precipitate is formed. This has the formula H4SiO4. 

Na2SiO3+H2O+2HC1-2NaC1+H4SiO4↓ When dried slowly, the acid becomes H2SiO3. At a high temperature only SiO2 remains. Silicic acid is so weak that carbonic acid displaces it. 

Na2SiO3+H2CO3-Na2CO3+H2SiO3 ↓ . 

Since silicon dioxide is the anhydride of silicic acid, it reacts with bases, either in solution or in the fused condition, to form silicates. 2NaOH+SiO2→Na2SiO3+H₂O. 

It also reacts, when heated, with the oxides and carbonates of metals. CaO+SiO,→CaSiO3. CaCO3+SiO2 CaSiO3+CO2 ↑ . Na2CO3+SiO2→Na2SiO3+CO2 ↑ . 

Sodium and potassium silicates are soluble; they are known as water glass. Sodium silicate is prepared by heating a mixture of sand, charcoal, and sodium carbonate in a furnace. Sodium silicate is used to preserve eggs. When eggs are immersed in it, the pores of the shells become filled with gelatinous water glass. The egg contents are thus sealed in, and decomposition is prevented. Sodium silicate is also used in making cements and artificial stone, in fireproofing wood and fabrics, and as a filler for soaps. It soon becomes gelatinous in the

air owing to the precipitation of silicic acid by carbonic acid. 

301. Natural silicates.-Most natural silicates have more complex formulas than sodium silicate. Thus, potash feldspar is 

KAISI3O8, and pure clay (kaolin) is H2Al2(SiO4)2.H2O. We may also write the formula of clay, Al2O3.2SiO2.2H2O. 

Other common silicates found in the earth are mica, asbestos, pumice stone, and lava. Garnets are crystals of calcium aluminum silicate, Ca,Al2(SiO4)3. Emeralds are pure, transparent forms of beryl, BeзAl2(SiO2)6, colored green by chromic oxide, Cr2O3. 

Granite is a mixture of the minerals quartz, mica, and feldspar (§ 1). In the process of "weathering" the feldspar of granite is decomposed by the carbonic acid of the air. Its potassium becomes a part of potassium carbonate. This is soluble and is washed away by the rain. The hydrate of aluminum silicate (clay) is left. The quartz of the feldspar becomes sand. Thus granite becomes converted into soil. A mixture of clay and sand forms the variety of soil known as a loam. If the clay contains much limestone, it is called a marl. A very fine variety of adsorbent clay is called fuller's earth. It is used as a filter, to clarify mineral oils.

Ultramarine is an artificial, blue pigment made by heating together kaolin, soda, and sulphur. This substance was prepared, for many centuries, out of lapis lazuli (a rare mineral), and was highly prized. It can now be made for a few cents a pound, not cnly in blue, but also in red, green, and other colors. 

302. Clay and pottery.—In a pure form clay is white.

It is then called kaolin. It melts at a very high temperature. Ordinary clay contains impurities and melts more readily. Its impurities are chiefly compounds of iron (sometimes of manganese), also limestone and sand. 

Clay is colloidal in structure (§ 85) and can take up varying amounts of water. Moist clay is easily workable (plastic) and may be made into desired shapes by the hand or the potter's wheel. It may also be cut into

pieces, such as bricks, and cast into molds. When the moist clay is heated (baked or "fired"), it loses water and becomes hard, like stone. Because of these properties clay is used for the mak- ing of objects of porcelain, china, stoneware, and earthenware. 

Earthenware objects, in which are included bricks, tiles, jugs, flower pots, and the like, are made of common clay. 

They are fired with fuel gas in kilns (ovens), but are not melted. After the firing they are porous. If a glazed surface is wanted, the firing is done in the presence of common salt. The vapor of the salt, reacting with the clay, forms a covering of sodium aluminum 

FIG. 156. 

Durant kilns, Bedford Village, New York. The brick oven or kiln is closed tightly before the articles are fired. silicate over the porous surface. The red color of baked earthenware objects is due to ferric (iron) oxide or silicate.

Fire bricks are used for the linings of fire places, stoves, and furnaces. They are made of clay containing no lime, but mixed with much sand or quartz, so as to make them less easily melted than building bricks. 

Porcelain and china are made from a wet mixture of pure, white kaolin, quartz, and feldspar. The plastic mass is shaped

into the desired form, dried, and "fired" in a kiln. The feldspar melts first and cements the materials together. The first heating takes place at a relatively low temperature and leaves the object porous. To add a glaze the potter covers the object with a mix- 

ture of powdered feldspar and quartz and repeats the firing at a higher temperature. If colored decoration is to be added, it is sometimes applied before the second heating; but often a third heating is used. The materials that produce the desired color are usually oxides of metals. These react with the glaze to give colored silicates. Porcelain is translucent, but ironstone china, made in a similar way, is opaque. 

It has not been heated long enough, or hot enough, to make the feldspar penetrate the kaolin as much as in porcelain. Porcelain is hard, and not affected by heat and chemicals as much as glass. It is used not only for household objects, but for crucibles, evaporating dishes, and other apparatus in chemical laboratories. 

FIG. 157. 

View of a modern brick plant at Gordon, Georgia. This is a continuous kiln. 

303. Glass.-Glass consists of two or more silicates mixed together. Ordinary, soft glass contains sodium and calcium silicates; it is a soda-lime glass. Calcium silicate is a crystalline substance, but when it is mixed with sodium silicate the product does not crystallize and does not melt at a definite temperature. Instead, it

softens before it melts, and in this condition can be worked and "blown" into any desired shape, such as dishes, bottles, tubes, and globes. Two pieces of glass can also be welded together. 

FIG. 158. 

Pittsburgh Plate Glass Co. 

The molten glass is "gathered" on the bell-shaped end of a blower's pipe. Skillfully the blower accumulates the quantity of glass which is needed to make cylinders of proper size. Note the cylinders already blown.

In the preparation of soda-lime glass sodium carbonate (soda), calcium carbonate (limestone), and sand are melted together. In large-scale operations tank furnaces are employed; on a smaller scale, fire-clay pots are used.

Tank furnaces may hold 1000 tons. As the materials react, carbon dioxide is set free and stirs up the mixture. 

For a glass harder to melt (hard glass or potash-lime glass) potassium carbonate is used instead of sodium carbonate. For an optical glass (flint glass) lead oxide is used instead of part of the limestone, and potassium car-

bonate instead of sodium carbonate. The lead silicate formed imparts to flint glass a high degree of refraction of light, also a luster. Flint glass is used for lenses, cut glass, and glass ornaments.

Soda-lime glass is used for window panes, bottles, "soft glass" tubing, and some chemical ware. It is acted upon rather easily by chemicals and even by water. Potashlime glass is less easily attacked by reagents and water, and melts at a higher temperature. High grade chemical glassware is made under such names as "non sol" and "pyrex." These glasses contain a great deal of silica and have some of the advantages of quartz, in that they melt at a high temperature and expand and contract very little for changes of temperature. As a result they do not crack readily from sudden heating and cooling. glasses contain borates as well as silicates. Some

Color is imparted to glass by metal compounds added in its manufacture. If the sand used contains iron compounds, the glass will be green, as in some window and bottle glass. Manganese dioxide, if added in definite amount, will oxidize the iron to the ferric condition and then neutralize the yellow color of the ferric silicate. Cupric compounds or cobalt compounds make glass blue; chromic compounds make it green; cuprous compounds make it red; while gold makes it ruby red. Carbon in the right amount imparts an amber color. Calcium phosphate and arsenious oxide make glass opaque and impart an iridescence to it. Calcium fluoride makes it white.

Designs and marks are cut into glass by emery or carborundum wheels ("cut glass"), or by means of hydrofluoric acid (§ 160), or by a sand blast.

Enamel consists of a low-melting glass. It is used to cover iron and other metals, in signs, sinks, and granite-ware or porcelain-lined dishes. Enamels usually contain boron compounds to give them a low melting point.

Shatter-proof glass, such as is used for automobile windshields, consists of two layers of glass with a layer of a cellulose product. between them. The three are cemented together under pressure. 

Even if such glass is cracked in an accident, its pieces do not fly about. 

304. Glass working.-Glass was formerly blown entirely by men; it is now blown by machines as well. 

Pittsburgh Plate Glass Co. 

FIG. 159. 

"The Bait." The big blowpipes are dipped into the molten glass and then gradually raised. Glass adheres to their ends and is blown, by compressed air, into large cylinders. 

Bottles are blown in molds, likewise other dishes with designs "pressed" into them. To make window glass the workman gathers a large lump of melted glass on one end of an iron blowpipe several feet long, and blows into the opposite end. Thus he forms a cylindrical bubble perhaps 6 feet long and 18 inches in diameter. With a

machine using compressed air larger bubbles may be blown. The glass bubble is cracked off from the blowpipe and split open by means of a hot iron. The cylinders are then heated until they just flatten into sheets. Plate glass is formed from melted glass which is poured out 

Pittsburgh Plate Glass Co. 

FIG. 160. 

This is the process of lowering the fragile cylinders of glass after they have been blown by compressed air. upon a hot table and rolled with a hot, metal roller. It must be ground smooth and polished. 

If hot glass objects are cooled too rapidly, they contract unevenly and are likely to crack when jarred. The process of annealing is used to prevent this. Annealing consists in drawing the glass object slowly through a long

oven, hot at one end and cool at the other, so that the glass may contract evenly. 

305. Boron.-Boron is prepared by the reduction of boron trioxide (B2O3), by means of aluminum. It is commonly obtained as a brown powder, but exists also in crystalline form. It burns to form boron trioxide. It is a member of the aluminum family, and in its compounds has a valence of three; but it also resembles silicon. 

Boron combines with nitrogen to form boron nitride (BN) and with chlorine to form boron chloride (BC13). Both are hydrolyzed by water. BN+3H₂O→HзBO3(boric acid) +NH; ↑ · BC13+3H20→H;BO3+3HC1 ↑ . 

306. Boric acid and borax.-The most important boron compounds are boric, or boracic acid (H,BO3) and borax, which is sodium tetraborate (Na2B4O7). When borax is crystallized from water, the product is a hydrate of the formula, Na2B4O7.5H2O or Na2B4O7.10H2O. In California there are deposits of a mineral having the formula, Ca2B6O11.5H2O. When this is boiled with a solution of sodium carbonate (Na2CO3), calcium carbonate is precipitated, and borax remains in solution. The borax crystals are obtained by a slow cooling of the solution. 

Another large deposit (rasorite) is found in the Mohave Desert of California. Its formula is Na2B4O7.4H2O. 

Boric acid is formed when a hot solution of borax is treated with concentrated hydrochloric or sulphuric acid. Na2B4O7+5H2O+2HC1→2NaC1+4H3BO3. 

The boric acid crystallizes as the solution cools, in white, shiny scales. When boric acid is heated, it loses water,

and finally its anhydride, boron trioxide (B2O3), remains.

2H,BO¿→3H2O+B203. 

A solution of boric acid is used in medicine as an antiseptic. Thus, it is applied as a wash for the eyes and the mouth. It is also a preservative. It is a very weak acid. It colors the Bunsen or alcohol flame green. 

Borax is partly hydrolyzed in solution; the solution is alkaline (§ 211; why?). Like boric acid, borax is an antiseptic and preservative. It is also used to soften hard water (§ 357). When the crystals of the hydrates of borax are heated, they lose water and swell up to form a porous mass. When this is heated further, it melts to form a clear glass.

The most important use of borax in chemistry and the arts is as a flux, to help get the oxides of metals, which are usually hard to melt, into compounds that melt readily. Thus, borax is used to remove oxides (rusts) from the surfaces of metals before soldering, so that the solder may stick to the metal. The borax combines with the rust to form a glassy compound. Borax is used to remove, rust from pieces of iron that are to be welded and to clean the surfaces of iron dishes before enamel is applied to them.

FIG. 161. 

A borax bead in a loop of platinum wire. 

The chemist melts borax upon platinum wires and so prepares "borax beads." With these he tests for the compounds of some of the metals. Thus, a borax bead heated with a minute amount of a cobalt compound acquires a blue color, while one heated with a chromium compound becomes green.

In uniting with oxides of metals borax acts as though it contained boron trioxide (B2O3). Na2B4O7-B2O3, 2NaBO2. 

The boron trioxide is an acid anhydride and unites with metal oxides to form salts (metaborates). B2O3, 2NaBO2+CoO→Co(BO2)2, 2NaBO2. 

307. Summary.-Silicon is a powder or a crystalline solid. It belongs to the carbon family and is a non-metal. It burns to form silicon dioxide. It is prepared by the reduction of silicon dioxide by magnesium or carbon. "Carborundum" is a form of silicon carbide. It is a valuable abrasive. 

Silicon dioxide, or silica, exists in quartz, sand, sandstone, flint, agate, and other minerals. A very finely divided, natural form is diatomaceous earth.

Sand is used as an abrasive, and as a constituent of glass, mortar, and concrete. 

Quartz is used for gems, as a constituent of quartz glass, and as a transmitter of ultra-violet light. 

Silicic acid is a weak acid. Its anhydride is silica. 

Sodium and potassium sili- 

Most silicates are insoluble. 

Silicates are salts of silicic acid. cates are water glass; they are soluble. 

Clay is the hydrate of an aluminum silicate. China and pottery are baked clay.

Glass is a mixture of silicates. Soft glass contains sodium silicate and calcium silicate. 

Enamel is low-melting glass used to cover metal dishes. 

Glass softens before it melts and in this condition may be rolled (plate glass), drawn out (tubing), or blown into molds (vessels). 

Boron, like silicon, is obtained both as a powder and in crystalline form. It burns to form boron trioxide, the anhydride of boric acid. Boric acid is a weak acid.

Borax is sodium tetraborate. The common crystals of borax are hydrates.

When borax crystals are heated, they lose water and finally

give a glassy substance. This unites with the oxides of metals. 

Because of this property melted borax is used to clean the surfaces of metals for soldering, welding, and the application of enamels. 

The chemist uses melted borax, in borax beads, to test for metals which give colored borates. 

308. Exercises.-1. Write the formulas of silicon hydride, fluoride, chloride, and sulphide. Write the formulas of the corresponding carbon compounds. 

2. Can you determine the molecular formula of silicon dioxide as you do that of carbon dioxide? Why? 

3. Why do many glass bottles and fruit jars have a greenish color? How could such vessels have been made colorless?

8. How could you prove that ordinary glass is somewhat soluble in water?

10. Write the formulas of boron oxide, hydride, fluoride, chloride, nitride, and sulphide. Suggest a formula for its phosphide (§ 254). 

11. Why is water glass used to preserve eggs? To make wood and cloth fireproof?

13. How does hard glass differ, in its constituents, from soft glass? How do both differ from flint glass?

309. The periodic system.-Many more elements are known now than were known in Lavoisier's day. As each new element has been discovered, it has been compared with elements already familiar. As a result, many 

FIG. 162. 

Dimitri Ivanovitch Mendelejeff resemblances between elements have been found, and similar elements have been grouped together in natural families. We have already studied the elements of the halogen family, the nitrogen family, and the carbon family. The alkali metals: lithium, sodium, and potassium, with the rare metals rubidium and caesium, form another natural family. When chemists accepted Avogadro's (1834-1907). Professor at the Uni- hypothesis, they were able to se- versity of St. Petersburg (Leningrad). Developed chemical industries of Russia and stated the periodic law. lect a definite atomic weight for each element. They then found that, within a natural family, there is a regular order of properties, following the order of the atomic weights. Review carefully the tables 364

of the halogen family (§ 168) and the nitrogen family (§ 262); then study the table of the alkali metals. 

ELEMENT ATOMIC 

SYMBOL

DENSITY WEIGHT POINT MELTING BOILING POINT 

Lithium.

186° C. 1400° C. Sodium.

Na 23 0.97 97 878 Potassium.

39 0.86 62.5 700 Rubidium.

Rb 85.4 1.53 38.5 696 Caesium.

Cs 133 1.87 26.5 670 In 1869 the Russian chemist, Mendelejeff, announced a classification not of a single family, but one in which all the elements were included. Omitting hydrogen he arranged the elements known to him in the order of their atomic weights. The 16 having the lowest atomic weights he arranged in this way: 

Li, 7 

Be, 9

B, 11 C, 12 Na, 23 K, 39 Mg, 24 Al, 27 Si, 28 N, 14 P, 31 O, 16 F, 19 

S, 32 C1, 35.5

Ca, 40 The arrangement in the first line put the elements in a definite order, not only according to their atomic weights, but also according to their properties. The series began with the active metal, lithium, and ended with the most active non-metal, fluorine. The element next above fluorine in atomic weight is sodium (at. wt. 23). This is an active metal, like lithium. In the second line, from sodium to chlorine, the gradation from active metal to active non-metal again takes place. In the third line,

potassium (at. wt. 39) belongs with lithium and sodium, and calcium (at.wt. 40) belongs with magnesium. 

Mendelejeff saw that, after the first line, an increase of atomic weight brought him each time to an element having properties like those of an element of lower atomic weight. In his arrangement the 8th element was like the first, the 15th was like the first and 8th, and so on. Elements of the same natural family fell into the same vertical column. So Mendelejeff said that periodically the properties of the elements are determined by their atomic weights. This is the periodic law. It may also be stated thus: The properties of the elements are periodic "functions" of their atomic weights. 

310. Predicting unknown elements.-Let us now add the next 14 elements known to Mendelejeff, and see how they fit into his periodic arrangement. At the top of each vertical column we will put the general formula of the highest oxide of the elements in that column, also the general formula of the hydrogen compounds of the non-metals; since these formulas are found to be the same for all the elements in any one column. The element having an atomic weight next higher than

calcium was titanium (at. wt. 48). This does not, however, resemble boron and aluminum, in the next column, but has a valence of 4, like carbon and silicon. The next elements: vanadium, chromium, and manganese, fit only fairly well into the families headed by nitrogen, oxygen, and fluorine, respectively. Then there is a set of 3 closely related elements: iron, cobalt, and nickel, which form an extra, eighth group. After these comes copper (at wt. 

63.6), which is put into group I. Copper in its cuprous compounds has a valence of 1. The next element, zinc (at. wt. 65), has a valence of 2 and belongs in the group with magnesium and calcium.

The next element known to Mendelejeff was arsenic (at. wt. 75). It forms compounds: AsH,, AS2O3, and 

As2O5, and therefore belongs to the nitrogen family. The last 4 of the 14 elements fit into the correct places. 

Selenium is like sulphur, bromine like chlorine, rubidium like potassium, and strontium like calcium. 

In the arrangement of these first 30 elements according to atomic weights and properties Mendelejeff found three gaps; one between calcium and titanium, and two between zinc and arsenic. He was so sure that these gaps were not accidental that he predicted that elements existed which would fill these places. He gave the name eka-boron to the missing element between calcium and titanium. He reasoned that this element should have the same relation in properties to its two neighboring elements that boron has to beryllium and carbon. He also reasoned that the first missing element after zinc (he called it ek-aluminum) should have the same relation in properties to strontium and titanium that aluminum

has to calcium and carbon. He called the missing element in the carbon family, eka-silicon. 

311. Predictions fulfilled.-Mendelejeff's predictions soon began to come true. In 1875 an element with the atomic weight 69.7 was discovered in France and named gallium. Its properties placed it in the first blank space after zinc. It was Mendelejeff's ek-aluminum. How nearly the properties predicted for this element agree with those actually found is shown in the following table:

In 1879 a new element, with an atomic weight of 44, was discovered in a Scandinavian mineral and named scandium. According to its properties it was eka-boron. 

In 1888 a third element predicted by Mendelejeff was discovered in Germany and named germanium. Because of its atomic weight (72.6) and other properties it was found to be identical with eka-silicon. 

As, one by one, the predictions of Mendelejeff came true, men realized that he had made one of the most remarkable discoveries of chemical science. With the finding of other elements the Mendelejeff table has had to be enlarged.
