312. Modern periodic table.-Study carefully the complete periodic table. Mendelejeff's arrangement of the elements had a place not only for scandium, gallium, and germanium, when they were discovered, but also for a whole family of elements which was not, in 1869, known to exist upon the earth. This family consists of the rare gases of the atmosphere, or "noble" gases: helium, neon, argon, krypton, and xenon, and of radon, obtained from radium. These elements are without chemical activity, hence their valence is O. One irregularity is that argon (at. wt. 39.9) comes before potassium (at. wt. 39.1).

Since the other vertical groups are numbered I-VIII, from the highest valence shown by their elements, the helium family is called the zero group. 

The Mendelejeff arrangement puts two natural families into each of Groups I-VII. In the table these are put into two vertical columns. The family consisting of the more active metals is put on the left; the family containing the less active metals, or more active non-metals, on the right. Thus, in Group I, copper, silver, and gold have a closer relationship to one another than to the alkali metals. They are, in fact, inactive metals, while the alkali metals are exceedingly active. 

The table does not include 14 closely related elements, called the rare-earth metals, with atomic weights ranging from 140 to 175. 

If we look now at the horizontal arrangement of the elements, we find 7 series. The first series contains 2 elements only: hydrogen and helium. Hydrogen is put with the alkali metals, because it often acts like a univalent metal. Helium is the first element of the zero group. Series 2 and 3 each contain 8 elements. Each begins with an active metal and ends with a "noble" gas. The element just before the noble gas is, in four cases, a halogen. 

Series 4 is a long series, containing 18 elements instead of 8. 

At the place in which series 2 and 3 have halogens (fluorine and chlorine), series 4 has manganese, a metal. However, manganese

resembles chlorine in valence: it forms an oxide, Mn2O7, like 

Cl207. It is not until we come to the 17th element of this series that we find a halogen: bromine. The 18th element is the noble gas, krypton. Series 5 shows the same general order as series 4. 

Series 6 contains 18 elements, like series 4 and 5, and, in addition, the 14 rare-earth metals, a total of 32. Series 7 is incomplete, with only 6 elements. The unknown element below caesium is called eka-caesium; the one below iodine is called eka-iodine. 

The total number of elements (known and unknown) in the table is 92. 

313. Uses of the periodic system. (1) The most striking application of the periodic system was that employed by Mendelejeff, in predicting unknown elements. (2) The periodic system has also stimulated chemists to make new determinations of atomic weights, so that the correct position of an element in the table might be found. (3) The periodic system has brought about a thorough study of the elements as a whole, so that the properties of even the rare metals might be learned and all the elements might be compared with one another. (4) The system is also a wonderful aid to the memory; for if we know the position of an element in the table we can infer many facts regarding the properties of the element and the formulas of its compounds. 

314. Elements and x-rays. Our knowledge regarding the relationship between the elements was greatly extended by Moseley, a brilliant, young, English scientist who died in 1915. Moseley used x-rays to study the metals and their compounds with non-metals. 

All of us know one use of x-rays: they allow us to take x-ray photographs of opaque objects, such as bones, teeth, or other dense structures inside the body. X-rays

are produced in a vacuum tube by the bombardment of a metal target, inside the tube, by means of cathode rays. 

Courtesy Lyman C. Newell

FIG. 163. 

Henry G. J. Moseley (1887-1915). Young 

English physicist who determined x-ray spectra of elements and stated atomic numbers. Killed in World War.

Cathode rays are produced when we pass a high-voltage, electric discharge through the vacuum tube. Cathode rays consist of negative electric particles (electrons) that stream from the cathode, or negative electrode (§ 35). We may compare this stream of electrons, which is shot at the metal target, with a swarm of bullets discharged from a rapid-fire gun. X-rays affect silver salts as light waves do; hence we can photograph them.

X-rays have the na- ture and velocity of light waves, but are much shorter. 

When we pass a sunbeam through a glass prism, we break ANTI- 

CATHODE CATHODE 

up the white light into light of different colors, with different wave lengths. The prism bends the shortest light waves (the violet) most; it bends the longest 

FIG. 164. 

X-ray tube.

X-RAYS waves (the red) least. The result is the band of colored light that we call the solar spectrum. X-rays,

like light rays, are of different wave lengths. They are too short to be separated by a prism; but crystals, such as those of common salt, bend them more or less, according to their different wave lengths. We thus obtain an x-ray spectrum. We cannot see this spectrum, but we can photograph it. 

315. Atomic numbers. When Moseley placed different elements on the target of an x-ray tube, and bombarded them with electrons (cathode rays), the x-rays which were produced had different wave lengths for different elements. Moseley proved this by reflecting the x-rays in each case from the surface of a crystal, and then taking a photograph of the result. 

When he arranged the elements in an order based upon their x-ray spectra, he found that the order corresponded very closely with that of the atomic weights. He determined results for metals from aluminum to gold. The other numbers had to be supplied, because the elements could not be used as targets. Moseley believed that this difference in the x-rays produced by different elements is due to differences in the internal structure, or make-up, of the atoms themselves. According to Moseley's work all the elements form a series, and the position of any element in the series may be stated by a number, beginning with 

FIG. 165. 

Theodore William Richards (18681928). Professor in Harvard University. Made accurate determinations of atomic weights.

hydrogen as 1 and ending with uranium as 92. These are the atomic numbers. 

In the accompanying table we have a duplicate of the periodic arrangement, except that atomic numbers are used instead of atomic weights.

87 88 89 90 91 92 *Atomic numbers 58-71 belong to the 14 metals of the rare earths. They are Ce 58, Pr 59, Nd 60, Il 61, Sm 62, Eu 63, Gd 64, Tb 65, Dy 66, Ho 67, Er 68, Tm 69, Yb 70, Lu 71.

In the Table of Atomic Numbers there is a place for every element, including the 14 metals of the rare earths, which have no place in the Periodic Table. Other irregularities of the Periodic Table disappear in the new table: the atomic number for argon comes before that of potassium, and the atomic number for tellurium comes before that of iodine. Mendelejeff said that in a period, or series, the properties of an element are determined by its atomic weight; chemists of today say that the properties of all elements are determined by their atomic numbers. 

By this they mean that the atom of any element, say, number 11, has a certain number of parts and a certain arrangement of those parts, and these are responsible for its chemical behavior.

In the next chapter we shall learn more regarding the application of atomic numbers. 

316. Summary.-Mendelejeff's periodic system is an arrangement of the elements in several horizontal series in the order of their atomic weights. Similar elements, having the same valence and other properties, fall into the same vertical column. 

The periodic law is that the properties of elements are periodic functions of the atomic weights. 

Mendelejeff stated that the gaps in the periodic system were due to undiscovered elements and predicted, with surprising accuracy, the properties of many of these elements. 

The modern periodic table includes hydrogen and the noble gases, contains 7 series, and provides for a total of 92 elements. 

The periodic system has been useful for: (1) the prediction of unknown elements; (2) the stimulation of work for obtaining precise atomic weights; (3) more study of the properties of elements, so that elements may be compared with one another; and (4) an aid to the memory. 

X-rays were used by Moseley to study the elements. Numbers obtained from the x-ray spectra produced by different elements

vary in a definite order. In general this is the same order as that of the atomic weights. 

Atomic numbers, from 1 to 92, are assigned to the elements in accordance with this order. Atomic numbers give the relation between the elements more accurately than atomic weights. 

Chemists believe that the atomic number of an element is closely related to the number and arrangement of the parts within the atoms of that element. 

317. Exercises.—1. Find the average of the atomic weights of the four elements surrounding magnesium in the periodic arrangement, and compare it with the atomic weight of magnesium. 

2. Calculate the approximate atomic weight of eka-boron (scandium) by subtracting from four times the atomic weight of aluminum the sum of the atomic weights of magnesium, boron, and silicon. 

3. Compare the densities of copper, silver, and gold with the order of their atomic weights. 

4. Name the members of the calcium family. State in detail how the order of properties corresponds with that of atomic weights. 

5. What are the members of the nitrogen family? What relation is there between their properties and atomic weights? 

6. How does the increase in atomic weight, within the nitrogen family, affect the metallic properties of the elements? 

7. In which part of the periodic table are the gaseous elements? 

The active non-metals? The active metals? 

8. Which of the alkali metals is most active? Which of the non-metals? What is the relation of these two elements to each other in the periodic table? 

9. Is it likely that the number of elements is unlimited? 

10. Give the formulas of the chloride, oxide, hydroxide, sulphate, and carbonate of gallium. 

11. What are the families in each of Groups I, II, IV, V, ånd VI?

CHAPTER XXIV  

318. Atoms are not indivisible. Scientists have long thought that certain elements must be closely related to one another. It seemed almost necessary to believe that such a group as the halogen family: fluorine, chlorine, bromine, and iodine, must have something in common in their structure to account for their similarity of behavior. The same statement is true also of the alkali metals: lithium, sodium, and potassium. The relationship between all the elements was emphasized by Mendelejeff's periodic table and to a still greater extent by Moseley's atomic numbers. 

The original idea of Dalton (§ 102) that the atoms are the final particles into which substances can be divided, was a very helpful one. Dalton's atomic theory, together with Avogadro's hypothesis, made it possible for chemists to obtain the atomic weights. But as long as men thought of the atom as the final unit of a substance, they could not imagine how the different elements have anything in common. This problem was solved for men's thinking by the discovery that the atoms of some elements dc actually break down, and that from all such broken atoms we obtain the same materials: positive and negative particles. Scientists still believe that the unit particle in chemical changes is the atom; but they also 377

believe that atoms themselves are made up of unit electric particles called protons and electrons. They also believe (§ 315) that the number and arrangement of these particles in the atoms of an element account for the properties of that element. 

How did this change of ideas come about? The story is a long one, and we have time for only a few steps in its progress. The first step has to do with the discovery of that strangely active element, radium. 

319. Discovery of radium.-In 1895 Wilhelm Roentgen (rent'gen), a German, discovered x-rays, which are like 

Herbert Photos, Inc.

FIG. 166. 

Marie Sklodowska Curie (1867- ). 

Professor at the Sorbonne, Paris.

Discoverer of polonium and radium. light rays, but much shorter, and which penetrate paper and flesh and affect a photographic plate (§ 314). In 1896 Henri 

Becquerel, a Frenchman, found that an ore called pitchblende also gives off active "rays"; these affect a photographic plate even when the plate is wrapped in black paper. The rays were called Becquerel rays. Pitchblende is an ore of the metal uranium, which has the highest atomic weight of any element. 

In searching for the source of the Becquerel rays Professor and Madame Curie removed the uranium from pitchblende and found that the uranium itself gives off some of these active rays. But they also found that the remainder of the pitchblende, from which the uranium had been removed, was still very active. By long, patient work they found out

why the residue was so active, for they obtained from it a very small amount of a substance which was more than a million times as active as the uranium. This was the salt of a new metal, which they called radium. Afterwards Mme. Curie obtained the metal radium itself. Its atomic weight is 226, and its properties place it in the same family as barium. Some other elements, such as uranium, polonium, and thorium, show the same behavior as radium, but to a smaller degree. They are all said to be radioactive.

A study of radium and its compounds shows: (1) They are constantly giving off heat. The air next to them is several degrees warmer than surrounding air. 

FIG. 167. 

Electroscope. The one on the right is charged; the other, not. (2) They give off active "rays" which cause numerous physical and chemical changes: (a) If the rays strike a body charged with electricity, such as an electroscope, they discharge it by making the air in contact with the body a conductor of electricity. We say that they “ionize" the air. Why? X-rays do the same. (b) The rays change oxygen to ozone, produce burns in flesh, kill bacteria, and decompose water.

(c) The rays, even when the radium compound is present in small amount, impart to some substances the power of glowing in the dark. One such substance is zinc sulphide. When this is mixed with a small proportion of a radium compound, it can be used for luminous watch and clock dials, and for other devices that will be visible at night. X-rays affect zinc sulphide likewise. (d) The rays affect a photographic plate. X-rays do the same 

(§ 314). 

BETA 

GAMMA ALPHA 

320. Classes of rays from radium.-The rays given off by radium and its compounds were also studied by the use of magnets. Thus it was found that the active rays from radium are a mixture of at least three kinds, called, from the first three Greek letters, alpha, beta (bā'tä), and gamma rays, respectively. The alpha and beta rays are really streams of 

FIG. 168. 

upon the "rays" given off by radium.

Action of a magnetic field charged particles and are bent (deflected) by the magnet. The alpha particles are helium atoms that lack two electrons each, and are therefore positively (2+) charged. The beta particles are electrons, which are the unit charges of negative (-) electricity. The gamma rays are x-rays; they are not affected by the magnet. 

The rate at which radium breaks down is such that half of a given specimen would be gone in about 1690 years, half of the remainder in 1690 years more, and so on. 

We have, therefore, evidence that an element, radium, has a temporary "life" and is all the time breaking down spontaneously, or disintegrating. We cannot stop, start, or regulate the change. As the radium breaks down, it gives off particles: helium and electrons. The electrons,

by their blows upon the bodies they strike, evolve energy in the form of x-rays and heat. 

321. How atoms are constructed. The fact that radium atoms actually break down led scientists to try to imagine how its atoms are constructed. They thus came to the belief that all atoms are made up of charged particles; they then set about to express, in terms of protons and electrons, what they already knew about atoms. First, there is the fact that atoms are electrically neutral. If this is true, then the numbers of protons (+) and electrons (-) in an atom must be equal. Then there is the question as to where in the atom the protons and electrons are. We explain ordinary chemical changes by assuming that the electrons are on the outside of the atom, and that there is an outermost part from which electrons can even escape. These outermost electrons may be added to other atoms which can hold them. If an atom loses one electron, it has one (+) charge in excess, and is therefore positively charged. If a neutral atom gains one electron (−), it is now negatively charged. 

Since the electron of the atom is the part that seems to be lost or gained, scientists assume that the nucleus is in the interior of the atom, and that the electrons are grouped about it. The atom therefore appears to have a nucleus which is unchangeable in any chemical reaction. 

The active parts of the atoms are the electrons. 

What about the size (mass) of the atoms? The atomic weights are real numbers. The atomic numbers also express a real relation between the elements. 

How are atomic numbers related to atomic weights? 

Protons are found to have about 1850 times the mass of electrons. The atomic weight of an element is there-

fore due mainly to the protons in the atom. In the case of such an element as carbon the atomic number is half the atomic weight. Scientists therefore assume that in the carbon atom half of the electrons are bound with the protons in the nucleus. The really free electrons are outside the nucleus; it is these that determine the atomic number. The atomic number is the number of free electrons in the atom. The problem of the constitution of the atom has to do with the arrangement of the free electrons. Moseley's atomic numbers show us the number of electrons outside the nucleus, far enough apart to be detected by their x-ray spectra. 

322. How the parts of an atom are arranged.—The most important part of an atom as regards chemical 

(+1) ± +1 +1 

change is its system of free electrons. If the hydrogen atom consists of one proton and one. He

FIG. 169. 

Protons and electrons in atoms of H, He, and Li. electron, we can picture its atom as like a central sun with a planet (the electron) placed at some distance from it and probably revolving around it. See Fig. 169.

The next element in order of size is helium, with the atomic number 2 and the atomic weight 4. The arrangement of its protons and electrons may be as in Fig. 169. 

What is inside the circle represents the nucleus. The diagram shows two electrons in the nucleus, "bound" by two of the protons. The other protons serve to hold the two outer (free) electrons. The excess of protons over electrons in the nucleus of the helium atom is 2. This is

also the atomic number of helium. The arrangement of protons and electrons in the helium atom is a very stable one, for, as we know, helium enters into no chemical changes.

Atomic number 3 belongs to lithium, also atomic weight 7. 

Lithium must have in its atom the protons and electrons of 7 hydrogen atoms, or of 3 hydrogen atoms and one helium atom. 

Lithium, like hydrogen, has a valence of 1 (Li*). The lithium atom may therefore have one outer electron, as hydrogen has. If the outer electron were loosely held, we could understand why lithium, like hydrogen, unites so readily with non-metals like fluorine and chlorine.

323. Rings of eight electrons; the "octet" theory. 

The element above neon is sodium (atomic number 11). 

This is an active metal, like lithium, and its valence is 1+, as in both lithium and hydrogen. We assume that the sodium atom, like the atoms of lithium and hydrogen, has one electron outside all the others, and easily lost. 

In the diagram (Fig. 170) the sodium atom contains a nucleus and a ring of 2 free electrons, as in helium. He

Be 

FIG. 171. 

Ne Na The nuclei are small dots, and the "free" electrons are arranged, in "shells," about them. 

Then, surrounding this "helium" ring, it has a ring of 8 free electrons, as in neon. Finally it has a single, outermost electron toward the formation of a new (third) ring. We note that the number of electrons in the outer ring stops at 8. According to the "octet" theory this is the largest number that an outer ring can hold. 

324. Diagrams according to the octet theory. Instead

of showing the protons in each diagram we can let a dot stand for the nucleus and small circles for the electrons. 

Fig. 171 gives the diagrams of the first 11 elements from this point of view. 

The diagrams of the atoms can be condensed still further, so as to show only the "ring" of outermost electrons, those which are called valence electrons. To 

Ishow the increase in number of the valence electrons from one to 8, Lewis and Langmuir, who devised the octet theory, use a cube, with its 8 corners, to represent the positions of the electrons (Fig. 

174). They assume that in the lithium atom only one "corner" has an electron. As the atomic numbers increase from atom to atom, more and more corners are occupied, until, in the fluorine atom, only one corner remains unfilled. The great activity of fluorine is due, in the language of this theory, to the tendency of fluorine to "complete its octet." In other words, the fluorine atom, with 7 electrons in the outer ring, is so near the stable arrangement, in which there are 8, that it eagerly seizes the needed electron from some other atom. Elements which readily lose electrons are the active metals and hydrogen. So, when lithium and fluorine react we picture to ourselves a rush of electrons from lithium atoms to fluorine atoms. 

Eminent American Chemists

FIG. 172. 

Gilbert Newton Lewis (1875- ).

Professor in the University of California. Brilliant investigator of the structure of atoms and molecules.

The fluorine atom, after completing its octet, has an outer ring of 8 electrons, as neon has. But this transfer of an electron from lithium to fluorine leaves the lithium atom positive (Li+). The fluorine atom was neutral when it had 7 electrons in its outer ring. 

It is now negative (F-). These opposite and equal electric charges

Lithium fluoride in solution gives the ions Li+ and F-. 

The reaction of oxygen with metals and with hydrogen can be described in the same way. The oxygen atom is neutral, but lacks 2 atoms of completing its octet. It can take one electron from each of two hydrogen or sodium atoms; it then has 

8 for its outer ring. But in doing so it gains an excess of 2 electrons, and has a valence of 2in the compounds H2O and Na2O.

326. The electron theory and chemical change. The student has already noticed that we need to define in new ways our ideas regarding elements, atoms, valence, and chemical change. But if he has studied this chapter thoughtfully, he will realize that chemists have not really

given up any part of the atomic theory that is important. 

In science we do not usually abandon theories; we build them into the new theories.

We have learned that elements are of two general classes: metals and non-metals. A metal, according to the electron theory, is an element with an atom having a small number of electrons in its outer ring. Usually there are 1, 2, or 3. Thus, the atom of sodium has 1, Na CI 

FIG. 176. 

Sodium, chlorine, and sodium chloride.

Na Cl that of magnesium 2, and that of aluminum 3, each according to its valence. A non-metal, on the other hand, has a larger number of electrons in its outer ring: more than 4. It has almost enough to complete its outer ring of 8. Thus fluorine and chlorine have 7 outer electrons; oxygen and sulphur have 6. Nitrogen has 5. Chemical union between a metal and a non-metal consists in the passage of electrons from the metal to the non-metal. 

Verify this from the cases of lithium and fluorine, sodium and chlorine, and magnesium and oxygen. By this change the non-metal gains a stable outer ring by completing its octet; the metal also gains a stable outer ring by losing its few extra electrons.

We may then define the valence of a metal as the number of electrons its atom can lose from its outer ring. 

The valence of a non-metal is the number of electrons its atom needs to complete an outer ring of 8. 

Many elements are between metals and non-metals in properties. According to the electron theory such elements may either lose electrons, or gain them, depending upon the conditions. Thus carbon forms the compounds CH4 and CO2. In CH4 the carbon atom holds the 4 electrons of 4 hydrogen atoms, and thus completes its outer ring of 8. In forming CO2, however, that is, in burning, the carbon atom loses 4 electrons; these complete the octets of the oxygen atoms.

The nitrogen atom gains 3 electrons in uniting with hydrogen to form a molecule of ammonia, NH3. Two nitrogen atoms also enter into the molecule N2O5, in which 10 electrons from the nitrogen atoms complete the octets of 5 oxygen atoms. 

Sulphur also shows this double behavior. Its atom can use 2 electrons from 2 hydrogen atoms to form H2S; it can also form 

SO3, in which it gives the 6 electrons of its outer ring to 3 atoms of oxygen.

Even hydrogen shows a tendency to have both (+) and (−) valence. Its atom can gain an electron as well as lose one. Thus, it forms lithium hydride (Li+H-) as well as hydrogen chloride (H+Cl-). In forming the molecule LiH the hydrogen atom acts like that of a halogen; it completes an outer ring of 2 electronsa helium ring. 

327. Elements with several atomic weights: isotopes.- 

One of the new ideas which seems very strange is that the same element may have two or more atomic weights. 

How can this be true? Thus, ordinary chlorine is made up of atoms with an apparent atomic weight of 35.46; but this can be separated into two forms with atomic weights of 35 and 37, respectively. These forms are mixed in ordinary chlorine in such a proportion that the

average is 35.46. The chlorine, no matter of which atomic weight, has only one atomic number-17. Sodium chloride made by the union of sodium with the one is the same as sodium chloride made from the other. Since the two forms of chlorine have the same place in the list of atomic numbers, they are called isotopes (I'sō-tōpes), meaning "having the same place." Note that the isotopes have whole numbers (35 and 37) for atomic weights, while the atomic weight of chlorine is not a whole number. 

Since the atomic number of the two forms of chlorine is the same, we believe that the number XX 

FIG. 177. 

37+ 20 Two isotopes of chlorine. What is the atomic number of each? of free electrons is the same in both isotopes, namely, 17. 

The atoms differ in the number of protons and electrons in the nucleus. Hence the chemical properties of the two isotopes are the same, although the atomic weights (which depend chiefly upon the number of protons) are different. 

Many other elements have isotopes. Thus, the lead obtained from uranium ores (which also produce radium) has an atomic weight of 206, while ordinary lead has an atomic weight of 207.2. Lead chloride is the same substance, no matter from which variety of lead it is made. 

Here again the difference in the atoms is found in the different number of protons and electrons in the nucleus. 

The free electrons (those in the "rings") are the same in number and have the same arrangement in both isotopes. 

There is another strange fact regarding atomic weights: in some cases one of the isotopes of one element has the

same atomic weight as another element. We then have two elements with the same atomic weight. They are called isobares, meaning "of equal weight." But isobares are entirely different from isotopes. They are two distinct elements, because the number and arrangement of free electrons is entirely different. In the Table of 

Atomic Numbers the isobares have entirely different positions. They happen to have the same number of protons in the nucleus, but their arrangement of electrons is different, and their properties are therefore different, too. 

328. Summary.—The idea that atoms are divisible came as a result of the discovery of radium. 

Radium disintegrates more rapidly than any other radioactive substance. It has a "half life" of about 1700 years. We cannot start, stop, or alter the rate of disintegration. 

When radium breaks down, it gives off alpha, beta, and gamma rays. Radon (radium emanation) is also formed. 

Alpha rays are positively charged helium atoms (He++). Beta rays are particles of negative electricity (-). Gamma rays are short x-rays. Besides rays, radium is constantly giving off energy as heat.

The electron theory is that all atoms are made up of equal numbers of (+) particles (protons) and (-) particles (electrons), arranged in definite ways. 

The proton is a hydrogen atom minus one electron (H+). An electron has a mass only about 1/1850 that of a proton. 

The mass of an atom (atomic weight) is practically the sum of the masses of its protons. 

All the protons and about half of the electrons of an atom are in its nucleus. The electrons not in the nucleus are free electrons. 

The number of free electrons in the atom of an element is the same as the atomic number of the element. 

The octet theory states that no element has more than 8 electrons in its outer ring. These are called the valence electrons. 

The reaction between metals and non-metals takes place because

the non-metals complete their rings of 8 (octets) by taking up the outer electrons of metals. 

A metal is an element the atom of which has a small number of outer (valence) electrons. The atom of a non-metal has almost enough outer electrons to complete a ring of 8. 

An electric

Metals are electric conductors because their atoms readily lose electrons and pass them on from one to another. current is a stream of electrons.

A compound of an active metal and an active non-metal is a polar compound. Polar compounds are ionized in water solution. 

The valence of a metal is the number of outer electrons it can give to a non-metal. The valence of a non-metal is the number of outer electrons it needs to complete its octet. 

The hydrogen atom acts like a halogen atom in lithium hydride; it also acts like a metal atom in hydrogen chloride. 

Isotopes are elements which have the same atomic number and therefore occupy the same place in the Periodic Table. They have the same number of outer electrons, but different numbers of protons in the nucleus. They have different atomic weights. 

Isobares are different elements which have the same atomic weight. They have the same number of protons in the nucleus, but an entirely different arrangement of the free electrons.

CHAPTER XXV METALS 

330. Properties of metals.-Metals are much more numerous than non-metals. The ancients knew several metals, but only one active non-metal-sulphur. The study of the rusting of metals (§ 5) formed the basis of modern chemistry. The reaction of the active metals with water (§ 36) gave an entirely new idea of what a base is. By reacting with acids the metals form hydrogen and salts (§ 37). The active metals displace not only hydrogen, but also less active metals. 2+ Fe°+Cu²++SO²→→→Fe²++SO4²+Cu° ↓ • As a result we can arrange the metals (and hydrogen) in a series called the activity, or displacement series, in which each metal can displace the metals below it, from solutions of their salts. The interpretation of these properties of the active metals is given by the electron theory, according to which the metal atoms have a small number of easily detached outer electrons (§§ 323 and 326); these account for the low valence of the metals and their great activity.

Metals are conductors of heat and the electric current. 

They are usually not transparent to light (opaque), but their surfaces reflect light. They have the metallic luster. 

The ancient mirror was an object of polished metal, perhaps a shield; the modern mirror has a thin film of metal (silver) spread over a glass surface. 395

ACTIVITY SERIES OF METALS 

Each metal displaces those below it from solutions of their salts.

397 The hydroxides of the active metals are strong bases. 

The salts formed by active metals with the radicals of strong acids have a neutral reaction and are not appreciably hydrolyzed by water. Illustrate the last statement by several examples. Some metals, like aluminum and zinc, have hydroxides that are bases toward strong acids, and yet are weak acids toward strong bases. Such hydroxides are said to be amphoteric (ăm'fō-ter'ĭk). 

331. How metals are found and prepared. The various elementary and compound substances found in the earth are called minerals. Those minerals used as sources of metals are called ores. Metals may be found native (free), or they may occur as oxides, sulphides, carbonates, or other compounds.

The art of extracting metals from their ores is called metallurgy (mět'ăl-ĕr'jí). If the operation includes the melting, or fusion, of the metal, it is called smelting. 

The oxides of metals are generally reduced by hot carbon (§ 275). Hydrogen might be used, if not too expensive.

Iron oxide and cupric oxide are reduced by coal or coke. 

Sulphides of metals are generally heated in a current of air ("roasted") before they are reduced by carbon. 2ZnS+302→2ZnO+2SO₂ ↑ • The activity series of the metals (§ 330) is not only a displacement series, but it also gives the metals in the order in which they are oxidized, and in which their oxides are reduced. Thus, the metals at the bottom of the list occur free in nature. If exposed to ordinary air, they do not rust (oxidize). We decompose their oxides by simply heating them. Which one have we decomposed in this way?

The oxides of the metals above copper we cannot decompose by heat alone; we must have some substance to combine with the oxygen. From copper to manganese the oxides can be reduced by a reducing agent. Above manganese the metals are so active that they must be set free by electrolysis. Thus, aluminum is obtained from its oxide, Al2O3, by means of the electric current. 

Sodium and potassium, the most important alkali metals, are likewise obtained by electrolysis. 

See $309 for a comparison of the properties of the five alkali metals.

Castner process for preparation of sodium. 

332. Sodium.-Sodium is the most common of the alkali metals. It was obtained by Davy from sodium hydroxide, and this is still its source in the Castner process. The other products of the electrolysis are oxygen and hydrogen (§ 207). Most of the sodium produced in North 

America is made at Niagara Falls.

The apparatus of the Castner process (Fig. 178) is an iron vessel that looks like an inverted bottle. It contains sodium hydroxide in a melted condition. The sodium hydroxide is kept melted by the resistance of the current (§ 274), assisted, when necessary, by a ring of burners beneath the iron vessel. The temperature is kept about 300° C: high enough to melt the hydroxide, but not high enough to cause the sodium to react with it. 2Na+2NaOH-2Na2O+H₂ ↑. 

The cathode, at which the sodium collects, is covered at its upper end with an iron cylinder. The lower part of the cylinder is really a gauze, perforated by many holes. The gauze allows a

METALS 399 free circulation of the melted hydroxide, but prevents the globules of melted sodium from reacting with the oxygen and water which are also produced. The sodium rises into the iron cylinder. Since the water produced is partly electrolyzed, hydrogen, as well as sodium, collects at the cathode. The hydrogen protects the sodium from oxidation. Oxygen is liberated at the anode and escapes through separate openings. The melted sodium is dipped out by means of ladles and poured into molds to harden. 

333. Properties.-Sodium has a white, metallic luster.

It is ordinarily so soft that it can be cut easily or pressed into wire form. It is harder when cold. Sodium burns readily in air, oxygen, chlorine, and the vapor of sulphur. 

When it burns in air, it has a brilliant, yellow flame. 

All sodium compounds impart this color to a Bunsen or alcohol flame. The formation of this color is a test for sodium compounds. Because of its activity, sodium should be kept under a liquid with which it does not react. Kerosene, or petroleum ether (also called ligroin; lig'rō-in) is used.

The product formed when sodium burns in air or oxygen is sodium peroxide, Na2O2. This is a salt of hydrogen peroxide (§ 69). It is the active material of "oxone," a commercial product which yields oxygen when treated with water. 2Na2O2+4HOH-4NaOH+2H2O2. 2H2O2→2H2O+O½ ↑ • Sodium monoxide, Na2O, is formed when sodium is heated strongly with sodium hydroxide (§ 332) in the absence of air. Sodium monoxide, like the peroxide, reacts with water to form the hydroxide. Write the equation. Sodium is used as a reducing agent, especially when mixed with mercury in the alloy, sodium amalgam.

334. Sodium hydroxide (NaOH).-Sodium hydroxide is also known as caustic soda. In the household it is called lye, or soda lye. The old process of preparing it is to boil a solution of sodium carbonate (soda) with slaked lime.

Na2CO3+Ca(OH)2→2NaOH+CaCO3 ↓ • The calcium carbonate is precipitated. The sodium hydroxide is obtained by the evaporation of the clear solution. It is a white solid with a slimy "feel," very deliquescent in moist air (§ 194), and very soluble in water.

The modern, commercial method of preparing sodium hydroxide is to electrolyze a concentrated solution of common salt. Sodium hydroxide and hydrogen are formed at the cathode, chlorine at the anode (§ 127). 2NaC1+2HOH→2NaOH+H₂ ↑ +Cl½ ↑ • The Nelson electrolytic cell is shown in Fig. 179. The anodes are of carbon (graphite); the cathode is a perforated steel plate lined with a porous diaphragm of asbestos. The diaphragm separates the electrodes. A current of salt solution flows between the electrodes, and some of the salt is electrolyzed. The chlorine produced is collected, and either liquefied, or converted into compounds used for bleaching (§ 134). The hydrogen is also collected. 

It may be used for fuel, or some of the hydrogen and chlorine may be caused to unite, forming "synthetic" hydrogen chloride. 

The sodium hydroxide remains in solution along with unchanged sodium chloride. The solution is evaporated until sodium chloride, which is less soluble than sodium hydroxide, crystallizes out. The sodium hydroxide solution is then evaporated, and the solid is obtained. It contains some sodium chloride, and must be purified further if pure sodium hydroxide is wanted. 

Sodium hydroxide is used in the laboratory as a base, as a drying agent (§ 83), and to absorb carbon dioxide.

METALS 

401 The amount produced in the United States in a year is about 660,000 tons. Two thirds of this amount is used in four great industries in about equal quantities: the manufacture of soap, rayon, chemicals (including lye), and the refining of petroleum.

N. OH N. CI 335. Sodium chloride (common salt).-Salt is obtained from rock deposits in the earth or from solution in salt wells, salt lakes, and the sea. 

The Dead Sea is about 22% salt, and Great Salt Lake, 20%. The Atlantic Ocean is about 3.6% sodium chloride. 

The evaporation of sea water and the water of salt lakes produces a crude salt. The salt water is usually run into shallow flats, or ponds, where the water is evaporated by the heat of the sun. Salt is obtained in this way on San Francisco Bay, on the coast of Sicily (near Syracuse), in Southern France, in India, and in many other places. In Northern Europe men obtain a concentrated salt solution from sea water by freezing out part of the water as ice. Only the last of the water need be evaporated by heat, and fuel is thus saved.

FIG. 179. 

Nelson cell for manufacture of sodium hydroxide. Hydrogen and chlorine are by-products.

Vast deposits of rock salt occur in Germany, Jugo- 

Slavia, Austria, and the United States. What is probably the world's largest salt bed is 100,000 square miles

in extent, in Kansas, Oklahoma, and Texas. It is 300 to 650 feet thick. Much salt is produced in California, Utah, and West Virginia. Michigan, New York, and

Ohio have extensive salt wells. In the United States the 

FIG. 180. 

Pyramids of salt obtained near Cadiz, Spain, by evaporation of sea water by the sun's heat. water from salt wells is usually evaporated under "reduced pressure," in vacuum pans. Much rock salt is not mined directly as a solid, but the deposits are flooded with water, and the resulting salt solution is then forced to the surface and there evaporated. The United States produces almost 8,000,000 tons of salt yearly. 

Animals as well as men require salt and make every effort to get it. Salt must be present in a definite amount in our blood, if

METALS 

403 we are to have health, and the hydrochloric acid of the gastric juice is prepared from it.

The salt used on our tables is usually not pure, but has small amounts of calcium chloride and magnesium chloride in it. These take up moisture from the air and cause the salt crystals to cake together. If we add a little starch to such salt, it covers the salt crystals, so that they do not stick together. If we add sodium bicarbonate (baking soda), it reacts with the impurities, forming insoluble carbonates.

Salt crystallizes in colorless cubes which arrange themselves in hopper-shaped masses (§ 80). Its solubility is only a little greater at 100° C. than at 0° (see solubility curve, §75). It melts at 

815° C. and goes into the gaseous state at perhaps 1400° C. 

Salt is used as food, as a preservative of meat and fish, and as a component of freezing mixtures. Chemical industries use it as a source of chlorine, sodium hydroxide, hydrochloric acid, sodium carbonate and bicarbonate, and in many other ways. Iodized salt contains a small proportion of sodium or potassium iodide. 

This is added to supply iodine in regions in which the water and food do not contain enough iodine. 

336. Sodium carbonate, or soda (Na2CO3).—There are some manufactured substances that are of the greatest importance to us. Sulphuric acid is one of them. Sodium carbonate is another. The manufacture of sodium carbonate has arisen since about 1800 A.D. Before that time practically all the soda used in the world was obtained by burning sea plants and dissolving the soda out of the ashes. The supply was small and the cost great. 

Cheap glass and cheap soap had to wait for cheap soda.

Two large deposits of natural soda have been discovered: one in British East Africa and one in Southern California. 

The LeBlanc process, developed during the French Revolution, was the first of the large-scale methods of preparing soda. It begins with common salt. The

sodium chloride is changed to sodium sulphate (§ 137), the sodium sulphate to sodium sulphide (§§ 180 and 190), and the sodium sulphide to sodium carbonate. (1) NaCl+H2SO4 NaHSO4+HC1 ↑ . NaCl+NaHSO4 Na2SO4+HCl ↑ . (2) Na2SO4+2C-Na2S+2CO₂ ↑ . (3) Na2S+CaCO3→Na2CO3+CaS. 

As the equations show, the raw materials required are salt, coal, and limestone, with sulphuric acid. The chief by-product (what is the meaning of this?) of value is hydrogen chloride. The sale of this by-product has kept the process going in factories in which the remainder of the process has been too costly. 

The present-day process of preparing soda is called the ammonia-soda, or Solvay, process. The primary product is the bicarbonate, NaHCO3, from which the normal carbonate is made. The materials needed for the Solvay process are salt, ammonia, carbon dioxide, and water. 

The most expensive of these is the ammonia. As we should expect, the ammonia and water unite to form ammonium hydroxide, and the carbon dioxide and water· unite to form carbonic acid. The base and the acid react (the carbonic acid being in excess) to form ammonium hydrogen carbonate (bicarbonate).

NH4OH+H2CO3→NH4HCO3+H2O. 

Finally a double decomposition takes place between the sodium chloride and ammonium bicarbonate. 

NaCl+NH,HCO,NaHCO3] +NHẠC1.

As the equation suggests, this reaction occurs because the sodium bicarbonate is precipitated, and thus removed from the

METALS 

405 "sphere of action." The bicarbonate is removed and dried, and then changed to the normal carbonate (soda) by heat. 2NaHCO3 Na2CO3+H2O ↑ +CO2 ↑ • If, after the normal carbonate (Na2CO3) has been purified, the bicarbonate (NaHCO3) is wanted, it is prepared by addition of an excess of carbon dioxide, that is, of carbonic acid, to the solution of the carbonate. Na2CO3+H2O+CO2→2NaHCO3. 

We are really dealing with a reversible reaction.

Na2CO3+H2CO3—2NaHCO3. 

The Solvay process is an example of wonderful economy. The carbon dioxide needed is produced by the heating of limestone. CaCO,CaO+CO, 1. 

The ammonium chloride, which remains in solution when the bicarbonate is precipitated, is not wasted, but is treated with slaked lime, Ca(OH)2, obtained from the quicklime (CaO); thus the ammonia is recovered, and used over and over again, with only a small loss.

Ca(OH)2+2NH4C1→CaCl2+2NH3 ↑ +2H2O. 

When the bicarbonate is heated, half its carbon dioxide is given off; this is likewise used again. The calcium chloride is the "by-product" of the process. It has many uses (§ 358). 

A third process of preparing soda is by electrolysis. In the electrolysis of salt, the products are sodium hydroxide, chlorine, and hydrogen (§§ 127 and 334). If the sodium hydroxide solution is treated with carbon dioxide in the right proportions, sodium. carbonate and bicarbonate are produced. 

337. Properties and uses.-Sodium carbonate in the anhydrous form (Na2CO3) is also called soda ash. It is a white powder which is moderately soluble in water. 

When it separates from solution, soda takes the form of

large, colorless crystals having the formula Na2CO3. 

10H2O, and is called sal soda or washing soda. The hydrate (washing soda) effloresces in the air (§ 82). The solution of sodium carbonate has a strongly alkaline reaction, because the carbonate is hydrolyzed by water (§ 211).

Over 40% of the soda produced is used in the making of glass (§ 303). Much is used in the manufacture of soap. A great deal is converted into sodium hydroxide for soap making (§ 334). Soda is an ingredient of most water softeners and washing powders. It is also used in the manufacture of paints, drugs, and paper, and in the refining of petroleum.

Sodium bicarbonate is also called sodium hydrogen carbonate and sodium acid carbonate. Its household names are baking soda and saleratus. It is less soluble than the carbonate. Its crystals are small and without crystal water.

Sodium bicarbonate is used chiefly as a source of carbon dioxide (in baking soda and baking powders; § 283), in effervescing mixtures, and in fire extinguishers and "chemical” engines. It is used in medicine to neutralize acids. It is somewhat unstable, and gives off carbon dioxide even at the temperature of boiling water. 

338. Sodium nitrate.-As we have already learned (§ 231), sodium nitrate is found, as Chile saltpeter, in the dry regions of Chile and Peru. The natural salt (“caliche") contains considerable sodium iodate, NaIO3. The crude salt is purified by crystallization from water. 

Owing to its great solubility sodium nitrate attracts the moisture of the air (is hygroscopic). It is therefore not as suitable as potassium nitrate for the preparation of

METALS 

407 gunpowder. A great deal of it is used as a fertilizer for soil and as a source of potassium nitrate and nitric acid. 

339. Sodium cyanide and other salts.-Sodium cyanide, NaCN, is used to extract gold and silver from their ores and to form an electroplating solution, especially for silver plating (§ 390). It is a source of hydrocyanic acid 

(§ 288). Write the equation. 

Sodium cyanide solution, like that of potassium cyanide, has a strongly alkaline reaction due to hydrolysis. 

The solutions of cyanides are very poisonous. 

Sodium cyanide is formed when sodium carbonate, carbon, and calcium cyanamide are heated together. Na2CO3+CaCN2+C→CaCO3+2NaCN. 

It is also produced when sodium is heated with a carbon compound containing nitrogen. Its formation is a test for the presence of nitrogen in such compounds. 

All common sodium salts are soluble. The same is true of the common salts of potassium and ammonium. 

Ordinary sodium phosphate is disodium hydrogen phosphate,

Na₂HPO4.12H2O (§ 255). The normal and acid sodium sulphates were described in § 137. The normal sulphate crystallizes from water in large, colorless crystals, Na2SO4.10H2O. It is called 

Glauber's salt. It effloresces readily. Sodium sulphate is used as a substitute for sodium carbonate in the manufacture of glass. Na2SO4+SiO2-Na2SiO3+SO3 ↑. 

Sodium sulphite, Na2SO3, is used as an antichlor, to remove the excess of chlorine from bleached cloth (§§ 134 and 184). 

Sodium thiosulphate, or "hypo," is Na2S2O3.5H2O (§ 190).

Sodium silicate, or water glass, is Na2SiO3 (§ 300). 

Borax is sodium tetraborate, Na2B4O7.10H2O (§ 306). 

Give the properties and uses of the last three salts.

340. Potassium.-In preparation and properties potassium resembles sodium. It is made by the electrolysis of potassium chloride or hydroxide. Its color is bluish white. 

The metal must be protected from the water and oxygen of the air. In burning it forms the tetroxide, K204. 

Potassium reacts with water even more violently than sodium does, so that the escaping hydrogen is set on fire by the heat of the reaction. 

While the vapors of sodium compounds color the Bunsen flame yellow, potassium compounds make it violet.

Sir Humphry Davy prepared both sodium and potassium by the electrolysis of their hydroxides in 1807. 

An American chemist, James Woodhouse of Philadelphia, prepared potassium, about the same year, by heating wood ashes containing charcoal. K2CO3+2C-2K+3CO. 

341. Importance of potassium compounds.-Potassium compounds are necessary to plant growth (§ 256). A soil obtains its potassium compounds by the natural decomposition (weathering) of feldspar, a potassium aluminum silicate (§ 301). When crops are grown regularly upon a given soil, they often remove so much of the "potash" that it must be supplied by artificial fertilizers. Hence there is a great demand, in civilized countries, for potassium salts. The most important sources of them at present are the Stassfurt deposits, in Germany, and similar deposits in France. These deposits contain thick layers of common salt, and, above these, potassium, magnesium, and calcium salts in large amount. The potassium salts: KCl and K₂SO4, are obtained in separate

METALS 

409 form by careful solution and crystallization. There is a large deposit of potassium salts in Russia. • Deposits of potassium compounds are being eagerly sought in North America. There are important ones in Searles Lake (California), in western Nebraska, and in Texas. 

Beet-sugar residues and the secretion, called "suint," found in sheep's wool, contain potassium compounds. Cream of tartar is potassium hydrogen tartrate, KнCHO. It is a white solid found in crude form in the juice of the grape, and used as the "acid" in baking powders. When cream of tartar is heated, it is decomposed, and the residue contains potassium carbonate, K2CO3. 

Plants contain potassium salts of organic acids; these salts are changed to potassium carbonate when the plants are burned. 

Hence we find "potash" in wood ashes. On the Pacific Coast a seaweed, the giant kelp, is collected and burned for the potash found in its ashes. 

342. Potassium carbonate and hydroxide.—Potassium carbonate is known as potash and as pearlash. It is a white solid which absorbs water from the air. Potash, and therefore potassium, gets its name from "pot" and "ash." The original method of obtaining it was to extract wood ashes with water and then to evaporate the solution in an iron pot or kettle. When the solution of potash is mixed with slaked lime, potassium hydroxide, or caustic potash, is produced.

K2CO3+Ca(OH)2→2KOH+CaCO3 ↓ · Potassium hydroxide is also made by the electrolysis of potassium chloride, as is sodium hydroxide. It is a white solid which deliquesces in the air. It is the most active of our common bases. Its solution "cuts" grease as caustic soda does, forming soaps. Potassium soaps usually have a low melting point, and are therefore called "soft" soaps. 1

343. Compounds of potassium with halogens.-Potas sium chlorate, KClO3, is a source of oxygen (§ 20). It is formed when chlorine in excess is passed into hot potassium hydroxide solution. 6KOH+3C12-5KC1+KC1O3+3H2O. 

The separation of potassium chlorate from the chloride is possible because the chlorate is much less soluble in cold water. When, therefore, the mixture containing the two salts is concentrated, and then cooled, the potassium chlorate crystallizes out first. 

Another method is to prepare calcium chlorate first from chlorine and "milk of lime," and then to put potassium chloride with it. 6Ca(OH)2+6C12-5CaCl2+Ca(ClO3)2+6H2O. 2KCl+Ca(ClO3)2→2KC1O3 ↓ +CaCl2. 

As an oxidizing agent potassium chlorate finds application in the formation of some dyes, in matches, in fireworks, and as "potash" tablets for sore throat. Its use in explosives is attended with danger. It must never be powdered in a mortar except when the mortar and pestle are clean, since even dust produces a violent explosion with it.

Potassium chloride is a white solid found in large amount in the Stassfurt deposits. It is the starting point in the manufacture of most other potassium compounds.

Potassium bromide and iodide resemble the chloride. They are prepared from the hydroxide by the action of bromine and iodine, respectively. 6KOH+3Br₂→5KBr+KBrO3+3H2O. 6KOH+ 312 5KI+ KIO3+3H₂O. 

The reaction in each case is like that for the preparation of potassium chlorate. If the bromate and iodate are not wanted,

METALS 

411 the solutions are evaporated to dryness, and then heated further. 

The bromate and iodate are thus decomposed, just as potassium chlorate is, and the bromide and iodide, respectively, are left. 2KBrO3-2KBг+302 ↑. 2KIO3→ 2KI+30½ ↑ · 

344. Potassium sulphates and other salts. When an excess of concentrated sulphuric acid is used with potassium chloride, bromide, or iodide, with potassium nitrate, and even with normal potassium sulphate, the acid sulphate is formed (§ 196). The acid sulphate has a strongly acid reaction. Is this true of all acid salts?

K2SO4+H2SO4—2KHSO4. 

When potassium hydroxide or acid potassium sulphate is neutralized with sulphuric acid, or when a sufficient amount of a potassium salt, such as the chloride, is heated with the acid sulphate. the normal sulphate is formed.

KC1+KHSO4→K2SO4+HC1 ↑ . 

Potassium sulphate (K2SO4) is used as fertilizer. It crystallizes from water without water of hydration. Is this true also of sodium sulphate? For the preparation and uses of potassium nitrate review §§ 227 and 231. 

Potassium cyanide resembles sodium cyanide and is used for the same purposes. 

Potassium thiocyanate (KSCN; also called potassium sulphocyanate) is used for its thiocyanate ions (SCN) to distinguish ferric from ferrous salts (§ 425). The corresponding ammonium salt (NH4SCN) is used for the same purpose. 

345. Lithium.-Lithium is the lightest metal; its density is 

0.53 g. per cu. cm. It has the atomic number 3. Minerals containing it are found in many places in small amount, and the

element can be detected in the ashes of beets and tobacco. Lithium combines readily with hydrogen (LiH) and with nitrogen (LiзN), as well as with oxygen and the halogens. Lithium hydride acts like a salt. In electrolysis the hydrogen goes to the positive pole (anode), as a halogen would. The vapors of lithium compounds give a bright red color to the colorless flame. 

346. Tests for the alkali metals. We commonly test for the alkali metals in their compounds by the flame test, that is, by the color which these compounds give to the colorless Bunsen flame. Thus, lithium salts color the flame bright red, sodium salts make it bright yellow, and potassium salts make it violet. To make the flame test we use a piece of platinum or nichrome wire sealed into a short glass tube. We heat the end of the wire, dip it into the substance to be tested, and then hold the wire in the Bunsen flame. If both sodium and potassium salts are present, the bright yellow color of the sodium hides the violet color of the potassium. To see the color of the potassium by itself we look at the flame through a thick piece of cobalt-blue glass. 

This cuts off the yellow light of the sodium.

FIG. 181. 

The spectrum of white light. 

In order to detect very small amounts of the alkali metals we use a spectroscope. 

347. The spectroscope. We know that white light is made up of light of many different colors. Review § 314. 

These colors, called primary colors, are violet, indigo,

METALS 

413 blue, green, yellow, orange, and red (VIBGYOR). The band of colors obtained from white light is called a spectrum. The rainbow is a spectrum of sunlight, or a solar spectrum. We can produce such a spectrum in a dark room if we admit a beam of sunlight, and pass the beam through a glass prism or grating. A grating consists of a piece of glass with many parallel lines ruled very close together, say, 10,000 or 15,000 to the inch. 

A spectrum is formed because the light waves of different colors leave the prism or grating at slightly different angles. The shortest waves (violet) are bent most; the longest waves (red) are bent least. If the beam of sunlight enters through a narrow slit, we obtain a series of images of the slit side by side. These form the colored band called the spectrum. FLAME

SCALE PRISM OBSERVER TELESCOPE 

FLAME

COLLIMATOR SCALE PRISM FIG. 182. 

A spectroscope and a cross-section of it.

OBSERVER 

The spectroscope was designed in 1859 by Bunsen and Kirchhoff. By means of it they discovered the alkali metals, rubidium and caesium. Later it was used in the discovery of helium and other elements. The spectroscope has a prism and three tubes called, respectively, the collimator (kŏl'I-mā'ter), scale tube, and telescope. When in use the prism is kept covered. The collimator

has a narrow slit for the admission of the light beam. The beam has, of course, the shape of the slit. The collimator also has lenses which direct the narrow beam of light to the prism as parallel rays. The prism (or grating) of a spectroscope converts the image of the slit into a series of parallel, colored images-the spectrum. The scale tube contains a graduated scale illuminated by some source of light. The telescope magnifies the scale and the spectrum.

The observer, looking through the telescope, sees the spectrum and the scale together, so that he can locate any part of the spectrum by its position on the scale. When white light, such as light from a burning gas flame or white-hot platinum, is admitted through the slit of the collimator, the observer sees a spectrum consisting of a band of the primary colors. When a sodium salt is held in a colorless Bunsen flame, in front of the slit in the collimator, the observer sees not a band, but a yellow line on that part of the scale which is yellow when white light is being examined. The yellow line is double when highly magnified, but is always in a definite position. The yellow light is due to the vapor of sodium, not the solid particles. The atoms of the element, when at a high enough temperature, give out light of definite wave lengths.

When the light of a heated potassium salt (potassium vapor) is examined, it is found to have two bright lines in the red and one very prominent line in the violet part of the spectrum. See the Frontispiece. The vapor of lithium compounds is distinguished by two lines in the red. The chemist can therefore determine the presence of even a very small amount of an element. 

Not only so, but he can detect an element when several other elements are present. No two elements have the same spectrum. 

If the compound of an element cannot be vaporized in the Bunsen flame, an electric arc, which is much hotter, is used. This produces a spark spectrum. The spectrum of a gas may be obtained if the gas is sealed in a "vacuum" tube and subjected to the spark discharge.

When the light given off by white-hot (incandescent) solids or liquids is examined, it is found to have a con-

METALS 

415 tinuous spectrum. It emits light of all colors. But in the case of a white-hot gas, such as the vapor of sodium or lithium, the spectrum consists of bright lines. Now, if we heat a solid element white hot, and let its light pass through the vapor of the same substance, the characteristic lines of the element show in the right places, but they are dark instead of colored. The spectrum is an absorption spectrum. It is a rule that a gas will absorb light of just those wave lengths which it itself can give off when white hot. 

How, then, is it with sunlight? The spectrum of sunlight is a continuous one; it must therefore be produced by solids or liquids in the nucleus of the sun. But the sun's spectrum is crossed by dark lines (called Fraunhofer's lines from the discoverer). These dark lines are in the exact positions on the spectroscope scale which correspond to the colored lines of well known elements. 

Thus, instead of the yellow line of sodium, a dark line appears in the spectrum of sunlight. Scientists therefore conclude that the yellow light of sodium is given off by the sun's nucleus, but that this light passes through sodium vapor in the sun's atmosphere, and is largely absorbed. As a result an absorption spectrum, with dark lines, is produced. In the same way the light spectrum of a star reveals the presence in that far-away body of the common elements of our own earth. 

348. Ammonium salts. As we have learned (§ 225), ammonium salts contain the NH4 radical (in solution, the NH4+ ion), which acts like a metal, and in electrolysis goes to the cathode. If the cathode is of mercury, a bulky solid, called ammonium amalgam, is formed. 

This contains ammonium dissolved in mercury. The

same substance is produced when a concentrated solution of ammonium chloride is treated with sodium amalgam. 

Ammonium seems to be formed, but it decomposes almost at once into ammonia and hydrogen. 

Since all the common ammonium salts are soluble, they are very useful in the laboratory as sources of acid radicals (negative ions). But there is another reason for the use of ammonium salts in preference to those of sodium or potassium. If we wish to get rid of the NH4+ ion at any time, we have only to evaporate the solution to dryness and then heat the dry residue. All the ammonium salts present break up when heated. Thus ammonium chloride gives ammonia and hydrogen chloride, which escape in gaseous form. Ammonium nitrate yields nitrous oxide and water (§ 237). Ammonium phosphate gives ammonia, which goes off as a gas, and phosphoric acid, which remains behind. In every case the ammonium ion is destroyed. 

Ammonium chloride (sal ammoniac) is a white, crystalline solid. 

It is used to clean an iron surface before soldering or galvanizing, and as the active reagent (with zinc) in "dry" electric cells. 

Ammonium sulphate (NH4)2SO4, is also a white solid. used mainly as a fertilizer, to introduce nitrogen into a soil. 

Ammonium sulphide (NH4)2S, is an important laboratory reagent because its solution contains sulphide ions (S2-) in much greater concentration than hydrogen sulphide itself. We prepare it by adding hydrogen sulphide to ammonia water. After the ammonia water has been saturated with hydrogen sulphide, it contains the hydrosulphide, NH4SH. Then we add as much ammonium hydroxide as we used originally; this produces the normal sulphide, (NH4)2S.

NH4OH+H2S-NH,SH+H2O. NH4SH+NH4OH→(NH4)2S+H2O.

METALS 

417 Ammonium sulphide is colorless when fresh; but when allowed to stand in contact with the oxygen of the air it becomes yellow. 

Some sulphur is set free and unites with the unchanged ammonium sulphide forming ammonium polysulphide, (NH4)2Sx, which is yellow. The "x" in the formula may be any number from 2 to 5. 

When we treat colorless ammonium sulphide directly with sulphur, we obtain the same product. 

Ammonium carbonate (NH4)2CO3, is another important laboratory reagent. Generally it is mixed with the bicarbonate, NH4HCO3. 

Review the Solvay process (§ 336) and suggest how these substances may be prepared. Ammonium carbonate is used as a source of carbonate ions (CO;2-), just as ammonium sulphide is used for its S²- ions. Does carbonic acid give a large concentration of CO2- ions? 

Ammonium carbonate is a salt of a weak base and a weak acid; it is therefore largely hydrolyzed in solution. The solution "softens" hard water (§ 357). The salt is called "solid" ammonia; it gives off gaseous ammonia, and is used in "smelling salts." The solid breaks up, when heated, into ammonia, carbon dioxide, and water -all gases; hence it is sometimes used as a baking powder. 

The test for all ammonium salts is that when they are heated with a strong base, such as slaked lime or sodium hydroxide, they yield ammonia. This may be detected by its odor and by its effect upon moist pink (or neutral) litmus. What is its effect? 

349. Summary.-Metallurgy is the art of extracting metals. from their ores.

The activity series of the metals gives a summary of the properties of the metals and their compounds. 

The alkali metals include lithium, sodium, and potassium and the radical ammonium.

This is the elec- 

Sodium is prepared by the Castner process. trolysis of melted sodium hydroxide. Sodium is an active metal which reacts with water and burns in air or oxygen. 

Sodium hydroxide (caustic soda) is prepared by the electrolysis of sodium chloride solution in the Nelson cell. Hydrogen and chlorine are the by-products. 

Sodium chloride (salt) is used in food and for the production of sodium compounds.

Soda (sodium carbonate) is made by the LeBlanc process, the Solvay process, and the electrolytic process. It is used in making soap and glass and to soften water.

Baking soda is sodium bicarbonate. All common sodium salts are soluble. Sodium salts color the Bunsen flame yellow. 

Potassium is an active metal made by electrolysis of its hydroxide. Its compounds color the Bunsen flame violet. 

Potassium salts are necessary for plant growth. Potash is potassium carbonate. It is present in wood ashes. 

The greatest commercial sources of potassium compounds are deposits found in Germany and France.

Potassium hydroxide is prepared by electrolysis of potassium chloride.

Potassium chlorate is prepared from chlorine and potassium hydroxide. All common potassium salts are soluble. 

The spectroscope is an instrument for detecting small amounts of metals by their "lines" in a spectrum. 

Ammonium salts contain the radical ammonium. Practically all ammonium salts are soluble. 

The test for an ammonium salt is the fact that it gives off ammonia when heated with an alkali hydroxide or with lime.

351. Members of the family. The most important substances we shall study in this chapter are limestone and lime. Limestone has had a great deal to do with man's history, for it supplied him with building stone, and, more important, with mortar to hold building stones and bricks together. The calcium family includes the metals calcium, strontium, and barium. The rare metal radium also belongs here. The valence of the metals is

421 352. Calcium.-Consult § 10 for the abundance of calcium in the earth's crust. The most abundant of its compounds is calcium carbonate, CaCO3. This has many natural forms, such as limestone, marble, chalk, calcite, and coral. The shells of many marine animals consist largely of calcium carbonate. Calcium sulphate, calcium phosphate, and calcium fluoride are important minerals. 

Write the formula of each one. Gypsum is the hydrate of calcium sulphate (CaSO4.2H₂O). 

Calcium is prepared by the electrolysis of melted calcium chloride. The cathode is a rod of copper and iron. As the calcium is set free, it collects at the cathode. The cathode is lifted automatically, so that a stick of metal several feet long is gradually raised out of the melted calcium chloride. The calcium chloride at the bottom of the anode is kept solid by pipes of cold water. The calcium chloride above it is kept melted by the resistance of the current. HIO H:O 

CALCIUM CHLORIDE. 

FIG. 183. 

Preparation of calcium by electrolysis of calcium chloride. HiO

Calcium is a white solid, a little harder than lead, and capable of being hammered into sheets and drawn into wire. It does not react readily with dry air until heated to its melting point. Then it burns vigorously, forming calcium oxide and calcium nitride (Ca3N2). Write the equation for each reaction. Calcium decomposes water as sodium does, but more slowly. 

353. Calcium oxide and hydroxide.-Calcium oxide and hydroxide are among the most useful substances known to man. They are used to prepare glass, mortar, and cement, to refine sugar and fuel gas, to soften water, and to extract metals from their ores. They are also

used in the preparation of ammonia, the hydroxides of sodium and potassium, bleaching powder and chlorates, and as disinfectants. Whitewash usually contains calcium hydroxide. Limewater is a saturated solution of calcium hydroxide. What is its solubility? See Appendix. "Milk of lime" is a suspension of undissolved calcium hydroxide in limewater.

Lime, or quicklime, is prepared when limestone or marble is heated above 600° C.

CaCO3=>CaO+CO,. 

The reaction is a reversible one, and succeeds on a commercial scale because the carbon dioxide is removed from the calcium oxide as rapidly as it is formed. 

LIME FIG. 184. 

LIME. 

STONE LONG FLAME 

Manufacture of lime from limestone.

The limestone is heated in a furnace called a lime-kiln. The two modern forms of limekilns are vertical kilns and horizontal kilns. 

The vertical kiln consists of a high chimney which is filled from the top. Several fire boxes are built outside this chimney at some distance from the bottom, and the hot flame from the fires enters the chimney and causes the decomposition of the limestone. The lime which has been formed settles below the fire boxes, becomes cool, and is removed, making way for fresh limestone at the top. 

In the horizontal lime-kiln the limestone is heated by blasts of hot gases, and the kiln is kept turning by means of machinery. Each of these processes is a continuous one, as limestone is put in at one end of the kiln, and lime is removed at the other end.

Lime is a white, porous solid which melts only at the temperature of the electric furnace. When heated with

CALCIUM FAMILY 

423 the oxyhydrogen flame or the oxyacetylene flame it gives out a brilliant, white light ("limelight"). The reaction between lime and water is called the slaking of lime. It takes place with the evolution of much heat and is a reversible reaction. Write the equation.

Write the equation. The heat de veloped when water accidentally comes in contact with quicklime often causes serious fires. When left in the air, quicklime unites with both the moisture and the carbon dioxide of the air, forming air-slaked lime. What substances would you expect to be present in air-slaked lime? 

354. Calcium carbonate.—In the form of limestone and marble calcium carbonate forms immense deposits in the earth's crust. The rock was formed under water, probably from the shells and mineral parts of marine animals. 

These accumulated during long periods of time. Pressure and high temperatures caused them to become compact layers of rock. Then the rock was raised above sea level. The forms of the original structures of the animals are often seen in fossils. Some limestone is plainly such a mass of shells that it is called shell rock. Marble is a limestone with a crystalline structure produced by heat and pressure. Iceland spar is a form having especially fine crystals (§ 80). It has the power of double refraction of light: an object seen through it appears double. 

Limestone combined with magnesium carbonate is called dolomite. When mixed with considerable clay, limestone forms marl, which is valuable in the making of cement (§ 381). Chalk was formerly used for blackboard crayon, but this is now largely made of other white materials. Marble is used as a building stone and as a source of lime. Calcium carbonate is only slightly dissolved by water, but it reacts with carbonic acid to form calcium bicar-

Hence limestone dis-
