Fountain of the Fairies, Carlsbad Cavern, New Mexico. dissolved by underground waters which trickle through it. This is the origin of limestone caves. CaCO3+H2O+CO₂ Ca (HCO3)2. 

The reaction between calcium carbonate, carbon dioxide, and water is a reversible one. When water containing calcium bicarbonate trickles through the roof of a cave, some of the carbon dioxide with which it was charged escapes. As a result some of the limestone in the water is deposited. The pendants of such deposits suspended from the roof are called stalactites; the pillars

CALCIUM FAMILY 

425 that rise from the floor to meet them are stalagmites. The limestone deposits made in the cave, like the formation of the cave itself, must represent long periods of time. 

355. Mortar.-We apply the name "mortar" to a mixture of slaked lime, sand, and water taken in proper proportions. When mortar is used upon inside walls, we speak of it as plaster. Frequently hair is mixed with the plaster to increase its cohesion. The chemical change which takes place when mortar "sets" consists chiefly in its losing water and absorbing carbon dioxide. The carbon dioxide unites with the slaked lime to form calcium carbonate. Sand is used in mortar and cement to prevent the mortar from contracting when it sets. Sand also makes the mortar porous, so that carbon dioxide can enter and unite with the calcium hydroxide. The drying of freshly plastered walls is often hastened by the use of open charcoal stoves, which increase the supply of carbon dioxide. In thick walls the setting of the mortar often takes years for its completion. 

356. Calcium sulphate.-Calcium sulphate occurs in the form of rocks as gypsum and as anhydrite, which, as the name indicates, is anhydrous calcium sulphate. What is the formula of gypsum? Alabaster is a form of gypsum. When gypsum is heated hot enough to drive off all its crystal water, it forms anhydrous calcium sulphate. This unites with water very slowly; but if gypsum is heated to about 130° C., it loses only three-fourths of its crystal water and produces plaster of Paris. 2CaSO4.2H2O⇒(CaSO4)2.H2O+3H₂O. 

When plaster of Paris is mixed with the right amount of water, it forms a paste which hardens, and expands in doing so. Because

of this property plaster of Paris is useful for making casts of art objects, surgical bandages, and dentists' casts, as a finish for plastered walls, and as a cement. The equation for the reaction is the reverse of the one representing the formation of plaster of Paris from gypsum. Stucco usually consists of plaster of Paris mixed with broken stone and a glue "solution". Wall-board is made of plaster of Paris held between sheets of stiff paper. 

Calcium sulphate is considerably more soluble than calcium carbonate, and is present in many natural waters. 

357. The softening of water.-We speak of rain water as "soft," because it wets the skin rather easily, and because soap forms suds or lather quickly in it. In the water of a well or spring the skin is not easily wet, and soap forms lather with great difficulty. We call such water hard water. See § 66. The problem of softening water consists in the removal of either its temporary hardness or its permanent hardness or both. To remove permanent hardness we soften, or "break," the water by means of a soluble carbonate, such as sodium carbonate or ammonium carbonate. The calcium (or magnesium) is thereby precipitated out in the form of an insoluble carbonate.

Na2CO3+CaSO4→CaCO; ↓ +Na2SO4. 

The softened water now contains a sodium salt instead of the calcium or magnesium salt. Since soap is itself a sodium salt, no reaction takes place between the soap and the softened water. 

If the water has only temporary hardness, we can "break" it by heating the water to boiling or by adding the amount of slaked lime that is needed.

Ca(HCO3)2 CaCO3 | +H2O+CO₂ ↑. Ca (HCO3)2+Ca(OH)2-2CaCO3+2H2O.

CALCIUM FAMILY 

427 In order to soften water containing both temporary and permanent hardness, sodium hydroxide may be used. The base reacts with the carbonic acid of the calcium bicarbonate, and forms the insoluble calcium carbonate; it also produces sodium carbonate, which reacts with the permanent hardness. Ca(HCO3)2+2NaOH→CaCO;↓ +Na2CO3+2H2O. CaSO4+Na2CO3-CaCO3 ↓ +Na2SO4. 

If the hard water contains no magnesium salts, ammonium compounds may be used to soften it, but magnesium compounds are not precipitated completely by ammonium hydroxide or ammonium carbonate. 

A modern method of softening water in the household or laundry is the permutit process. The "softener" is a manufactured product, called permutit. We may consider permutit to be sodium aluminum silicate, NaAlSiO4. Its reaction with calcium sulphate is shown in the equation, 2NaAlSiO4+CaSO4 Na2SO4+Ca (AlSiO4)2. 

Since large amounts of calcium sulphate pass over the permutit, it is converted, after awhile, into the calcium compound. It must then be regenerated. Putting in an excess of salt produces the result.

Ca (AlSiO4)2+2NaC1→2NaAlSiO4+CaCl2. 

The permutit is now ready to remove calcium salts once more. 

Magnesium salts react with permutit like calcium salts. 

358. Other compounds of calcium.-Calcium forms many useful compounds aside from those already described. Anhydrous calcium chloride is used as a drying agent (§ 83). Owing to its great attraction for the water of the air, it can also be used to keep down the dust on roads and in coal mines. It absorbs moisture from the air, forming a solution which wets the dust, and prevents it from blowing away in the wind. The hydrate, CaCl2.- 

6H2O, when mixed with snow or chopped ice, produces a

freezing mixture having a temperature of about -50° C. 

The concentrated solution of calcium chloride is used as a brine in ice manufacture (§ 224). 

The occurrence of calcium phosphate in bones and as mineral deposits (rock phosphate) has been given in $ 256.

Bleaching powder, CaOCl2, is a very important compound, made from chlorine and calcium hydroxide. Its preparation and its behavior with dilute acids are described in § 134. The hydrolysis of bleaching powder is represented by the equation:

CaOCl2+2H2O→Ca(OH)2+HC1+HOC1. 

The powder is used not only as a bleaching agent, but also to sterilize water (§ 65). 

Calcium silicate, CaSiO3, is present along with sodium silicate in glass (§ 303). Calcium sulphide, CaS, is formed in the reduction of calcium sulphate with carbon. It is an intermediate product in the LeBlanc process of making soda (§ 336). The sulphides of calcium, strontium, and barium glow, or phosphoresce, after being exposed to sunlight, and are therefore used in making luminous paints. Calcium carbide, CaC2, is the source of acetylene and of many commercial organic compounds, such as acetic acid. It is a grayish solid prepared by heating lime with coke in the electric furnace (§§ 275 and 293).

CaO+3C→CaC,+CO 1. 

Calcium cyanamide is formed when calcium carbide is heated in an electric furnace in a stream of nitrogen (§ 238). It is used as a fertilizer for soil. Calcium bisulphite, Ca (HSO3)2, is used extensively for bleaching wood pulp in paper manufacture. Calcium fluoride is used as a source of hydrofluoric acid and in the manufacture of steel. 

359. Strontium compounds. Strontium is a metal re-

CALCIUM FAMILY 

429 sembling calcium, but much less abundant. Like calcium, it is prepared by the electrolysis of its chloride. Strontium reacts with water as calcium does. Write the equation for the reaction. Strontium oxide might be formed by heating its carbonate, as calcium oxide is formed from calcium carbonate, but the reaction takes place at a much higher temperature. It is easiest to prepare strontium oxide by heating the nitrate, Sr(NO3)2. See § 231, and write the equation. The least soluble strontium salt is the carbonate. When strontium sulphate ("insoluble") is heated with a sodium carbonate solution, the still less soluble carbonate is formed. Write the equation for this reaction. Strontium salts give a red color to the Bunsen flame. This color, along with the insolubility of strontium carbonate, serves as a test for strontium compounds. 

360. Barium compounds.-Barium, like calcium and strontium, is obtained by the electrolysis of its chloride. 

The metal has recently become more important commercially. The modern method of preparing it is to reduce a mixture of barium oxide and barium peroxide by means of aluminum in a furnace at an exceedingly low pressure. The metal barium distills off, and is condensed. 

Barium is white like silver, soft, and can be hammered into sheets. It takes fire spontaneously when exposed to moist air or to hydrogen. What are the products? The chief ore of barium is known as barite, or heavy spar, which is barium sulphate. Its density (about 4.5 g. per cu.cm.) is greater than that of common minerals. Another ore of barium is the carbonate, BaCO3. To decompose barium carbonate requires a still higher temperature than to decompose strontium carbonate; barium oxide is therefore prepared from the nitrate. It may also be ob-

tained by the reduction of barium carbonate with charcoal.

BaCO3+C−BaO+2CO 1. 

By this reaction we accomplish the same result as if we removed the carbon dioxide by heating, since the carbon dioxide is reduced to carbon monoxide, and is thus removed from the sphere of action. 

Barium hydroxide crystallizes from water as Ba(OH)2.8H2O. 

Its solution is called baryta water, and may be used in the laboratory in place of limewater. Barium chloride crystallizes as 

BaCl2.2H2O. Barium sulphate is an exceedingly insoluble compound, yet it is the most abundant ore of barium. In order to prepare other barium compounds from it, it must be reduced to barium sulphide.

BaSO4+4C-BaS+4CO ↑. 

Such compounds as the chloride and nitrate can then be prepared from the sulphide. How?

Barium peroxide is formed by heating the oxide in the air. 

The reaction is reversible. 2BaO+022BaO2. 

The test for barium salts is the green color which they impart to the Bunsen flame. They should be moistened with pure hydrochloric acid before being put into the flame. The test for barium ions, that is, barium salts in solution, is the formation of an insoluble precipitate of barium sulphate when they are treated with sulphate ions. 

361. Radium. As we have already learned (§ 319), radium was found by Mme. Curie in the ores that also contained barium. The metal radium resembles barium, and forms an insoluble sulphate similar to barium sulphate. For the preparation of radium chloride the sulphate is reduced by means of hot carbon, and the sulphide,

CALCIUM FAMILY 

431 RaS, is then treated with hydrochloric acid. The separation of radium chloride from barium chloride is possible because they differ slightly in solubility. 

The most remarkable thing about radium compounds is, of course, their radioactivity, which is due to the gradual decomposition of the radium atoms. In this process they give off a great deal of energy. 

One of the uses of radium salts is to produce luminous paint, such as is put upon the dials of watches and clocks. The luminous paint contains zinc sulphide with small amounts of other salts and a trace of a radium salt. When the alpha particles given off by the radium strike the zinc sulphide, light is produced. Luminous paint containing radium does not need to be exposed to sunlight. Radium is obtained from ores found in Bohemia, Colorado, and Utah. At present the most important ores come from the Belgian Congo. Radium has been prepared in the free state by the electrolysis of its bromide, Ra Br2. 

362. Summary.-The calcium family includes calcium, strontium, barium, and radium. None of these is found free in nature. 

The most common calcium compound, the carbonate, is found as limestone, marble, and in other forms. Gypsum is the hydrate of calcium sulphate.

Calcium is prepared by the electrolysis of melted calcium chloride. It decomposes water, but more slowly than sodium. 

Calcium oxide is our most common dehydrating agent, and calcium hydroxide is our cheapest base. The oxide (lime) is prepared by heating limestone in a lime-kiln. 

Lime takes up moisture and carbon dioxide from the air. 

Limewater, a solution of the hydroxide, serves as a test for carbon dioxide.

In the presence of water and carbon dioxide, limestone becomes calcium bicarbonate, which is soluble. As a result limestone caves are formed. The deposits found in limestone caves are due to the precipitation of calcium carbonate when calcium bicarbonate is decomposed.

Mortar is a mixture of slaked lime, sand, and water. When it sets, it absorbs carbon dioxide from the air and becomes limestone.

Gypsum is used for plaster of Paris. When plaster of Paris is moistened, it sets, forming gypsum. 

Hard water usually contains calcium sulphate or calcium bicarbonate or both. The hardness due to calcium bicarbonate can be broken by boiling. Hardness due to calcium sulphate requires water softeners. 

Bleaching powder is prepared from slaked lime and chlorine.

Strontium oxide is prepared by heating the nitrate. Strontium carbonate is the least soluble strontium salt. 

Strontium salts give a red color with the Bunsen flame. With calcium salts the color is orange; with barium salts, green. 

Barium is a soft, white metal like silver. It is prepared by electrolysis, or by reducing its oxide in a furnace at a very low pressure.

Barium oxide is prepared by heating its nitrate or by reducing barium carbonate with charcoal. Barium hydroxide solution is called baryta water.

Radium compounds are found in nature along with barium compounds. The activity of radium is due to the gradual breaking down of its atoms.

CHAPTER XXVII MAGNESIUM-ZINC FAMILY 

364. Members of the family. In this chapter we consider the elements magnesium, zinc, cadmium, and mercury. In which group of the periodic system are they found? The less well-known element, beryllium (Be), also belongs in this group. Zinc and cadmium resemble one another greatly; magnesium is intermediate between these two and calcium. All the elements of this group have the valence 2+. In mercurous compounds mercury also has a valence of 1+. Note HgCl and HgCl2; Hg₂O and HgO. All these elements except mercury react readily with oxygen and with dilute acids. Heated magnesium, zinc, and cadmium react with steam. What are the equations? 

365. Magnesium and its oxide.-Magnesium is a white, lustrous metal. Its density is low (1.74), and it is oxidized slowly in the air. When heated, it burns vigorously to form a mixture of the oxide and nitride. What are their formulas? See §§ 90 and 222. Burning magnesium continues to burn if placed in carbon dioxide. 

The light given off by burning magnesium is intensely brilliant. It contains much light of short wave length, and is therefore well suited to photography. A magnesium flashlight consists of powdered magnesium and an 434

MAGNESIUM-ZINC FAMILY 

435 oxidizing agent. A similar mixture produces military flares and other signal lights. 

Magnesium is found in nature in the form of its chloride, sulphate, and carbonate (magnesite). What are their formulas? It is also found in dolomite (MgCO3.- 

CaCO3) and in many silicates such as soapstone (talc), meerschaum, and asbestos.

One method of preparing magnesium is to electrolyze a double chloride (MgCl2.KCl.6H2O) called carnallite. 

A more modern method is to electrolyze a solution of magnesium oxide in fused magnesium fluoride containing the fluorides of other metals. Besides its use in flashlight powders, magnesium is employed as a de-oxidizing agent. 

It is a component of several alloys of very low density. 

Such are magnalium (Mg,Cu,Al) and duralumin (Mg, Mn, Cu,Al).

Magnesium oxide is known as magnesia. It is formed when the metal burns, and when heat is applied to the hydroxide and carbonate. Magnesium carbonate loses carbon dioxide at a much lower temperature than limestone does. Magnesia is very hard to melt (refractory) and therefore very useful in making crucibles, fire-bricks, and electric furnaces. A light form of magnesia produces "magnesia cement" when mixed with a solution of magnesium chloride. This is an excellent heat insulator and fire-proofing material. 

366. Magnesium salts.-Magnesium oxide does not slake readily to form the hydroxide. The hydroxide is best formed by double decomposition.

MgCl2+2NaOH→Mg(OH)2 ↓ +Na2SO4. 

With ammonium hydroxide the reaction is incomplete because

it is reversible. If an excess of an ammonium salt, such as NH4Cl, is present, ammonium hydroxide will not precipitate magnesium hydroxide at all. 

Magnesium hydroxide is very insoluble, but it reacts with dilute acids because the part in solution at any instant, although small, is at once removed by the acid. 

Mg(OH)2 Mg(OH)2 Mg2++20H-. solid dissolved ionized 

367. Preparation of zinc.-Zinc does not occur free.

It is found as the sulphide (zinc blende), the carbonate, and the orthosilicate. Write the formulas of these compounds (§ 199). In the smelting of the metal the ore is generally roasted to convert it into the oxide. The mixture of zinc oxide with charcoal is then heated in retorts; the metal distils off and is condensed. Some of the vapor condenses in powder form, and is called zinc dust. The remainder condenses as a liquid and is cast into bars or plates. The zinc thus obtained can be used for most commercial purposes, but is not pure. To obtain pure zinc we electrolyze its chloride. 

368. Properties and uses of zinc.-Zinc is used chiefly as a protective covering for iron (galvanized iron). It is a white metal with a marked metallic lustre. It is useful as a covering because it does not change readily in dry air. However, it soon is dulled in lustre by a coating of zinc carbonate. How is this formed? As we ordinarily know it, zinc is brittle; but when heated to between 

100° C. and 150° C. it loses its brittleness and can be rolled into sheets and drawn out in wire form. · At higher temperatures it becomes brittle again and can be beaten into powder. Above its melting point zinc takes fire and burns to form zinc oxide. Zinc reacts with steam much

MAGNESIUM-ZINC FAMILY 

437 less readily than magnesium. With the hydroxides of sodium and potassium it forms zincates and hydrogen. 2NaOH+Zn-Na₂ZnO2+H2 ↑. 

Pure zinc reacts very slowly with acids; if we wish to increase the speed of the reaction, we put another metal, such as copper or platinum, in contact with it. See § 37.

The efficiency of zinc as a covering for iron depends upon the fact that the zinc and iron, in contact with the carbonic acid of the air, really act like an electric cell. The zinc, the more active metal, reacts with the carbonic acid, and the iron is left unattacked as long as any zinc remains.

When iron is galvanized, it is dipped into a bath of liquid zinc. 

Galvanized iron is used for wire netting, iron roofing and rain gutters, water-tanks, and many other purposes. The metal zinc itself is also used for the same purposes, as well as for the parts of washing-machines. A familiar use of zinc is to form the positive plates of electric batteries. Brass, German silver, and many other alloys contain zinc. 

369. Zinc compounds. Soluble salts of zinc are poisonous, hence zinc should not be used for cooking utensils.

Zinc oxide (ZnO) is formed when zinc is burned, and when zinc carbonate or nitrate is heated. Write the equations. When hot, zinc oxide is yellow; when cold, it is white. It is sold as the pigment, zinc white. Zinc hydroxide results when zinc salts are treated with soluble hydroxides. The precipitate redissolves when an excess of the alkali hydroxide is used; a soluble zincate is formed. Zinc chloride and zinc sulphate are formed when the metal reacts with the corresponding acids. Zinc sulphate crystallizes from water in the form of the hydrate, ZnSO4.7H2O. It is called white vitriol. Zinc chloride melts easily and is usually obtained in stick form. It is

deliquescent in the air, and is useful as a dehydrating agent.

Zinc sulphide belongs to the moderately insoluble sulphides (§ 180). It is precipitated only in small amounts by hydrogen sulphide, because the reaction is reversible. 

If an acid is first added to the zinc salt, zinc sulphide is not precipitated at all by hydrogen sulphide. An alkali sulphide, such as ammonium sulphide, must be used. 

Lithopone is a modern pigment used instead of zinc white. It is prepared by mixing the solutions of barium sulphide and zinc sulphate. A mixture of insoluble white precipitates is obtained. These are dried and heated and then ground up with water and dried again to form the commercial product. 

370. Cadmium and its compounds.-Cadmium resembles zinc, and its ores occur in nature mixed with those of zinc. As there is a considerable demand for pure zinc, cadmium is obtained as a by-product in methods of zinc purification. It is a white metal, slightly harder than zinc, and can be rolled into sheets and drawn into wire. It is useful chiefly in the production of alloys. It is also being used as a protective coating for iron and steel. Cadmium sulphide forms a bright, yellow pigment, which, of course, is not changed by the presence of hydrogen sulphide in the air. 

Why not? Cadmium sulphide is precipitated by hydrogen sulphide even when dilute acids are present. In this respect it differs from zinc. 

371. Mercury.-Something less than 6000 tons of mercury are produced every year, chiefly in Spain, Italy, the United States, and Mexico. The principal ore is cinnabar, which is mercuric sulphide. The ore is roasted, and sulphur dioxide and mercury are formed. The sulphur dioxide is allowed to escape, and the mercury vapors are condensed. Mercury is silvery white and very lustrous.

MAGNESIUM-ZINC FAMILY 

439 It freezes at -39° C. and boils at 357° C. It does not change in the air until its temperature reaches 300° C. 

Then it is oxidized to mercuric oxide, as in the classic experiment of Lavoisier. It tarnishes in air containing hydrogen sulphide. What is formed? The vapor of mercury and soluble mercury salts are poisonous. 

Mercury does not react with hydrochloric acid. It does not react with sulphuric acid in the cold; but when the concentrated, hot acid is used, the reaction resembles that with copper, lead, and similar metals. Nitric acid, which first acts as an oxidizing agent (§ 230), converts mercury into either mercurous or mercuric nitrate. 

What are their formulas? If dilute cold nitric acid is used, and there is an excess of mercury, mercurous nitrate is formed. With the concentrated acid the mercuric salt is produced, especially if all the mercury is used up. Mercury is used for thermometers, barometers, and mercury air-pumps, and for amalgams, such as sodium amalgam and dental fillings. It is also employed to extract gold and silver from their ores. 

372. Mercury compounds. We have seen that mercuric oxide is formed when mercury is heated above 300° C. in contact with air. It is also prepared by the action of heat upon mercuric nitrate. What are the other products? See § 231. As obtained from these sources, it is red. It can be precipitated from a mercuric salt by the action of an alkali. When so formed, it is usually yellow. Mercuric hydroxide is unstable, and the oxide is formed instead.

Hg(NO3)2+2NaOH÷2NaNO3+HgO ↓ +H₂O. 

Mercurous oxide is formed when we treat a mercurous salt with an alkali. The two mercury chlorides are known as calomel and corrosive sublimate, respectively. 

One of these is readily changed into the other. Since

mercuric chloride is intensely poisonous and calomel is used as a medicine, it is important to know how each one must be kept. Mercurous chloride is precipitated when a solution of a chloride is added to a mercurous salt such as the nitrate. It is usually made, however, by heating a mixture of mercury and mercuric chloride, or by heating a mixture of mercuric sulphate, salt, and mercury. When exposed to light, mercurous chloride becomes dark, because mercury is set free. The reaction between mercury and mercuric chloride is reversible. 

Hg+HgCl₂2HgCl. 

Mercurous chloride (calomel) must therefore be kept in the dark, or it will contain corrosive sublimate. Mercuric chloride is prepared by heating (subliming) a mixture of mercuric sulphate and common salt. Write the equation.

Mercuric chloride is used extensively in surgery as an antiseptic.

When ammonium hydroxide is added to mercuric salts, white precipitates are produced; with mercurous salts the precipitates are black. The black precipitates consist of white solids mixed with finely divided mercury. 

HgCl2+2NH3→HgNH,C1} +NHẠC. 

2HgC1+2NH3→HgNH„Cl ↓ +NH₁C1+Hg ↓ . 

Cinnabar, the ore of mercury, is red. The mercuric sulphide which we obtain by rubbing mercury and sulphur together in the mortar, or by precipitation from mercuric salts by means of hydrogen sulphide, is black. But when the black mercuric sulphide is heated, it sublimes, giving the red variety. Red mercuric sulphide is the pigment, Chinese vermilion. 

Mercuric fulminate, Hg(ONC)2, is a white, crystalline solid easily decomposed by a blow; it is therefore used in percussion caps.

MAGNESIUM-ZINC FAMILY 

441 Mercurochrome is the mercury derivative of an organic dye called fluorescein. 

373. Summary.-Magnesium is intermediate in its properties between the elements of the zinc family and the calcium family. 

All the elements of the family are active except mercury. 

Magnesium burns vigorously in air, also in carbon dioxide. Its carbonate is magnesite, and its double carbonate with calcium is dolomite. 

Magnesium is prepared by the electrolysis of magnesium potassium chloride or of magnesium oxide dissolved in fused magnesium fluoride. It is used in magnesium flashlights and in alloys of very low density. 

Magnesium oxide is magnesia. It is very hard to melt, and is useful for apparatus to be employed at high temperatures. It is also a good heat insulator and fire-proofing material. 

Magnesium oxide slakes very slowly with water. Magnesium hydroxide is very insoluble in water, but reacts with acids to produce magnesium salts. 

Zinc occurs largely as the sulphide. In order to obtain the metal we roast the sulphide to change it into the oxide, and reduce the oxide with charcoal. 

Zinc is active with acids, but does not change readily in air. 

Its chief use is as a protective covering for iron, as the active plate of electric batteries, and as a component of several alloys. 

Zinc hydroxide is precipitated from zinc salts by alkalies, but is soluble in an excess of the alkali. 

Zinc oxide is zinc white. Lithopone is a mixture of zinc sulphide and barium sulphate. Both are pigments which are not blackened by hydrogen sulphide. 

Cadmium is a white metal used in low-melting alloys. Cadmium sulphide is a yellow pigment. 

Mercury is obtained from its sulphide, cinnabar, by roasting. 

The metal is oxidized by air above 300° C., forming the oxide. 

Nitric acid and concentrated, hot sulphuric acid react with mercury, but hydrochloric acid and dilute sulphuric acid do not. 

Mercury forms mercurous and mercuric compounds. Mercurous chloride is called calomel; mercuric chloride is corrosive sublimate. 

Mercuric fulminate is used in percussion caps.

CHAPTER XXVIII ALUMINUM 

375. Preparation of aluminum.-Aluminum is the most abundant of all the metals, yet it was not prepared until 1828. The method used by Wöhler was to heat anhydrous aluminum chloride with potassium; afterwards sodium was used instead. AIC13+3Na-3NaC1+Al.

The modern method consists in the electrolysis of pure aluminum oxide. It was difficult to carry out this reaction. until 

Hall in 1887 discovered that the aluminum oxide could be electrolyzed readily if it was dissolved in the melted mineral, cryolite. Aluminum forms a part of important minerals like feldspar and mica. Granite is a mixture of feldspar with quartz Discoverer of beryllium and alumi- 

Alvina Lenke Studio

FIG. 186. 

Friedrich Wöhler (1800-1882). num. Investigator of organic compounds. and mica. Clay is a decomposed granite and contains much aluminum. But none of these minerals has, up to the present, been successfully used as a source of aluminum. 

The pure aluminum oxide used in the Hall process is 443

obtained from bauxite, which is a hydrate of aluminum oxide containing some ferric oxide. 

Courtesy Aluminum Company of America

FIG. 187. 

Charles Martin Hall (1863-1914).

Inventor of the electrolytic method of preparing aluminum.

In 1883 the United States produced only 83 pounds of aluminum; at the present time it produces about 90,000 tons annually. The world's production is now about 250,000 tons. 

In the Hall process the electrolysis of aluminum oxide is carried out in an iron cell, the bottom and sides of which are lined with carbon. The carbon lining forms the cathode, while the anode consists of large carbons suspended by metal rods.

When the current is turned on, the resistance encountered in the cryolite produces heat enough to melt the cryolite. 

The aluminum oxide is then mixed with the melted cryolite. As the electrolysis goes on, aluminum collects at the bottom of the cell and -can be drawn off. Oxygen reaches the carbon anodes and reacts with them to form carbon monoxide, which escapes. The process is a continuous one, for alumi-

445 376. Properties of aluminum.-Aluminum is soft and white; it may be hammered into sheets and drawn into wire. Its density is only 2.7 g. per cu.cm., or about a third of the density of iron. It melts at 659° C. and boils at 1800° C. It is a good conductor of the electric 

FIG. 189. 

Courtesy Aluminum Company of America

A room full of electrolytic cells producing aluminum. current. It readily forms alloys with such metals as copper, tin, zinc, iron, and magnesium. 

In air or water at ordinary temperatures aluminum undergoes practically no change. At about 700° C. it begins to oxidize, and at white heat it burns vigorously, forming the oxide. Toward acids the compact form of aluminum, such as we use in aluminum utensils, seems quite indifferent, but when the smooth surface has been

etched by means of an alkali solution the aluminum reacts readily with hydrochloric acid. Dilute nitric and sulphuric acids have almost no effect. Concentrated, hot sulphuric acid reacts with aluminum as it does with copper and mercury. What are the products? Aluminum reacts with the hydroxides of potassium and sodium, forming aluminates of these metals, and hydrogen. 2A1+6NaOH→2Na3A1O3+3H₂ ↑ . 

Aluminum combines directly with chlorine, bromine, sulphur, carbon, and nitrogen. Write the equation for each reaction. 

377. Uses of aluminum.-The uses of aluminum depend upon its physical and chemical properties. The fact that it is a good conductor of electricity makes it useful for transmitting electric power. The fact that it is a good conductor of heat and does not tarnish makes it suitable for kitchen utensils. Its low density is an advantage for both these uses, as well as in the construction of street cars, railway cars, and automobiles. Its chemical activity toward oxygen makes it useful as a combustible and as a reducing agent. It adds the property of low density to alloys made from it. It can also be suspended in oil and used as a paint on iron surfaces. 

A great deal of aluminum is used in the manufacture of iron and steel. The aluminum removes any oxygen which may remain in combination with the iron. Aluminum powder is used for flashlights; it also removes the oxygen of many oxides which are very hard to reduce by means of carbon. Such are the oxides of chromium, manganese, tungsten, and other metals. In the cases named the object is to obtain the metals in the uncom-

ALUMINUM 

447 bined state; in other cases the reduction of the metal oxide is carried out for the enormous quantity of heat which is liberated. Thermit is the name given to a mixture of iron oxide and aluminum powder. When the reaction is started by means of a piece of burning magnesium, a temperature of 3000° C. can be obtained. 

Thermit finds extensive use in welding pieces of steel together. Thus a piece of broken machinery can be rapidly mended, and steel rails used for conducting the electric current can likewise be joined together. 

378. Aluminum oxide and hydroxide.-Aluminum oxide (alumina) is found in the form of gems like the ruby and the sapphire. Corundum is another natural form of the same substance. Emery, which is used for grinding and polishing, is a form of corundum. Bauxite, as we have learned, is the hydrate of aluminum oxide. Alundum is an artificial corundum made from bauxite. 

Aluminum hydroxide is formed when an alkali hydroxide reacts with the solution of an aluminum salt. AlCl3+3NaOH→Al(OH); ↓ +3NaCl. 

The precipitate of aluminum hydroxide disappears if an excess of sodium hydroxide is used, owing to the formation of sodium aluminate.

HзAlO3+3NaOH→NaAlO3+3H₂O. 

Aluminum hydroxide ionizes in two ways; in one case it acts as an acid and in the other as a base. forms are in equilibrium with each other. 

These two Al(OH)3 Al(OH)3 A13++30H-.

H3A1033H++A103³-. 

How would you write the ionic equation for the reac-

tion of hydrochloric acid with aluminum hydroxide? For the reaction of sodium hydroxide? When ammonium hydroxide is used with aluminum salts the aluminum hydroxide does not dissolve so readily, owing to the smaller degree of ionization of ammonium hydroxide. A substance like aluminum hydroxide, which is a base towards strong acids and an acid towards strong bases, is said to to have an amphoteric behavior. 

379. Mordants. We have already learned in § 65 that alum is often added to water to purify it. The alum reacts with some of the hardness of the water and produces a gelatinous precipitate of aluminum hydroxide. The aluminum hydroxide adsorbs particles suspended in the water and thus clarifies the water. If there is a dye suspended in the water, the aluminum hydroxide adsorbs it in the same way. The resulting substance forms a kind of pigment called a lake. 

Aluminum hydroxide which is deposited on fibers of cloth adsorbs the cloth, and thus can be used to make dyes adhere to fabrics. The aluminum hydroxide is said to "bite into" the fabric and is therefore called a mordant. 

Dyes are of two general classes: 

1. Direct dyes, which unite directly with the fiber to form insoluble compounds. 

2. Indirect dyes, which require either a substance like tannic acid, or a substance which is a weak acid or base (mordant) to make them unite with the fabric. 

Direct dyes are sometimes called substantive dyes, while indirect dyes are called adjective dyes.

When aluminum hydroxide is to be used as a mordant, the cloth is soaked in a solution of an aluminum salt, such as aluminum acetate, and the aluminum acetate is

ALUMINUM 

449 then hydrolyzed by means of steam or ammonium hydroxide. In making calico the pattern is printed with a mordant, and the cloth thus treated is soaked in a dye which combines with the mordant but not with the cloth. 

The portion of the dye that has been absorbed by the cloth is then washed out. If different patterns are printed with different mordants, the same dye can be made to produce different colors. 

380. Other aluminum compounds. Aluminum reacts with chlorine or with dry hydrogen chloride to produce anhydrous aluminum chloride. This is a white powder which readily takes up moisture from the air. It is used to prepare a number of organic compounds. When it is dissolved in water, and the solution is evaporated until it crystallizes, the hydrate (AlCl3.6H2O) separates out. 

This does not yield the anhydrous chloride when the water of crystallization is driven off, since the water hydrolyzes the salt. Hydrochloric acid and aluminum hydroxide are formed. AlCl3+3H20-A1(OH)3+3HC1.

Alum is the most common aluminum compound of the household. The general name, alum, is applied to the double salts formed by a sulphate like aluminum sulphate with the sulphate of a univalent metal such as potassium or ammonium. Potash alum is K2SO4.Al2(SO4)3.24H2O.

Other univalent metals may take the place of potassium, and other trivalent metals, such as chromium, may take the place of the aluminum. An alum need not necessarily contain aluminum. Write the formula of potassium chromic alum.

Aluminum hydroxide is so weak a base that all ordinary aluminum salts are hydrolyzed and have an acid re-

action. When the acid formed by the hydrolysis is also weak, the salt cannot exist in water solution, but is completely decomposed. Thus aluminum carbonate and water react to produce aluminum hydroxide and carbonic acid. The aluminum hydroxide is precipitated, and carbon dioxide escapes. 2A13++6C1-+6Na++3CO2-→Al2(CO3)3+6Na++6C1-. Al2(CO3)3+6H++60H¬→2A1(OH)3 ↓ +3H2CO3(CO2 ↑ +H2O). 

Aluminum sulphide is hydrolyzed in the same way.

Al2S3+6H++60H→→2A1(OH); ↓ +3H₂S ↑ . 

The fact that aluminum carbonate is hydrolyzed by water explains the use of alum in some baking powders (§ 283). When the baking powder is moistened, the aluminum carbonate which is first formed is decomposed, and carbon dioxide escapes. dough.

This lightens the 381. Cement and concrete.-Cement is formed when limestone and clay, or limestone containing naturally the right proportion of aluminum silicate, are heated together. 

In place of clay, shale or blast furnace slag may be used. 

When the product is ground to a fine powder and mixed with water, it sets to form a hard, stony mass. 

It can even do so under water, hence the name hydraulic cement. 

The commercial form of cement is generally known as Portland cement from the English town of that name. 

Cement must contain the right proportions of calcium oxide (CaO), aluminum oxide (Al2O3), and silica (SiO2). 

The crude materials used furnish these constituents. The substances named are not present in separate form, but are combined chiefly in calcium silicate (SiO2.3CaO) and

ALUMINUM 

451 calcium aluminate (Al2O3.3CaO). Usually a small amount of gypsum is added to improve the hardening properties of the cement. The hardening process is due not to the taking up of carbon dioxide as in the case of lime mortar 

Alvina Lenke Studio

FIG. 190. 

A cement kiln which is turned by machinery (rotary kiln). We see the flame and inside of the kiln as far as the camera could "reach." 99 (§ 355), but to the union of the constituents of the cement with water.

Concrete is the form in which we generally use cement. 

It is a mixture of cement with water, sand, and crushed stone or gravel. It forms a hard, stony mass and is used in place of stone for sidewalks, the foundations and floors of buildings, and for the walls of dams and bridge supports under water. By reinforced concrete we mean

concrete which has rods of iron or steel embedded in it to strengthen it. 

382. Summary.—Aluminum is the most abundant metal. In the Hall process it is prepared by the electrolysis of aluminum oxide dissolved in melted cryolite. 

Aluminum is soft, white, malleable, and ductile. It is unchanged in ordinary air, but oxidizes at red heat. It reacts with hydrochloric acid and concentrated, hot sulphuric acid.

Aluminum reacts with the hydroxides of potassium and sodium, forming aluminates and hydrogen. Aluminum combines directly with the halogens, sulphur, carbon, and nitrogen. 

Aluminum is used as a conductor of electricity, for household utensils, and for the construction of cars and airplanes in which a low density is a great advantage. It is a powerful reducing agent, and is used in getting such metals as chromium and manganese from their oxides. A mixture of iron oxide and aluminum forms thermit; the heat of reaction produces a very high temperature.

Aluminum is used in the manufacture of iron and steel to remove the last of the oxygen which may remain. 

Aluminum oxide forms gems like the ruby and sapphire. Emery is a form of aluminum oxide. 

Aluminum hydroxide is a white precipitate formed when an alkali reacts with an aluminum salt. The precipitate dissolves in an excess of alkali. Aluminum hydroxide ionizes both as an acid and as a base.

Aluminum hydroxide forms a mordant which attaches dyes to the fibers of cloth.

The common aluminum salts are the chloride, the sulphate, and potash alum. All of them have an acid reaction due to partial hydrolysis. Aluminum carbonate and sulphide are entirely hydrolyzed by water.

Cement is a mixture chiefly of calcium silicate and calcium aluminate in certain proportions. It sets because of the union of its constituents with water.

ALUMINUM 

453 Concrete is a mixture of cement with water, sand, and stone. 

Reinforced concre e has rods of iron or steel embedded in it.

CHAPTER XXIX  

384. The copper group. The metals which we study in this chapter are copper, silver, gold, and platinum. All except copper are called "noble" metals. They are all inactive metals and at the bottom of the activity series (§§ 37 and 330). They do not ordinarily react with air or water or dilute hydrochloric or sulphuric acid. Copper and silver react with nitric acid, but gold and platinum do not. Because they are so inactive these metals occur uncombined (native) in the earth's crust. They have been known to man for thousands of years. 

385. Copper. Copper is the most abundant and active element of this group. It was mined in North America, as native copper, before the white man came, and the deposits along the southern shore of Lake Superior are still productive. There are many ores containg copper in combination. Chalcopyrite (kāl'kō-pīr'īte; Cu₂S.Fe2S3 or 

CuFeS2) and malachite (māl'ä-kīte; Cu(OH)2.CuCO3) are important ores. The last is a basic carbonate of copper. 

It is green, and specimens often are of such beauty that they are polished and used as ornaments. Azurite, Cu(OH)2.2CuCO3, has a deep blue color. Ruby copper or cuprite is Cu₂O.

Native copper is obtained from its ores by processes of crushing and washing which give the copper in concen- 454

trated form. The concentrates are then melted. Melted copper, which is dense, collects under the other materials and is drawn off. 

If the ore is cuprous sulphide (Cu₂S), it is partly changed into the oxide by roasting, and the mixture of sulphide and oxide is then heated to a high temperature.

Cu₂S+2Cu₂O-SO₂ ↑+6Cu. 

The more complex ores of copper must be treated by special methods, depending upon the nature of the ore. 

In general, the processes have the following steps: (1) The concentration and washing of the ores and their roasting in a current of air. 

HEARTH FIG. 191. 

A reverberatory furnace. The curved roof directs the flames and hot gases down upon the hearth.

The copper is thus obtained as copper oxide, and iron, if present, as iron oxide. (2) The roasted ore is smelted in a reverberatory furnace with coke and acid silicates. The coke reduces the copper oxide to copper. The iron oxide unites with the silicates forming iron silicate. The heavier copper and copper sulphide collect underneath. This heavier material is called copper matte. (3) In the third operation the matte is converted into blister copper by means of a blast of hot air. The furnace used is called a converter. By this process sulphur and arsenic are removed as gaseous oxides.

The converter is lined with silica, and the nongaseous oxides unite with it to form slags, which are silicates of the metals. (4) In the converter some of the copper is oxidized and must now be reduced. In the poling process the copper containing copper oxide is stirred with long poles of green wood. The gases given off by the wood reduce the copper oxide. (5) To prepare refined copper we use the thick slabs of crude

copper as the anodes of electrolytic cells. A pure copper plate is used as the cathode, and the cell is filled with copper sulphate solution. As the current passes, the copper at the anode gradually disappears and its copper collects upon the cathode. The copper obtained in this way is very pure. If the crude copper contained 

FIG. 192. 

Converters at the smelter of the United Verde Copper Company, Clarkdale, Arizona. gold and silver, these are deposited as "mud" at the bottom of the refining tanks. They are collected and purified. 

386. Properties and uses of copper.-The color of copper is distinctly red. The metal can be hammered out in sheets and drawn into wire. It is the best electric conductor except silver. Its density is about 9 grams per cu.cm. Moist air containing carbon dioxide gradually

tarnishes copper to form a green substance (verdigris; ver'di-gris) similar to the ore, malachite. We have already studied the reaction between copper and acids 

Herbert Photos, Inc. 

FIG. 193. 

Tin cans being used to precipitate copper from waste water. 

Anaconda Copper Mining 

Co., Butte, Montana. (§§ 181 and 230). In the presence of air dilute acids react with copper forming cupric salts. 2Cu+O2+2H2SO4-2CuSO4+2H2O. 

Copper burns in chlorine, but reacts only very slowly with hydrochloric acid. Copper salts are poisonous, hence care must be used in cooking or storing foods in copper dishes.

Copper has a multitude of uses. It is the common

conductor of the electric current, and is used to protect iron from rusting (copper-plating). Coins, boilers, dishes, and rain-gutters are made of it, likewise copper electrotypes from which the pages of books are printed. Copper forms a part of many alloys. Brass contains copper and zinc and sometimes other metals. Bronze contains copper, zinc, and tin. Gun metal and bell metal contain copper and tin. German silver contains copper, zinc, and nickel. Aluminum bronze contains copper and aluminum. 

387. Copper-plating. In order to plate an object with copper we put it into the solution of a copper salt and connect it with the cathode of an electrolytic cell. A plate of copper is used as the anode, just as in the refining of impure copper (§ 384). In preparing electrotype plates an impression, or mold, of the type is first made, and this is dusted with powdered copper to give it a conducting surface. The mold is then used as the cathode of an electrolytic bath and receives a coating of copper. 

To strengthen this copper deposit, the back of the electrotype is filled with type metal. The electrotype is an exact reproduction of the type from which the mold is made. 

388. Copper compounds.-Most of the common compounds of copper are cupric compounds. What is the valence of the metal in these compounds? In the cuprous compounds we usually think of the copper as univalent. Cuprous oxide is found in nature as ruby copper. Cupric oxide is formed when cupric nitrate, carbonate, or hydroxide is heated. Cupric hydroxide, Cu(OH)2, is produced when sodium hydroxide or potassium hydroxide is added to the solution of a cupric salt. If the solution is hot, the precipitate consists of the oxide. Both cuprous sulphide and cupric sulphide are black solids. Cuprous sulphide is formed when copper is burned in sulphur. Cupric sulphide results when cupric

salts in solution are treated with soluble sulphides. Blue vitriol is the hydrate of cupric sulphate (CuSO4.5H2O). It is produced when copper reacts with dilute sulphuric acid in the presence of air, also when cupric oxide reacts with the same acid. It is also formed when concentrated, hot sulphuric acid reacts with copper. 

Anhydrous cupric sulphate is white, and can be recrystallized from concentrated sulphuric acid in white crystals. It is used as a dehydrating agent. Blue vitriol is used in copper-plating, as a preservative of wood, as a source of certain pigments, and as a germicide. Its solution is used in gravity batteries. 

A common fungicide containing copper is called the Bordeaux mixture. Very minute amounts of copper sulphate are used in ponds and water reservoirs to destroy certain plants called algae. 

A test for cupric salts in solution is the deep-blue color produced by ammonia water. The cupric ions unite with ammonia to produce complex positive ions, Cu(NH3)4²+. Cu2++SO42-+4NH, Cu(NH3)²++SO4². 

389. Silver. Like copper, silver is found in the uncombined, or native, state. Its ores are either the chloride or the sulphide. Frequently silver sulphide is combined with the sulphides of other metals. Natural lead sulphide (galena) usually contains silver. The world's silver supply comes chiefly from the United States, Mexico, South 

America, Australia, and India.

Two processes for obtaining silver from its ores are the cyanide process and the zinc, or Parkes' process. In the cyanide process silver compounds are treated with a dilute solution of sodium cyanide (§ 339). The silver is converted into a double cyanide: NaCN.AgCN or NaAg(CN)2.

Ag₂S+4NaCN-2NaAg(CN)2+Na₂S. 

The silver is then precipitated from the double cyanide by the use of zinc or aluminum.

In the zinc process silver is removed from a mixture of melted lead and silver by means of zinc, which is added to the mixture. 

Zinc and lead do not mix readily, but zinc and silver do. The zinc, containing silver, floats; it is skimmed off and distilled. The zinc goes off as a vapor and is condensed to be used over again. 

The silver and some lead, together with gold, if present, remain behind. This mixture is then heated in a shallow furnace in a blast of air. The lead is oxidized to lead oxide and thus removed. 

Gold and silver remain. These are separated by means of nitric or hot, concentrated sulphuric acid, in which the gold does not dissolve. The silver salt, e.g. silver sulphate, is then treated with copper, which displaces the silver. The silver is melted and cast into bars, or ingots. 

In the assaying of a silver ore containing lead, the two metals may be separated by heating them in a bone-ash dish called a cupel. The lead is oxidized to lead oxide, which is absorbed by the porous cupel, while the silver remains. In this way the proportion of silver in such a mixture can be determined. 

390. Properties and uses of silver.-Silver is a brilliant, white metal which, when polished, is one of the best light reflectors known. It is malleable and ductile and the best known conductor of heat and electricity. 

It melts at 960° C. and boils at 1955° C. It can therefore be purified by distillation. Silver is not oxidized by pure air but absorbs about 22 times its own volume of oxygen when in the melted state. This oxygen is given off when the silver becomes solid. Nitric acid and hot, concentrated sulphuric acid react with silver to produce the corresponding salts. Write the equations (§§ 181 and 

230). Hydrochloric acid has no reaction with silver.

When silver tarnishes in air, the compound formed is not silver oxide, but silver sulphide. "Oxidized silver" is formed when a soluble sulphide acts upon silver. 

Silver is used chiefly for coins, jewelry, tableware,

mirrors, and for plating other metals. The pure metal is so soft that it wears off rapidly when handled; it is therefore alloyed with copper to make it harder. The silver coins of the United States contain 90% silver and 10% copper. Sterling silver contains 92.5% silver, and is said to be 925 "fine."

In the process of silver-plating, a solution containing the double cyanide of silver and potassium (or sodium) is used. This has the formula KAg(CN)2. A bar of silver is placed at the anode, and the object to be plated is attached to the cathode. The plated surface is usually rough, but it is polished by means of chalk. The double cyanide solution is formed when potassium cyanide is added to a silver nitrate solution until the silver cyanide which is first precipitated is redissolved. AgNO3+KCN→AgCN ↓ +KNO3. AgCN+KCN→KAg(CN)2. 

Silver mirrors are formed by coating glass with silver.

The silver solution used is made from silver nitrate and ammonia. When a reducing agent, such as formaldehyde or grape sugar, is put with this solution, and the solution is gently heated, a bright deposit of silver is formed on the glass. This is dried and covered with a varnish to protect it. 

391. Silver compounds. In all its common compounds silver has a valence of 1. Its most important compound is silver nitrate, AgNO3. This is crystallized from water in white plates. In ancient times silver was named after the moon (luna). This name is still preserved in lunar caustic, an old name for silver nitrate. 

Silver sulphate also forms white, soluble crystals. When sodium hydroxide is added to a silver nitrate solution, the first product is probably the hydroxide, AgOH, but this decomposes at once to

give the oxide, Ag2O. The compounds of silver with the halogens are especially important because they are used in photography. 

Silver chloride is a curdy, white precipitate which reacts with ammonia. We commonly say that the silver chloride "dissolves” in ammonia water. What really happens is that a complex salt, Ag(NH3)2Cl, is formed which is soluble. The positive ion of this substance is Ag(NH3)2+.

To test for silver salts (that is, for silver ions) in a solution, we use a soluble chloride. If silver is present, a curdy, white precipitate of silver chloride is produced. This darkens in the light and dissolves in ammonia water. When the solution in ammonia water is treated with dilute nitric acid, the silver chloride is reprecipitated. 

392. Photography.-Photography consists of the operations called exposure, developing, fixing, and printing. 

The plate or film contains silver bromide and silver iodide dispersed in gelatine. When the plate is exposed in a camera, the light admitted produces changes in the silver salts. The silver salts after exposure are insoluble in certain chemicals. Developing consists in treating the exposed plate with some reducing agent. As a result an image appears, due to the precipitation of larger or smaller quantities of silver which produce light and shade. Where the light was strong, the silver deposit is heavier than where there was little action. The exposure to light causes the reduction to silver to take place more easily than in the unexposed salts. 

Developing completes the change which was begun by exposure to light. Fixing, the next operation, removes the silver salts which were not acted upon by the developing. The common fixing agent is sodium thiosulphate, also called "hypo." This action makes the negative permanent, or "fixes" it. In the negative darker objects appear light, and lighter objects appear dark. The sensitive paper used in printing from a negative contains a larger

proportion of silver chloride than the original film. This acts more slowly when exposed to light than silver bromide does. 

When light passes through the negative to the print, the part of the negative which was darkest permits less light to pass than the one which contains less of the precipitated silver. As a result the light and dark regions of the print are arranged just as they were in the object photographed. The prints, like the negatives, are "fixed" by the removal of the unchanged silver salt. An additional operation is to tone the picture by immersing it in a bath of a gold compound or a platinum compound before it is fixed. (§ 395). In this way a part of silver is replaced by gold or platinum. 

Films used in motion pictures are prepared in much the same way as those used for single photographs.

The film in this case is a transparent ribbon of celluloid or similar material which has a thin coating that contains the silver salts. The negative is developed after exposure, then fixed and dried. The films used in the projector are positive films printed from the negative ones. 

At present about 150 tons of silver are used in the preparation of motion-picture films in this country every year. 

393. Gold.-Gold was perhaps the first metal found by early man owing to the fact that it occurs uncombined and has so attractive an appearance. Gold usually occurs in small flakes scattered through quartz rocks. It is also found in sands which have been formed by the weathering of such rocks. Occasionally gold nuggets are found. The largest known weighed 180 or 190 pounds.

Gold also occurs in telluride ores, combined with tellurium. 

Such an ore is (AgAu)Te2, an important deposit of which is found in Colorado. We have seen that gold is often obtained in the refining of crude copper and silver (§§ 384 and 388). It is also found in lead ores. 

394. Extraction of gold.-An early way of separating gold from sand was to shake the mixture with water in a pan. This was the panning process. The lighter

particles of sand and gravel could thus be washed away, while the heavy particles of gold remained. This process is imitated on a large scale in placer mining and hydraulic mining, in which large quantities of water are used to wash the crushed ore. The water carries away the light particles and leaves the gold and other heavy metals behind. An improvement of this process is to use mercury, which forms a solution (amalgam) with the gold. 

The mercury is then distilled and used over again. The gold remains.

There are two processes of extracting gold in which the metal is converted into a gold compound and then separated in water solution. In the chlorination process the ore is treated with chlorine or with a mixture of bleaching powder and dilute sulphuric acid. Gold chloride, AuCl3, is formed. This is dissolved in water and the solution is treated with hydrogen sulphide. Gold sulphide is the product. When this is heated, it is decomposed, and gold remains.

The second chemical process for extracting gold is the cyanide process. This gives the gold in the form of a double cyanide of gold and sodium: NaCN.AuCN, or NaAu(CN)2. From this compound the gold is precipitated by zinc. 4Au+8NaCN+2H2O+O2-4NaAu (CN)2+4NaOH. 2NaAu(CN)2+Zn→Na₂Zn(CN).+2Au ↓ . 

Whatever the process used, the gold obtained is mixed with other metals. The refining of gold, or its "parting" from other metals, consists in treating the mixture with nitric acid or hot, concentrated sulphuric acid, which converts the other metals into soluble salts and leaves the gold. Refining by electrolysis is the method used in the United States mint. 

395. Properties and uses of gold.-Gold is yellow by reflected light; but when light passes through thin sheets of it, the metal is green. When its particles are of col-

loidal size, the gold may appear green, blue or even red. 

Gold may be beaten into gold leaf and drawn out into the thinnest wire. A sheet of it may be only 300.000 of an inch thick. It is the most malleable and ductile substance known. 1 Gold unites with chlorine, forming gold chloride, and with bromine, giving the bromide. Single acids do not react with it, but aqua regia produces chlorauric acid, HAuCl or HCl.AuCl3. By the reaction between sodium chloride and chlorauric acid, crystals of sodium chloraurate, NaAuCl4.2H2O are obtained. The solution of this salt is used in the toning of photographic prints. 

Gold, like silver, is too soft for constant use, and is hardened by means of copper, sometimes also by silver. 

Pure gold is said to be 24 carats "fine." Pure gold is also said to have a fineness of 1000. United States gold coins are 900 fine. Those of great Britain are 916.7 fine. 

Gold of 18 carats is 75% gold and 25% of other metals.

Gold is the standard of the money systems of most nations. Because it is so easily shaped by hammering (malleable), and has such slight chemical action, gold is used for dental fillings. Many alloys of gold are used in jewelry, and objects are also gold-plated. Green gold usually contains silver or cadmium alloyed with the gold. 

The common test for a gold salt in solution is the formation of colloidal gold under the name of "purple of Cassius." This is obtained when auric chloride, AuCl3, is treated with stannous chloride, SnCl2. 

396. Platinum.-Platinum, like gold, is found uncombined, in fine grains or nuggets. It is usually mixed with other metals. About half of the world's supply comes from Russia; part of the remainder from Canada, Cali-

fornia, and Oregon. One method of working platinum ores is to treat them with aqua regia. This produces chlorplatinic acid, H₂PtCl, or PtCl4.2HCl. When this compound is treated with ammonium chloride, ammonium chlorplatinate, (NH4)2PtCl, is precipitated. When this is heated, it is decomposed. (NH4)2PtC16⇒2NH4C1 ↑ +2C1½ ↑ +Pt. 

397. Properties and uses.-Platinum is soft and white, and very malleable and ductile. Its density is very high: nearly 21.4. It melts at 1755° C. Air does not react with it, and no single acid attacks the pure metal. By aqua regia it is converted into chlorplatinic acid. Chlorine water and bromine water also react with it directly. 

Melted potassium hydroxide or sodium hydroxide reacts with it to form a platinate. Platinum expands and contracts less than other metals, consequently it is used when wire must be sealed into glass. It is used in the form of wire, as foil, and in chemical dishes. Platinum dishes should never be heated in smoky flames, nor in contact with substances which liberate phosphorus, lead, antimony, or other metals. About half of the platinum produced is used in jewelry.

From the chemical point of view, finely divided platinum has a great deal of importance because of its use as a catalyst. It is employed as platinum black, spongy platinum, and as platinized asbestos. For use in jewelry, as well as in chemical utensils, platinum is usually alloyed with other metals, to give it greater hardness. 

398. Summary.-Copper and the precious metals are inactive. chemically and therefore placed at the bottom of the activity series.

Copper is mined both as native copper and in the form of ores. 

Copper is refined by electrolysis. Gold and silver which occur with the copper are deposited in the refining tanks. 

The properties of copper make it a very useful metal. It is used as an electric conductor, to protect iron from rusting, in coins, boilers, and dishes, and for the electrotypes of books. Brass is an alloy containing copper. Bronze, German silver, and aluminum bronze also contain it. 

In copper-plating a bath of a copper salt is used, and the object to be plated is made the cathode. 

Copper forms both cuprous and cupric compounds, but all its common salts are cupric compounds.

Bordeaux mixture is a fungicide containing copper compounds. 

Silver ores are usually found with lead ores. When silver is present in sufficient amount, melted zinc is added to the mixture. 

This dissolves out the silver but very little of the lead (Parkes' process).

In the cyanide process the silver is extracted by means of a solution of sodium cyanide. 

Silver is tarnished in air containing sulphur compounds. It reacts with nitric and hot, concentrated sulphuric acids. 

In silver-plating the bath contains a double cyanide. The object to be plated is attached to the cathode. Silver mirrors are made by floating a silver nitrate solution containing ammonia upon the glass, and adding a reducing agent. 

Photography depends upon the effect of light on the silver salts in the film or plate. Exposure begins the reduction of the silver salts to silver; developing carries the reduction to completion. Fixing removes the unchanged silver salts and produces the negative.

The test for silver salts in solution is the formation of the chloride, which is soluble in ammonia water. 

Gold was probably the first metal found by man. 

The "parting" of gold and silver consists in using nitric acid or hot, concentrated sulphuric acid, which leaves the gold unaffected. 

Gold is the most malleable and most ductile of all the metals. 

It reacts with chlorine and bromine and with aqua regia.

Gold is alloyed with other metals to give it hardness. 

Platinum is a soft, white metal of high density and high melting point. Aqua regia converts it into chlorplatinic acid, which is soluble.

Because of its inactivity platinum is used for jewelry, chemical dishes, wire, and foil. Finely divided platinum has great importance as a catalyst.
