CHAPTER XXX 

TIN AND LEAD 400. The occurrence and preparation of tin.—Tin has been known for thousands of years, and is a component of bronze, which gave its name to the Bronze Age of human culture. Its chief ore is tin-stone, SnO2. The world's supply comes chiefly from the Malay States, Bolivia, and the East Indies. In ancient times there were famous deposits in Cornwall, England, but these are nearly exhausted. The extraction of tin from tin oxide would be simple if the tin oxide did not contain such large amounts of other oxides. The crude ore must first be concentrated so as to remove some of these impurities. 

What remains is then reduced with anthracite coal in a furnace. The resulting metal is drawn off and cast into bars.

SnO2+C→Sn+CO2 ↑ · One method of purifying the crude tin obtained from the furnace is to heat it slowly on a sloping hearth. The tin melts at a lower temperature than the impurities with which it is mixed, and runs down the hearth. Another method, called "tossing," consists in pouring the metal in a stream through the air. By this treatment the impurities solidify, or are oxidized, while the tin is still liquid. 

401. Properties and uses of tin.-Tin melts at 232° C. and boils at 2270° C. It is white, and has a brilliant luster 470

TIN AND LEAD 

471 which does not easily tarnish. When a bar of tin is bent, it gives out a sound which is called the "cry of tin"; this is probably due to the fact that the tin crystals rub against one another. Like many other elements, tin has allotropic forms. When tin is at the ordinary temperature, it is very malleable. When heated, it becomes brittle, and at low temperatures it crumbles to a gray powder. The ordinary form of tin is stable above 20° C. 

Below this temperature organ pipes, rain gutters, and other objects made of tin are subject to "tin disease." 

If the change is started, they may crumble to powder. 

Tin takes fire in the air and burns to stannic oxide, SnO2. It reacts with hydrochloric acid to give stannous chloride (SnCl2) and hydrogen. Stannic chloride (SnCl4) is prepared from tin and chlorine. Hot, concentrated sulphuric acid reacts with tin as it does with copper (§ 181). Concentrated nitric acid oxidizes tin to form metastannic acid, H₂SnO3. The uses of tin depend chiefly upon its permanence in air. Tin plate is made by dipping iron into melted tin. Tin cans and tin cooking utensils are really iron coated with tin. Other metals, such as brass and copper, are also covered with tin to prevent corrosion. Tin is present in many alloys, such as bronze, bell-metal, solder, and britannia metal. 

Pewter contains tin and lead. The pure tin, also called block tin, is used for condensers in water distillation. Tin is also used as tin foil.

Tin is not as efficient a protective covering for iron as zinc, because iron is more active than tin, while less active than zinc. 

If the tin covering is scratched, the iron rusts under the tin. 

Zinc acts as a protective covering as long as any of it remains. 

402. Tin compounds. Tin forms two sets of compounds, stannous and stannic compounds. In stannous compounds the metal has a valence of 2+; in stannic compounds, 4+. The tin also acts like a non-metal, forming stannites and stannates. Stannous oxide is SnO;

stannic oxide, SnO2. Stannous hydroxide is Sn(OH)2. 

It is formed when a stannous salt is treated with sodium hydroxide. When the alkali is used in excess, the solution contains a stannite. This is a powerful reducing agent because of its tendency to form a stannate, in which the valence of the tin is 4+. Tin belongs in the same natural family with carbon and silicon. Hence its dioxide, SnO2, is the anhydride of an acid, H₂SnO3 or H4SnO4. When stannic oxide is heated with the hydroxides or oxides of metals, it reacts with them to form stannates.

Stannous chloride, SnCl2, is formed when hydrochloric acid reacts with tin. If the solution is sufficiently concentrated, colorless crystals of the hydrate, SnCl2.2H2O, are formed. Because stannous chloride is easily oxidized to stannic chloride, it is a powerful reducing agent. It reduces mercuric chloride first to mercurous chloride and then to mercury. 2HgCl2+SnCl2→2HgC1 | +SnCl4. 

2HgCl+SnCl2→2Hg ↓ +SnCl4. 

Stannous chloride is used commercially as a mordant. 

Stannic chloride, SnCl4, is prepared as an anhydrous substance from dry chlorine and heated tin. Much stannic chloride is now obtained from waste tin plate. It is a colorless, fuming liquid which is readily hydrolyzed by water. SnCl4 +4HOH→Sn(OH)₁+4HC1 ↑ . 

In chemical analysis we meet with the two sulphides of tin: stannous sulphide (SnS) and stannic sulphide (SnS2). Stannous sulphide is formed when hydrogen sulphide is added to the solution of a stannous salt. Stannic salts gives stannic sulphide. 

SnCl2+H2S-SnS +2HC1. SnCl4+2H2S-SnS2+4HCI.

Stannous sulphide is brown; stannic sulphide is yellow. When

TIN AND LEAD 473 heated, stannic sulphide is decomposed, giving stannous sulphide and sulphur. Both stannous sulphide and stannic sulphide react with a solution of ammonium sulphide containing sulphur. 

SnS+ (NH4)2S+S-(NH4)2SnS3. 

SnS2+ (NH4)2S→(NH4)2SnS3.

As a result of this reaction the tin sulphides are said to "dissolve" in yellow ammonium sulphide. The ammonium salt formed is called ammonium sulphostannate. 

403. Lead. The chief commercial ore of lead is its sulphide, PbS, known as galena. This has been used as a source of the metal from ancient times. There are many different processes by which the lead is obtained from its ore. Most of the ores contain comparatively small amounts of lead. These must first be concentrated. Usually the next operation is to roast the concentrated ore in an air blast. The roasting is carried on just far enough to leave some lead sulphide unchanged and mixed with lead oxide and lead sulphate. When the oxidation has produced the right result, the roasting is stopped, and the mixture is heated to a high temperature. 

The following equations show some of the reactions: (1) for the roasting, and (2) for the reduction to lead: 2PbS+302-2PbO+2SO2 ↑. PbS+202-PbSO4.

PbS+2PbO→3Pb+SO½ ↑ · PbS+PbSO4-2Pb+2SO2 ↑. 

In some processes iron, or an ore of iron, together with coke and limestone, are put with the roasted ore, and the whole charge is heated in a blast furnace. The reactions are complicated, but the final result is that the sulphur of the unchanged lead sulphide is combined with the iron as ferrous sulphide (FeS), while the carbon of the original charge escapes as carbon dioxide. The

limestone is used to take up the silica, so as to remove it in the form of slag.

The lead obtained from the blast furnace is known as lead bullion. If this is pure enough, it does not need further refining. 

If it contains much silver or gold, the Parkes process is used to extract them (§ 388). 

404. Properties and uses.-A fresh surface of lead is blue-gray, but bright. The lustre soon fades because of oxidation. The oxidized material on the surface protects the lead underneath. Lead is soft but not as malleable or ductile as many other metals. Lead melts about 100° higher than tin: 327° C. At about 300° C. it becomes plastic and in this condition can be forced through steel dies to form lead wire or pipe.

Lead is an inactive metal partly because its surface becomes coated soon after exposure to the air. It reacts only very slightly with pure water, but air and soft water together react with it to form lead hydroxide, Pb(OH)2. 

This is somewhat soluble. If carbon dioxide is present, lead reacts with water even more readily. When hard water is run through lead pipe, a coating of lead carbonate and sulphate is soon formed which protects it. Water which has stood in new lead pipes is dangerous to drink. 

Dilute hydrochloric and sulphuric acids react very slowly with lead; but even a weak acid, such as acetic acid, will react with it in the presence of air, if the salt formed is a soluble one. Nitric acid reacts readily with lead, so does hot, concentrated sulphuric acid (§§ 181 and 230).

Lead ions (Pb2+) are readily displaced from the solution of lead salts by more active metals. If a strip of sheet zinc or iron is suspended in a lead acetate solution, the precipitated lead separates in a spreading, crystalline form called a lead tree.

TIN AND LEAD 475 All soluble lead compounds are poisonous. lates in the body until it causes poisoning. due to the lead compounds in paint.

The lead accumu- "Painters' colic" is

Lead pipes have been used for carrying water since the days of the Romans. Sheet lead is used to line the lead chambers in the chamber process of preparing sulphuric acid. Much lead is converted into shot and bullets. As we have already seen (§ 259), a little arsenic is added to shot in order that the melting point of the lead may be lowered until the drops of lead have time to become spherical. Lead is used in many alloys, such as type metal, solder, wire for fuses, and other low melting mixtures. The common storage battery has plates of lead. Terneplate is iron coated with an alloy of lead and tin. 

405. Lead compounds.-In its compounds lead shows a valence of both 2+ and 4+. In this respect it resembles other members of the carbon family. Its common salts contain lead in the bivalent form. In lead dioxide (PbO2) and lead tetrachloride (PbC14) the metal has a valence of 4+. 

Lead forms three common oxides: PbO, called litharge;

Pb304, called red lead or minium; and PbO2, lead dioxide. 

Lead monoxide (litharge) is produced in large quantities when lead is oxidized, as in the separation of lead and silver (§ 389). It is yellowish red and melts rather easily. Litharge and glycerin form a useful glass cement; the mixture sets to form a hard mass. Litharge is also used in making optical glass (§ 303), glazes for pottery and enamelware, and the plates of storage batteries. It serves as a catalyst in "boiled" linseed oil, because it speeds up the absorption of oxygen from the air. Red lead (Pb304) also forms a cement with glycerin. It is formed when litharge or lead is roasted at about 400° C. We may write its formula as PbO2.2PbO. When it

is treated with nitric acid, PbO2 is left undissolved as a brown powder. The PbO is converted into lead nitrate. 

Lead dioxide is formed not only from red lead, but by the treatment of litharge with bleaching powder. Lead dioxide and red lead both oxidize hydrochloric acid, forming chlorine.

Lead chloride, PbCl2, is precipitated when solutions of lead salts are treated with a soluble chloride. It is much more soluble, however, than silver chloride and mercurous chloride. If the three are precipitated together, lead chloride may be removed from the mixture by means of hot water, in which the lead chloride is fairly soluble. If lead dioxide is treated with very cold, concentrated hydrochloric acid, lead tetrachloride is produced. It is unstable and is decomposed into lead chloride and chlorine. Lead nitrate, acetate, and sulphate are all white solids. The crystals of lead acetate have the formula, Pb(C2H3O2)2.3H2O. The substance is called sugar of lead. Lead sulphate is insoluble in water but somewhat soluble in concentrated sulphuric acid. The solution probably contains lead bisulphate, Pb(HSO4)2. Lead hydroxide, Pb(OH)2, acts not only like a base but like a weak acid which we may call plumbous acid. Thus it dissolves in an excess of sodium hydroxide.

H₂PbO2+2NaOH→Na₂PbO2+2H2O. 

Plumbic acid is H4PbO4 or H2PbO3. Lead dioxide is its anhydride; this reacts with alkalies, giving plumbates. 2KOH+PbO2→K2PbO3+H2O. 

Red lead is really plumbic plumbate, Pb2PbO4. Lead sulphide, PbS, has the same composition as the common lead ore, galena. 

It is formed as a black precipitate when lead salts are treated with soluble sulphides. When heated in the air (roasted) lead sulphide is oxidized to the sulphate. "Ethyl gas" is a commercial form of gasoline which contains an organic lead compound, lead tetraethyl, Pb(C2H5)4. It is used to prevent "knocking" in gasoline engines.

TIN AND LEAD 477 406. White lead.-White lead contains lead hydroxide and lead carbonate combined in different proportions, as basic lead carbonates. It may be represented by the formula, Pb(OH)2.2PbCO3. It is used for paints because it has such "covering power." In paints it is ground up with linseed oil.

There are several methods used in preparing white lead. The standard method is the Dutch process, which has been used for a long time. In this process thin, perforated discs of lead, called buckles, are placed in earthenware pots. The pots are placed in tiers above dilute acetic acid and are surrounded by tanbark. The tanbark ferments and gives off heat and carbon dioxide. The heat vaporizes the acetic acid. The carbon dioxide, together with oxygen from the air and the vapor of acetic acid, acts upon the lead to produce basic lead carbonate, or white lead. The process requires several months. The probable equations are: 2Pb+O2+ 2HC2H3O2-2Pb(OH) C2H3O2. 3Pb(OH)C2H3O2+2CO2+H2O-Pb(OH)2.2PbCO3+3HC2H3O2. 

In order to shorten the time for preparing white lead, several processes have been tried. In the Carter process a stream of finely divided lead is obtained by blowing steam or air through melted lead. The lead dust obtained in this way is treated with dilute acetic acid and carbon dioxide in revolving cylinders. As the cylinders revolve they rub off the basic lead carbonate as fast as it is formed.

Paints containing white lead blacken with hydrogen sulphide; hence substitutes for the lead compound have been widely used. 

Among them are zinc white (ZnO), permanent white (BaSO4), and lithopone (BaSO4+ZnS). 

407. Storage batteries.-The storage battery which we commonly use (§ 189) contains lead plates or grids and

a bath of dilute sulphuric acid. One set of grids is covered with finely divided lead while the other contains lead dioxide. This is the condition when the battery is charged. Originally both sets of grids were covered with lead monoxide, PbO, but the action of the current caused one set to be reduced to lead while the other took up the available oxygen to form PbO2. Neither the lead nor the lead dioxide is soluble in the sulphuric acid, but if lead monoxide is formed, it reacts, producing PbSO4. 

When the battery is being discharged the equations are: PbO2+H2SO4→PbSO4+H₂O+(0). Pb+(0)+H2SO4→PbSO4+H2O. 

When the reactions represented in these equations have gone to completion, both the lead on one plate and the lead dioxide on the other have disappeared. No more energy can be obtained from a battery in this condition. 

As the equations show, sulphuric acid has been changed into lead sulphate. When the battery is recharged, the changes shown in the equations are reversed. One set of plates again contains finely divided lead and the other lead dioxide, and the battery may again be used as a source of the current. We can express both changes by a reversible equation: 

2PbSO4+2H2O+ energy

Pb+PbO₂+2H2SO4. 

If this equation is read from left to right it represents the charging of the battery. When read from right to left it represents the discharge. It is plain that we do not really store electricity in a storage battery. What we do is to change electric energy to chemical energy.

Then we get back electric energy from the chemical energy we have stored away. 

Another storage cell is known as the Edison cell. When the cell is charged, its plates consist of nickelic hydroxide and finely

TIN AND LEAD 

479 divided iron, respectively. During discharge nickelous hydroxide and ferrous hydroxide are formed. The battery solution consists of a solution of potassium hydroxide. 2Ni(OH)3+Fe 2Ni(OH)2+Fe(OH)2+energy. 

408. Summary.—As a component of bronze, tin was known to early man. Its chief ore is stannic oxide. Aside from mechanical operations the smelting of tin consists in reducing stannic oxide with carbon.

Tin has a low melting point and a luster which does not readily tarnish. It serves as a protective covering for iron and as a component of low-melting alloys. 

Tin reacts with hydrochloric acid to give stannous chloride and with chlorine to give stannic chloride. Nitric acid oxidizes tin to metastannic acid.

Stannous hydroxide is amphoteric like zinc hydroxide. Stannic oxide reacts with the hydroxides of metals to form stannates. 

Stannous and stannic sulphides are precipitated by hydrogen sulphide. Both "dissolve" in yellow ammonium sulphide. 

The chief ore of lead is its sulphide, galena. To get lead from its sulphide we either roast it and then reduce the oxide with carbon, or we roast it partly and then heat it to a high temperature. 

If the lead contains much silver or gold, these are extracted by the Parkes process.

Lead tarnishes in the air, but only on the surface. Before it reaches its melting point it becomes plastic, and can be forced through dies to form wire or pipe.

Lead reacts with soft water containing air to form lead hydroxide. Water which has stood in new, lead pipes is dangerous to drink.

Lead reacts very slowly with dilute hydrochloric and sulphuric acids. It reacts readily with nitric acid and with hot, concentrated sulphuric acid.

Because of its resistance to corrosion lead is used for dishes in chemical processes, and as the lining of the lead chambers, in sulphuric acid manufacture.

Lead is used in many alloys and for the plates of the common storage battery.

In its compounds lead shows a valence of either 2+ or 4+. 

Its most common oxide is litharge. Lead dioxide and red lead oxidize hydrochloric acid, forming chlorine. 

Lead nitrate and acetate are soluble salts. Lead sulphate is insoluble. A test for lead salts in solution is the formation of a white precipitate of lead chloride when they are treated with hydrochloric acid. This dissolves in hot water. 

White lead is basic lead carbonate. It is used because of its covering power.

In the common storage battery the electric current produces chemical changes by which lead oxide is reduced on one plate to lead, and oxidized on the other plate to produce lead dioxide. 

When the battery is being discharged, the chemical changes are reversed and chemical energy is changed into electrical energy.

CHAPTER XXXI  

410. Members of the family.-Iron is the most important of the metals. Aluminum is the only metal more abundant than iron in the earth's crust. Many persons assume that the use of iron followed that of bronze, because so few iron implements are found among the remains of ancient peoples. But iron corrodes so easily that even if it had been used by men of the "Bronze Age," it would probably have disappeared in the form of rust. 

The iron family also includes cobalt and nickel. 

The chief commercial ores of iron are hematite, which is ferric oxide; brown iron ore, which contains the hydroxide; and siderite, which is ferrous carbonate (FeCO3). 

The magnetic oxide of iron is found in magnetite (Fe3O4). 

Iron pyrite is FeS2. Most of the iron ore mined in the

United States is hematite and comes from the region about Lake Superior. Alabama also has a large deposit; so have New York, Colorado, and California. France and

Germany have the most important iron deposits in Europe. 

411. Preparation of iron.-Iron is produced in enormous amounts. The ores used are chiefly oxides or can be changed into oxides. They are reduced by heating them with some form of carbon. A flux, usually limestone, must be present to combine with the silica and the aluminum compounds mixed with the carbon and the ore. 482

The product is slag, a mixture of the silicates of calcium and aluminum. All the other forms of iron, including steel, are prepared from the crude iron produced in the blast furnace. This crude iron is called pig iron. Pig iron contains about 93% of iron and about 4% of carbon; 230A 

FIG. 194. 

General view of blast furnace, showing the washers, scrubbers, and stoves. Cars for the hot slag are lined up along the pouring floor of the cast house. the remainder is manganese, silicon, sulphur, and phosphorus. From this crude iron all the iron and steel products of commerce are prepared. 

A blast furnace will produce every twenty-four hours about 600 tons of iron, perhaps 500 tons of slag, and 1400 tons of gases. It will use up in the same time about 

1200 tons of air.

412. Blast furnace.-A blast furnace is a hollow cylinder about 100 feet high and 20 to 25 feet in diameter. 

The outside is a sheath of iron. The inside is lined with fire brick, and the space between the two is filled with brick. Ore, coke, and limestone in the proportion of 2 tons of ore to 1 ton of coke and of a ton of limestone, GASES 

ORE, COKE,

LIMESTONE 

are fed into the furnace at the top. 

There is a special charging device, called a bell, which allows refilling to go on without the escape of furnace gases. Great blowers force a blast of hot air into the furnace through pipes (tuyeres; pronounced tweers) at the bottom. Stoves, each about 100 feet high and 20 feet in diameter, preheat the air so that the combustion in the furnace shall go on at a higher temperature than it would with cold air. 

At the hottest part of the furnace, just above the tuyeres, a set of pipes carrying water cools the walls of the furnace. The gases produced in the furnace escape through "downcomers," which conduct the gases to the stoves or to some of the necessary engine furnaces to be burned as fuel. 

FIG. 195. 

HOT AIR

BLAST -TUYERES 

MELTED SLAG 

-MELTED 

cross-section of blast furnace. IRON The liquid iron and slag formed in the blast furnace run down into the crucible at the bottom of the furnace. The slag floats upon the iron. Each is drawn off at suitable intervals through tap-holes. The iron is run into sand molds and forms the bars called "pigs." It may also be poured directly into steel molds. 

The pig iron may be carried to converters while it is still melted, and made into steel. Blast furnaces must be operated with great

care.

The ores, the coke, and the limestone must be analyzed so that their composition is known. Blast furnaces usually continue in operation for months at a time. 

The reactions inside the blast furnace are complicated. The final result is that the ferric oxide of the ore is reduced to iron, and the coke is oxidized to carbon monoxide and carbon dioxide. 

The solid coke cannot come in contact with all parts of the ore, but the gaseous carbon monoxide can. The real reducing agent in the blast furnace is carbon monoxide. Fe2O3+3CO-2Fe+3CO2. 

The reaction is a reversible one. It is carried to the right because a large excess of carbon monoxide is present. Carbon dioxide is constantly being reduced to carbon monoxide by the excess of the coke (§ 285).

Pig iron contains many of the impurities of the materials put into the furnace. The waste gases which escape through the downcomers resemble producer gas in composition. They are about carbon monoxide; the rest is mainly the nitrogen of the original air. As already stated, they are used for fuel. The slag of the blast furnace finds an important use in the manufacture of Portland cement. 

413. Forms of iron. Some of the forms of commercial iron are gray iron, malleable iron, wrought iron, steel in its various forms, and the special alloys of iron with other elements, chiefly with metals. The steel may be Bessemer, open-hearth, or electric-furnace steel. The differences between these kinds of iron is partly due to a difference in physical structure and partly to the presence of other substances. Among these are, of course, the impurities in the crude iron; but the most important ingredient is iron carbide, Fe3C. The carbon always present in crude iron may be combined in iron carbide, or it may be mixed with the iron in the form of graphite. Iron carbide has a great hardness and gives this quality to iron.

414. Cast iron.-Cast iron gets its name from the fact that it produces castings when the molten iron is poured into molds. It is essentially the pig iron of the blast furnace. It has the property of expanding when it solidifies, and therefore fills the mold, producing a casting of sharp outline. Cast iron is the lowest-melting form of iron. It cannot be welded or forged or given a temper. 

Cast iron may be either gray or white. If the pig iron cools slowly, its iron carbide decomposes, producing iron and graphite. 

Such cast iron is gray. It has a low melting point and can be cut with tools. If the melted pig iron cools very rapidly, the iron carbide does not have time to break up, and the cast iron is white. 

The white iron is very hard and brittle and cannot be cut with ordinary tools.

Malleable iron is a pig iron which has been annealed for two or three days at red heat and then allowed to cool slowly. The effect of this treatment is to allow the iron carbide of the cast iron to decompose; as a result the carbon is scattered more or less evenly through the iron. The treatment is usually carried out after the castings have been made. The product is somewhat malleable and has greater strength than cast iron. Such iron may be worked with tools and is less expensive than steel. 

415. Wrought iron. The large amount of carbon and silicon present in cast iron give the cast iron a low melting point (pig iron melts at 1075° C.), a crystalline structure, and the property of expanding when it becomes solid. If we remove nearly all the carbon and silicon, the iron becomes malleable, ductile, and tough, and has a melting point of about 1500° C. It may be as pure as 

99.8% iron. Wrought iron has the property of becoming plastic below its melting point. In this condition it can be forged into any shape, and two pieces can be welded together. Wrought iron is used for temporary magnets,

such as the cores of dynamo armatures and electromagnets.

Wrought iron is, a pig iron which has gone through the puddling process. The pig iron is heated in a puddling furnace which takes perhaps 500 pounds of the metal at a charge. The melted pig iron is heated in an air current (iron oxide is usually added as well), until the impurities of the cast iron are oxidized. The melted mass is stirred, or puddled, so that the oxygen of the air and of the iron oxide can get at the impurities of the cast iron. As a result the carbon, silicon, and phosphorus are largely removed. 

As the proportion of pure iron with its higher melting point becomes greater, the iron becomes pasty and can be made into a "bloom," or ball. The bloom is pressed or hammered into shape.

When wrought iron is examined under the microscope, it is found to have a fibrous structure, due to fibers of slag. This accounts for its toughness. When two pieces of wrought iron are welded together, borax or sand (acid anhydrides) are used to remove the film of iron oxide which covers the wrought iron. If borax is used, iron borate is formed; if sand is used, iron silicate is the product. 

Iron must actually touch iron, if the welding is to be successful. 

Wrought iron still containing a little slag resists corrosion. This quality, added to its toughness and other properties, makes it valuable for cables, anchors, chains, and wire. 

416. Steel. The production of steel on a large scale was made possible by the work of Bessemer in England and Kelly in Pennsylvania. Steel has gradually displaced both cast iron and wrought iron. It combines the qualities of both. It may be cast like cast iron, and forged and welded like wrought iron. At the same time it can be tempered, that is, can be given different degrees of hardness. Steel usually contains less than 2% of carbon and very little sulphur and phosphorus. Steel containing about 0.2% of carbon resembles wrought iron very closely and is called mild steel. Structural steel, used for build-

ings, and tool steel have a higher carbon content. Steel melts below wrought iron, and higher than cast iron. 

417. The Bessemer process. The most important modern methods of making steel are the Bessemer, the 

FLAMES MELTED IRON 

open-hearth, and the electric processes. The Bessemer process consists in removing practically all the impurities from pig iron by treating it with a blast of air until the carbon, the manganese, and the silicon are entirely removed. Then an alloy containing these same three elements in desired proportions is added to the purified iron. This alloy is known as ferromanganese or spiegeleisen (spē'gel-i'sĕn). The furnace in which the Bessemer process is carried out is called a converter. 

FIG. 196. 

AIR BLAST 

A cross-section of a Bessemer converter.

The appearance of the converter when it is in "blow" is a picturesque and inspiring sight. In the Bessemer process two varieties are recognized: the acid process and the basic process. 

In the acid process the converter is lined with silica, which is an acid anhydride and therefore capable of combining with oxides of metals to produce silicates. These are collected in the lining of the converter. This process cannot remove sulphur and phosphorus from the iron. Why not?

The basic Bessemer process is largely used in Europe. The converter is lined with calcium oxide and magnesium oxide. The lining therefore removes sulphur and phosphorus from the iron. 

Why? The slag made in the basic process is valuable as a source of phosphates, and is used as a fertilizer for soil, under the name of Thomas slag.

The steel obtained in the Bessemer process is cast into ingots.

These are either allowed to cool, so that they become just solid, and are then worked at once into rails, plates, structural steel, and other forms; or they are kept until wanted, and are then reheated just before being pressed into these forms. 

418. The openhearth process.—The open-hearth process is carried out in an open-hearth furnace.

This is large but shallow, of the type known as a reverberatory furnace. The charge may be in excess of 100 tons and consists usually of pig iron and scrap iron, sometimes also of iron oxide. Sometimes the melted pig iron is brought di 

FIG. 197. 

A Bessemer converter in action at the Lackawanna, N. Y., plant of the Bethlehem Steel Co. rectly from the blast furnace to the open-hearth furnace for conversion into steel. An oxidizing flame is directed down upon the charge by the roof of the furnace. The gas and air which produce the flame are preheated, so that the flame is very hot. There is a basic open-hearth process and an acid one, as in the case of the Bessemer process. What is the difference between them? Steel made in the open-hearth furnace is very tough and elastic, and is excellent for the bearings of machinery, for

boiler plate, for the structural work of bridges, and many other uses.

In the open-hearth process, as in the Bessemer process, the carbon, manganese, and silicon are first removed by 32 285A 

FIG. 198. 

Tapping a 200-ton tilting open-hearth furnace at the Bethlehem, Pa., plant. Note the comparative height of the men near the center of the picture. oxidation, and then the exact quantities desired are returned in the form of ferromanganese. 

419. Electric and crucible steel.-In this section we bring together the newest and the oldest of the modern methods of preparing steel. For several years the Bessemer and open-hearth processes have been displacing the crucible process, and now the electric process seems likely to displace the Bessemer process. 

The electric process is essentially an open-hearth process in which

the heat is produced by electricity rather than by combustion. 

At present the electric process is especially employed in the making of alloy steels.

The crucible process consists essentially in melting together very pure wrought iron and pure cast iron. The crucibles used are made of graphite, and the gases which get at the charge during heating are free from oxygen. Crucible steel is therefore very pure as regards iron oxide and adsorbed gases. 

420. Alloy steels. The ordinary forms of steel are carbon steels, in which the properties of iron are improved by the presence of carbon in different proportions and in different forms of combination and dispersion. 

There are a great many steel alloys in which other metals are added to add desired qualities to the steel. Among these alloys are manganese steel, vanadium steel, tungsten steel, nickel steel, chrome steel, carboloy, and cobalt steels.

Manganese steel, containing up to 20% manganese, is very hard even when heated up and then cooled slowly. It does not lose its temper. It is used in railroad "frogs" which must stand great wear, also in bank vaults. 

Vanadium steel usually contains chromium as well. It is used for the frames and springs of automobiles and other structures that must stand twisting and vibration. 

Tungsten steel (8 to 20% tungsten and 3 to 5% chromium) is used for high-speed metal turning. The steel is self-hardening; it does not require heating or sudden cooling. It holds its temper when red-hot from friction.

Nickel steel resists rusting and has great elasticity. It is used for armor plate and for the shafts of propellers and of water turbines. Platinite and invar (§ 428) are iron-nickel alloys.

Chrome steel of a certain composition resists burning with the acetylene blowpipe and is used for bank vaults. Chrome steel also resists corrosion. Stainless steel contains from 12 to 18% chromium and is used for cutlery, fishing tackle, cooking utensils,

and apparatus needed in the preparation of acids. Rustless iron is another chrome-steel alloy. 

421. How steel is hardened. The hardness of steel depends partly upon the substances present in it and partly upon the method by which it is heated and cooled. 

If heated steel is allowed to cool slowly, the steel becomes soft. During the slow cooling the change of iron carbide into iron and carbon has time to take place, and the carbon becomes graphite, which has little effect in hardening iron. If steel is heated red hot and then cooled suddenly by plunging it into water or oil, it becomes very hard. If the hard steel is heated again, and then allowed to cool slowly, it becomes soft and cannot be given a sharp edge by grinding. The temper of the steel has been "drawn" by the heating and slow cooling. Steel is usually tempered by heating it up slowly and watching it carefully until its surface acquires a particular color, due to a film of oxides. The steel is then plunged into water or oil. The colored films tell the temperature of the steel. They are generally removed from the finished product, but we sometimes see them in saws, watch and clock springs, and other steel objects.

COLOR TEMPERATURE USE 

blue-black blue 

320° C. 277-300 saws, chisels watch springs purple 270 brown 255-265 bright yellow 243 table knives shears pocket knives pale yellow 220-232 razor blades

422. The metal iron.-None of the commercial forms of iron is pure iron. Pure iron probably does not rust. 

Wrought iron is the purest commercial form. As we already know, soft or wrought iron does not form a permanent magnet, while steel does. When iron is ignited in oxygen it burns to form the magnetic oxide, Fe3O4, and gives off a great deal of heat and light in the process. 

The same product results when iron at red heat decomposes steam (§ 36). Iron reacts readily with dilute acids, forming ferrous salts in which the iron has the valence of 2+. The oxygen of the air or other oxidizing agents change these to ferric salts. Ferric salts in solution (Fe3+) are readily reduced to ferrous salts (Fe2+) by reducing agents like hydrogen sulphide, active hydrogen, and stannous chloride.

In the presence of water vapor and carbon dioxide iron rusts, or corrodes. In order to prevent corrosion we cover iron with other metals or with paint. We have already learned that tin plate is iron covered with tin, and that galvanized iron has a covering of zinc. Nickel and chromium are also used to plate iron. Oxidation of the iron surface produces a protective covering. When iron is heated in steam, it becomes rust-resisting, as in Russia iron. What oxide is formed? When iron is dipped into melted niter, it gains a similar covering. 

423. Ferrous compounds. Ferrous compounds are in general unstable and are changed by the oxygen of the air to ferric compounds. Ferrous oxide is produced when ferric oxide is heated with hydrogen at 300° C. When exposed to air it is at once oxidized. Ferrous hydroxide, Fe(OH)2, is white in the absence of air. It is obtained as a greenish precipitate when the solution of an alkali

is added to a ferrous salt. It soon becomes brown because of the formation of ferric hydroxide. Ferrous sulphide is produced when iron reacts with sulphur. Large quantities are obtained when lead ores containing sulphur are heated with iron to liberate the lead (§ 403). It is also produced when a soluble sulphide, such as ammonium sulphide, is added to the solution of a ferrous salt. Why does not hydrogen sulphide precipitate ferrous sulphide? 

See § 180. Iron pyrite, FeS2, has the color of brass. 

Ferrous chloride and ferrous sulphate are formed when the corresponding acids react with iron. Both are readily oxidized to ferric salts. 4FeCl2+4HC1+O₂-4FeCl3+2H2O. 

Write the corresponding equation for the oxidation of ferrous sulphate in the presence of dilute sulphuric acid. 

Ferrous sulphate combines with water to form green vitriol, or "copperas," FeSO4.7H2O. The double salt, (NH4)2SO4. FeSO4.6H2O, is called Mohr's salt. It is much less easily oxidized than ferrous sulphate alone. 

424. Ferric compounds. Ferric oxide, Fe2O3, occurs in nature as the ore hematite. Ferric hydroxide is formed when an alkali hydroxide is added to the solution of a ferric salt. It forms a bulky, reddish-brown precipitate. 

The magnetic oxide of iron, Fe3O4, is formed when iron burns in oxygen or reacts with steam. It is found in nature as magnetite. Some specimens are natural magnets, called lodestones. We may write its formula FeO.Fe2O3. 

Ferric chloride is prepared when iron is heated in chlorine. It is then in the anhydrous condition. Its solution is formed from chlorine and a solution of ferrous chloride, and when ferrous

chloride or iron is treated with aqua regia. When crystallized from water it forms a yellow solid, FeCl3.6H2O. 

Writing ink is prepared from ferrous sulphate and tannic acid.

The resulting substance, ferrous tannate, is light in color, but becomes dark when oxidized by the air, owing to the formation of ferric tannate, which is a black, insoluble substance. The ink contains a colloid, such as gum arabic or dextrin, and a dye to give it a dark color before it is oxidized. We can usually remove an old ink stain from cloth by reducing the ferric tannate to the ferrous salt. A solution of ammonium oxalate, (NH4)2C2O4, is used. The cloth may need to be soaked in the solution for several hours. 

425. Double cyanides containing iron. The double cyanides containing iron are of special interest because we can get from them two blue pigments called Prussian blue and Turnbull's blue. The source of the double cyanides is potassium ferrocyanide, also called "yellow prussiate of potash." We write its formula K4Fe(CN)6, or Fe(CN)2.4KCN. The latter formula shows that the iron is in the ferrous state. It is made when potassium carbonate, iron, and nitrogenous organic substances, such as hoofs and horns, are heated together. The product is extracted with water and crystallized. When a ferric salt is added to a solution of potassium ferrocyanide, a precipitate of Prussian blue is formed. 4FeCl3+3K4Fe(CN) 6-→Fe4[Fe(CN)6]3 +12KC1. 

With a ferrous salt, potassium ferrocyanide gives a white precipitate.

FeCl2 + K4Fe(CN) 6→K₂Fe[Fe(CN)6] ↓ +2KC1. 

When chlorine is added to potassium ferrocyanide solution, a solution of potassium ferricyanide, K,Fe(CN)6, is

obtained. On evaporation, the solution deposits a red, crystalline solid. 2K4Fe(CN) 6+Cl₂-2K3Fe(CN)6+2KC1. 

The formula of potassium ferricyanide may also be written Fe(CN)3.3KCN. This shows that the chlorine oxidized the iron from the ferrous to the ferric condition. When a ferrous salt reacts with potassium ferricyanide, a precipitate of Turnbull's blue is obtained; this is Fes[Fe(CN)6]2. With a ferric salt, potassium ferricyanide gives no precipitate, but a brown solution. 

In preparing blue-print paper we take advantage of the reaction between ferrous salts and potassium ferricyanide. The unexposed paper contains a ferric salt and potassium ferricyanide. Exposure to light reduces the ferric salt to a ferrous salt, and Turnbull's blue is produced. When the exposed paper is washed with water, the unchanged materials are removed, and a blue-print, such as is used in architects' drawings, is obtained. 3FeCl2+2K3Fe(CN)6→Fe3[Fe(CN)6]2↓ +6KC1. 

The tests for ferrous and ferric ions in solution include their reactions with solutions of potassium ferrocyanide and potassium ferricyanide. One other cyanogen compound is used in these tests; this is potassium thiocyanate (KSCN), also called potassium sulphocyanate. The corresponding ammonium salt is also used. 

Ferric salts produce a blood-red solution with a solution of a thiocyanate. The corresponding ferrous compound is colorless. FeCl3+3KSCN Fe (SCN):+3KC1. 

426. Cobalt. The name, cobalt, is derived from the 

German kobald, which means the same as our word goblin. The metal received its name because some of its ores looked like the ores of other metals, but did not yield the expected metals when smelted. Goblins were supposed to have bewitched the ore. Nickel ores which looked like copper ores refused to give copper. Hence they were said to contain kupfernickel, or the copper of

Old Nick. The smelting of these metals is still troublesome. The two principal cobalt ores are cobaltite, CoASS, and smaltite, CoAs2. Usually these accompany nickel ores, as in the Sudbury region in Ontario. Much of the world's cobalt supply comes from the Belgian Congo. 

The ores are roasted to produce cobalt oxide, CoO, and this is reduced by heating it with hydrogen or carbon. 

The metal is not pure white, but has a reddish luster. Like iron it can be magnetized. Cobalt is a hard metal but malleable and ductile. It reacts most readily with nitric acid. The air does not tarnish it. The compounds of cobalt have been used for a long time as pigments, but the metal has had no uses until recently. Cobalt is finding increasing use at the present time in alloys. A cobalt-chromium alloy called cochrome resists corrosion by acids effectively. Certain alloy steels used for making magnets contain cobalt. Carboloy, one of the hardest artificial cutting materials, is tungsten carbide and cobalt. 

427. Cobalt compounds.-Cobalt forms oxides corresponding to the three common oxides of iron; their formulas are: CoO, C0203, Co3O4. The first is cobaltous oxide, the second, cobaltic oxide. Cobaltous solutions are pink and contain Co²+ ions. When the pink solutions are treated with concentrated hydrochloric or sulphuric. acid, they turn blue. If a pink cobalt salt is used as an ink on white or pink paper, it leaves invisible writing under the ordinary, damp conditions of the air. When this invisible writing is heated so as to dry it, it becomes blue and visible. Inks containing cobalt salts are called sympathetic inks.

When sodium hydroxide solution is added to a cobaltous salt, the precipitate is a blue, basic salt. When heated this is changed to a red precipitate, Co(OH)2. When a cobalt salt or cobaltous

oxide is heated with the materials which produce glass, cobaltblue glass is obtained. This is cobalt silicate. When ground up it forms smalt, a pigment used for coloring glass and porcelain. 

A test for cobalt compounds is that when they are heated with a bead of borax or of sodium metaphosphate they color the bead blue (306). Cobaltous salts give a black precipitate (CoS) when treated with a solution of ammonium sulphide. 

428. Nickel.-Nickel is a white metal but has a yellowish cast. It is magnetic, like iron and cobalt, but whereas cobalt has had few uses, nickel has been used for a long time to plate other metals, especially iron. It is also used in such alloys as German silver, nickel steel, and the United States' five-cent piece. Modern alloys of nickel are monel metal, nichrome, chromel, invar, and illium. Monel metal contains nickel and copper and a little manganese and iron. It resists corrosion, is strong, and can be welded, cast, and worked with tools. It can also be drawn into wire. Nichrome and chromel contain nickel and chromium. Nichrome forms the wire used in the heating units of electric stoves and toasters. Invar gets its name from "invariable," because it expands and contracts so little with changes of temperature. It contains nickel and iron, and is used for pendulums and steel tapes. Platinite is an iron-nickel alloy that expands and contracts to the same degree that platinum does. It is used in sealing wires into electric-light bulbs. Illium is an alloy of nickel with copper, chromium, and other metals.

Like cobalt, nickel may be obtained from its oxide by heating it with hydrogen or aluminum, also by the use of carbon. The chief nickel ores are pentlandite, (Ni, Cu, Fe)S, and garnierite, a double silicate of nickel and magnesium. The Sudbury region in 

Ontario yields most of the nickel which is used.

429. Nickel compounds.-Nickelous oxide is NiO, and the hydroxide, Ni(OH)2. Both are green. Nickelic oxide is Ni₂O3, and the hydroxide, Ni(OH)3. Both are black. 

The nickel salts in common use are all nickelous salts. 

Nickel reacts with hydrochloric and sulphuric acids, but most readily with nitric acid. In solution, nickelous salts are green. When an alkali sulphide, such as ammonium sulphide, is added to a nickel salt, a black precipitate of nickel sulphide, NiS, is obtained. 

The Mond process for purifying nickel consists in converting it into nickel carbonyl, Ni(CO)4. This is formed when carbon monoxide reacts with nickel. It is a colorless liquid, boiling at 43° C. At 150-180° C. the carbonyl is decomposed, giving pure nickel and carbon monoxide. Ni+4CO Ni(CO)4. 

Cobalt does not form a similar compound, hence the method may be used to separate the two metals. When nickel salts are heated with a borax bead in the oxidizing flame, the bead becomes brown. 

430. Summary.-Iron is man's most useful metal. Its ores are chiefly oxides and are reduced by means of carbon. The apparatus for producing commercial iron is called a blast furnace. Pig iron is the product of the blast furnace. 

The materials used in the blast furnace are the ore, coke, and limestone. Pig iron and slag collect at the bottom of the furnace 

The waste gases of a blast furnace are used for fuel. 

The chief commercial forms of iron are cast iron, malleable iron, wrought iron, steel, and steel alloys.

Cast iron is the lowest-melting form of iron. It cannot be welded, forged, or tempered, but it can be cast into molds. 

Malleable iron is an impure wrought iron.

Wrought iron is the purest form of commercial iron. It may be forged and welded, but cannot be cast or tempered. It is very

malleable, ductile, and tough. Wrought iron is produced in the puddling process, in which the impurities of cast iron are oxidized and removed.

Steel is intermediate between cast iron and wrought iron. It may be cast and also forged and welded. It may also be tempered. The processes of preparing steel are the Bessemer, openhearth, and electric processes. In the Bessemer process the impurities are oxidized in a converter. An alloy containing the proper proportions of carbon, manganese, and silicon is then added. 

The open-hearth process carries out the removal of the carbon, manganese, and silicon by means of a blast of air as the Bessemer process does. Then the desired quantities of these elements are returned in the form of ferromanganese. 

The crucible process consists in melting together pure wrought iron and pure cast iron in graphite crucibles. 

Steel is alloyed with many other elements to give it certain desired qualities. 

The tempering of steel consists in heating it to a definite temperature and then cooling it quickly in water or oil. Its hardness depends upon the temperature to which it was heated before being suddenly cooled. 

Iron is active in the air (rusts) and acts upon dilute acids, forming salts. Iron with dilute sulphuric or hydrochloric acid forms a ferrous salt. 

The corrosion of iron is delayed by covering it with tin, zinc, or other metals as a plating, or by oxidizing the surface to make it rust-resisting.

Ferric salts are formed when ferrous salts are oxidized. 

Ink contains ferrous tannate; this is oxidized by the air to ferric tannate. 

Iron forms both ferrocyanides and ferricyanides. Potassium ferrocyanide is yellow prussiate of potash. It is oxidized by chlorine to potassium ferricyanide. Prussian blue is ferric ferrocyanide. Turnbull's blue is ferrous ferricyanide. Blue-print paper contains a ferric salt and potassium ferricyanide. 

The reactions of ferrous and ferric salts with ferrocyanides, ferricyanides, and thiocyanates constitute tests for iron ions in solution.

Cobalt and nickel were once considered bewitched forms of iron and copper. Like iron they can be magnetized. Cobalt is used chiefly in alloys and in the form of some of its salts. Cobalt salts color a borax bead blue.

Nickel is used as a protective covering for iron, also in alloys. 

Monel metal, nichrome, and chromel are modern nickel alloys. 

Nickel reacts with all common acids to produce nickelous salts. 

Nickel salts color a borax bead brown in the oxidizing flame.

CHAPTER XXXII  

432. Chromium.-Chromium and manganese are not in the same periodic group. Chromium is in the group with sulphur, and manganese in the group with chlorine. 

In their higher stages of oxidation both of them form compounds which give off oxygen readily and so are used as oxidizing agents. The name chromium is from a word meaning "color," and was given the element because of the many colored compounds which it forms. 

Chromium is very rare by comparison with iron. The chief ore of chromium is chromite, Fe(CrO2)2. The formula may be also written: FeO.Cr2O3. Pure chromium is prepared by heating chromic oxide, Cr2O3, with powdered aluminum (§ 377). It is a white metal with a blue cast, very hard, brittle, and difficult to melt. 

A great deal of the chromium used is not separated from the iron with which it is combined in the ore, chromite. When chromite is reduced in an electric furnace with carbon, the alloy, ferrochrome, is produced. This may be purified to give an alloy containing perhaps 75% chromium. Chromium may also be prepared by the electrolysis of some chromic salt. When so prepared, it is a malleable metal.

Chromium does not tarnish under ordinary conditions, but when heated very hot it burns, forming chromic oxide. At red heat chromium liberates hydrogen from steam. Ordinarily the metal reacts with dilute acids, forming salts and hydrogen. Chro- 503

mium finds an extensive use not only in alloys, such as chrome steel, but also as a protective covering for metals. It forms a hard, bright surface, which has great resistance to wear. 

433. Chromium salts.-The common chromium compounds are chromous salts, chromic salts, chromates, and dichromates. Soluble chromous salts contain the ion 

Cr²+. Their solutions are blue if kept away from the air. 

If exposed, they are at once oxidized to chromic salts (Cr3+) which are green or violet. The principal chromic salts are the chloride (CrCl), the sulphate, Cr2(SO4)3, and chrome alum, K2SO4.Cr2(SO4)3.24H2O. formula shows, chrome alum is the hydrate of a double sulphate. It is an alum (§ 380), but it has chromium (Cr³+) in place of aluminum (Al3+). Chrome alum is used to form mordants in dyeing, and to tan leather (chrome leather). 

As its

In solutions of chromates and dichromates, the chromium is in the negative ions (CrO42-) and (Cr2O7²¯), respectively. The most common chromates and dichromates are the sodium and potassium salts: Na2CrO4 and K2CrO4; Na2Cr2O7 and K2Cr2O7. They are all soluble. Chromates are yellow; dichromates, orange-red. 

All chromium compounds color the borax bead bright green. 

434. Chromium oxides and hydroxides. Chromium forms three oxides corresponding to three sets of compounds. Chromous oxide is Cr²+02-, and chromic oxide is Cr½³+03²¯. The third oxide, Cr+О¸²¯, we call chromic anhydride. With water it forms chromic acid, H2CrO4. 

Three hydroxides correspond to these oxides: Cr(OH)2, Cr(OH)3, and Cr(OH). The last one we write in a de-

hydrated form corresponding to H2SO4. It is chromic acid.

Cr(OH)6 H2CrO4+2H2O. 

Chromic oxide, Cr2O3, forms chrome green, a wellknown pigment. Chromic hydroxide, Cr(OH)3, is formed as a green precipitate when an alkali is added to a chromic salt.

Cr2(SO4)3+6NaOH→2Cr(OH)3 | +зNa2SO4. 

When the alkali is added in excess, the chromic hydroxide is redissolved. Chromic hydroxide has, therefore, the properties of a weak acid, as aluminum hydroxide has. Both these hydroxides also react ("dissolve") with acids; they have a double (amphoteric) behavior. Write the equation for the reaction between chromic hydroxide and hydrochloric acid; between chromic hydroxide and sodium hydroxide. See § 378.

Since chromic hydroxide is a weak base, chromic carbonate is hydrolyzed by water just as aluminum carbonate is. Carbon dioxide escapes, and chromic hydroxide is precipitated. Use the equation given under aluminum (§ 380) as a model, and write the equation for the reaction of a chromium salt with sodium carbonate. Also write the equation for the hydrolysis of chromic sulphide. 

435. Chromic anhydride.-Chromic anhydride, as we learned in § 434, gives chromic acid (H2CrO4) when it unites with water. Write the equation. When a saturated solution of potassium dichromate is treated with concentrated sulphuric acid, bright, red crystals of chromic anhydride are produced. These are usually seen in a freshly prepared cleaning solution, such as is used in chemical laboratories.

K2Cr2O7+H2SO4→K2SO4+H2Cr2O7. H2Cr2O7 H2O+2CrO3 ↓·

When heated, chromic anhydride breaks down into chromic oxide and oxygen. 4CrO3-2Cr2O3+302 ↑. 

If some substance is present to take up the oxygen, the oxidation is often violent. If sulphuric acid is present, chromic sulphate is formed by the reaction of the acid with chromic oxide.

Cr₂O3+3H2SO4→Cr2(SO4)3+3H2O. 

We can understand the chemical relation between chromic salts and chromates best, if we fix our attention upon the relation between chromic oxide and chromic anhydride.

Cr₂O3+(30)→2CrO3. 

Chromic oxide is the anhydride of chromic hydroxide, Cr(OH)3. It is basic; that is, it reacts with acids. The products are chromic salts. Chromic anhydride, on the other hand, is acid in character. It unites with bases. 

If we wish to oxidize chromic oxide to chromic anhydride, we must have a base (or an alkali carbonate) present to unite with the anhydride as rapidly as it is formed. The product will be a chromate. Cr₂O3+(30)+4NaOH-2Na2CrO4+2H2O. Cr₂O3+(30)+2Na₂CO:→2Na₂CrO4+2CO₂ ↑ . 

The common chromium ore, chromite (FeO.Cr2O3), contains chromic oxide. To convert it into a chromate we roast (oxidize) it in a furnace with potassium carbonate. We thus obtain potassium chromate, K2CrO4. 

In order to prepare a chromic salt from a chromate, we reverse the reactions just given. Chromic oxide is a basic anhydride. It

reacts with acids to produce chromic salts. The change from a chromate to a chromic salt involves the reduction of chromic anhydride to chromic oxide. An acid must be therefore present. 

The acid reacts with the chromic oxide as fast as it is formed. 

The reducing agent takes up the oxygen. 2CrO3+3H2SO4→Cr2(SO4)3+3H2O+(30). 3H2SO3 (reducing agent)+(30)→3H2SO4. 3H2S (reducing agent)+(30)→3H₂O+3S ↓ · The potassium dichromate-sulphuric acid mixture is used for cleaning dishes because it furnishes active oxygen to oxidize grease, tar, and other organic "dirt." 

436. Chromates and dichromates.-Chromic anhydride unites with water in two proportions. We may write chromic acid as H₂O.CrO3; the formula of dichromic acid is H2O.2CrO3, or H2Cr2O7. The corresponding potassium salts may be written K2CrO4 (K2O.CrO3) and K2Cr2O, (K20.2CrO3). Both contain the same anhydride, CrO3. 

If to a chromate solution (yellow) we add sulphuric acid, it turns orange red, owing to the formation of a dichromate. 2K2CrO4+H2SO4-K2Cr2O7+K2SO4+H2O. 

If to a dichromate solution we add an alkali, the solution turns yellow.

K2Cr2O7+2KOH→2K2CrO4+H2O. 

Dichromates, like chromates, form chromate ions, CrO42, in solution; hence either gives a precipitate of a chromate when added to salts of lead, silver, barium, and other metals.

K2Cr2O7+H2O→K2CrO4+H2CrO4. Pb(NO3)2+K2CrO4→PbCrO4 ↓ +2KNO3. Pb(NO3)2+H2CrO4→→PbCrO4 ↓ +2HNO3.

Lead chromate is the pigment, chrome yellow. Potassium dichromate, K2Cr2O7, separates from solution in large, red crystals. 

Sodium dichromate crystals are Na2Cr2O7.2H₂O. 

Dichromates are used in processes of tanning, dyeing, decolorizing oils, and as oxidizing agents in the laboratory. When alcohol (C2H6O) is mixed with a potassium dichromate solution containing dilute sulphuric acid, the reaction produces a great deal of heat, and the alcohol is oxidized to aldehyde (C2H4O). K2Cr2O7+4H2SO4+3C2H6O→ K2SO4+Cr2(SO4)3+7H2O+3C2H4O ↑ . 

Study this equation, and trace in it the chemical changes that take place, especially what becomes of the chromic anhydride of the potassium dichromate. 

437. Manganese.-Manganese is found chiefly as manganese dioxide (MnO2) in the mineral pyrolusite (pī'rōlūs'ite). The steel mills of the United States consume a great deal, but much of this must be imported. Russia and India are the largest producers. 

Pure manganese is prepared by heating the oxide with aluminum. It is a gray metal, resembling iron, but harder and more brittle. It cannot be magnetized. It melts at 

1230° C. Manganese is oxidized readily by moist air, and reacts slowly with water. It reacts with dilute acids to form manganous salts, such as MnCl2 and MnSO4. 

What is the other product?

Manganese is used chiefly in the form of alloys with iron. Such are spiegeleisen and ferromanganese, used in making steel (§ 417). 

In order to prepare it for such uses, the pure metal need not be obtained, but mixtures of iron oxide and manganese oxide can be reduced by hot carbon. Manganese steel, containing perhaps 

15% manganese, is used for rails that must endure heavy wear. 

A bronze containing manganese is not easily corroded by sea water,

and is used for the blades of propellers. What are the other components of bronze? See § 386. 

438. Manganese salts and oxides. Like chromium, manganese forms compounds in which the metal has a low valence, and others in which it has a high valence. 

All the common manganese salts are manganous salts, in which the valence of manganese is 2+. There are also manganates and permanganates, such as K2MnO4 and KMnO4, respectively, in which the metal forms part of a negative (acid) radical. In manganates the metal has a valence of 6+, and in permanganates of 7+. 22- 2The oxide of manganese which corresponds to manganous salts is Mn²+O²-. The oxide present in manganates is Mn+O32; that of permanganates is Mn2+07². There are also the oxides, Mn,³+O3², Mn4+02², and Mn3O4, or MnO2.2MnO. When the higher oxides of manganese react with sulphuric acid, they all yield manganous salts. 

All the oxygen in excess of that in manganous oxide is liberated. 2MnO2+2H2SO4-2MnSO4+2H₂O+O₂ ↑ · Manganous hydroxide is a white, insoluble solid. How may it be prepared? Its anhydride, MnO, is green. 

Both are basic in character, like the corresponding ferrous and chromous compounds; they do not react with alkalies. 

Manganese heptoxide, Mn2O7, is an explosive liquid resembling chlorine heptoxide, Cl2O7. 

Soluble manganous salts are usually pink. The most common ones are the chloride and sulphate. What are their formulas?

Manganous carbonate is a white, insoluble solid. Manganous sulphide (MnS) is pink. It is precipitated when ammonium sulphide reacts with a manganous salt. Write the equation.

Manganese dioxide gives off 1/3 of its oxygen when heated to 535° C. The oxide Mn3O4 remains. We have used the dioxide as an oxidizing agent to liberate chlorine (§ 128) and as a catalyst in the decomposition of potassium chlorate. Manganese dioxide is used to neutralize the green color due to ferrous compounds in commercial glass. In dry cells it is an oxidizing agent and counteracts the polarization of the cells. 

Manganese compounds react with a hot borax bead. In the oxidizing flame the bead has the color of an amethyst; in the reducing flame the bead becomes colorless. 

439. Manganates and permanganates.-Manganates are salts of manganic acid (H₂MnO4 or H₂O.MnO3). 

When a manganous compound is oxidized to a manganate, the reaction is essentially the change of MnO to MnO3. 

The valence of manganese is raised from 2+ to 6+. The oxidation must take place in the presence of a base, since MnO3 is the anhydride of an acid. If we heat MnO, KNO3, and KOH together, the equations are: 2KNO3-2KNO₂+(20). MnO+(20)+2KOH→K₂MnO4+H2O. 

We prepare potassium manganate by heating a mixture of a manganese compound with potassium hydroxide and an oxidizing agent. The melted mixture becomes green. 

Out of this mixture we can obtain green crystals of the manganate, K2MnO4. The manganate is stable only when an excess of alkali is present. If we treat it with an acid and a reducing agent, the manganate is changed back to a manganous salt. 

440. Potassium permanganate.-When potassium manganate is dissolved in water, it is hydrolyzed, giving potassium hydroxide, manganese dioxide, and potassium permanganate, KMnO4. The change proceeds better if

carbon dioxide is bubbled through the solution, to convert some of the potassium hydroxide into potassium carbonate. 3K2MnO4+2H2O→2KMnO4+MnO2+4KOH. 

If ozone is used to carry out the oxidation, no manganese dioxide is formed. 2K2MnO4+H2O+03→2KMnO4+2KOH+02. 

Potassium permanganate crystallizes from water in dark prisms. Its solution is purple.

Like potassium dichromate, potassium permanganate is used as an oxidizing agent. If an acid and a reducing agent are present, the manganese of the permanganate becomes reduced to the manganous state, and a manganous salt is formed. In dilute solution this is colorless, consequently the purple solution loses its color when its oxidizing power is over. The solution is therefore used in chemical analysis to determine how much of a substance able to be oxidized is present. Of course, the weight of permanganate in every cubic centimeter must be known.

Potassium permanganate contains the anhydride, Mn2O7. When one formula weight of this decomposes, in acid solution, it gives 5 atomic weights of oxygen. Mn2O7-2Mn0+(50). 

Consequently 2 formula weights of potassium permanganate give 5 atomic weights of oxygen to be used in oxidations. 2KMnO4+3H2SO4→K2SO4+2MnSO4+3H2O+(50). 

How many formula weights of potassium sulphite, K2SO3, can be oxidized to potassium sulphate by the oxygen given off by two

formula weights of potassium permanganate, according to this equation? Write the equation.

When potassium permanganate is used in alkaline solutions, its reduction goes only to manganese dioxide, which is precipitated. Mn2O7-2MnO₂+(30). 2KMnO4+H2O-2KOH+2MnO2 ↓ +(30). 

How does the amount of available oxygen compare with that under acid conditions? Potassium permanganate solution is unstable in the light, and must be kept in a dark place to prevent decomposition. It forms a brown stain (MnO2) upon the skin and upon cloth and filter paper. The stain is removed by dilute oxalic or hydrochloric acid. Potassium permanganate is used as a disinfecting agent and for other oxidizing purposes. It is used in some dyeing processes. 

441. Summary.-Chromium is a white metal of high melting point, prepared from its oxide and powdered aluminum, also by the electrolysis of a chromic salt. It reacts with dilute acids and, at red heat, with steam. Chromium is used in alloys, such as chrome steel, and as a plating for other metals. 

The common chromic salts form green solutions due to the presence of the chromic ion (Cr3+). Chromates and dichromates contain chromic anhydride. 

Chromic hydroxide is a weak acid and base, like aluminum hydroxide. Its carbonate and sulphide are completely hydrolyzed by water.

Chromic anhydride unites with water to form chromic acid. 

When chromic anhydride is heated, it gives chromic oxide and oxygen.

Chromic salts are prepared from chromates or dichromates by reducing them in the presence of an acid. 

Chromates and dichromates are prepared from chromic compounds by oxidizing them in the presence of a base. The change of a chromate into a dichromate, or the reverse, does not involve oxidation or reduction, since both contain chromic anhydride. 

Chromates and dichromates are used as oxidizing agents. When so used they are reduced to chromic salts.

Manganese is a gray metal like iron, but harder. It reacts with dilute acids, producing manganous salts. The pure metal is prepared from the oxide and aluminum. The chief use of manganese is in iron alloys. Manganese steel is very hard. 

The most common manganese compounds are manganese dioxide, manganous salts, and permanganates. The oxide corresponding to the manganous salts is MnO. The one corresponding to manganates is MnO3, and to permanganates, Mn2O7. 

Manganous salts are oxidized to manganates in the presence of bases. Manganates and permanganates are reduced to manganous salts in the presence of acids. 

Potassium permanganate is used as an oxidizing agent because of the ease with which it gives up oxygen. Its solution is purple, but becomes colorless as soon as it has been reduced to the manganous state.

Under acid conditions two formula weights of potassium permanganate give five atomic weights of active oxygen. Under alkaline conditions the same amount of potassium permanganate gives only three atomic weights of oxygen.

CHAPTER XXXIII  443. Meaning of organic chemistry.-In § 265 we learned of the large number of carbon compounds. Since the carbon compounds of nature are produced by animals and plants, which are organisms, it was formerly supposed that these natural organic compounds are in some way different from the compounds of the other elements (inorganic compounds). However, thousands of socalled organic compounds have been prepared by chemists, and the term organic chemistry means simply "chemistry of the carbon compounds." The compounds of carbon are usually studied in classes, or groups, because a good many compounds have similar properties and therefore belong together. The following are some of these classes: 

1. Hydrocarbons 6. Aldehydes 

2. Alcohols 

3. Ethers 

4. Halogen compounds 

5. Amines 

7. Organic acids 

8. Esters or organic salts 

9. Carbohydrates

Each of these classes is represented by many important substances. Thus, under hydrocarbons we study petroleum and natural gas; under alcohols, not only ordinary alcohol but glycerin; under halogen compounds, chloroform and iodoform; under esters we consider fats, oils, 515

and soaps; while under carbohydrates we find such important substances as sugar, starch, cellulose, paper, and rayon. 

444. Hydrocarbons.-Hydrocarbons are compounds of hydrogen and carbon. Among them are marsh gas and acetylene, which we have already studied (§§ 289 and 

293). Two other important hydrocarbons are ethylene and benzene. 

FIG. 199. 

Louis Pasteur (1822-1895). Organic chemist, brilliant investigator of ferments, and one of the founders of bacteriology.

Marsh gas gets its name from the fact that it is formed in stagnant water in which vegetable matter is decaying. When such water is stirred, bubbles of marsh gas rise to the top and may be collected. Its chemical name is methane. The gas is found in natural gas and the fire damp of mines (§ 25); it can also be prepared in the laboratory.

From its percentage composition we calculate the simplest formula of methane as CH4. The weight of the gram-molecular volume (22.4 liters) is 16 grams; hence 

CH4 is the correct formula. Why? There is another hydrocarbon, called ethane, which resembles methane. 

We calculate its simplest formula as CH3. But the weight of 22.4 liters of ethane is 30 grams; hence the true formula is not CH3, but C2H6. Explain this. Chemists have found many hydrocarbons which resemble methane in their chemical properties and which are, therefore,

H-C-H said to belong to the methane series. In the constitutional formulas of these hydrocarbons, as in that of methane (§ 112), we assume that the valence of carbon is 4. H H H-C-C-H H H HHH H-C-C-C-H HHH methane ethane propane HHHH H-C C-H HHHH HH-C-H isobutane normal butane

There are two constitutional formulas for butane, C4H10, because there are two butanes. These are said to be isomers. The number of possible arrangements of the atoms in the hydrocarbon pentane, C5H12, is three, and the three isomers are known substances. In the case of hexane, C6H14, five isomers are known. 

The number of isomers increases rapidly as the number of carbon and hydrogen atoms increases. A hydrocarbon, C60H122, is known. 

Thousands of isomers of this formula are possible. 

Methane and the other hydrocarbons of the series are rather inactive chemically and are known as paraffins, which means "little activity." All of them burn to form carbon dioxide and water (§§ 152 and 293). 

445. Ethylene.-When alcohol and concentrated sulphuric acid are mixed, they react according to the equation:

C2H5OH+H2SO4 →C2H₂HSO4+H2O. 

When the mixture is heated, a colorless gas is evolved, which is ethylene, C2H4. C2H5HSO4→C2H4 ↑ +H2SO4.

From its percentage composition we calculate the simplest formula of ethylene as CH2. But 22.4 liters of it weigh 28 grams, therefore the true formula must be C2H4. When we compare the formula of ethylene with that of ethane, C2H6, we see that ethylene has two hydrogen atoms less than ethane. Under proper conditions the ethylene unites with hydrogen and forms ethane. Ethylene also reacts very energetically with chlorine and with bromine.

C2H4+H2→C2H6. C2H4+Br2→C2H4Bг2. 

Because ethylene can unite directly with elements like hydrogen and the halogens, we think of it as an unsaturated hydrocarbon, and write its formula with a "double bond" to show that one valence of each of its two carbon atoms is not being used to hold other atoms. When ethylene reacts with hydrogen or with bromine, these unused valences are brought into action, so that a saturated compound is produced.

H H H H H-Ċ-Ċ-H+Br₂→H-Ċ-Ċ-H. 

Br Br

There are also other hydrocarbons of the ethylene series. 

Such are propylene (C3H6) and butylene (C4H8). 

Ethylene burns, producing carbon dioxide and water. Write the balanced molecular equation. 

446. Acetylene.-Acetylene is formed when calcium carbide reacts with water (§ 293). Like ethylene it is active and unites with hydrogen and with halogens. But while one molecular weight of ethylene unites with two atomic weights of hydrogen, one molecular weight of acetylene unites with four. We therefore write its formula with a "triple bond" to show that each of its

two carbon atoms can take up two hydrogen or other univalent atoms. 

Br Br

H-C=C-H+2Br₂→ H¬Ċ-Ċ-H. 

Br Br

There are other hydrocarbons resembling acetylene, which are said to belong to the acetylene series. If we compare the formulas of ethane, ethylene, and acetylene, and use "n" for the number of carbon atoms in each of the three, we find that the number of hydrogen atoms is represented by 2n+2, 2n, and 2n-2, respectively. 

CnH2n+2; CnH2n; CnH2n-2.

All the members of the methane series can be represented by the same general formula as methane, all the hydrocarbons of the ethylene series by the same general formula as ethylene, and so on. 

447. Benzene. The three hydrocarbons just described are gases under ordinary conditions; benzene, C.H., is ordinarily a liquid. This boils about 80° C. and crystallizes to a white solid that melts at 6° C. The simplest formula for benzene as obtained from its percentage composition would be CH, but the weight of the grammolecular volume of its vapor shows that the real formula is CH6. Chemists write its constitutional formula with its six carbon atoms in a "ring."

H-C C-H H. benzene

Benzene and toluene are the first two members of the benzene series of hydrocarbons. Several of the members of this series are obtained when soft coal is heated in byproduct coke ovens (§§ 271 and 289), and from the tar produced at the same time. Benzene derivatives are often spoken of as coal-tar products (§ 465). 

448. Petroleum.-As found in the earth, petroleum is a dark, oily liquid containing dissolved gases which escape 

FIG. 200. 

Part of Signal Hill oil field, near Long Beach, California. when the petroleum is pumped to the surface. It consists of a mixture of hydrocarbons. These can be separated by distillation into many commercial products.

The distillation of such a mixture is called "fractionation," and the liquids which distill over between certain temperatures are spoken of as fractions. Thus, in the distillation of petroleum a fraction which boils at about 18° C. can be obtained. Between 18° and 250° many fractions can be collected. These are, in succession, gasoline, benzine (not benzene), petroleum ether, ligroin, and kerosene. Some of these fractions are used as solvents in "dry cleaning," others as fuels. The higher-boiling fractions must be distilled at reduced pressure to prevent their "cracking." Thus are obtained lubricating oils, vaseline, and paraffin (white wax). Petroleum coke remains undistilled.

Before petroleum can be distilled it must be freed from impurities, especially from organic nitrogen and sulphur compounds. 

These are largely removed by shaking the crude petroleum first with concentrated sulphuric acid, then with sodium hydroxide solution, and finally with water. The refiner can collect the fractions from distillation between whatever temperatures he wishes. When petroleum was first refined, kerosene, used as a fuel and illuminant, was the most valuable product. Nowadays gasoline, used in gasoline engines, is far more important. To produce more gasoline, which has a low boiling point, the higher fractions (formerly used for kerosene) are "cracked." In this process large quantities of gases are produced which cannot be readily condensed to liquids. These may be used as fuel gas, but are far more valuable if converted into useful organic compounds. 

Thus propylene, C3H6, a member of the ethylene series, can be cheaply converted into isopropyl alcohol, a substance which was formerly expensive. Petroleum products are being converted more and more into organic materials of commercial value other than fuels. 

449. Natural gas.-Natural gas is obtained from the earth when wells are drilled into the underground pockets where it has collected. Much natural gas has a very large proportion of

methane. This burns with a colorless, hot flame which makes it very useful, not only for the heating of houses, but for factories, such as those for the manufacture of glass. Some natural gas contains considerable helium, which is used for filling the gas chambers of dirigible airships. Some natural gas contains a considerable amount of hydrocarbons that can easily be liquefied. 

These are used in low-boiling gasolines. 

450. Alcohol.-Common alcohol, such as is obtained from the fermentation of sugar, molasses, or grain, is known to the chemist as ethyl alcohol. It is a combustible liquid which boils at about 78° C. and which can be cooled to a very low temperature (about -114° C.) before it freezes. It is used in automobile radiators to dilute the water and prevent it from freezing in cold weather. Denatured alcohol is alcohol to which other substances have been added, so that it will not be used as a beverage.

In its chemical properties alcohol resembles water in that it reacts with sodium. Hydrogen is formed, as with water, but less rapidly. The other product is a white solid called sodium ethylate. Alcohol also behaves like the hydroxide of a metal in that it reacts with strong acids, such as sulphuric acid, to produce water and ethyl hydrogen sulphate, C₂H,HSO4. The equation for this reaction was given in § 445. Because of these reactions chemists give the formula C2H5OH to ethyl alcohol. 

Alcohol is not, however, a base; it does not affect litmus. 

Its constitutional formula shows that it is hydroxyethane.

H H H-C-C-O-H H H ethyl alcohol

There is a series of alcohols, as there is of hydrocarbons. Ethyl alcohol is related to ethane, methyl alcohol to methane, and propyl alcohol to propane. In the accompanying table these relations are shown.
