Methyl alcohol is obtained in the distillation of wood, and is also called wood alcohol. Both methyl and ethyl alcohols burn with colorless, hot flames. They are used as solvents for fats and gums and in the manufacture of varnishes and dyes. Ethyl alcohol is used in the preparation of artificial fruit essences and perfumes. It is also used to preserve specimens of organic material from decay. Because of its low freezing point it is used in alcohol thermometers. When alcohol is prepared by fermentation, small amounts of other alcohols of higher boiling point (see table) are also formed. The mixture is called fusel oil. The alcohol obtained by distillation contains about 95% alcohol and 5% water. Absolute alcohol, containing only a small proportion of water, is prepared by distilling alcohol from lime. 

Carbolic acid, also called phenol (C6H5OH), is an alcohol derived from benzene, as ethyl alcohol is derived from ethane. 

451. Ethyl ether.-Ethyl ether is also called sulphuric ether. It is prepared from ethyl alcohol and sulphuric acid. It is a colorless liquid having a sweet odor. It boils at 35° C., and its vapor is very inflammable. Because of this fact ether must be kept away from flames.

Ether is used as a solvent and as an anesthetic. Its simplest formula is (C2H5)2O. The chemist also calls it di-ethyl oxide and writes its formula C2H5-O-C₂H5. 

452. Organic compounds containing chlorine.-We have already learned (§ 133) that chlorine can act by substitution upon methane.

CH4+C12-CH3C1+HC1. 

The substitution of chlorine for hydrogen can be continued to produce the compounds CH2Cl2, CHCl3, and even CCl4. The last of these we call carbon tetrachloride (see § 275). The compound CHCl3 is chloroform. Chloral has the formula CC1,CHO. Iodoform has the formula CHI3. Iodoform is a yellow solid used as a dressing in surgery. 

If ethyl alcohol is treated with iodine and red phosphorus, and the mixture is then distilled, a heavy, colorless liquid is produced. This is ethyl iodide, C₂H¿I. If methyl alcohol is used instead of ethyl alcohol, methyl iodide (CH,I) is formed. These compounds resemble the methyl chloride (CH,Cl) formed when chlorine acts upon methane. In each case a hydrogen atom of a hydrocarbon molecule is replaced by a halogen atom. 

453. Aldehyde. We have learned in § 436 that if ethyl alcohol is mixed with a potassium dichromate solution containing dilute sulphuric acid, a great deal of heat is produced, and the alcohol is oxidized to a compound called aldehyde (C2H40). Review the equation.

H H 2H-C-C−OH + O2 · →2H-C H H alcohol + 2H2O aldehyde

Aldehyde boils at 21° C. and dissolves readily in water. 

It is used to "silver" mirrors. It precipitates silver from silver nitrate solution containing ammonia (§ 390), and is itself oxidized to acetic acid. 

2CH3CHO (aldehyde) + O2→2CH3COOH (acetic acid). 

Another common aldehyde is formaldehyde, H.CHO. 

It is produced when the vapor of methyl (wood) alcohol, mixed with air, is passed through a tube containing hot copper. Formalin is a 40% solution of gaseous formaldehyde in water. It is used as a disinfectant and preservative.

When formaldehyde is heated with carbolic acid, the two react to produce resins which are very valuable as substitutes for amber, ebony, and other substances. One brand is called bakelite, after its inventor. Another plastic made from formaldehyde is lactolith, or galalith. In this case formaldehyde reacts with casein (from milk). The solid produced is used as a substitute for ivory and celluloid.

Benzaldehyde is found in almonds and the pits of cherries and other stony fruits. It is called "oil of bitter almonds." When oxidized it gives benzoic acid, as aldehyde gives acetic acid. 2C6H5CHO+02→2C6H5COOH. 

Fehling's solution is a mixture used as a test for aldehydes, grape sugar, and similar organic reducing agents. It is made up in two solutions. For solution I dissolve 34.64 g. of blue vitriol and 1 cu. cm. of dilute sulphuric acid in water, and dilute the solution to exactly 500 cu. cm. For solution II dissolve 175 g. of sodium potassium tartrate and 50 g. of sodium hydroxide in water, and make the volume up to exactly 500 cu. cm. 

Solutions I and II should be kept separate, in bottles with rubber stoppers. Equal volumes (5 cu. cm. of each) are mixed just before they are used. The dark-blue mixture is heated to boiling, and the solution to be tested is added. If reduction

takes place, a precipitate of cuprous oxide (usually yellow or red) is formed. 

454. Acetic acid.—Acetic acid (CH3COOH) is the acid of vinegar. Vinegar is formed when fruit juices, such as those of the apple or grape, are allowed to ferment. At first the sugars present are changed by yeast into alcohol, carbon dioxide, and other products (§ 450). Then the dilute alcohol is fermented further (oxidized) by the vinegar mold to produce acetic acid.

On a commercial scale vinegar is prepared in barrels or vats containing shavings over which the fermented fruit juice trickles. The shavings are inoculated with vinegar mold. Acetic acid is also formed in the dry distillation of wood, along with methyl (wood) alcohol and acetone. H-C-C O-H acetic acid но H H-C-C-C-H H H acetone

Acetic acid, if anhydrous, forms colorless crystals melting at about 17° C. (glacial acetic acid). It is very soluble in water. 

It forms several important salts, such as sodium acetate, NaC2H3O2.3H₂O, and lead acetate, Pb(C2H3O2)2.3H₂O (§§ 245 and 405).

There is a long list of organic acids. Formic acid, HCOOH, is formed in the bodies of ants and stinging nettles. We prepare it from oxalic acid. Oxalic acid, H2C2O4.2H2O, forms large crystals, very soluble in water. It is a reducing agent because it can be oxidized to carbon dioxide. 2H2C2O4+02→2H2O+4CO2. 

Butyric acid is formed when butter becomes rancid. Benzoic acid, C6H5.COOH, is formed by the oxidation of benzaldehyde. 

Lactic acid, CH3.CHOH.COOH, is the acid found in sour milk,

dill pickles, and sauerkraut. It is formed in milk by the fermentation of the milk sugar. Tartaric acid, H2C4H4O6, is a white, crystalline solid. It is formed from cream of tartar, which is potassium hydrogen tartrate, KHC4H4O6. 

Citric acid is the acid of lemons, oranges, grape fruit, and other citrous fruits. It forms crystals of the composition H3C6H5O7. H₂O. 

Salicylic (sǎl'I-silk) acid is C&H4OH.COOH. It can be prepared from methyl salicylate, CH4OH.COOCH3, which is present in oil of wintergreen.

455. Fats and oils; esters.-Oils are low-melting fats.

Among the common oils are cottonseed oil, linseed oil (from flax), palm oil, olive oil, and corn oil. Among fats are those of butter, beef suet, mutton tallow, and lard. 

The fats and oils are neither acids nor alcohols, but when they are treated with superheated steam, they are · hydrolyzed, giving acids and glycerol. Glycerol is a complex alcohol, C3H5(OH)3. The acids produced by the hydrolysis of fats and oils are chiefly palmitic, stearic, and oleic acids. Butter fat also gives butyric acid.

Compounds which by hydrolysis give acids and alcohols are called esters.

C17H35COO-CH2 HOH C17H35COOH HOCH₂ C17H35COO-ÇH +HOH→C17H35COOH+HOCH C17H35COO-CH₂ HOH C17H35COOH HOCH₂ glyceryl stearate steam stearic acid glycerol Glyceryl stearate is called stearin; glyceryl palmitate is palmitin; glyceryl oleate is olein. These esters (and others) are mixed in the fats and oils. Olein is liquid at ordinary temperatures; consequently fats containing much of it are low-melting, or liquid. 

Coconut oil contains glyceryl laurate. The low-melting fats (oils) are changed into the high-melting ones by hydrogenation (§ 41) of their unsaturated molecules. 

An ester much simpler than a fat is formed from acetic acid and ethyl alcohol. It is ethyl acetate. CH3COOH+HOC2H5 →CH3COOC₂H¿+H₂O. acid alcohol ester The reaction is reversible, but by the use of concentrated sulphuric acid (to remove the water) it becomes nearly complete. 

The reaction of glycerol with nitric acid to produce glyceryl nitrate (nitroglycerine) is described in § 233. 

Ethyl acetate is a fragrant liquid. Many of the characteristic odors and tastes of fruit and flowers are due to esters. Thus amyl acetate has the odor and taste of bananas, and methyl salicylate gives its flavor to oil of wintergreen. 

456. Soap.-A soap is the salt of one of the higher organic acids, such as stearic, palmitic, or oleic acid. Our common, hard soaps are mixtures of the sodium salts of these acids. They are produced when fats are boiled with sodium hydroxide. Soft soaps consist of the potassium salts of the organic acids named. (C17H35COO)3C3H5+зNaOH →зC17H35COONa+C3H5(OH)3. sodium stearate glycerol stearin We say that the fats are "saponified" by the alkali. 

To saponify means to make into soap. We also apply

the term saponification to the hydrolysis of any ester, even without the presence of an alkali, as when a fat is converted by superheated steam into an acid and an alcohol (§ 455). 

33-1 SOAP KETTLE ROOM FIG. 201. 

Swift and Company. 

Soap Kettle Room. The kettles are three stories high. 

In the manufacture of soap large iron kettles are used. These may be three or four stories high and may hold ten to twelve carloads of soap. The kettles are lined with steam coils to furnish heat. The ingredients (fat and sodium hydroxide solution) are heated and stirred until the reaction is complete. Salt is then added to the mixture, and the soap rises to the top as a curdy The glycerine formed at the same time is largely in the salt solution at the bottom. mass.

If the soap is a laundry soap, the curdy mass is run from the kettle into a mold, or "frame," holding perhaps 1000 pounds of

soap. When the curd becomes solid, the sides of the mold are removed, and the large block of soap which remains is cut, by means of wire cutters, into slabs, then strips, and finally into cakes. These are dried, pressed into shape, and automatically wrapped.

When toilet soap is to be made, the liquid soap is dried on a screen, to produce chips. These are perfumed or colored, and then worked together in a mill until they form a smooth mixture. 

Finally the soap is cut into cakes which are pressed, dried, and wrapped. 

FIG. 202. 

Procter and Gamble Co. "Master bars" of soap, ready to be cut into cakes.

Floating soaps contain air bubbles which have been beaten into them. Transparent soaps may contain glycerine or sugar. 

If soap is dissolved in alcohol, and the excess alcohol is distilled off, a transparent soap is likewise obtained. A liquid soap usually contains glycerine or alcohol to make it flow.

When soap is dissolved in hard water, insoluble calcium or magnesium soaps are obtained. These form a "scum" (§ 66). 2C17H35COONa+CaSO4 Na2SO4 + (C17H35COO)2Ca ↓ . 

457. How soap cleanses.-Soap is used for two kinds. of cleaning: (1) to remove grease; (2) to remove dirt, such as soot or soil. When a soap solution is rubbed upon greasy cloth, it breaks up the grease into tiny droplets and surrounds them. The particles of grease are thus separated from the cloth and from one another, and can be rinsed away by the soapy water. The droplets of grease are dispersed through soapy water to form an emulsion, just as butter fat is dispersed through water in milk..

A soap solution is a colloidal suspension, not a true solution. Partial hydrolysis of the soap by the water produces the colloidal particles. Without the presence of such particles water does not emulsify grease. 

The presence of colloidal particles in soapy water also accounts for the ability of soap to remove dirt. The colloidal particles of soap scattered through the water have a very large surface and adsorb the dirt particles. 

Thus, if there is some powdered charcoal to be removed from a cloth, the colloidal particles of soap adsorb the charcoal, and the charcoal passes into the soapy water. 

458. Sugar. We have already learned (§§ 29 and 282) that sugars belong to the class of carbohydrates, and that they are produced by photosynthesis out of carbon dioxide and water. Our most common sugar is cane sugar, C12H22O11. The chemist calls it sucrose. It is the same substance whether it comes from the sugar cane, sugar beets, maple sap, honey, sorghum, or other sources.

There are many other sugars different from sucrose. 

Such are grape sugar, fruit sugar, malt sugar, and sugar of milk. Each of these is a distinct substance, with its own set of properties. Malt sugar (maltose) and milk sugar (lactose) have the same formula as cane sugar (C12H22O11), but they have different constitutional formulas. They are isomers (§ 444) of sucrose. 

While dilute fruit juices, such as those of apples, cherries, and grapes, ferment readily in air, cane sugar does not. In fact concentrated solutions of cane sugar are used in jams and jellies to preserve fruit from fermentation. In order that cane sugar may be fermented a special enzyme, or organic catalyst (called invertase) must be present. In the presence of invertase cane sugar is hydrolyzed, giving grape sugar (glucose) and fruit sugar (fructose). Each of these has the formula C6H12O6. C12H22O11+H2O →C6H12O6+C6H12O6. glucose fructose sucrose

The glucose and fructose may then be fermented by zymase, a ferment present in yeast. The chief products are alcohol and carbon dioxide (§ 283).

C6H12O6→2CO2 ↑ +2C2H¿O. 

Cane sugar is also hydrolyzed to glucose and fructose when heated with a dilute acid.

Sucrose melts at about 160° C. At about 200° it is changed into caramel. This is a brown substance used for coloring and flavoring foods, candies, and effervescent drinks.

Cane sugar is prepared from both sugar cane and beets. The cane is crushed between rollers, and the juice is treated with

calcium hydroxide to neutralize it and to make it clear. The purified sugar solution is filtered. It is then evaporated in "vacuum pans," so that the water boils off at about 65° C. At this temperature the sugar is not decomposed as it would be if the water were removed at ordinary pressure. The crystals of sugar separate out, and molasses remains. The two are separated by rotation in funnel-shaped sieves. The molasses escapes, and raw, or brown sugar remains. A second crop of sugar crystals is usually obtained from the molasses. 

We obtain sugar from sugar beets by slicing the beets and extracting the sugar with water. The impurities of the sugar solution are precipitated with slaked lime, and the purified solution is evaporated in vacuum pans, as in the case of cane sugar.

FIG. 203. 

The refining of raw sugar consists in redissolving the sugar in hot water, adding substances to clot the impurities, and then filtering the solution through animal charcoal (bone black). The charcoal adsorbs the coloring substances (§ 269), and produces a light-colored syrup. This is concentrated in vacuum pans, and cooled until the white sugar crystals separate out.

The large vacuum pans in the plant of the 

Savannah (Georgia) Sugar Refining Corporation. In these pans the sugar solution is boiled down until it "grains." 

459. Glucose and fructose.-Glucose is also called grape sugar and dextrose. It is a white, crystalline sugar which melts at 146° C. when anhydrous. It is less sweet than sucrose. Glucose is found in grapes and raisins and

in many plants. Glucose is a reducing agent. It reduces a silver nitrate solution (containing ammonia; § 390) to silver, and also reduces Fehling's solution (§ 453) to cuprous oxide. Cane sugar does not reduce these solutions.

When cane sugar is hydrolyzed (§ 458), the product (a mixture of glucose and fructose) is called invert sugar. Fructose is also formed by the hydrolysis of a starch called inulin, present in artichokes and dahlia roots. Fructose melts at 92-95°. It is very sweet. It is used as a substitute for sucrose. 

Glucose makes up about 20-30% of corn syrup, which is formed by the acid hydrolysis of starch. (C6H10O5)n (starch)+nH₂O →nC6H12O6 (glucose). 

Corn syrup is used in the manufacture of jellies, candies, and table syrup.

When corn syrup is evaporated, the product is corn sugar. This contains not only glucose, but maltose and dextrin. 

460. Starch. Starch is prepared in plants by photosynthesis and is stored up in roots, tubers, and seeds. 

It is prepared commercially from potatoes, Indian corn, wheat, and rice. Starch consists of tiny grains having varied markings according to its source. 

When starch is prepared from corn, the corn is soaked in water so that it swells up and softens, and the grains are then broken by coarse grinding. By this treatment the "germ" of the corn grain is loosened, and being light, floats; it can then be skimmed off from the rest of the grain. The "germs" are pressed, and yield corn oil. The remainder of the corn grain is then ground very fine, so that the starch grains are loosened. The outer coating (bran) is removed by cloth sieves, and the mass of starch and gluten (protein) is suspended in water, and run

slowly through long troughs. The starch grains are heavier than the gluten, and settle. They are removed and dried.

Starch is the most common carbohydrate used as food. Its grains are covered with a thin coating which protect them from the digestive fluids, consequently raw starch cannot be digested. 

Cooking bursts this coating. The digestive ferments then cause the hydrolysis of the starch, and glucose is formed. The glucose can be digested and used by the body. 

Bread is about half starch. Wheat flour is perhaps 70% starch, with about 15% each of water and gluten, besides a small amount of minerals. The paste formed when starch is cooked forms a colloidal suspension with water. We call it starch "solution." 

Iodine turns starch blue (§ 165). Starch is used for the "starching" of clothes, as a paste, and in the manufacture of cloth, paper, and glucose. 

461. Dextrin.-The dextrins are substances formed by the heating of starch, either by itself or in the presence of a dilute acid. As a result of the treatment the starch is changed to a sticky material; this is used as a mucilage for postage stamps and envelopes, as well as in the manufacture of textiles. 

When bread is baked, the outside is hotter than the inside, and its starch is changed to a dextrin, which forms the crust. 

The crust is sweet because of the dextrin it contains. 

462. Cellulose.-Cellulose is a carbohydrate having a composition similar to that of starch. We write its formula (C6H1005)x. It forms the framework of plants: the walls of plant cells. Wood, cotton, linen, hemp, and flax are largely cellulose. Filter paper is almost pure cellulose. Wood pulp is produced when logs of spruce or hemlock are pressed against rotating grindstones, so that the wood is finely shredded. Wood contains fibers of cellulose fastened together by a gummy substance called lignin. This may be removed by heating the ground

wood or chips with calcium bisulphite (§ 184). Thus the cellulose used for most of our paper is obtained. 

Cellulose is more inactive than starch and sugar, but by hydrolysis it can be partly converted into glucose. 

Cellulose is insoluble in most solvents, even in hot sodium hydroxide solution. If a cloth containing cotton and wool is boiled with sodium hydroxide solution, the wool disappears, while the cotton remains. Mercerized cotton is cotton which has been treated with sodium hydroxide, and then washed and stretched before drying. It has a silky appearance.

Cellulose is an alcohol, although a very complex one. It therefore forms esters (§ 455). If we use the simple formula C6H1005 for cellulose, the reaction with concentrated nitric acid and sulphuric acid is

C&H10O5+3HNO3 →C&H7O2. (NO3)3+3H2O. 

The sulphuric acid removes the water, as in the formation of nitroglycerine. The cellulose nitrates were described in § 233. 

The nitrates of cellulose are often called pyroxylin (pi-rŎx'Ï-lin). 

This is used to prepare lacquers like "Duco," varnishes, cements, and artificial leather. When dissolved in suitable solvents and then deposited in films, cellulose nitrates (pyroxylin) form movingpicture films. The non-combustible films are made of cellulose acetates instead of nitrates. 

463. Rayon.-Rayon is a fabric made out of cellulose. 

The short fibers called cotton linters and the fibers of wood pulp are useless for cloth, but if their cellulose is first converted into a soluble form and then forced through a small nozzle, it forms long threads which can be woven into cloth. The cellulose is somewhat altered in the treatment, so that it gains the appearance of silk. 

Hence it is used as a silk substitute.

The most important process for producing rayon is the viscose process. Wood pulp or cotton linters are treated with sodium hydroxide solution and then with carbon disulphide. The product is a viscous liquid, known as viscose. This is pressed through nozzles into dilute sulphuric acid, in which the cellulose is "regenerated." It may be in the form of threads or ribbons. 

When the cellulose is regenerated as sheets, a transparent material called cellophane is produced. This is used as a wrapping for candy and foods (sausage casings) and as a protective cover for the mouths of bottles. 

464. Paper. Paper consists of a mat of threads of cellulose. It may be made from linen rags, wood pulp, cornstalks, straw, and other fibrous materials. The best paper is made from linen rags. Most of our ordinary paper is manufactured from bleached wood pulp, after the lignin of the wood has been removed. Several processes are necessary. 

1. The pulp is "beaten" with water so that the threads of cellulose become very fine. It is then floated upon sieves and forced between heated rollers to dry it and produce continuous sheets. Thus we get blotting paper and tissue paper. 

2. Often a "filler" of clay or gypsum is added to produce a closer texture, as in glazed wrapping paper. Newspaper "stock' is mostly ground wood, mixed with some "chemical" wood pulp obtained by the bisulphite process. 

3. Writing paper must be "sized," so that the ink will not spread. Rosin or glue is used as sizing material. 

4. The paper receives a final polish by being run through rollers or "calenders."

Parchment paper is paper which has been treated with sulphuric acid, so that its cellulose is changed into a tough material. 

465. Nitrobenzene and aniline.-When we treat gly-

cerol or cellulose with a mixture of nitric and sulphuric acids, we obtain glyceryl nitrate or cellulose nitrate. 

When we treat the hydrocarbon benzene with the same mixture, we obtain nitrobenzene, C¿H¿NO2. This is a yellow liquid boiling at 210° C. 

C&H6+HNO3-C6H5NO2+H2O. 

If we treat toluene, C6H5CHз, with the nitric-sulphuric acid mixture, nitrotoluene is formed. CH3C6H5+HNO3 →CH3C6H4NO2+H2O. 

If the treatment is continued, we obtain trinitrotoluene, the explosive known as T.N.T. This is a colorless solid melting at 82° C.

CH3C6H5+3HNO3 →CH¿C6H2(NO2)3+3H2O. 

When we reduce nitrobenzene with active (nascent) hydrogen, we obtain aniline, C6H5NH2. C6H5NO2+3H2 →C6H5NH2+2H2O. 

Aniline is a colorless liquid which unites with acids directly, as ammonia does, to form salts.

C6H5NH2+HC1→C6H5NH2.HCl (or C6H5NH¸C1). 

We may call aniline "phenylamine," to show that it is ammonia with hydrogen replaced by the C&H group. There are many other amines, such as methylamine (CH3NH2) and ethylamine (C2H5NH2). From aniline and the corresponding toluene derivative the dyes known as aniline dyes can be obtained. 

466. Classes of foods. We have already learned of the elements that make up our bodies (§ 29). We must replenish these from the food we eat. We need food for three main reasons: (1) to give us energy; (2) to furnish

materials for growth and repair; (3) to regulate the action of the organs of the body. Only substances which serve these purposes can be called foods. 

We may classify foods under six heads: 

1. Carbohydrates 

2. Fats 

3. Proteins 

5. Water 

4. Minerals 

6. Vitamins

The carbohydrates and fats contain only carbon, hydrogen, and oxygen. They give us muscular energy and heat. They are oxidized as we use them. What are the products? The fats have a larger proportion of carbon and hydrogen than the carbohydrates, and yield more energy. A pound of fat produces 24 times as much heat as a pound of starch or sugar. An excess of fats or carbohydrates in our food is usually stored as fat. This gives the body a reserve of fuel for use when needed. 

Saccharin, C6H4.CO.SO2.NH, has about 500 times the sweetening power of cane sugar. It is used as a sugar substitute, but has no food value. 

FIG. 204. 

Ira Remsen (1846-1927). Professor and President, The Johns Hopkins 

University. Eminent author and teacher. Discoverer of saccharin.

Proteins are called nitrogen foods. They contain carbon, hydrogen, oxygen, nitrogen, sulphur, and other constituents. They furnish some energy when oxidized, but we eat them chiefly for the growth and repair of the cells. We obtain them in milk, fish, lean meat, cheese,

eggs, nuts, peas, and beans. Proteins are present in grains, and therefore in foods made with flour; but these foods do not contain enough for our needs unless we eat them in very large quantities. 

Minerals are furnished by most of our foods. They contain the elements needed for growth, as of bones and of teeth, and for regulating the body. We need to make a special effort to get enough phosphorus, calcium, and iron. We must likewise see to it that we get enough water, in addition to what we obtain from soups and milk. 

467. Vitamins.-Vitamins are also called food-accessory substances. They are found in very small amounts in our foods, but their absence produces illness or retarded growth. Vitamin A is found in milk and egg yolk, in cod-liver oil, and in vegetables like spinach. A shortage of this vitamin causes children to be stunted and weak, to have a poor resistance against disease, and sometimes to have weak eyes.

Vitamin B is found in whole grains, such as oatmeal, graham flour, and unpolished rice, also in cabbage, tomatoes, beans, potatoes, milk, and eggs. It is not found in "refined" foods, such as white flour, starch, polished rice, lard, vegetable fats, or sugars.

Vitamin B seems to consist of two parts: Vitamin F and vitamin G. 

Lack of these in proper proportions seems to cause beri-beri and pellagra, as well as loss of appetite and slow growth. 

Vitamin C is found most abundantly in fresh fruits and vegetables. It disappears from foods which have been kept through a long winter, or cooked too long. Tomatoes, even when canned in tin, are a rich source of this vitamin. Scurvy often results from a shortage of it.

Vitamin D enables the body to use the calcium and phosphorus of food most efficiently for bone building. It prevents rickets, which shows itself in bow legs, enlarged knees and ankles, and slow teething. Cod-liver oil is an excellent source of it.

Vitamin E is necessary for the proper development of the young of the higher animals. 

468. Summary.-Hydrocarbons of the methane series have the general formula CnH2n+2. They are saturated hydrocarbons. 

Constitutional formulas show what atoms in a molecule are attached to one another. 

Isomers are compounds with the same formula but different arrangements of the atoms in the molecules. 

Ethylene and acetylene are unsaturated hydrocarbons. By uniting with sufficient hydrogen they form saturated hydrocarbons of the methane series. 

Benzene has a six-carbon "ring." Toluene is methylbenzene.

Both are obtained in the distillation of coal in by-product coke ovens.

Petroleum is a mixture of gaseous, liquid, and solid hydrocarbons.

Ethyl alcohol is ethyl hydroxide. Its molecule contains an (OH) group in place of one hydrogen atom of ethane. 

Ether is di-ethyl oxide. Its molecule has two ethyl radicals united by an atom of oxygen. 

Methyl chloride is a chlorine substitution product of methane. 

Chloroform is trichlormethane. 

Ethyl alcohol is oxidized to acetaldehyde; methyl alcohol is oxidized to formaldehyde. 

Aldehydes are reducing agents. 

They are oxidized to acids. 

Acetic acid is present in vinegar. It is formed by the oxidation of the ethyl alcohol in fermented fruit juices. The oxidation takes place in the presence of the vinegar mold. 

Fats and oils are esters. By hydrolysis, esters yield acids and alcohols. The alcohol obtained from fats is glycerol. 

Soap is the sodium or potassium salt of the acids obtained by hydrolysis of the fats. It is made from fats by the action of an alkali. 

Soap cleanses: (1) because soapy water forms an emulsion with grease; (2) because the colloidal particles of soap absorb solid dirt. 

Sugars, starch, and cellulose are carbohydrates. 

Cane sugar is obtained from cane and beets. It is not fer-

mented by zymase (from yeast) until it is first hydrolyzed to glucose and fructose.

Glucose and fructose are isomers. Both are fermented by yeast. 

Starch is our most common carbohydrate food. It is hydrolyzed by dilute acids, producing glucose. When heated, it gives dextrins. A dilute solution of iodine turns starch blue. 

Cellulose forms the cell walls of plants. It is more inactive than sugar or starch, but is hydrolyzed, giving glucose. 

Cellulose is an alcohol, and forms esters with acids. Cellulose nitrates are present in gun cotton, collodion, celluloid, and cellulose varnishes.

Rayon is a fabric made by converting cellulose into soluble or suspended form and then regenerating the cellulose in threads or ribbon.

Paper is a mat of threads of cellulose. Writing paper must be filled and sized. 

Nitrobenzene is prepared from benzene and the nitric-sulphuric acid mixture. It is a substitution product of benzene. 

Aniline is obtained by the reduction of nitrobenzene. It is phenylamine. Amines are substitution products of ammonia. 

The classes of foods are carbohydrates, fats, proteins, minerals, water, and vitamins.

We must plan our meals wisely if we are to obtain the foods needed for normal health and growth.

APPENDIX CORRECTION OF THE BAROMETER READING 

In careful work with gases the reading of the barometer must usually be corrected before it can be used in calculation. (1) Correction for temperature.-The standard height of the barometer is 760 mm. when the temperature is O°C. Mercury expands when heated and contracts when cooled. Thus the mercury column in the barometer is 3 mm. higher at 23°C. than at O°C. This amount should be subtracted from the barometer reading at 23°C., if the volume is to be reduced to O°C. The table of these corrections is in Appendix XIII. For some work the correction is considered too small, and is omitted. (2) Correction for water vapor.-When a gas is measured over water, we must correct the barometer reading for the pressure of water vapor. This is a larger correction than that for temperature, and is almost always applied. To understand the reason for this correction we must realize that when a vessel containing a gas stands over water some of the water in contact with the gas turns into vapor, just as water evaporates into the air. We say that the water vapor saturates the space occupied by the gas. This water vapor has pressure.

The pressure of a gas collected over water is thus the sum of two partial pressures: one due to the water vapor and one due to the dry gas itself. The barometer reading gives the sum of the two pressures. In order to get the true pressure of the dry gas we must subtract the pressure of the water vapor from that shown by the barometer.

The pressure of water vapor is often called aqueous tension; it increases with the temperature. At O°C. it is 4.6 mm. (both for ice and water), at 100°C. it is 760 mm. (both for water and steam). 545

The table of the pressure of water vapor is given in Appendix XII. 

When a gas is collected over mercury, the pressure of mercury vapor is considered O and is disregarded. 

Suppose that a gas stands over water, and its temperature is 

22° while the barometer reading is 745 mm. What is the correct pressure of the dry gas? By looking at the table we find that at 

22°C. the pressure of water vapor is 19.7 mm. The nearest whole number (20 mm.) is sufficiently correct. This amount is subtracted from 745. The correct pressure of the dry gas is, therefore, 725. It is this number that is used in calculating the volume.

TABLE VIII. HARDNESS OF SUBSTANCES 

We compare the hardness of two bodies by rubbing them together; the one having the greater hardness scratches or indents the other. In the scale of hardness bodies are usually between 1 and 10. The diamond is the only substance of hardness 10. 

Substances that can be indented by the finger nail have a hardness of less than 1. A substance of hardness 6.5 is scratched by a substance of hardness 7, but scratches a substance of hardness 6. 

The list below is taken from the Smithsonian Tables, as they are given in the Handbook of Chemistry and Physics, published by the 

Chemical Rubber Company, Cleveland, Ohio. The Handbook has a longer list.
